# PEG-LEG GREG — BOOK IV — ACT III: THE FARTHER ROAD

**Manuscript-only NotebookLM source. Chapters 389–440.**

# CHAPTER 389

## THE TRAVELER

I left Merecross without buying the Night Rest.

This should be recorded as character growth.

Possibly illness.

Morning after Glasswake smelled like wet stone, burned lamp oil, river mud, and people regretting decisions involving alcohol.

Merecross looked tired.

Beautiful still.

Less impossible.

Silver residue clung under bridge arches and along gutter seams, faint enough now that ordinary sunlight could compete.

People swept festival paper from streets.

Workers took down temporary rails.

One drunk man slept beneath sign reading KEEP LANDING CLEAR.

City returned to itself badly.

I had things to do.

First, oval.

River Survey clerk gave me a square lockbox, receipt board, and expression implying this was less interesting than breakfast.

Good.

Vessa stood beside me.

"Wrapped?"

"Yes."

"Any response since night?"

"No."

"Any deliberate test?"

"No."

"Good."

I placed oval inside without unwrapping.

Clerk sealed box with wax strip and stamped receipt twice.

One copy Survey Office.

One me.

Storage term: held until Greg returns or written transfer authorized.

No storage fee while assigned River Survey field crew.

That felt generous until I remembered city was preventing me from taking unknown threshold object into old reflective lock infrastructure.

Mutual benefit.

I signed.

Oval stayed Merecross.

Second, transit.

Pavin was awake somehow.

Unfair.

His hair disagreed.

"Field crew?"

"If Maro clears route."

"Transit wagon return still unavailable today. North ford vehicle status unchanged from last posted restriction. Your covered lodging ends when you sign Survey crew roll because at that point you're no longer waiting on contracted return."

"Reasonable."

"Room charge through morning covered. Anything after noon yours unless Survey takes it."

"I'm checking out."

"Then nothing owed."

He stamped transit paper CLOSED BY VOLUNTARY ASSIGNMENT.

Not stranded.

Not sent home.

Changed status.

I liked that.

"South span?"

"Still closed."

"Cause?"

"Still engineers."

Excellent.

No miracle bridge repair because protagonist needed travel.

Third, letter.

East dispatch clerk accepted folded note to Cala/Lyssa/Tavi.

"Bell Ford runner at third bell. East cart after if road open."

"Good."

"Return reply?"

"No address past Merecross yet."

I wrote Survey Office as temporary routing point and added FIELD CREW / HOLD IF DEPARTED.

Not instant communication.

Enough.

Then food.

Cala had trained fear into me remotely.

I ate.

Twice maybe.

No audit.

At first bell plus some, Survey yard filled.

Not rushed.

Compressed.

Different.

Cases went cart.

Blue gauges.

Black river-light.

Red repair.

Plain wood camp.

My normal load smaller than it had been three days ago because leaving made bullshit obvious.

One spare shirt.

One spare sock.

Soap.

Notebook.

Pencils.

Repair wrap.

Letters now reduced because latest gone east.

Tavi rock.

Papers.

Money.

No oval.

No Night Rest.

Still no spoon.

Kel checked harness.

"New buckle."

"Baret."

"Good."

That was entire inspection from him.

Sori checked my documents longer.

"Oval receipt."

I handed.

"Transit closeout."

Handed.

"Field application."

Handed.

"Emergency contacts."

"On application."

She looked.

"Yes."

"Anything else?"

"You tell me."

Trap.

I thought.

"My field start payment happens at crew roll, not before."

"Good."

"Return pass is Merecross."

"Good."

"Personal detours don't count paid field days."

"Good."

"Oval cannot travel."

"You have learned repetition."

"Painfully."

Orin appeared carrying stack of permit folders.

"You leaving?"

"Trying."

He looked at cart.

Then me.

"I still hate you."

"You started work early."

"Sori said if I was late after losing field slot she'd make me alphabetize wet permits."

Sori from table said, "I said damp."

"Worse."

He handed me folded paper.

"For road."

"What?"

"List of places at Alder Basin not to eat."

"You've been?"

"Once."

"Useful."

"No. One place burned noodles. Another served me fish with eye facing up. I found that accusatory."

"Scientific route intelligence."

"Exactly."

I put paper away.

He looked more serious.

"Come back."

Simple.

"Planning to."

"Good. Because if Sori keeps me another week I need someone else to annoy her."

Sori: "I can hear you."

"I know."

Then Maro came in fast.

Everyone looked.

His boots were wet to ankle.

"North Glass Cut daylight read is usable."

There.

Vessa did not smile.

"Define."

"Primary maintained path open. Reflective edge displacement elevated on east-facing stone between Cut markers nine and fourteen. Two old waste shelves still throwing afterimage under oblique sun. No black pockets observed in Cut channel this morning. River skiff window to Alder Basin favorable until late afternoon."

Dask said, "No black pockets observed is not none."

Maro looked at him.

"Yes, Dask."

"Good."

Vessa: "Route change?"

"Minor. We skip east shelf shortcut. Maintained path only. Slower by maybe half bell."

"West bank mud?"

"Below warning mark."

"Lock notice?"

Sori answered.

"West Locks accepts current survey priority. No chamber entry. North Glass Cut access window stamped through tomorrow second bell."

Vessa nodded.

"We roll second bell."

There.

Adventure scheduled.

Of course paperwork survived.

Then one problem appeared.

Camp case two was too heavy.

Not catastrophic.

Annoying.

Kel weighed by hanging scale.

"Twenty-three over cart rear allowance after water barrels."

Dask said, "Twenty-three pounds?"

"Yes."

"That's not subtle."

"Thank you."

Sori checked manifest.

"Food crate increased after departure shift."

Vessa: "What changed?"

"Two extra travel-delay meals per person added because route window uncertain."

"Necessary."

Kel: "Then something moves front or gets left."

Everyone began talking.

Not chaos.

Crew problem.

Red repair case maybe front.

Gauge case cannot.

Water cannot.

Camp poles could split.

Food could move partly passenger bench but then boarding clearance changed.

Sori worried seal custody.

Dask worried vibration on gauges.

Maro said black cases should stay shaded.

Vessa asked Kel rear axle margin.

He started explaining.

I listened.

Normally I would have joined eventually.

Made joke.

Asked two questions.

Let people solve specialty.

Instead something clicked.

Not magic.

Older.

Very old.

The part of me that used to organize six people before dawn because something larger wanted us dead by lunch.

I stopped being comfortable.

Not tense.

Narrow.

"Kel. Rear excess twenty-three. How much front margin?"

He looked at me.

"Fourteen safe without changing steering feel."

"Passenger bench floor capacity if aisle stays clear?"

"Eight, maybe ten if low."

"Sori, which sealed loads cannot split?"

"Food can split before crew roll if both subcrates resealed and manifest amended. Gauge cannot. Sample empty cases can. Repair can but Vessa hates it."

"Vessa?"

"I hate it because red case is designed one-location response."

"Keep red intact. Maro, black-case shade requirement is direct sun or heat generally?"

"Direct sun. Moderate ambient fine."

"Kel, can black case go passenger floor low without blocking Greg transfer?"

He looked cart.

Measured with eyes.

Then actual hand span.

"One can. Not both."

"We only need nine more after front fourteen if rear excess twenty-three. Put one black case passenger floor if Maro accepts. Split food nine pounds into front sealed subcrate, rest stays rear. That moves twenty-three total without touching gauges, repair, water, or aisle."

Silence.

Not dramatic silence.

People calculating.

Sori said, "Food subcrate paperwork takes three minutes."

Maro: "Black case on floor needs cloth cover from direct door light during stops."

Kel: "Weight works."

Vessa looked at me.

"Do it."

Everyone moved.

I moved too.

No glow.

No S-class aura.

Just problem gone.

Orin, standing nearby with permit folders, stared.

"You could have done that during trial?"

I looked at him.

"Probably."

"Why didn't you?"

"Didn't need to."

There.

He made face.

"That's irritating."

"Yes."

Vessa heard.

Of course.

"Greg."

"Yes?"

"That mode useful."

"Sometimes."

"Do not save it for entertainment."

Fair.

"I wasn't."

"Then what?"

I thought.

"Most problems don't need everything I have."

Vessa held my eyes one second longer.

"Field sometimes does."

There.

Nudge.

Not promise.

Crew finished repack before second bell.

Sori amended manifest.

Kel reweighed.

Rear within margin.

Front within margin.

Passenger aisle clear.

Maro black case covered.

No one applauded.

Good.

S-class had never meant applause.

Mostly paperwork after surviving.

Crew roll happened under yard awning.

Vessa read names.

Vessa Harn.

Maro Pell.

Sori Vale.

Dask Renn.

Kel Toren.

Greg.

We answered.

Sori handed me six copper.

Seventeen to twenty-three.

Field start.

Actual.

Then she handed pay receipt.

Of course.

"Sign."

I signed.

Cash twenty-three.

Vale twenty-three.

Symmetry offensive.

"You planning pay debt from road?" Sori asked.

"Different Vale."

"I know."

"Then no."

"Good."

Kel loaded my crutches into socket for first cart boarding.

I used fixed strap.

Right foot.

Settle.

No heroics.

Vessa took front bench.

Kel driver.

Maro beside equipment.

Dask immediately complained black case was six inches from where he expected.

Sori said expectation was not manifest field.

Traveling crew.

We rolled.

Merecross North Gate approached slowly because first morning after Glasswake traffic was stupid.

Festival carts leaving.

Supply carts entering.

Pilgrims maybe.

Tourists certainly.

A family carrying six mirror masks and one screaming baby.

City walls showed pale residue in cracks like frost made of light.

At gate, guard checked Survey priority.

Then waved us through.

Simple.

No ceremony.

One second inside Merecross.

Next outside.

That was all.

I turned anyway.

Merecross rose behind us layered around river.

Bridges.

Terraces.

Glasswake residue still silver under center span.

Not home.

Already somewhere I had people.

Dema.

Orin.

Tessa and impossible twenty-eight-copper object.

Pavin.

That was dangerous too.

Accumulation happened fast if you let it.

Road north followed river first.

Not Carrow road.

Wider.

Hard-packed center with drainage stones every twenty paces.

Silver flags from Bell Ford route disappeared. Here roadside posts carried dark green ribbons tied beneath little ceramic caps.

I asked.

Maro said, "Flood gnats. Caps hold bitter resin when warm. Ribbons show whether roadside air is still enough for swarms."

"Do they work?"

"Enough that road office keeps buying them. Ask entomologist for biology."

Good.

Dask said, "They're not gnats technically."

Sori: "Do not."

"But they aren't."

"We just left city."

Kel laughed.

First mile.

Fields replaced warehouses.

Low stone walls.

Wet grain terraces.

Fruit trees with white cloth tied around trunks.

Not decoration.

Maybe pests.

Maybe Glasswake residue.

I did not ask everything.

Growth.

River beside road still carried afterlight.

Not full sheets.

Occasional silver underside where current hit rocks.

At one bend, sunlight struck water and produced a vertical pale line fifteen feet above river before fading.

Everyone except me ignored.

I stared.

Sori noticed.

"You can look."

"Maro said work later."

"We're traveling."

Permission.

I looked.

Good.

Around third mile, road changed.

Post-wake traffic jam.

Not city crowd.

Three freight wagons stopped ahead at narrow stone causeway over marsh channel.

One wagon angled wrong.

Horse team nervous.

People shouting.

Kel slowed.

Vessa stood enough to see.

"What?"

Road worker waved.

"Causeway edge marker is throwing double line. Drivers won't cross loaded until Road reads it."

Maro leaned.

"Glass residue?"

"Probably."

Dask made noise.

Worker corrected himself immediately.

"Possible Glass residue."

Dask relaxed.

Terrible influence.

Vessa asked, "Delay?"

"Road reader coming from North Post. Half bell maybe."

Half bell mattered because skiff window Alder Basin favorable until late afternoon.

Not crisis.

Pressure.

Vessa looked route map.

"Alternate?"

Kel: "West farm track adds bell and mud."

Maro: "Not worth unless delay passes half."

We waited.

Comfortable Greg returned.

No need everything.

I ate travel bread.

Dask complained crumbs near gauge lid.

Sori told him lid was closed.

Then horse on front wagon sidestepped hard because causeway double line shifted.

Driver cursed.

Nobody fell.

Road worker moved people back.

I watched marker.

Two pale vertical edges at causeway lip.

One real marker post.

One light image offset maybe four inches riverward.

West service stairs again, larger consequence.

I planted crutch tip on road beside cart.

Solid.

No reason to intervene.

Then Vessa asked, "Greg. What did you do on West Stairs?"

There.

"Contact before trust. Crutch tip established physical tread edge when light displaced visual edge."

"Can that help driver?"

"Maybe identify actual causeway boundary at one point. Not certify load surface."

"Road worker?"

She called him.

He listened.

"We use sounding rods for that when reader arrives."

"Then wait for reader," I said.

Vessa nodded.

No protagonist shortcut.

Five minutes later Road reader arrived with two assistants and exactly a sounding rod.

Good world.

They confirmed visual displacement, not physical edge movement, then walked each wagon across one at time with guide rope.

We lost less than half bell.

Travel resumed.

Greg did not solve because existing system already knew how.

Also useful.

At midday we stopped under long shelter built against rock face.

Not inn.

Road rest.

Stone trough.

Bench.

Roof.

Posted distances:

MERECROSS 4
ALDER BASIN 11
NORTH GLASS CUT TURN 7

Units not explicitly labeled beyond local road convention.

Do not universalize.

Eleven to Basin.

We would not reach by cart alone today because skiff connection ahead shortened route.

Kel watered horse.

Maro checked river.

Sori ate standing.

Dask rearranged pencils by length for no reason he admitted.

Vessa sat beside me.

"Earlier. Cart load."

"Yes."

"Was that first-life habit?"

"Yes."

"Why don't you work like that all time?"

There.

User question in world.

I considered lying because answer sounded arrogant.

"Because it's expensive."

"Mana?"

"No. Me."

She waited.

"Attention. Patience. Temper. I get narrower. Faster. I stop giving things room they usually deserve. People too. Good when situation needs output. Bad way to live."

Vessa nodded slowly.

"Can you choose it?"

"Usually."

"Usually?"

"Sometimes it chooses me first."

"And power?"

I looked at her.

"Different switch. Related."

"Current body?"

"Current body limits output. Doesn't erase method."

That was closest truth.

First life S-class was not one spell.

It was thousands of decisions made quickly enough that people called result talent.

Mana mattered.

Body mattered.

Experience mattered.

And when everything aligned, I had once been very difficult to stop.

Current me had one leg, Bronze reserves, unreliable external effects, and shoulder soreness from festival work.

Also me.

Vessa said, "I don't need S-class."

"Good."

"I may need you locked in."

"Then say so."

"I will."

Simple.

Contract expanded without paper.

Probably Sori would hate that phrasing.

At rest end, Dask approached.

"Four-attempt comparison eventually."

"Eventually."

"Not today."

"Good."

"I dislike agreeing."

"Practice."

We rolled again.

Road left river after another mile and entered low marsh country.

New smell.

Peat.

Wet grass.

Bitter resin from roadside caps warming in sun.

Small dark insects gathered in places where green ribbons hung limp.

Flood not-gnats technically.

Dask opened mouth.

Sori pointed pencil at him.

He closed.

Growth everywhere.

At afternoon junction, river returned wider and slower.

Alder skiff landing waited below road.

Two long cargo skiffs tied under reed roof.

Kel stopped cart.

"This is where wheels stop."

There.

Actual travel change.

Cases would transfer to skiff.

Horse/cart stored under Survey contract and retrieved later by local driver? Kel explained cart itself would continue via road with hired basin porter, while core crew and required cases used river shortcut to Alder Basin.

Not all gear.

Sori checked transfer manifest.

Vessa assigned loads.

I looked at boat.

Orin would hate this.

I smiled.

Kel saw.

"What?"

"Thinking of someone."

"Bad sign."

We began transfer.

My crutches into socket.

Dock post.

Kel forearm.

Right foot.

Hull moved.

Freeze.

Settle.

Routine now.

Not graceful.

Competent enough.

Maro boarded after.

Dask nearly forgot black notebook on cart and Sori caught him.

"I was testing you."

"You fail tests you invent."

Vessa took bow seat.

Kel stern control.

Cargo centered.

Sori custody.

Dask gauges.

Maro river.

Me records.

Traveler.

Merecross no longer visible.

Carrow much farther still.

Ahead river narrowed between reed banks toward Alder Basin.

First time in this life I had left one city for another place without knowing exactly when I would reverse direction.

Not lost.

Not exiled.

Not transported by somebody else's disaster.

Going.

I liked word.

Kel pushed skiff away from dock.

Current took us.

Adventure was quieter than expected.

Mostly water against wood.

Good.

I had learned not to distrust quiet just because door was open.

# CHAPTER 390

## THE PASSENGER

Being on a boat was easier when nobody expected me to get on or off it.

This was my first major finding of field work.

Kel handled stern.

Vessa watched river ahead.

Maro watched river everywhere else.

Dask watched gauges.

Sori watched Dask.

I sat with record case wedged against right thigh and watched reeds.

Excellent job.

Reeds were tall enough to hide houses.

Sometimes they did.

A roof would appear behind green wall, then chimney, then whole little riverside house on posts, then disappear again.

Some had narrow docks.

Some had cages hanging beneath docks just above water.

Fish traps, probably.

Could ask.

Did not.

I was growing.

Tavi would hate it.

River after Glasswake still carried wrong light.

Not dramatic like Merecross.

Thin pale seams moved under brown-green water, usually near shade.

Every so often one climbed reed stalk for few inches and vanished.

Maro noticed me watching.

"Residue."

"I wasn't asking."

"Didn't say you were."

Maro pointed toward next bend.

"Out here it catches different. Less cut stone. More root, mud, old channel glass."

"Channel glass?"

"There. Asking face."

Fuck.

He smiled slightly.

"Old flood deposits. Small reflective mineral lenses mixed into clay. Some natural. Some from older works upstream. Depends where."

Dask looked up.

"Calling all of it channel glass is imprecise."

"Calling all of it anything else takes afternoon."

"Classification matters."

Sori said, "Not to passenger."

Passenger.

There.

I liked it.

Nobody needed me for next twenty minutes.

Maybe thirty.

I leaned against side and watched world move.

That used to bother me.

First life, late enough, idle travel became wasted time.

Train mana.

Review route.

Check equipment.

Sleep if efficient.

Eat if needed.

Talk only if useful.

There had always been something I could turn into preparation.

Because there was always another fight.

Another rank.

Another contract.

Another thing that would kill someone if I had been lazy at wrong hour.

Now I had a fat account in a bank nobody else could see.

Not power exactly.

Proof.

I had been good once.

Very good.

Strong enough that entire categories of fear stopped feeling permanent.

That did not mean this body could cash every check old me had written.

It couldn't.

Bronze.

One leg.

External effects unreliable enough I still remembered every clean one.

Shoulders that got sore.

Hands that blistered.

Mana that ended.

But account existed.

Method.

Judgment.

Memory of what improvement felt like from inside.

I knew, somewhere under all current inconvenience, that if life cornered me badly enough I could start withdrawing.

Train harder.

Study narrower.

Turn days into repetitions.

Turn repetitions into reliable effects.

Turn reliable effects into combinations.

Fix body problems one by one where they could be fixed.

Work around ones that couldn't.

Climb.

Maybe not into same man.

Probably shouldn't.

But into some version of him.

Enough.

That knowledge made laziness feel safer.

Not stupidity.

Not negligence.

Room.

I did not need to squeeze every day until it screamed.

If I needed more from myself later, I knew where more lived.

That was worth something.

Retirement fund.

Do not touch unless world becomes expensive.

I smiled.

Sori looked over.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You smiled at reeds."

"Good reeds."

She returned to papers.

"Passenger."

Perfect.

Then river narrowed.

Kel stopped humming.

Hadn't noticed he was humming until he stopped.

Vessa shifted forward.

Maro put hand on gunwale.

Not alarm.

Attention.

I sat up.

Ahead, two skiffs waited along west bank.

Not tied.

Held by poles.

One carried baskets stacked under wax cloth.

Other carried three people and a goat.

Goat looked furious.

River between them and opposite bank seemed normal.

Mostly.

Except a line of reeds lay flat across water.

Not broken.

Bent.

All pointing upstream.

Kel said, "Root drag."

Maro said, "Maybe."

Dask sighed.

"Thank you."

Maro ignored him.

Vessa called to nearest boatman.

"How long?"

Older woman standing with pole answered.

"Started after first bell. Opened twice. Closed again when grain boat tried middle."

"Bottom snag?"

"Nothing on pole."

"Current pull?"

"Not enough to explain reeds."

Kel slowed us before waiting boats.

"We hold."

Fine.

Field adventure.

Waiting in boat.

Goat screamed.

I respected it.

Maro took narrow brass disk from black case.

Not artifact exactly.

Tool.

Maybe magical tool.

Everything here had categories I did not know yet.

He hung disk from cord over water without touching surface.

Disk turned quarter circle.

Stopped.

Turned back.

Dask wrote time.

Maro moved three feet downstream and repeated.

Different response.

Vessa asked, "Passable?"

"Not enough yet."

Boatman with goat said, "I crossed this every day for nine years."

Kel said, "Then waiting one morning won't ruin streak."

"Goat disagrees."

Goat screamed again.

Kel nodded.

"Strong position."

I looked at flattened reeds.

Old me supplied possibilities automatically.

Subsurface flow reversal.

Root mat catching return.

Something pulling mana gradient.

Predator.

Buried structure.

Artifact.

None earned.

So I shut up.

That was competence too.

Maro took another reading.

Then frowned.

"It isn't stationary."

Vessa: "Moving which way?"

"Downstream. Slow. Maybe."

Dask said, "Your first point changed twelve degrees."

"Yes."

"Second changed five."

"Yes."

"That is not same movement."

"Correct."

Dask looked almost pleased.

Vessa turned to Kel.

"Bank route?"

"No landing for cart connection until Red Hook. Skiff can pole shallows west if we unload two cases and walk bank thirty yards. Mud unknown."

My favorite phrase.

Mud unknown.

Vessa looked at me automatically.

Then didn't ask.

Good.

I was passenger until needed.

Maro said, "Give it ten minutes. If drift continues, center may clear."

We waited.

I ate dried apple.

Goat watched me.

I offered none.

Boundaries.

Eight minutes later, reeds began lifting.

Not together.

West side first.

Then center.

Upstream-pointing bend relaxed until stalks stood again.

Water never visibly changed.

Maro's disk stopped turning backward.

Kel looked at older boatwoman.

She nodded.

"I'll take first."

Vessa said, "We'll take second."

No Greg solution.

No need.

Older woman poled through center slowly.

Nothing happened.

Basket boat followed.

Then goat boat.

Goat screamed halfway across but seemed unrelated.

Our turn.

Kel moved us through.

At middle, my left crutch shaft gave tiny buzz where it lay secured beside me.

I noticed.

Said, "Crutch shaft vibration. Brief. Middle channel. No contact with water."

Dask looked immediately.

"Which crutch?"

"Left."

"Duration?"

"Less than one breath."

Maro said, "Any right?"

"Not noticed."

"Don't reproduce."

"Wasn't planning to."

Dask wrote.

Then we were through.

That was it.

I liked that too.

World could be strange without owing me explanation.

Alder Basin appeared after another hour.

First thing I saw was roof.

One enormous low roof, green with moss, stretched along riverbank like somebody built warehouse for weather.

Then basin opened.

Not town exactly.

Not city.

A bowl of water and work.

River widened into broad shallow fan around stone islands.

Dozens of narrow channels split and rejoined between them.

Walkways crossed on timber trestles.

Buildings sat higher than Merecross, most on stone feet or thick wooden piles.

Ropes ran everywhere.

Not decorative.

Working lines between docks, roofs, cranes, drying frames.

Long racks held pale translucent sheets that flashed when sun hit them.

Fish?

Glass?

Leather?

Asking face.

I restrained myself.

Good Greg.

Boats moved constantly.

Flat-bottom cargo punts.

Little one-person reed skiffs.

Two broad ferry platforms pulled along fixed ropes rather than rowed.

One platform carried cow.

Cow calmer than goat.

Alder Basin smelled like mud, smoke, boiled grain, fish oil, and something sharp-sweet I couldn't place.

Maro breathed in.

"Home."

I looked at him.

"You're from here?"

"Near. West shelf."

Dask said, "He means six miles west."

"Near."

Sori gathered papers.

"Nobody ask Maro where anything is unless you want answer involving what used to stand there twenty years ago."

Maro said, "That's how location works."

Kel aimed skiff toward survey landing.

Survey landing here was three poles, floating plank, and sign tilted enough to insult literacy.

CARROW had theatre.

Merecross had paperwork.

Alder Basin apparently had ropes.

I liked it already.

Transfer off skiff went worse than transfer on.

Not dangerously.

Embarrassingly.

Floating plank sat lower than Merecross dock and moved sideways when I put right foot down.

I froze like taught.

Kel held forearm.

Waited.

Plank settled.

I moved.

Then shoulder bag caught one dock post.

I swore.

Sori said, "Graceful."

"Passenger."

"Former passenger."

Cruel.

I made land.

No applause.

Good.

Our road cart had not arrived.

Of course.

Vessa checked board at landing.

"Road delay at Reed Three. Axle inspection. Expected before fifth bell."

Kel frowned.

"Whose axle?"

"Doesn't say."

"Useful."

Sori: "It says inspection, not break."

"Still useful."

We had essential cases from skiff.

Camp bulk and more food on cart.

Vessa looked at sky.

"We have three hours before we need decide lodging fallback. Survey house first. Maro, office. Sori, custody. Kel, cart notice. Dask, gauges. Greg, with me."

Work again.

Alder Basin Survey House sat across two rope ferries and one raised plank lane.

I learned this because Vessa walked there.

Fast.

Normal Greg returned.

Meaning I said, "You know I have one leg, right?"

Without slowing she said, "Application mentioned it."

"Good. Worried paperwork missed detail."

"Keep up."

"Cruel employer."

"Temporary employee."

There.

Comfortable.

I kept up enough.

First rope ferry was simple platform with waist rail and overhead line. Operator pulled hand-over-hand while passengers stood.

I hated it immediately.

Not fear.

Geometry.

Moving platform plus crutches plus people who thought standing near exit made them arrive faster.

I planted wide.

Vessa watched once.

Didn't hover.

Good.

Across channel, market started.

Not central square.

Long strips along raised walkways.

Goods hung from rails because ground was mostly water or mud.

Bundles of reed fiber.

Fish smoked black.

Ceramic jars sealed with green wax.

Tools with broad wooden handles.

Small cages of pale insects that glowed faintly under shade cloth.

There.

Artifact pressure maybe.

Not artifact.

Interesting.

One stall had three metal objects under glass domes.

I looked.

Kept walking.

Retirement fund did not mean spending account.

Vessa noticed.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You looked."

"I am capable of looking without buying."

"Merecross suggests otherwise."

"I bought nothing in Merecross."

"You tried to understand twenty-eight-copper object while owning sixteen."

"Free."

"Until it isn't."

Fair.

Survey House was old mill office converted badly.

One wall still had enormous wooden wheel axle entering through stone though wheel outside was gone.

Clerk behind desk looked at Vessa's papers.

Then me.

Then crutches.

Then papers again.

Not hostile.

Accounting.

"Record hand?"

"Yes," Vessa said.

"North Cut?"

"Tomorrow if route holds."

Clerk sucked teeth.

"East steps wet."

Vessa: "We have morning read."

"Morning read was before Basin spill."

There.

Vessa went still.

"What spill?"

Clerk pointed toward back board.

Fresh notice pinned crooked.

WEST SHELF SETTLING POOL 4
RETURN-LIME SLURRY RELEASE
SECONDARY CHANNEL CLOSED BELOW GREEN POSTS
CAUSE: VALVE SEAT FAILURE, UNDER REVIEW
NO DRINKING DRAW / NO WADING / NO NETTING

Maro had not seen because notice newer than his departure.

Vessa read twice.

"When?"

"Half bell ago."

"Volume?"

"Initial notice says six holding drums equivalent, maybe eight."

"Into which secondary?"

Clerk traced map.

Channel fed toward north access side.

Not directly North Glass Cut.

Near enough to matter.

Vessa asked, "Any Cut closure?"

"Not yet. Field reader sent."

Adventure.

Actually field now.

She looked at me.

I waited.

No switch.

Not yet.

Vessa said, "We're not solving spill. We need know whether route remains valid."

"Good," I said.

Clerk looked at me.

"You new?"

"Very."

"Keep that attitude. People come Basin and decide every wet problem belongs to them."

"I have enough problems."

He nodded approval.

Vessa started questions.

Closure boundary.

Expected reader return.

Alternate north route.

Camp options if delayed.

I took notes.

Clean.

No performance.

Then clerk mentioned something that made my pencil stop.

"Old settling line has three return gates. Two are ordinary. Third is retired glass-lock. If slurry reached that, they'll hold north channel until they know whether lock took charge."

Glass-lock.

I looked up.

"Took charge?"

Asking face fully deployed.

Clerk said, "Stored return. Old industrial artifact. Maybe artifact. Depends who writes inventory."

Maro entered behind us just in time.

"Which lock?"

"Four-C."

Maro's expression changed.

Not panic.

Interest with teeth.

"Four-C was decommissioned."

"Retired, not removed."

Dask, somehow also arriving, said, "Those are different words."

Sori behind him said, "Nobody encourage him."

I looked at Vessa.

She looked at notice.

Then crew.

"We wait for field reader. Nobody goes near closed channel."

Correct.

Maro said, "If Four-C took charge, discharge timing matters for Cut access."

"Then reader tells us."

"Yes."

He sounded unhappy.

Good specialists hated waiting too.

I wrote:

ALDER BASIN
RETURN-LIME SPILL
SECONDARY CLOSED
FOUR-C RETIRED GLASS-LOCK MAY OR MAY NOT MATTER
NO CLAIM YET

Then under it:

DO NOT VOLUNTEER FOR INDUSTRIAL ACCIDENT

Sori leaned over shoulder.

"Good note."

"Learned recently."

Cart arrived before field reader.

Axle inspection had been ours.

Nothing broken.

One wheel collar heating more than Kel liked.

He greased and reset it himself while swearing at road contractor's maintenance standards.

Camp bulk arrived intact.

Vessa secured two rooms at Survey House bunk annex under route-delay coverage.

Not inn.

Six bunks.

One room smelled like wet rope.

Other smelled like older wet rope.

I chose first.

Luxury.

By evening, field reader still hadn't returned.

Route decision moved to dawn.

That could have annoyed me.

Instead I had Alder Basin.

We were allowed two hours off duty after meal, with instruction not to cross green-post closures, not to get drunk enough to miss dawn, and not to buy anything that needed crew transport without asking.

Vessa looked directly at me for last one.

"This feels targeted."

"It is."

So I went market.

Alone.

Not far.

Raised walkways glowed with oil lamps and little insect cages.

Pale insects brightened when people passed close, then dimmed.

Children ran fingers along cage bars to make waves of light.

A woman sold waterproof paper by sheet.

A man sharpened knives using wheel turned by tiny water channel under stall.

Someone cooked flatbread on black stone.

Orin's list said nothing about it.

Promising.

I bought food with meal allowance? No.

Survey meal already eaten.

Did not need second dinner.

Cala would disagree.

I kept copper.

Then found metal objects under glass domes again.

Different stall from one I passed.

Three pieces.

One looked like bent nail.

One like flat brass tongue.

One like little iron cage with no door.

Seller sat reading.

I stopped.

She did not look up.

"If you ask what they do, answer costs nothing and is mostly disappointing."

"Excellent sales method."

"Keeps idiots short."

"What do they do?"

She looked up.

Maybe fifty.

One eye clouded white.

"Nail hums near active return-lime. Brass tongue warms near open pressure seam. Cage catches one kind of marsh light for about minute if it feels cooperative."

"Prices?"

"Two. Three. Seven."

Copper, presumably.

Cheap enough to matter.

Not cheap enough to impulse all.

"Documented?"

She smiled.

There.

Good question.

"Nail, yes. Basin Works surplus mark, retired because sensitivity inconsistent beyond ten paces. Tongue, workshop claim only. Cage, absolutely not."

"Why seven?"

"Because tourists like it."

Rational market.

Beautiful.

I pointed nail.

"Two copper for inconsistent industrial detector seems high."

"Then don't buy."

"Could be useful tomorrow."

"Then two seems low."

Also rational.

I liked her.

Did not buy.

Yet.

Because Survey crew probably had better instruments.

Because I had twenty-three copper and twenty-three copper Vale debt and did not need turn symmetry into destiny.

Because retired tool near current spill was exactly kind of thing my brain wanted to overvalue from timing.

I asked seller name.

"Hara Venn."

"Greg."

"Theatre?"

I stared.

"Why does everyone know?"

"You stand like performer when pretending not interested."

Insulting.

I left.

Passenger day.

No giant accomplishment.

No maximum output.

No magic.

New river.

New basin.

New problem that did not belong to me.

Potentially useful object I did not buy.

And somewhere north, a retired glass-lock might have swallowed enough magical industrial runoff to change our route.

I had reserves.

Money now.

Skill before.

Power eventually.

Most important, I did not have to spend all of any of them just because they existed.

Passenger.

For today, that was enough.

# CHAPTER 391

## THE FOLLOWER

The field reader came back before dawn with lime on one boot and an opinion about everyone who had built Alder Basin before she was born.

This seemed promising.

She entered Survey House while I was eating something that might have been porridge if porridge had suffered.

Short woman.

Gray scarf over black hair.

Long waxed coat cut above knee.

One boot white to ankle with dried slurry splatter.

Other clean.

She carried a three-pronged brass fork almost as long as my forearm and looked furious at architecture.

"Four-C took partial charge," she said.

No greeting.

Maro stood immediately.

Vessa had already been awake.

Of course.

"How much?" Vessa asked.

"Enough to hold lower secondary. Not enough to trigger full spill gate. Maybe thirty percent of old face response."

Dask appeared from nowhere.

"Thirty percent measured how?"

The woman looked at him.

"Good morning, Dask."

"Good morning, Nessa. Thirty percent measured how?"

There.

They knew each other.

Nessa set brass fork on table.

"Old reference plate still reads. Four-C left quadrant takes tone. Center does not. Return seep at green post nine changes fork harmonic. I am not calling that thirty percent in report. I am calling it partial charge. Thirty is for people who need breakfast language."

Dask looked satisfied.

"Good."

"I hate you."

"Known."

Sori came in with papers.

"Please continue hating each other after route decision."

Vessa pointed at basin map.

"North Cut?"

Nessa leaned over.

"Main lower approach stays closed until Four-C bleeds below threshold or Works isolates the old face. Could be midday. Could be longer."

"Upper route?"

Nessa tapped east side.

"Dry ropewalk from Shelf Two to Cut marker six. Open if west trestle passes morning check. It avoids slurry channel."

Kel entered carrying tea.

"Cart?"

"Not on ropewalk."

"Of course."

"You can take pack mule to Shelf Two. From there hand carry only. Works freight cage can move sealed cases across first span if operator accepts manifest."

Sori already writing.

"What about black case?"

Nessa looked at Maro.

"Shade it."

Maro nodded.

Vessa: "How much slower?"

"If freight cage available, maybe one bell. If not, two. You still reach Cut before second bell access stamp expires if you move first bell."

There.

Not closure.

Detour.

Better.

Vessa looked around table.

"We move first bell."

I ate faster.

Nessa looked at me.

Then crutches.

Then Vessa.

"He's yours?"

"Record hand."

"Ropewalk has three open-grate sections and one narrow switchback. No ladders. Two stair runs. Fifteen and twenty-eight. Rail both."

Good.

Actual information.

"How wide narrow?" I asked.

Nessa looked at me again.

"Three feet at worst turn. Maybe little less where repair plank bulges."

"Surface?"

"Timber, iron grate, stone at Cut side. Wet this morning but not slimed."

"Any visual displacement?"

"Some. Mostly rail-shadow doubling after wake. Not like west stairs if you saw those."

"Saw those."

"Then contact first if edge disagrees."

"Good."

She nodded once.

That was apparently interview complete.

I liked her.

We did not buy Hara's nail.

Important.

The official field reader had a calibrated brass fork, route notices, old reference plates, and knowledge of what Four-C actually meant.

My two-copper retired detector remained where it belonged.

Under somebody else's glass dome.

Growth continued.

By first bell Alder Basin was awake in layers.

Fish market first.

Then ferries.

Then mills.

Then people who wanted breakfast after doing all three.

We loaded smaller field configuration.

Cart bulk stayed Survey House under stamped hold.

Kel hated this because cart bulk was apparently his emotional support object.

"We're leaving red case?"

Vessa said, "Upper ropewalk freight limit."

"I know why."

"Then why ask?"

"I want record that I dislike it."

Sori said, "Recorded spiritually."

Travel load:

Black river-light case.

Blue gauge case.

Plain sample case.

Food for day.

Water.

Repair roll split from red case under Kel supervision.

Personal loads.

No camp bulk.

If route failed, we came back.

If route worked, Works freight wagon at Cut north yard could take us onward later.

Infrastructure.

Adventure's less attractive cousin.

Necessary.

We took mule cart to Shelf Two.

Mule was named Porridge.

This felt targeted.

Driver swore name predated breakfast.

I did not believe him.

Alder Basin west shelf rose gradually out of water channels into industrial terraces.

Not factories exactly.

Yards.

Settling pools.

Kiln roofs.

Long sheds with open sides.

Pipes thick as my thigh crossed overhead on stone piers.

Some mundane.

Some with little ceramic tags tied every few feet.

Return lines.

Pressure lines.

Drain lines.

Maybe all three.

Asking face.

Restraint.

Nessa sat opposite me on mule cart.

"You're trying very hard not to ask."

"Personal development."

"Bad choice. Ask useful ones."

Permission.

Dangerous.

I pointed at ceramic tags.

"Color coding local or Works-wide?"

"Local Basin Works. Blue edge water. White edge return-lime. Red corner pressure assist. Black slash retired but physically present."

"Four-C black slash?"

"Should be. Half its tags missing."

"Comforting."

"It was built before tags standardized."

"More comforting."

She looked at crutches.

"Metal shafts?"

"Yes."

"You felt anything in river yesterday?"

There.

Maro had told her.

"Brief vibration left shaft. Under one breath. Mid-channel root drag. Crutch secured, not touching water. Right not noticed."

"Could be boat frame transmission."

"Yes."

"Could be return line under water."

"Yes."

"Could be nothing worth keeping."

"Yes."

Nessa narrowed eyes.

"You are easier than tourists."

"Vessa hired me for boring."

Vessa from front said, "Correct."

Mule cart stopped at green post barrier.

Not our route yet.

Spill channel lay below terrace to left.

White-gray water moved through narrow cut between mud banks.

Not dramatic poison river.

Ugly industrial runoff.

Green posts every twenty feet with cord between.

Works crew in wax aprons stood beyond with long poles, buckets, and one large wooden frame around something in water.

Four-C was farther downstream.

Could barely see.

Stone arch sunk into bank.

Metal face inside.

Old.

Very old.

One side carried faint pale sheen.

My brain wanted closer look.

No.

Nessa did not even slow.

"Closed channel."

"I know."

"Good."

Follower.

That was today.

Not leader.

Not solver.

Follow person who knew where floor was.

Shelf Two ropewalk began behind lime kiln.

At first looked like elevated warehouse catwalk.

Then I saw distance.

It ran along upper basin wall for maybe half mile, crossing channels and old settling cuts on timber and iron.

Three freight cages hung from overhead cable system at first span.

Not magical.

Probably.

Counterweights in tower.

Workers loaded cases into wood cage, rang bell, and two men at opposite platform hauled line while counterweight descended.

Sori presented manifest.

Operator looked at black case.

"River-light?"

"Yes."

"No passengers with it."

Kel said, "Why?"

"Because rule."

Sori smiled in way that meant violence through paperwork.

"Which rule?"

Operator pointed sign.

REFLECTIVE / REACTIVE FIELD CASES
CARGO CAGE ONLY
NO RIDERS
MAX 180 LB

There.

Fine.

Cases across.

People walked ropewalk.

I preferred that anyway.

First section timber planks with waist rail both sides.

Easy.

Second turned to open iron grate.

Water thirty feet below.

Not dangerous if grate stayed grate.

My crutch tips fit between openings if placed stupidly.

So I did not place stupidly.

Shorter plants.

Slight outward angle.

Check each contact.

Slow.

Nessa walked ahead but glanced back every few seconds.

Not hovering.

Route responsibility.

Dask behind me said, "Your pace is fourteen percent slower on grate than timber."

"Did you time me?"

"Yes."

"Why?"

"You are moving object in field route."

"I'm coworker."

"Both can be true."

Sori farther back said, "Do not put Greg in equipment inventory."

Dask considered.

"Would simplify depreciation."

"Dask."

"Not doing it."

I laughed.

Comfortable.

Fifteen-step stair run came after first span.

Rail right.

I took it.

No issue.

At top, my shoulders had warmed.

Not tired.

Working.

Nessa waited.

"Need anything?"

"No."

"Actual no or pride no?"

"Actual."

"Good."

We continued.

Second span crossed active settling yard.

Below us workers raked pale crust from shallow pools with long wooden combs.

Air smelled sharp-sweet.

There.

Mystery smell from yesterday.

Return-lime.

Probably processed version.

Nessa confirmed without asking.

"Fresh lime cake. Don't lick railing."

"Was about to."

"You look type."

Maro said, "He is."

Betrayal spreading.

Halfway across second span, black case in freight cage stopped.

Not ours specifically.

Whole cage.

Cable sagged.

Operator on far side rang two short bells.

Everyone on ropewalk stopped automatically.

I stopped because everyone else did.

Follower.

Worker shouted from tower.

"Counterweight catch!"

Kel swore.

Vessa called, "Load stable?"

"Stable! Cage brake set!"

Nessa looked down cable line.

"Common?"

Operator shrugged.

"When wet."

Dask said, "That answer is emotionally common, mechanically incomplete."

Nobody listened.

Repair worker climbed tower stairs.

We waited.

Again.

Adventure.

Waiting over industrial yard.

Greg could lock in.

Could probably map load, counterweight, brake geometry, crew options.

Didn't.

Because repair worker already climbing.

Because cage stable.

Because nobody asked.

Retirement fund stayed invested.

I leaned on rail.

Nessa noticed.

"You always this calm?"

"No."

"You look calm."

"Different."

"How?"

I thought.

"I know what panic costs."

She looked at me longer.

Then nodded.

"Fair."

Repair took twelve minutes.

Wet catch tooth had not seated fully.

Worker reset, tested empty travel three feet each direction, then moved cage.

No Greg.

Excellent.

At narrow switchback, problem became mine again.

Three feet was generous until actual turn.

Repair plank bulged inward.

Nessa had said maybe little less.

Little less mattered.

Crutch plus body plus right-side harness plus shoulder bag.

I stopped before entering.

Kel behind me nearly walked into Dask, who nearly blamed geometry.

"Need what?" Vessa asked.

"Move shoulder bag to front. Then I take turn backward one step, rotate on right foot, crutches one at time."

Nessa looked.

"That's how porters do long poles through."

"Good."

Bag front.

Left crutch through.

Right foot.

Body turn.

Right crutch.

One backward plant.

Rotate.

Ugly.

Worked.

Sori watched.

"Elegant."

"Cruel culture."

"You survived."

"Barely socially."

Twenty-eight-step stair run came next.

Rail left this time.

Less convenient.

My left side had no foot to trust rail against.

Still usable.

I took first twelve.

Paused.

Not because needed full rest.

Because rhythm starting sloppy.

Reset hand.

Continue.

At twenty-one shoulders began complaining.

Twenty-eight top.

I stepped aside.

"Twelve breaths," I said.

Vessa nodded.

Nobody argued.

Nessa counted none.

Good person.

At breath nine I probably could move.

Stayed twelve because I had said twelve and there was no prize for lying by three breaths.

Then continued.

North Glass Cut appeared without announcement.

One moment ropewalk curved through trees and stone industrial wall.

Next wall ended.

World opened.

Cut was not canyon.

Not exactly.

River had carved narrow passage through pale dark-veined stone, maybe fifty feet below upper path.

Both sides stepped in shelves.

Some natural.

Some obviously cut.

Old glass waste glittered in seams like frozen fish scales.

Maintained path ran along west wall under waist-high inner rail and taller outer posts strung with dull cloth ribbons.

Sun hit east shelves.

Afterimage happened immediately.

One shelf edge appeared twice.

Real dark line.

Pale copy three inches above it.

Then copy shifted when I blinked.

Wonderful.

Nessa said, "Welcome to bad eyesight as infrastructure."

"I love it."

"You will stop."

Maro smiled.

Dask corrected, "Afterimage is not bad eyesight."

Sori said, "Let metaphor live."

At Cut marker six, route reader waited.

Different person.

Tall man with orange cloth around neck and sounding rod in hand.

He and Nessa compared stamps.

Official handoff.

Nessa's authority ended at marker six.

His began.

There.

Systems.

He introduced himself as **Toren Ash**.

No relation to Kel established.

Kel immediately said, "No."

Toren looked at him.

"No what?"

"People will ask."

"Ask what?"

"Toren. Toren."

The man looked confused.

Sori said, "His surname is Toren. Your first name is Toren."

"Oh."

Kel: "No relation."

Toren Ash shrugged.

"Could be. My grandmother lied."

Kel stared.

"Why would you say that?"

"Because she did."

I liked North Glass Cut already.

Toren Ash gave route briefing.

Markers six through eight normal.

Nine through fourteen elevated afterimage, east shelf source.

Do not follow apparent upper edge.

Stay maintained west path.

Marker eleven had one loose outer post under repair flag, not load-bearing.

Marker thirteen seep wet but below slip threshold at last check.

Marker fourteen had reflective waste shelf throwing intermittent side flare.

If flare arrives, stop and face wall until it passes.

No black pockets observed since dawn.

Again, observed.

Not none.

Greg understood language now.

We moved.

This was real route.

Not test yard.

Not west stairs preview.

My body noticed difference.

Path varied between five feet and maybe eight.

Stone underfoot uneven but maintained.

Right foot fine.

Crutch tips sometimes landed on pale mineral seams that looked slick but weren't.

Sometimes dark seams looked dry and were damp.

Contact first.

Trust pressure more than light.

At marker nine, afterimage doubled my right crutch shadow.

One shadow followed shaft.

Other lagged half second.

Deeply unpleasant.

I laughed.

Vessa looked back.

"Problem?"

"No. Horrible. I like it."

"Stop liking hazards."

"Can't."

At marker ten, Toren Ash stopped us.

"Side flare building."

I saw nothing.

Maro did.

"East shelf third seam."

Dask opened case.

Sori said, "Do not create paperwork."

"Observation creates paperwork."

"Your enthusiasm creates extra copies."

Toren raised hand.

"Wall."

Everyone faced west wall.

I did too.

Follower.

Five seconds.

Ten.

Then whole path flashed pale from side.

Even facing wall, my peripheral vision filled white-green.

Closed eyes.

Afterimage remained as purple line.

Toren waited.

"Clear."

We turned.

River below looked normal.

Mostly.

Maro said, "Strong for morning."

Toren: "Wake residue. Usually drops by tomorrow."

Dask: "Usually distribution?"

Toren sighed.

"Ask office."

Good man.

At marker eleven, loose post flagged yellow.

Nobody touched.

Marker twelve path narrowed around inward stone bulge.

Easy enough.

Then marker thirteen.

Seep.

Wet band across path maybe two feet wide.

Toren tested with boot and rod.

"Still below slip threshold. One at time."

Kel first.

Then Vessa.

Maro.

Sori.

Dask.

Me last before Toren.

Crutch tip touched wet stone.

Held.

Second held.

Right foot.

Good.

Halfway, pale afterimage made wet band look six inches farther left than it was.

I ignored eyes.

Contact.

Move.

Done.

No applause.

Excellent.

Marker fourteen side flare did not come.

Instead we got something stranger.

Small metal plate bolted into inner wall began ticking.

Once.

Pause.

Twice.

Pause.

Once.

Toren Ash stopped.

"That's new."

There.

Bad words.

Maro came closer but not touching.

Plate maybe hand-sized.

Old.

Three screw heads.

Black slash ceramic tag beside it.

Retired physically present.

Dask whispered, "Return repeater."

Toren said, "Retired repeater."

"Yes."

"Hasn't ticked in four years I've worked route."

Maro looked at east shelf.

"Could Four-C charge propagate old line?"

Nessa was gone.

Different specialist now.

Toren said, "Maybe line still connects under old work."

Vessa: "Hazard?"

Toren considered.

"Not by itself. Plate has no active face. It's indicator. If old line carrying, means something upstream/downstream still returning."

Dask opened notebook.

"Sequence one-two-one."

Plate ticked again.

One.

Two.

One.

Regular.

Greg felt old part of brain wake slightly.

Pattern.

Timing.

Could count.

Could map.

Could lock in.

Didn't.

Not needed yet.

I started observer time because that was my job.

"Repeat interval approximately seven breaths between sequence starts," I said.

Dask nodded.

"Keep."

There.

Normal competence.

Toren called Cut office by line bell mounted farther path.

Three rings.

Wait.

Two.

Answer came from somewhere ahead.

He spoke code I did not know.

Sori asked for translation afterward.

"Route hold marker fourteen. Retired repeater active. Need line authority."

We waited.

Again.

I was becoming professional waiter.

Ten minutes later, answer bell.

Toren listened.

"Works says Four-C is bleeding into old return line. They expected west branch, not Cut repeater. No closure yet. They want us through marker fourteen before they isolate branch."

Vessa: "Window?"

"Twenty minutes. After that they isolate. If isolation fails, Cut closes until inspection."

There.

Now movement mattered.

Not panic.

Twenty minutes.

We had six people, three primary cases, personal loads, and about half mile maintained path to north yard.

Vessa looked at Kel.

"Pace?"

"Cases manageable."

Maro: "No stopping for readings unless route says."

Sori: "Custody stays."

Dask looked pained.

Vessa looked at me.

Not "lock in."

Not yet.

"Can you make pace?"

I checked body.

Hands good.

Shoulders worked but not spent.

Right leg good.

No skin complaint.

"Yes. If path stays like last section. If it changes, I'll say."

"Good."

We moved.

Faster.

Not running.

Field pace.

Toren led.

Kel carried blue case.

Maro black.

Sori plain custody.

Dask light notebook bag.

Greg personal harness/record.

Vessa floated wherever problem likely.

Marker fifteen.

Sixteen.

Afterimage less severe once east shelf angle changed.

Good.

Marker seventeen had short stair, eight steps.

No issue.

Eighteen bridge plank over narrow cut.

Rail both.

One at time.

Time burned.

Toren did not rush unsafe.

Good.

At marker nineteen, line bell behind us rang once long.

Toren looked back.

"Isolation starting."

How much time?

Unknown.

We kept moving.

My breathing increased.

Not distress.

Work.

Twenty.

Path climbed.

There.

Long incline I had not been promised because not special enough for briefing.

Of course.

I shortened stride.

Crutches forward.

Right foot.

Repeat.

No jokes.

Not locked in.

Just working.

Halfway shoulder burn started.

Could stop.

Could ask.

Did I need?

No.

Not yet.

Reserve was not excuse to be stupid.

But neither was comfort excuse to quit before necessary.

Top.

I said, "Six breaths."

Vessa stopped whole line? No.

She pointed side bay.

"Take six. We continue cases twenty yards to yard gate. Toren stays."

Perfect.

Crew flowed around me.

No drama.

I took six.

Actual six.

Then Toren and I followed.

North yard gate appeared through stone cut.

Wide timber doors open.

Works workers waving us through.

Line bell behind rang three short.

Toren said, "Branch isolated."

"Successful?"

"We'll find out."

We crossed gate.

Then workers closed it.

Not because danger immediate.

Procedure.

Cut temporarily held behind us pending confirmation.

We were through.

There.

Adventure did not need explosion.

Sometimes door closed after you crossed it.

North Glass Cut yard was small compared Alder Basin.

Stone sheds.

One stable.

Freight wagon waiting.

Road beyond climbed toward dark hills.

West Locks farther than visible.

Merecross nowhere.

Carrow much farther.

Good.

Vessa checked time.

"Four minutes inside window."

Dask said, "Depending when you define start."

Everyone looked at him.

He raised hands.

"Not important."

Growth.

Sori laughed hard enough to cough.

Toren Ash handed route sheet to Vessa.

"You're north side. If isolation holds, Cut reopens later. If not, you're not coming back this way soon."

There.

Vessa nodded.

"West Locks route?"

"Open as of first bell. Freight road has washout at marker thirty-two, wagon detour signed."

Kel sighed.

"Of course."

Toren looked at me.

"You did fine."

Simple.

I liked it more than I should.

Competence still mattered.

Being seen mattered.

Didn't mean I had to turn every walk into proof.

"Thanks."

He pointed at my crutches.

"Contact-first was right call. Don't trust east shelf until after tomorrow."

"Planning not to."

He left back toward gate because apparently his job involved returning into thing we just escaped.

Specialists were insane.

We ate standing beside freight shed.

Bread.

Cheese.

Pickled something.

Dask said repeater sequence could be useful if Four-C isolation timing compared later.

Maro said maybe.

Sori said he could compare after food.

Kel inspected waiting wagon before touching lunch.

Vessa read new road notice.

I sat on low stone and stretched shoulders.

Normal Greg.

Follower.

That was what worked today.

Follow Nessa through Basin works.

Follow Toren through Cut.

Follow Vessa's pace.

Follow my own body when it said six breaths.

No need to lead every room just because I could imagine doing it.

No need to spend reserve just to prove account balance.

But when marker fourteen started ticking and window narrowed, something inside me had leaned forward.

Not spent.

Available.

Good.

I looked north.

Road climbed out of Cut yard between pale stone ridges.

Farther than I had ever gone in second life by choice.

Vessa folded notice.

"We take wagon to West Locks road camp. If washout detour is as signed, overnight before locks."

Kel finally ate.

"How far?"

"Six road markers to camp."

Dask opened mouth.

Sori pointed bread at him.

"Do not define marker distance."

He closed mouth.

I stood.

Follower.

For now.

Road waited.

# CHAPTER 392

## THE CAMPER

The wagon north of Glass Cut had suspension designed by someone who hated kidneys.

Kel disagreed.

"It has suspension."

"Technically."

"That's what suspension means."

"Then it has hostile suspension."

Dask, across from me, looked interested.

"Hostility is difficult to measure."

Sori said, "Don't."

"I wasn't going to."

"You were."

"I was considering variables."

"That's how you start."

Vessa sat beside driver and ignored us.

Maro had fallen asleep.

Actually asleep.

Head against rolled tarp.

Mouth slightly open.

River-light specialist defeated by wagon.

I respected him more.

North road climbed away from Cut through low pine and pale stone.

Different country again.

Alder Basin had been wet work and ropes.

Glass Cut had been stone pretending eyesight was optional.

Here road ran dry between scrub, small farms, and occasional black marker posts with white ceramic caps.

I asked what they were.

Kel said, "Old flood return marks."

Dask opened mouth.

Kel added, "That is what everyone calls them."

Dask closed mouth.

Growth.

The road itself carried Glasswake residue in little ways.

Some wagon ruts shone when shadow crossed them.

Not in sun.

Shadow.

A fence nail blinked blue once as we passed.

One puddle reflected the wagon five seconds after wagon had left it.

I turned around to watch.

Sori caught me.

"Passenger again?"

"Research."

"With your mouth open?"

I closed mouth.

"Advanced research."

We reached marker thirty-two before midday.

Washout had eaten outer half of road.

Signed detour went uphill through orchard track.

Sign was good.

Rare luxury.

Kel stopped anyway.

"Wagon width."

Vessa got down.

We all did because apparently field work meant leaving perfectly good seats whenever terrain became interesting.

Detour track was narrow but firm.

Apple trees on one side.

Drop into muddy drainage on other.

Not cliff.

Enough to ruin wheel.

Kel measured with his body first.

One pace between inner rut and safe outer shoulder.

Then used folding stick.

"We fit."

Vessa: "Margin?"

"Enough if nobody gets creative."

I looked at Dask.

He said, "Why me?"

"No reason."

We walked first bend while Kel drove wagon empty of passengers.

Fine.

Second bend tighter.

Still fine.

Third had fallen apple branch across inner side.

Kel stopped.

I looked.

Branch maybe four inches thick at base.

Could move it.

Probably.

Then farmer appeared from orchard carrying saw.

"Leave it."

There.

Local person.

He cut branch.

We waited six minutes.

No Greg.

Retirement fund thriving.

Farmer said washout happened two nights before Glasswake, not because of it.

Important because people liked blaming magic for weather too.

Sori wrote route note.

Kel gave farmer half dried pear from lunch bag.

Farmer accepted like this was normal road tax.

Maybe it was.

Past orchard, road widened.

We rode.

By second bell I was bored enough to count ceramic caps.

Twenty-seven.

Then lost count because Dask asked why.

"Because I was bored."

"You could have asked me."

"For what?"

"Something to count."

"That's worse."

He looked genuinely offended.

Maro woke.

"Where are we?"

Kel said, "North."

Maro stared at him.

"I hate you."

"Good nap?"

"Yes."

Travel.

I liked it.

Nothing happened for almost an hour.

That felt new enough to matter.

Then we saw first lock tower.

Far away.

Black against afternoon sky.

Not West Locks proper yet.

Outer relay tower.

Tall narrow structure with three horizontal arms like someone built windmill and forgot wheel.

White cloth strips hung from upper arm.

None moved.

Wind definitely existed.

I looked at Maro.

He was already looking.

"Normal?"

"Sometimes."

Dask said, "Define sometimes."

Maro closed eyes.

Sori patted his knee.

"Don't kill him before camp."

We passed tower without stopping.

Vessa said it belonged Locks maintenance, not our current station.

Good.

Not every weird thing belonged to us.

Road camp sat another two miles north.

Calling it camp was generous.

Three roofed platforms.

One stone fire ring.

A hand pump.

Two privy sheds.

A low lockbox hut with survey seal.

And enough flat ground for wagon, which immediately made Kel happier than architecture deserved.

West Locks themselves were not visible.

But I could hear them.

Deep mechanical knocks every few minutes.

Not regular.

Water moving somewhere beyond hills.

Camp keeper was a woman named **Pella Dorn** with shaved sides of head, long braid, and one thumb missing at first joint.

She checked Vessa's papers.

"Six."

"Six," Vessa confirmed.

Pella looked at me.

"You the record hand?"

"Unfortunately."

"Then you get dry platform."

Dask said, "Why does he get dry platform?"

"Because last one ruined three days of paper sleeping under drip."

Dask considered.

"Reasonable."

Sori said, "I also have paper."

"You look mean enough to keep rain away."

Sori smiled.

"I like her."

Camp rules.

Water pump tested before use.

No washing dishes in drainage trough.

Food hung in iron box after dark because glass-mice came up from lock channels.

There.

"Glass mice?"

Pella looked at me.

"Mice. Eat glass salts. Teeth shine. Steal soap."

"Magical?"

"They're mice."

"That didn't answer."

"It answered enough."

Excellent.

No open flame after second night bell if east wind because lock vapor sometimes carried.

No sleeping against white marker stones.

"Why?"

Pella: "Cold."

"Just cold?"

"Usually."

Maro said, "Listen to her."

I would.

Vessa assigned camp.

Kel wagon and mule.

Sori food inventory.

Dask equipment check.

Maro evening light read.

Greg water and sleeping platforms.

I stared.

"Promotion."

"You wanted adventure."

"I wanted at least one sword."

"You have pump."

Cruel.

Pump handle heavy but functional.

I filled two buckets.

Carried one at time because two plus crutches would be stupid.

Pella had shoulder yoke hanging nearby.

I looked at it.

Pella followed my look.

"Bad match with crutches. Needs both hands clear at the turns."

"One bucket at a time."

"Yes."

Six trips.

Could have optimized.

Didn't.

Water existed at end.

Then bedding.

Survey bedrolls went dry platform.

Mine against inner post, not white marker stone.

Dask placed his exactly three feet from equipment cases.

Sori moved it while he wasn't looking.

He came back.

Stopped.

Looked at bedroll.

Looked at her.

"Why?"

"Testing environmental awareness."

"Move it back."

"You move it."

He did, muttering.

Crew life.

There.

Not coworkers in office anymore.

People who knew who snored soon.

Kel cooked.

This surprised me.

Not badly either.

Pot of beans, smoked meat, onion, some bitter green Pella had hanging by hut.

Maro chopped.

Sori claimed supervisory authority and was ignored.

I sat on platform edge cutting bread because knife plus seated body was job I could do without turning dinner into accessibility seminar.

Dask tried to calculate portions.

Kel took ladle away.

"People aren't gauges."

"Food is measurable."

"Not by you."

Pella ate with us.

Vessa asked Locks status.

Pella chewed before answering.

"West Three running slow. West Four closed for tooth replacement. Five open cargo only. Foot route east gallery."

"Glasswake effects?"

"Two false gate tones last night. One this morning. Maintenance says no physical movement with either. East gallery had floor shine at dawn. Gone by noon."

Maro: "Black pockets?"

"None seen."

Dask: "Observed none?"

Pella pointed spoon at him.

"I will put you outside iron box."

Sori laughed hard enough to cough.

Dask looked at Vessa.

"Threat?"

"Boundary."

I liked camp.

Pella continued.

"Road north after Locks open. Marsh shelf wet. Two wagons came through. One says reed hounds near Mile Reed."

There.

Finally.

"Reed hounds?" I asked.

Pella looked delighted to have new idiot.

"Hounds made of reeds."

"Actually?"

"No."

Kel said, "Don't encourage her."

Pella grinned.

"Long-legged marsh dogs. Tan. Hunt rats and little deer. Sometimes follow camps for scraps. Not monsters unless you're sausage."

"I am partly sausage now."

"Sleep high."

Maro said, "They're usually shy."

Pella: "Usually."

I pointed at her.

"You enjoy that word."

"Keeps people alive."

Fair.

After dinner Vessa posted watches.

Not because danger high.

Because equipment, animals, road camp, normal field procedure.

Kel first.

Greg second.

Maro third.

Vessa fourth.

Sori and Dask off because Sori had dawn custody prep and Dask had first-light gauge calibration.

I said, "Favoritism."

Sori said, "Competence allocation."

"I hate administration."

"No you don't."

True.

Second watch began sometime after moon rose.

Kel woke me by tapping boot against platform.

"Quiet."

"Good quiet?"

"Mostly. Heard hounds once east. Lock knocked twice. Pella says normal."

"Anything glowing?"

"Dask's teeth when he sleeps."

I looked toward Dask.

"Really?"

Kel walked away.

Asshole.

I got up.

Harness.

Crutches.

Blanket around shoulders.

Camp at night felt smaller.

Fire down to coals.

Wagon dark shape.

Mule asleep standing.

Survey cases locked in hut except overnight gauge box under platform.

Deep lock knocks came through ground more than air.

Thunk.

Long pause.

Thunk-thunk.

Water answered somewhere far off.

I sat near fire ring.

Watch did not require standing dramatically.

Good.

I listened.

First life watches had been different.

Sometimes six people asleep behind me because if I failed they died.

Sometimes monsters close enough I could smell them.

Sometimes enemy adventurers.

Sometimes nothing for eight hours except cold and thoughts.

This was closer to last one.

I liked last one now.

At nineteen, again, I had learned boredom could be luxury.

Something moved near food box.

Tiny.

Scratch.

I looked.

Two points of pale blue light.

Then four.

Then six.

Glass mice.

Excellent.

Three little gray bodies emerged from drainage grass.

Normal mice except incisors glowed faint blue-white when mouths opened.

One climbed iron food-box leg.

Reached grease collar.

Slid down.

Tried again.

Persistent.

I whispered, "Respect."

Another mouse went toward soap bag hanging from peg.

Pella had warned.

I tapped crutch shaft lightly against stone.

Mouse froze.

Ran.

Others followed.

No spell.

No battle.

Bronze adventurer defeats mice with noise.

S-class legacy secure.

Five minutes later they returned from other side.

Smarter than expected.

I moved soap bag higher.

Victory through logistics.

Then east dark made sound.

Not mouse.

Soft footfall.

Pause.

Soft footfall.

I looked toward wagon.

Mule's ears were forward.

There.

Animal noticed too.

I stood.

Not locked in.

Attention sharpened one notch.

Tan shape at edge of moonlight.

Long legs.

Narrow chest.

Dog-like head.

Reed hound.

One.

Maybe.

It stood beyond drainage trough, watching camp.

I watched back.

No weapon in hand.

Crutch could become weapon.

Knife at belt.

Old habits offered distance, angle, approach line, mule position, sleeping crew, food box, wind.

Quietly.

No need to spend more.

Hound sniffed.

Took one step.

I said, normal voice, "No."

It stopped.

Good dog.

Maybe.

Another shape moved behind first.

Smaller.

Then third.

Three.

Not attack posture.

Interested.

Probably food.

Pella's partly sausage problem.

I picked up small stone.

Threw it to land in dirt between us.

Not at animal.

Hounds jumped back.

One barked once.

Weird bark.

Almost cough.

Maro woke behind me.

"What?"

"Three reed hounds. East."

He sat up slowly.

Looked.

"Don't feed."

"Hadn't planned."

"They'll follow wagon tomorrow if they learn camp gives food."

"Pella said shy."

"Those are young."

One hound edged sideways.

Maro stood.

No hurry.

He took empty pot lid.

Hit it once with spoon.

CLANG.

All three vanished.

I stared at him.

"Advanced river-light technique?"

"Yes."

"Teach me."

"S-class prerequisite."

Fuck him.

He sat beside me.

"I'll stay until they don't come back."

"Your watch is next."

"I know."

"Then sleep."

"I'm awake."

"Your problem."

We sat.

Quiet.

After maybe ten minutes he said, "You miss home?"

There.

Not technical.

"Yes."

"Want to go back?"

I thought.

"Not yet."

He nodded.

"Good answer."

"Yours?"

"This is home-ish."

"Six miles west."

"Near."

"Dask was right."

"Don't say that."

We listened to locks.

Maro eventually said, "When I was younger I thought leaving meant choosing somewhere else."

Longer speech from him.

Rare.

"Then I left. Came back. Left again. Places don't care about your categories. You can belong somewhere and still be gone. You can hate a place and know every drain. You can love people and not want their street today."

I looked at him.

"That sounded rehearsed."

"It was not."

"Worse."

"I regret talking."

"Good."

He smiled.

Then pointed east.

One hound had returned.

Farther away.

Watching.

Maro lifted pot lid.

It left before clang.

Learning.

"Smart," I said.

"Enough."

There.

Enough.

I understood word differently now.

Not mediocre.

Not settling.

Enough could be exact amount required.

Enough strength.

Enough money.

Enough attention.

Enough Greg.

Reserve stayed reserve until enough stopped being enough.

Maro took watch early because awake.

I went back to bed.

Slept badly for first half hour because Dask did, in fact, grind teeth.

They did not glow.

Kel lied.

Morning arrived gray.

Pella had coffee substitute already boiling.

Tasted like burnt root and punishment.

I drank two cups.

Vessa had route sheet open.

"West Three slow. Four closed. Five cargo only. We take east gallery on foot, cases through Five freight cradle, regroup north landing."

There.

More infrastructure.

"Then?" I asked.

"Marsh shelf road. Mile Reed. North station by evening if pace holds."

"Hounds?"

Maro said, "Greg made friends."

"I did not."

Pella: "You feed them?"

"No."

"Then not friends."

Good.

Dask emerged looking terrible.

Sori said, "Your teeth are loud."

He stopped.

"What?"

"You grind."

"No."

Five people looked at him.

Dask became offended by consensus.

"That is not measurement."

Camp packed.

Bedrolls rolled.

Food box checked.

Soap survived.

Glass mice had eaten tiny corner of one label on empty salt packet.

Sori stared at damage like personal betrayal.

Pella said, "Told you."

We loaded wagon for short run to Locks approach.

I looked north.

First lock tower stood much closer now.

Behind it, two more arms rose above ridge.

Water knocked deep under ground.

Road camp already felt temporary.

One night.

One dinner.

One watch.

One stupid mouse campaign.

Enough to know where Dask slept, how Kel cooked, that Sori laughed hardest when someone threatened Dask, that Maro got philosophical when awake too long, and Vessa drank coffee substitute without making face because she was either stronger than all of us or dead inside.

Accumulation.

Small.

Real.

Camper.

I had slept somewhere that wasn't home.

Again.

This time it didn't feel like interruption.

It felt like route.

# CHAPTER 393

## THE RECORDER

West Locks sounded like giants arguing under floor.

Thunk.

Pause.

Grinding hum.

Thunk-thunk.

Then water.

A lot of water.

We left road camp before sun reached first lock tower.

Pella stood by wagon with coffee punishment in one hand.

"If east gallery shines, don't chase pretty."

"Wasn't planning to."

"Good. Keep it that way."

Kel climbed driver's bench.

Sori checked seals.

Dask checked Sori checking seals.

Maro yawned into sleeve.

Vessa said, "Move."

Camp disappeared behind first rise.

One night.

Already gone.

West Locks appeared properly ten minutes later.

Not one lock.

A system.

Three channels cut through low stone hills.

Gates taller than houses.

Black iron teeth fitted into pale masonry.

Walkways crossed at several heights.

Counterweight towers stood over channels with horizontal arms and white cloth strips like the relay tower we'd passed yesterday.

Some strips moved now.

Some did not.

Water sat at different levels on different sides of gates.

One basin almost still.

Another churned brown-white below half-open spill bars.

Freight cranes moved crates over channels without touching road.

Workers in dark wax coats shouted numbers nobody repeated back because apparently Locks people trusted screaming.

I liked it immediately.

Bad sign.

Vessa said, "Eyes on route."

"They are."

"Keep them there."

Approach office was small stone box wedged between West Three and Four.

Lock clerk wore red scarf and had ink on both wrists.

She read Sori's papers while talking to someone through wall tube.

"Survey six, east gallery foot, Five cradle cargo, north landing regroup. West Three slow but running. Four still dead. Five cargo window open until second bell unless priority grain comes early."

Sori: "False tones?"

"One before dawn. No gate movement."

Maro: "Floor shine?"

"East gallery lower panel again at first light. Maintenance marked and shaded. Walkable. Don't scrape it."

Dask: "Tone source localized?"

Clerk looked at him.

"No."

"Recorded intervals?"

"Yes."

"Can I see?"

"After your cargo window."

Dask physically suffered.

Vessa smiled a little.

"Move."

West Five freight cradle sat under square tower.

Not cage.

Cradle.

Long metal platform suspended by four chains, wide enough for two carts but currently loaded with grain sacks from opposite side.

No people allowed with reactive cases.

Same logic as Basin freight cage, bigger machine.

Our black case, gauge case, red repair case, food, and bedroll bundles went onto marked corners.

Sori checked each serial.

Cradle worker named **Hobb Fen** checked her check.

Dask watched both.

I watched Dask.

"You need hobby."

"I have gauges."

"That is work."

"Incorrect."

Hobb said, "If it makes him happy, don't ruin it."

Sori: "It does not make him happy."

Dask considered.

"It makes errors less likely."

There.

Best he had.

Cradle moved.

Chains tightened.

Counterweight dropped inside tower.

Platform slid out over West Five channel.

Below, water moved fast between open lower slots.

White strips on tower arm snapped once despite no gust.

Maro looked up.

So did I.

Nobody stopped cradle.

Not our decision.

East gallery entrance sat fifty yards downstream.

Foot route tunneled through gate wall itself.

There.

Different grammar.

Not catwalk.

Stone corridor wide enough for three people.

Rectangular openings every fifteen feet showed water at shoulder height on one side.

Then lower.

Then gone.

Because gallery moved through changing lock levels.

Floor slightly sloped.

Iron strips crossed stone at regular intervals.

Some warm.

Some cold.

I knew because crutch tips told me before hand did.

Not magic.

Temperature.

Probably.

At first lower panel, maintenance shade cloth covered section of wall and floor.

Yellow chalk boundary.

Worker stood beside it.

"Walk inner edge. Don't touch cloth."

We did.

Under cloth, pale light moved faintly.

Not bright.

Like moonlight under bedsheet.

Dask slowed.

Worker said, "Keep moving."

Dask kept moving.

Growth under threat.

I recorded as instructed:

EAST GALLERY LOWER PANEL
FIRST BELL + 18
SHADED BY LOCKS MAINTENANCE
PALE FLOOR/WALL RETURN VISIBLE THROUGH CLOTH
NO CONTACT

Maro looked over.

"Good."

Simple.

Useful.

We kept going.

First false tone happened while we were inside gate wall.

One deep bell note.

Not loud.

Felt through stone.

Everyone stopped.

Locks workers stopped too.

There.

Meaningful.

Second note did not come.

Worker nearest us looked at ceiling.

Waited.

Then shouted down gallery.

"No movement Four!"

Another voice answered farther away.

"No movement Three!"

Then wall tube whistled.

Worker picked up.

Listened.

"False tone. East tube, no gate travel. Continue foot."

Vessa looked at me.

"Record witness."

I did.

Tone time.

Location.

No observed mechanical movement from our gallery position.

Worker calls from Four/Three.

Authority message through wall tube.

No cause.

Dask said, "Duration approximately one and one-quarter seconds."

I looked at him.

"You have instrument?"

"No."

"Then observer estimate."

He looked offended.

Then nodded.

"Observer estimate."

Sori whispered, "He's learning."

"Terrifying."

East gallery rose toward West Four.

Four was open only enough to expose repair chamber above waterline.

Huge black gear tooth sat on wooden cradle beside wall.

Replacement tooth.

Almost as tall as me.

Actual gate gear had gap where old tooth had been removed.

Three workers fitted pins and measured seat.

No magic visible.

Maybe none.

Maybe everything.

Do not ask every noun.

At midpoint gallery narrowed around old support pier.

Not difficult.

Crutches through.

Right foot.

Body.

Done.

No identity crisis.

Then floor knocked under us.

Thunk-thunk.

Not bell.

Mechanical.

Water level in window opening dropped maybe six inches in seconds.

Maro watched.

"Three cycling."

Lock worker nodded.

"Slow cycle."

Dask asked, "How slow?"

Worker said, "Slow enough people complain."

Dask nearly died.

We reached West Five crossing just as our cradle arrived north side.

Hobb's counterpart checked cargo before release.

One bedroll bundle had shifted three inches inside marked box.

Sori recorded shift.

Nothing damaged.

Kel reset strap.

Normal.

Then one small brass custody tag slid off top of black case.

I saw it moving toward cradle grate.

Close.

Half pace maybe.

Could reach? No.

Crutches planted.

Sori was two steps away.

Hobb's counterpart reached too late.

I felt old instinct.

Tiny external push.

Not lock in.

Not big.

Same family as Dask's rolling marker.

This time deliberate.

Short.

Clean.

The brass tag changed angle.

Not stopped.

Just enough.

Hit raised case corner instead of grate.

Sori caught it.

Silence.

There.

Fuck.

Dask was not here.

Blessings existed.

Vessa was.

Of course.

She looked at me.

I looked at tag.

"Authorized?" I asked.

"No," she said.

"Safety?"

"Paperwork."

"Custody tag."

"Still paperwork."

Sori held tag.

"Expensive paperwork."

Vessa: "Did you touch equipment?"

"No. External effect only. Target was loose brass tag. No contact with case intended."

"Distance?"

"Half pace. Maybe less."

"One attempt?"

"Yes."

"Any pain?"

I checked.

"No."

"Any effect on anything else observed?"

"None observed."

She looked at Sori.

Sori: "I saw tag alter path. I didn't see case move."

Hobb's counterpart said, "I saw shiny thing not fall."

Useful witness.
