Vessa sighed.

"Record separately. Not field-procedure approval."

"Understood."

There.

Second practical result.

First deliberate one outside training.

I did not feel stronger.

I felt annoyed at myself.

Could have yelled.

Sori might have caught anyway.

But effect worked.

That mattered.

Retirement fund analogy was dangerous if every successful trick became withdrawal.

This wasn't.

More like finding forgotten coin in coat.

Still mine.

Still real.

Protocol total, though.

One deliberate external attempt.

129 became 130.

I wrote it before memory could improve itself.

EXTERNAL ATTEMPT 130
FIELD / WEST FIVE NORTH CRADLE
~0.5 PACE OR LESS
LOOSE BRASS CUSTODY TAG MOVING
DELIBERATE
PATH CHANGE OBSERVED
NO CASE MOVEMENT OBSERVED
NO PAIN
NOT AUTHORIZED PROCEDURE

Hessa would hate handwriting.

Good.

West Locks north landing opened onto broad stone apron.

Behind us gate towers knocked and rang.

Ahead, landscape flattened.

Marsh.

Real marsh.

Low water glinted between brown-green grass.

Raised road ran on packed reed mats and gravel, slightly crowned above wet ground.

Tall poles marked edges every hundred yards.

Some had little hanging shells.

Wind made shells click.

Mile Reed was apparently not town.

Pella had used it like place.

It was marker district.

One enormous dead reed bundle wrapped around a stone post, taller than person, with painted number plate beneath.

Maro said, "Old distance marker."

Dask: "Not a mile."

Maro: "Nobody asked."

Sori: "I was going to."

Maro looked betrayed.

Road crew clerk at north landing stamped our exit.

Vessa asked route status.

"Marsh shelf wet but open. Two soft sections before Mile Reed. North station reports no closure. Reed hounds seen dawn, six or seven, kept distance from freight."

Six or seven.

Young three from camp maybe part.

Or cousins.

Not problem yet.

Kel collected wagon waiting north side.

Different wagon from camp route.

Lower wheels.

Wide tires.

Marsh wagon.

He approved.

"This one has suspension."

I climbed in.

"Hostile?"

"Normal."

We would see.

Road onto marsh felt softer even through wagon.

Ground moved subtly under wheel weight.

Not sinking.

Flexing.

Reed mats under gravel distributed load.

Nice.

Regional engineering.

Some sections had paired white stakes where road crew had added fresh bundles.

Water on both sides showed little bubbles.

Birds with long red legs picked insects from shallows.

One lifted and revealed four wings.

I stared.

Maro said nothing.

I refused asking face.

He waited.

I waited.

Dask broke first.

"They are splitwings."

I smiled.

"Thank you."

Maro looked disappointed.

We passed first soft section by walking beside empty wagon while Kel took it through one at a time.

Road worker had laid new reed bundles that morning.

No drama.

Second soft section had wagon ahead stuck to hub.

Merchant cursed mud.

Two road workers already unloading sacks.

Vessa asked if passage open.

Worker said, "Ten minutes if they stop arguing with gravity."

Merchant said, "I heard that."

"Good."

We waited.

Normal Greg.

Enough.

I sat on road edge with right leg stretched and checked hands.

No blister break.

Small hot spot at left palm.

Rewrapped grip cloth one turn tighter.

Sori sat beside me.

"Magic."

There.

"Apparently."

"You meant to?"

"Yes."

"Different from disk?"

"Disk was instinct before I thought. This one I chose."

"Useful."

"Maybe."

"You hate saying useful about yourself?"

"No. I hate turning two things into law."

She looked at me.

"Dask infection."

"Hessa infection."

"Worse?"

"Much."

She nodded.

"Vessa wasn't angry."

"She was Vessa."

"That is sometimes angry."

Fair.

Sori picked grass stem.

"You could train that while we travel?"

Question.

Simple.

Could.

Probably should, eventually.

Retirement account.

Withdraw little?

No.

Training wasn't emergency withdrawal.

That analogy had limits.

Maintenance mattered.

Compounding mattered too.

I smiled.

"Maybe. Controlled. Not while working."

"Dask will hear that from three miles away."

"Don't tell him."

She smiled.

"I already want to."

Merchant wagon moved.

We continued.

By late afternoon marsh widened until no trees showed west.

West Locks towers disappeared behind haze.

Road became narrow line through water and reed.

One reed hound appeared on far bank.

Adult.

Longer legs than camp juveniles.

Tan coat dark at shoulders.

It watched wagon.

Then trotted parallel fifty yards away.

Another appeared behind.

Then three.

Nobody reached for weapon.

Kel said, "Pella's friends."

"Not mine."

Maro: "You fed them emotionally."

"I said no."

"Strong bond."

They followed for almost mile.

Never closer than forty yards.

At one point one bounded through reeds and came out with rat in mouth.

Excellent.

Not interested in us.

Mostly.

Mile Reed marker stood at road fork.

Huge bundled reed post blackened with age.

Small shelter beside it.

No settlement.

A woman sold boiled eggs and smoked eel from basket.

Road traffic made market wherever road paused.

Vessa allowed ten minutes.

Survey food covered meal later, but this was not meal.

I bought nothing.

Not because poor.

Because smoked eel looked judgmental.

Orin would understand.

Dask bought egg.

Then measured shell thickness with thumbnail.

Seller told him to leave.

He ate egg outside basket range.

North station appeared before evening.

Stone base.

Wood upper room.

Signal mast with three black arms.

One stable.

One angled.

One moving slowly despite wind direction.

Here we go.

Station keeper came down steps before wagon stopped.

"Survey?"

Vessa handed papers.

Keeper skimmed.

"West Locks?"

"Crossed."

"Any false tones?"

"One in east gallery. No observed gate movement. Recorded."

Keeper nodded.

"Good. We got one through mast twenty minutes ago."

Maro looked up.

"Tone relay?"

"Maybe. Arm two moved with it. No wind match."

Dask was already opening notebook.

Of course.

Vessa looked at me.

Not lock in.

Just work.

"Greg, records with Dask."

"Yes."

There.

Recorder.

That was today.

Not hero.

Not S-class ghost.

Not passenger anymore.

I had crossed working magic older than some towns, watched gates lie with bells, deliberately moved a tiny piece of brass without touching it, and spent most of day writing down what other people actually knew.

Good job.

Useful job.

At north station table I opened notebook to clean page.

Dask sat opposite.

He glanced at my external-attempt note.

Stopped.

Looked up slowly.

"You did what?"

Fuck.

Sori, from doorway, laughed before I answered.

Recorder.

Apparently tomorrow might be worse.

# CHAPTER 394

## THE PRACTITIONER

Dask stared at my notebook like it had personally insulted him.

"You did what?"

"Moved tag."

"On purpose?"

"Yes."

"How far?"

"Half pace. Maybe less."

"Moving target?"

"Sliding."

"Single attempt?"

"Yes."

"And you didn't tell me?"

"I wrote it down."

He looked at notebook.

Then me.

"That is not telling me."

Sori, sorting custody sheets at other end of table, said, "It is literally telling everyone forever."

"Not same."

"Better, actually."

North station had one main room, one narrow records room, and apparently enough opinions for six people.

Outside, mast arm two moved again.

Slow.

Wrong direction for wind.

Nobody panicked.

Station keeper, **Rett Morn**, leaned out door and watched.

"There."

Dask abandoned me instantly.

Good.

He ran outside with gauge board.

Maro followed.

Vessa pointed at me.

"Records."

"I know."

"Good."

Normal work.

Excellent.

I took station log from Rett.

Arm two movement first observed twenty-three minutes before our arrival.

Single low tone through mast shortly before that.

No weather change recorded.

No freight bell scheduled.

No lock command due.

West Locks false tone earlier separated by more than an hour.

Do not connect because both tones.

I wrote exact times where known.

Approximate where not.

Rett watched my page.

"You always write uncertainty like accusation?"

"It deserves representation."

"Huh."

He had one of those faces built for weather.

Deep lines, red nose, gray beard cut short.

Probably fifty.

Maybe forty and north wind had opinions.

He tapped station log.

"Arm moved twice before you came. Once after. Never did that last year."

"How old mast?"

"Older than station roof. Younger than stone base."

"Useful."

"You asked."

Fair.

Outside, Dask called, "Greg!"

I went.

Mast stood thirty feet from station on square stone plinth.

Three black arms mounted on vertical shaft.

Arm one east-west.

Two angled northwest.

Three nearly vertical.

Wind came south.

White thread tied by Rett to arm two fluttered south-to-north.

Arm itself crept west.

Not with thread.

Maro had brass disk out.

Dask had two rulers, because of course.

"Record," Vessa said.

I did.

Arm moved maybe two finger-widths over forty breaths.

Then stopped.

No tone.

No visible shaft movement on arms one or three.

Maro said, "Could be residual return in mast body."

Dask: "Could be bearing creep."

Rett: "Could be haunted."

Sori came outside.

"Official category?"

"Very."

Rett smiled.

Vessa said, "No climbing. No disassembly. Station sends notice to regional relay desk. We compare record, then move on schedule."

There.

Bounded.

North station was not our new home.

Dinner was station stew.

I asked what was in it.

Rett said, "Food."

I stopped asking.

Dask did not.

He identified turnip, barley, smoked bird, and one herb Rett refused to name on principle.

Then Dask returned to me.

"Four attempts."

I ate.

"Hello to you too."

"We agreed."

"We tentatively agreed to controlled comparison after future work if conditions allow."

He stared.

"You remembered wording."

"I know you."

"Four attempts. Same target. Controlled. Not while job active."

Vessa was eating nearby.

Dask looked at her.

"Permission?"

She chewed.

Swallowed.

"When?"

"After meal. Before dark. Flat ground behind station. No field equipment. One target. Four attempts. Greg stops if pain, instability, unusual spill, or anything I dislike."

"You dislike everything," Sori said.

"Not measurements."

Vessa looked at me.

"Want to?"

There.

Important distinction.

"Yes."

I did.

Not because emergency.

Not because proving.

Because two practical successes changed value of information.

And maybe there was difference between draining retirement account and checking interest rate.

Vessa nodded.

"Twenty minutes. Then done. This does not become nightly ritual unless we decide later."

"Agreed."

Dask looked offended by limit and delighted by permission simultaneously.

After meal we went behind station.

Flat packed dirt.

Low stone wall.

No public crowd.

No field gear nearby except Dask's notebook and one measuring cord.

Target was not brass disk.

Dask chose wooden button.

I objected.

"Different material."

"Exactly."

"Then not comparison."

He paused.

Looked at me.

"Correct."

Sori, who had apparently come to watch, said, "He admitted error. Mark date."

Dask ignored her.

He fetched small brass washer from personal repair tin.

Close enough to disk/tag material family, though shape different.

We set it on smooth board.

Stationary.

Distance one pace.

I sat.

Crutches laid beside me.

No twisting reach.

No urgency.

Dask marked line.

"Four attempts. Same instruction each time. Move washer right. Not distance target. Any visible displacement counts separately from direction quality."

"How much rest?"

"Thirty breaths minimum between attempts. More if you want."

"Good."

Vessa stood ten feet away.

Maro leaned on wall.

Kel had somehow appeared with cup.

Sori had papers she was pretending to read.

Great.

Private science.

Attempt one.

I looked at washer.

Not magic theory.

Not grand memory.

Same small internal reach I had used for tag.

Push.

Nothing.

Maybe sensation.

No movement.

"No observed movement," I said.

Dask wrote.

Thirty breaths.

Attempt two.

I changed nothing deliberately.

Same posture.

Same target.

Same intent.

Push.

Washer twitched.

Tiny.

Left.

Wrong direction.

Dask made noise like injured scholar.

"Movement observed, direction wrong."

"Yes."

"Approximately two millimeters."

"Observer?"

He glared.

"Estimated two millimeters by board marks."

Better.

Thirty breaths.

Attempt three.

I wanted to correct.

That was trap.

One result did not justify technique change.

Same setup.

Push right.

Washer moved.

Clean.

Maybe half inch.

Right.

Stopped.

There.

Nobody spoke for second.

Then Dask said, very carefully, "Visible displacement right. Approximately one-half inch."

Maro: "No other observed movement."

Sori: "Board stayed."

Vessa looked at me.

Not impressed face.

More interested.

I smiled.

Couldn't help.

Three.

Not mastery.

But three clean practical-ish successes now across two days? No.

Careful.

Ch385 spontaneous disk.

Ch393 deliberate moving tag.

Now one controlled stationary washer.

Different contexts.

Useful.

Thirty breaths.

Attempt four.

Same.

Push.

Washer vibrated.

Did not travel.

Dask crouched.

"Movement?"

"Vibration only," Maro said.

"Direction?"

"No net displacement I can see."

Sori: "Same."

Dask wrote.

Done.

I sat back.

No pain.

Slight pressure behind eyes.

Not headache.

Fatigue maybe.

I said it.

"Mild pressure behind eyes after fourth. No pain."

Dask wrote that too.

Vessa asked, "Could you do more?"

Probably.

"Yes."

"Should you?"

There.

"No reason."

She nodded.

Good.

Dask looked at results.

Attempt one none.

Two movement wrong direction.

Three clean right.

Four vibration/no net displacement.

"Three of four show something," he said.

"Two of four show displacement."

"One clean intended."

"Yes."

"Combined with tag and disk..."

"Careful."

He stopped.

Actually stopped.

Then nodded.

"Different contexts."

Growth.

I liked him slightly.

Sori said, "Disgusting."

"What?"

"Both of you learning restraint."

Kel drank from cup.

"Won't last."

Maro said, "Probably not."

We logged all four as **controlled external attempts**.

External ledger one-thirty to one-thirty-four.

No shaping.

No minimal.

No rank.

No breakthrough fanfare.

Just better evidence.

I felt good anyway.

That was allowed.

Maybe more than good.

Hungry.

Not for food.

There.

Dangerous.

Old feeling.

One clean result made next attempt imaginable.

Next ten.

Next hundred.

Train until twitch became inch, inch became reliable, reliable became useful under movement, pressure, distance.

I knew that staircase.

I had climbed versions of it before.

Retirement account had door.

I put hand on knob.

Did not open.

Not tonight.

But now I knew lock still worked.

That mattered.

We slept in station loft.

No camp watch required because Rett had mast watch and locked yard.

Dask wanted to discuss washer until midnight.

Sori threatened to smother him with folded blanket.

He lasted another six minutes.

Morning route notice came before first bell.

North road split.

Main freight line continued northwest toward **Harrow Fen**, larger marsh settlement and relay market.

Survey route turned north-east along old levee toward **Three Posts station**, then farther toward upper West Locks return beds.

Vessa read notice.

"We take levee."

Good.

New road.

Rett handed her regional relay response about mast.

Not answer.

Instruction:

KEEP ARM TWO IN POSITION.
DO NOT RESET MANUALLY.
RECORD TONES / MOVEMENT.
MAINTENANCE TEAM DISPATCHED FROM HARROW FEN.
NO ROUTE CLOSURE.

Bounded again.

No conspiracy.

No us.

We left after breakfast.

North station shrank behind us.

Levee road ran raised above marsh on packed stone and old timber matting.

Water both sides.

Tall reeds.

Wide sky.

No wagon at first.

Kel led pack mule because levee width narrowed under repair sections.

We walked.

My hands felt yesterday.

Not bad.

Grip hot spot wrapped.

Shoulders okay.

Magic pressure gone.

I did not test while walking.

Important.

Dask asked once.

"Later?"

"Not while moving."

"Agreed."

Look at us.

Professionals.

At second mile, levee passed little roadside shrine.

Not religious maybe.

Three iron hooks set into stone post with bits of ribbon, old coins, broken ceramic, one tiny cracked mirror.

I slowed.

Maro said, "Travelers leave things when levee holds through flood."

"Does it help?"

"Levee?"

"Offerings."

"Depends who you ask."

Dask said, "No controlled data."

Sori said, "Nobody asked you."

I had copper.

Could leave one.

Didn't.

Not because cynical.

Because road had held for me one mile.

Maybe earn ritual first.

Farther on, marsh opened enough to see Harrow Fen in distance to west.

Low roofs on raised berms.

Tall relay tower.

Market awnings bright red and yellow.

Big enough to tempt.

Not our route.

Greg wanted to go.

Of course.

Artifacts.

People.

Food.

A place named Fen sounded promising.

Vessa saw me looking.

"Not our route."

"I know."

"Return route?"

"Maybe."

Good enough.

Around midday levee dipped to old stone bridge over drainage cut.

Bridge narrow.

One rail missing.

Local repair sign said PASS ONE AT TIME / NO CART.

Fine.

Kel took mule around shallow ford below.

We crossed individually.

Maro first.

Vessa.

Sori.

Dask.

Me last.

Right crutch planted.

Left.

Right foot.

Halfway, something pale moved under water.

Long.

Too long for fish I knew.

I stopped because body stopped.

Not fear exactly.

Recognition without name.

Shape turned.

White back.

Four dark fins? Legs?

Then gone under bridge shadow.

I said, "Large pale animal under cut. Unknown."

Maro came back to rail end.

"How large?"

"Maybe six feet. Long body. Four dark side shapes. One pass."

"Direction?"

"Downstream."

He looked water.

Nothing.

"Could be mud sailer."

Dask from other side said, "Could be several things."

Maro: "Yes."

No one climbed down.

Good.

We continued.

Three Posts station appeared late afternoon exactly as advertised.

Three posts.

Actually four.

I hated immediately.

One was broken.

Fine.

Station itself small timber shelter beside raised signal frame.

No keeper visible.

Door open.

Vessa called.

No answer.

There.

Different pressure.

Not catastrophe yet.

Kel checked mule.

Maro looked signal frame.

Sori looked posting board.

Dask looked everything.

I looked ground.

Tracks.

Several.

Boots.

Cart? No.

One drag mark toward north reeds.

Could mean anything.

Vessa said, "Nobody spread."

Good.

We stayed together.

Inside station, kettle warm.

Not hot.

One chair tipped.

Ledger open on table.

Last line half written.

`SECOND TONE FROM WEST. ARM THREE...`

Then nothing.

There.

Reserve stirred.

Not open.

Not yet.

Vessa looked at me.

"Greg."

"Locked?"

She held my eyes.

"Not yet. Record first."

There.

Correct.

I took notebook.

Practitioner.

Magic, yes.

But mostly practice of knowing when not to become something larger too soon.

# CHAPTER 395

## THE FIGHTER

Record first.

Fine.

I recorded.

Warm kettle.

Open door.

Tipped chair.

Ledger unfinished.

Three cups on shelf.

Two used.

One clean.

Mud on floor.

Not much.

One dark smear beside table leg.

I crouched as far as crouching on one leg with crutches allowed.

"Blood?"

Sori came beside me.

"Could be rust."

Maro touched nothing.

Looked.

"Could be reed stain."

Good.

Could be lots.

I wrote **dark reddish-brown smear, source unknown**.

Dask stared at half line in ledger.

"Arm three what?"

"Stopped writing," Sori said.

"Yes."

"That's all we know."

"I know."

He sounded offended by sentence rather than her.

Vessa stood in doorway.

"Kel. Ground. Maro with him. Ten paces first. Greg and Sori inside. Dask frame from ground only. Nobody follows anything yet."

We moved.

There.

No panic.

Kel came back after maybe two minutes.

"Three people left station north. Maybe four. Hard ground loses one. Drag is heel, not body. Somebody wasn't lifting right foot."

"Running?" Vessa asked.

"First twenty paces, yes. Then mixed."

Maro said, "Reeds broken farther north. Fresh enough."

"Animal?"

"Some. Not enough yet."

Dask called from signal frame.

"Arm three is seven degrees west of rest mark."

Vessa looked up.

"Measured?"

"Against fixed index."

"Good."

I checked ledger page before unfinished line.

Routine.

Weather.

First tone from west at third bell plus nineteen.

Arm three no visible response.

Second tone at third bell plus thirty-one.

Then half line.

Twelve minutes.

I wrote it.

Sori checked prior staffing sheet.

"Three assigned today. Keeper Noll Senn. Assistant Vara Tesh. Road hand Pellin Or."

"Weapons?" I asked.

She looked at me.

"Not listed."

Fair.

Kel returned to doorway.

"Found one knife sheath by north reeds. Empty. Station issue mark."

Pressure changed.

Not enough.

Then something screamed outside.

Human.

Far.

North.

One scream.

Cut short.

There.

Vessa looked at me.

"Lock in."

Account opened.

Not power.

Not yet.

Everything else.

The room got simple.

Three missing.

One impaired gait.

Empty sheath.

Fresh broken reeds.

Scream north.

Crew six.

One mule.

Unknown threat.

Marsh terrain.

No local map in my head.

So use people who had one.

"Maro. Fastest safe line toward scream that doesn't put us single file in reeds."

He answered immediately.

"Levee twenty paces north, then east drainage shelf. Wider."

"Kel, mule stays. Anything here we can use as reach weapon?"

"Two station poles."

"Bring one. Keep one for Vessa."

Vessa did not object.

Good.

"Sori, three missing names. Keep them separate when we find people. Don't let us decide identity because we want one."

"Yes."

"Dask, you're rear and count tones. Don't stare at frame while walking."

"I don't do that."

"You do."

"I can stop."

"Do."

Vessa said, "Greg."

I looked.

"You direct movement. I own abort."

"Good."

There.

No argument.

We moved.

Kel handed me station pole.

Ash wood.

Six feet.

Iron cap.

Awkward with crutches.

I gave it back.

"No. You."

He took it.

I had knife.

Crutches.

Body.

Enough.

North levee was narrow but open.

Maro led fifteen paces.

Not farther.

Vessa behind him.

Me center.

Sori and Dask.

Kel rear with pole.

Wrong order for fight maybe.

Right order for not losing record people in marsh.

Another sound.

Not scream.

Barking cough.

Reed hound.

Then another.

Then human voice.

"Get off!"

Close.

Maro pointed east.

Drainage shelf widened into mud-flat between reed walls.

We saw first person.

Man on ground.

Alive.

One leg tangled in collapsed signal rope or reed line.

Two reed hounds around him.

Adult.

Not the shy road watchers.

One had blood on muzzle.

Third animal farther back worried something in reeds.

Vessa said, "People first."

I was already counting.

Man ground.

One woman standing behind low stone marker with broken pole in both hands.

Second person.

Third missing.

Hounds three visible.

Maybe more.

Distance twenty-five paces.

Wind cross-left.

Mud shelf firm center, dark soft edges.

The hound nearest man lunged when he kicked.

"HEY!"

My voice cracked across flat.

All three heads turned.

Good.

Not because magic.

Because predator attention was cheap.

"Kel, left of me. Pole forward. Maro, stones. Sori and Dask stay levee. Vessa with them until we have line."

Vessa said, "Do it."

We advanced.

Not charge.

Crutch.

Crutch.

Foot.

Fast enough.

Kel's pole gave reach.

Maro picked fist-sized stones from levee edge.

First hound came toward us.

Long-legged tan thing.

Bigger close.

Maybe seventy pounds.

Narrow body.

Wide jaw.

Not monster.

Still teeth.

I knew teeth.

First life had taught me glamorous truth that most things with mouths wanted your soft parts.

"Don't chase if it breaks," I said.

Kel: "Wasn't planning."

Hound feinted right.

Kel tracked with pole tip.

Good.

Second hound stayed by injured man.

Third disappeared into reeds.

Bad.

"Maro. Third."

"I saw."

He shifted angle.

First hound committed.

Fast.

Kel jabbed.

Animal twisted around pole better than dog should.

Came inside reach.

Toward me.

Of course.

One leg.

Easy shape.

Except I had fought things before.

A lot.

Left crutch planted.

Right crutch came free.

Not swing.

Thrust.

Metal cuff into hound's shoulder as it entered.

I turned body with impact instead of fighting it straight.

Animal went sideways.

Not far.

Enough.

Jaw snapped where thigh had been.

Right foot pivoted.

Left side absent.

Old body wanted step that did not exist.

Current body knew better.

I dropped right crutch tip into mud behind animal's foreleg and shoved across its line.

Not elegant.

Hound stumbled.

Kel's pole arrived across neck and drove it away.

"Back!"

We backed together.

No victory pose.

Hound circled.

My heart had barely started.

There.

That was old too.

Not courage.

Timing.

Fear came later when useful.

Second hound lunged at man again.

Woman behind marker screamed and swung broken pole.

Missed.

"Maro, second!"

Stone flew.

Hit ribs.

Hound turned.

Good.

"Kel, hold this one."

"Got it."

I moved right.

Crutch.

Crutch.

Foot.

Faster.

Mud sucked one tip.

Corrected without looking.

Second hound saw me.

I saw man.

Older, maybe forties.

Right ankle tangled.

Blood on left forearm.

Not massive.

Conscious.

"Can you use hands?"

"Yes!"

"Untangle, don't kick."

"It's fucking biting me!"

"Then hurry."

Hound came.

I had knife now.

Didn't want knife.

Knife meant close.

Crutch meant close too, but longer close.

Right crutch off ground.

Animal slowed before lunge.

There.

Shoulders lower.

Weight back then forward.

Same lie predators had used in two lives.

I jabbed first.

Not at head.

Nose moved too much.

Front shoulder.

It slipped outside.

Expected.

Knife hand stayed low.

Hound snapped at crutch shaft.

Teeth hit wood.

I let it bite for half heartbeat.

Then rotated shaft hard.

Jaw alignment broke.

Animal released.

I struck down across muzzle with forearm and crutch handle.

It yelped.

Backed.

"Now," I told man.

He ripped foot free.

Badly.

Crawled toward marker.

Woman grabbed collar and hauled.

Third hound exploded from reeds behind her.

There.

Wrong side.

Too far.

Maro shouted.

Kel couldn't cross me.

Vessa had station pole now.

Sori and Dask on levee.

Third hound went for woman's back.

No time for clean.

I reached.

Not with body.

With thing that barely worked.

External.

Not brass.

Not washer.

Animal.

Terrible target.

Moving.

Heavy.

I did not try to move hound.

Too much.

I hit what mattered.

Front paw.

Tiny sideways demand at moment it planted.

Something answered.

Maybe.

Paw landed wrong by inches.

Maybe mud.

Maybe me.

Hound's shoulder dipped.

Its leap clipped stone marker instead of woman's spine.

Vessa's pole smashed across ribs.

Animal rolled.

Got up immediately.

"No claim!" Dask yelled from levee.

Fucking Dask.

"Later!"

First hound broke from Kel and ran toward pack.

Not us.

Good.

"Let it!"

Kel let it.

Second backed after first.

Third snarled at Vessa.

Blood at mouth now from stone maybe.

Vessa held pole.

I stepped beside her.

One crutch forward.

Knife still low.

Maro left.

Kel right.

Four humans facing one animal.

Hound looked.

Calculated.

Smart enough.

It left.

We did not chase.

Silence came hard.

Then injured man said, "Fuck."

Good summary.

"Names," Sori called.

There.

Back to job.

Woman: Vara Tesh.

Injured man: Noll Senn.

Keeper.

Third missing: Pellin Or.

Sori repeated each.

Confirmed.

No assumption.

Vara pointed into reeds where third hound had been worrying something.

"Pellin's there. He fell. They dragged him off shelf."

Vessa looked at me.

Still locked.

I hated next calculation.

Two found.

One injured.

Hounds withdrew but not gone.

Pellin unknown.

Reeds dense.

Could be alive.

Could be bait by accident, not intention.

Predators might return.

"Maro, can we enter without losing sight of each other?"

"Three abreast for first thirty paces. Then no."

"Any water cut?"

"Likely."

Vessa: "We do thirty. If no visual, back and reassess."

Good.

"Kel stays with Noll and Vara. Sori with them. Dask watches rear and counts animals, not tones."

Dask said, "I can count both."

"Animals first."

"Fine."

Maro, Vessa, me.

Into reeds.

I hated reeds.

Visibility shoulder-width.

Ground wet.

Crutch tips sank two inches some plants.

Maro tested with boot.

I tested with crutch.

Vessa carried pole.

Blood on reeds after eight paces.

Not much.

Broken stalks.

Drag.

Then voice.

Weak.

"Here."

Pellin.

Alive.

Twelve paces farther.

He lay half in shallow drainage cut, one hand gripping root, trouser leg torn from knee to calf.

Blood.

More than Noll.

No hound on him.

One hound across cut.

Watching.

Different one maybe.

Hard to know.

"Don't move," Vessa said.

Pellin laughed once.

"Wasn't."

Good.

Cut maybe six feet across.

Water knee-deep? Unknown.

Hound opposite.

Maro whispered, "Shelf bends left ten paces. We can reach him our side."

"Do that."

We moved.

Hound paralleled opposite bank.

No attack line yet.

Pellin saw me.

"Who are you?"

"Terrible time."

"Fair."

We reached him.

Leg injury ugly but not arterial from what I could see.

I was not healer.

Vessa knew more field first aid than me here.

She compressed with folded cloth.

Maro watched hound.

I watched reeds behind.

Then barking cough answered from our side.

Close.

One.

Two.

Shit.

Enough stopped being enough.

Not power.

Output.

I stopped being conversational.

"Vessa, how long to bind?"

"One minute."

"Maro, opposite hound stays opposite?"

"Yes."

"Our side two, maybe more. Exit line same. We do not carry Pellin through reeds with hounds pressing. Need them to break first."

Vessa looked up.

"Can you make that happen?"

"Probably."

There.

Not definitely.

Probably was enough.

I pulled knife.

Passed it to Maro.

"You keep blade. I need both crutches."

He took it.

I shifted grip.

One crutch normal left support.

Right crutch higher.

Not a sword.

Lever.

Staff half.

Weapon because anything became weapon when needed.

First hound came through reeds.

I heard before saw.

"Right."

Maro turned.

Animal burst.

I stepped toward it.

That surprised everyone, including hound.

Right crutch thrust under jaw.

Up.

Not hard enough to crush.

Hard enough to lift head.

Maro hit flank with knife pommel, not blade.

Hound twisted.

I pivoted on right foot, let left crutch become axis, and drove shoulder into animal's ribs as it passed.

Pain flashed through my shoulder.

Fine.

Hound hit reeds.

Second came immediately.

No pause.

Better pack behavior.

It targeted planted right leg.

Smart.

I gave it crutch instead.

Wood shaft crosswise.

Jaw closed.

This time I didn't rotate.

I shoved shaft deeper sideways and used bite as handle.

Animal's head turned.

Maro struck hindquarter.

Hound released.

I kicked mud into face.

Cheap.

Effective enough.

It backed.

First recovered.

Two now.

Opposite-bank hound started barking.

Calling?

Maybe.

No time to theorize.

"Noise!" I shouted.

Maro understood.

He slammed knife pommel against Vessa's iron pole.

CLANG.

Again.

Vessa joined, pole against stone edge.

CLANG.

I yelled.

Not words.

Just volume.

Hounds hesitated.

Predators liked isolated injured things.

We became loud ugly group.

One still came.

Fine.

This one I hit.

Really hit.

Right crutch swung short from shoulder into side of skull as it crossed.

Not pretty.

Impact numbed hand.

Animal folded one step.

I followed with shaft thrust to chest.

It fell into reeds.

Got up.

Ran.

Second followed.

Opposite hound disappeared.

We kept noise ten more breaths.

Then stopped.

My right hand shook.

Not fear.

Impact.

Shoulder hurt.

Current body sent invoice.

Fine.

"Bound," Vessa said.

Good.

Pellin stared at me.

"Bronze?"

"Apparently."

Maro laughed once.

Wrong time.

Useful anyway.

Extraction.

We did not carry Pellin like hero painting.

Vessa and Maro got him upright.

I moved first, testing ground and calling stable placements because crutches made me unusually good at caring which patches lied.

There.

Competence was weird.

S-class veteran leading injured man out of reeds by saying, "Left patch soft. Root here. Step wide."

Worked.

At shelf, Kel and Sori took Pellin.

Noll's arm wrapped.

Vara had bruised shoulder, no bite.

Dask said, "Four hounds observed total. Possibly five if opposite-bank animal differed from initial third."

"Good," I said.

He blinked.

I was still locked.

Too sharp.

"Any behind us?"

"None observed for thirty breaths."

"Good."

Vessa touched my shoulder.

Not hurt one.

"Down."

There.

Switch.

Harder than on.

I breathed.

Looked at station.

Looked at people.

Noll alive.

Vara alive.

Pellin alive.

Crew alive.

No hound dead that I knew.

Good.

My hand hurt.

Shoulder hurt.

Heart finally decided event had happened and started pounding.

"I hate dogs," I said.

Maro said, "You liked them yesterday."

"Those were farther away."

Normal enough.

Sori looked at me strangely.

Not afraid.

Recalculating.

Dask too.

Kel less.

Kel had probably already decided I was weird.

Vessa said, "Station. First aid. Then statements."

Nobody argued.

Back inside, mundane explanation arrived in pieces.

Noll had heard second west tone.

Arm three moved hard north then stopped west of rest.

He and Vara went outside.

Pellin saw reed hounds near signal rope anchor and went to drive them off because they were worrying something in grass.

One animal lunged.

Pellin ran.

Noll and Vara followed to help.

Pellin slipped off shelf.

Noll tangled right foot in old dropped signal line.

Vara tried to hold animals with broken marker pole.

No conspiracy.

No mysterious attacker.

Bad decisions.

Predators.

Terrain.

A station abandoned because three people all ran toward same problem.

Human.

The dark smear inside?

Reed dye from Vara's wet glove when she grabbed table.

Tipped chair?

Noll hit it leaving.

Warm kettle?

They had made tea.

There.

Mystery murdered by explanation.

Good.

The false tones and arm movement remained unexplained.

Separate problem.

Also good.

Sori took statements.

Dask inspected signal frame with Noll from ground.

Maro checked hound activity from safe shelf.

Kel found dropped station knife in reeds near Noll's first position.

I sat.

Vessa checked my shoulder.

"Lift."

I lifted.

Pain.

"Again."

"Still shoulder."

"Funny."

"Learning from you."

No obvious dislocation.

Bruise likely.

Right palm sore from impact.

Crutch shaft had tooth grooves.

That bothered me more.

"Those are expensive."

Vessa stared.

"You're alive."

"Both can matter."

She sat across.

"You locked when asked."

"Yes."

"You gave orders inside my command."

"Yes."

"Most were good."

"Most?"

There.

She smiled tiny.

"You're back."

Fuck.

"Which was bad?"

"Stepping toward first hound in reeds."

"It changed its timing."

"And risked your face."

"Face unpopular anyway."

"Greg."

I stopped.

"Fair."

She leaned elbows on knees.

"That thing you do."

"Which thing?"

"You get quieter. Faster. You stop asking questions you already know how to bound."

I looked at hands.

"Old work."

"I figured."

"Not magic."

"I figured that too."

"Current body can't cash all checks old brain writes."

"I noticed."

Shoulder.

Yes.

"But?" I asked.

"But useful."

There.

Competent.

Not praise exactly.

Better.

Dask appeared in doorway.

"The hound stumble."

Of course.

"No."

"I need to ask."

"Later."

"Did you attempt external effect?"

"Yes. One. Moving front paw at plant. No controlled target. No way to separate mud, animal motion, or effect."

He inhaled.

"Count it?"

I thought.

"Attempt, yes. Success, unknown."

He nodded immediately.

Good.

External one-thirty-five.

Not breakthrough.

One desperate ugly attempt with ambiguous result.

Real field use anyway.

That mattered.

Sori called from table.

"Greg, statement."

I went.

Recorded what I did.

Including crutch strikes.

Including magic attempt.

Including that Vessa authorized lock-in, not individual tactics one by one.

Sori looked up.

"You really wrote 'threw mud into animal's face.'"

"It happened."

"I'm keeping it."

"Please don't make it heroic."

"Impossible. Mud ruins tone."

Good.

Evening came while we were still at Three Posts.

Route north delayed.

Obviously.

Pellin could not travel immediately.

Noll refused evacuation until relief arrived because station needed staff.

Vessa sent signal request for relief and medical cart from nearest north maintenance post.

Estimated after dark if road clear.

We would stay.

Fine.

Adventure sometimes meant not leaving because someone got bitten.

I ate station stew with left hand because right hurt.

Dask sat opposite.

He kept looking at tooth marks in crutch.

"Don't."

"I'm not."

"You are visibly thinking."

"I am always thinking."

"Try less."

He lasted maybe thirty seconds.

"Your first strike angle was unusual."

There.

I sighed.

"Because I wasn't trying to hit its head."

"Why?"

"Moving target. Shoulder bigger. Change line first. Damage second."

Kel from stove said, "That's why you shoved instead of swung."

"First time."

"Second?"

"Second I used bite."

Sori looked horrified.

"You used the bite."

"It had already committed jaw to shaft. That's a handle."

"That is an upsetting sentence."

Maro said, "I liked pot lid better."

"Me too."

Vessa ate without joining.

Then said, "Tomorrow, if relief arrives and Pellin is stable, we continue."

There.

Still route.

Not quest.

Not monster hunt.

We were not hunting hounds.

We had found people.

Protected them.

Fought because fighting happened between those two things.

My shoulder throbbed.

I flexed right hand.

Bronze body.

Old timing.

One leg.

Two crutches.

Enough.

For today.

Fighter.

I had almost forgotten how ordinary that word used to feel.

# CHAPTER 396

## THE PATIENT

Morning hurt more.

Of course it did.

Night had been generous enough to let me believe shoulder was only annoyed.

Morning clarified.

Shoulder was furious.

Right palm stiff.

Forearm sore.

Left side fine.

Right foot fine.

Good.

Important inventory.

I sat on station bench and lifted arm slowly.

Forward.

Fine until shoulder height.

Sideways.

Worse.

Back.

Absolutely not.

Vessa watched from doorway.

"Still funny?"

"Less."

"Good."

"Cruel."

"Useful."

She came over.

Pressed around shoulder.

Not gentle.

"Sharp?"

"There."

"Here?"

"Less."

"Here?"

"I hate you."

"That's not a location."

Sori from table said, "Upper outside shoulder. He means upper outside shoulder."

"Traitor."

"Recorder."

Dask was already awake.

Of course.

He had my damaged crutch across knees.

I stared.

"Why do you have that?"

"Inspection."

"Permission?"

He looked at crutch.

Then me.

"Implied by bite marks."

"No."

"Fair."

He handed it back immediately.

Growth.

I inspected myself.

Four tooth grooves along upper shaft.

One deeper than rest.

No crack I could see.

Metal cuff fine.

Tip seated.

Hand grip tight.

Kel crouched beside me.

"Roll it."

I rolled shaft across bench.

No wobble.

He thumbed deepest groove.

"Surface bite."

"You certified crutches now?"

"No. I know wood."

"Better."

Vessa said, "No fighting with it today."

"Planning none."

"No hard leverage either."

"Less fun."

"Greg."

"Yes."

There.

Patient.

Apparently that was today.

Not patient as calm person.

Patient as injured idiot forced to accept information.

I hated role already.

Relief cart had arrived after dark.

Two med hands and one station replacement.

Pellin survived night without worsening bleed.

Good.

His leg looked awful anyway.

One med hand, **Jessa Ril**, had broad wrists, gray hair tied tight, and tone of someone who considered everyone between her and bandages an administrative problem.

She checked Pellin first.

Then Noll.

Then Vara.

Then me because Vessa told her.

I objected.

"I'm not station staff."

Jessa said, "Pain doesn't respect payroll. Sit."

Fine.

She rotated shoulder more carefully than Vessa.

Still hurt.

"Bruised soft tissue, maybe strain. No obvious break. No obvious joint displacement. You get numbness?"

"No."

"Weak grip?"

I squeezed fingers.

Right weaker because sore.

"Pain-limited."

"Any neck pain?"

"No."

"Good. Keep load down today. Cold wrap now, warm later if swelling stays low. If pain climbs instead of settles, stop being clever."

"I can try."

She looked at Vessa.

"Can he?"

Vessa said, "Intermittently."

Everyone loved me.

Jessa wrapped shoulder with cool clay cloth.

Felt wonderful.

I hated that too.

Pellin was going south in medical cart to Harrow Fen infirmary.

There.

Harrow Fen again.

He looked annoyed by direction.

"I was supposed to finish north line."

Jessa said, "You're supposed to keep leg."

"Both can happen."

"Not today."

I liked her.

Noll stayed at station with replacement keeper **Beren Holt**.

Vara stayed half day to finish incident handoff, then would ride south with supply wagon if shoulder worsened.

No deaths.

Good ending for yesterday.

Not dramatic enough for songs.

Better.

Breakfast quiet at first.

Not awkward.

Just tired.

Then Dask said, "I reviewed the fight sequence."

Sori put spoon down.

"No."

"I wasn't asking Greg to repeat anything."

"Still no."

"I only have one question."

"You never have one question."

"One primary question."

Kel stood.

"I'm checking mule."

Coward.

Maro drank tea and looked at wall.

Vessa kept eating.

Dask pointed at my crutch.

"When first hound entered inside Kel's pole, you changed right crutch from support to thrust before shifting balance."

"Yes."

"Why didn't you fall?"

Good question.

Actual question.

I thought.

"Because left crutch and right foot already formed stable triangle. Right crutch wasn't carrying much at that moment."

He blinked.

"You knew exact load?"

"No. Felt it."

"First-life habit?"

"Partly. Current-body habit too."

There.

Important.

Not everything useful belonged dead old man.

This body had taught me things first one never needed.

Crutch angles.

One-foot pivots.

How much weight a shoulder could take before hand placement got stupid.

How ground lied differently when you only got one foot to vote.

S-class memory helped.

Nineteen-year-old Greg had done work too.

I liked that.

Dask nodded slowly.

"So not transferable."

"Not directly."

"Good."

Sori looked suspicious.

"Why good?"

"Because I was afraid he had secret equation."

"You would marry equation."

"Depends equation."

Maro finally laughed.

There.

Normal.

Vessa finished bowl.

"Route."

Everyone reset.

Beren had current north line report.

Three Posts to **Lark Spur** open.

Upper return-bed road wet but usable on foot and pack mule.

No wagon until Lark Spur.

Signal frames between stations operating manually because of false tones.

Important.

Not closed.

Manual.

Workers physically confirm before moving anything consequential.

Good system response.

Beren said, "You may hear tones. Ignore unless local hand confirms."

Dask looked wounded.

"Ignore?"

"Record if you want. Don't obey them."

Better wording.

Vessa asked, "Distance?"

"Six local miles to Lark Spur. Two raised runs, one bog bridge, then return-bed ridge."

"Shelter?"

"Halfway stone shed. Lark has station bunks if you arrive before night clerk sells them to freight crew."

"Sells?"

"Assigns. But he acts like landlord."

Sori wrote name.

"Who?"

"Elden Marr."

"Good."

I asked, "Anything likely to bite us?"

Beren looked at me.

"Usually no."

Fuck this region.

We left after first bell.

Pellin left south at same time.

He lifted hand from cart.

"Bronze."

I lifted left hand.

"Leg."

He looked down at bandaged leg.

"Fair."

Jessa slapped cart side.

They rolled west-south toward Harrow Fen road.

For one moment I wanted to go with them.

Not because injured.

Market awnings.

Food.

Artifacts.

Civilization.

Then north levee waited.

I wanted that too.

Good problem.

We walked.

Slowly.

Meaning I walked slowly.

Everyone else had to suffer pace or spread.

Vessa chose pace.

I objected after quarter mile.

"You can go normal. I can trail with Kel."

"No."

"I'm not fragile."

"No."

"That's not argument."

"Correct."

Sori said, "Patient."

I hated title more.

Shoulder hated crutch load after fifteen minutes.

So we adjusted.

Not heroic.

Practical.

Harness strap changed to take shoulder bag weight off right side.

Kel carried record case for first run.

I used right crutch shorter plants.

Vessa cut pace by maybe ten percent.

Maro took lead.

Dask was told not to quantify slowdown out loud.

He agreed with visible pain.

Road north left reeds gradually.

Upper return beds were wide shallow basins cut into marsh shelf, mostly dry now.

Cracked pale clay under water stains.

Low stone ridges separated each bed.

Every hundred paces, black iron stakes stood with small glass cups mounted near top.

Some cups empty.

Some held dull silver liquid.

I looked.

Dask looked too.

Neither asked.

Maro noticed.

"Old return samplers."

Dask inhaled.

Maro held up finger.

"No. That's enough."

Beautiful.

Second raised run crossed on timber matting.

My right hand hurt more than shoulder there.

Grip pressure.

I said, "Ten breaths."

Vessa stopped.

Nobody commented.

That mattered more after yesterday.

Because they had seen what I could do when pushed.

And nobody treated rest as contradiction.

Good.

Competence did not require pretending body hadn't sent bill.

We continued.

At third mile, manual signal crew stood beside low mast.

Two people.

One woman on lever.

One man with sighting glass.

As we approached, mast sounded one low tone.

Nobody moved.

They waited.

Woman looked toward next ridge.

Man checked sighting glass.

Then raised green paddle manually.

Only then did woman move lever.

Signal arm shifted.

There.

Infrastructure learning from uncertainty.

Dask whispered, "Redundancy."

"Yes."

"Good redundancy."

"Yes."

He looked almost emotional.

Sori said, "Don't cry."

"I don't cry at redundancy."

"You would."

"Maybe once."

Manual crew had news.

False tones continued intermittently west and south.

No unexplained physical gate movements confirmed north of Three Posts.

Arm drift happened at two stations, one west-facing and one north-facing.

Maintenance from Harrow Fen moving circuit.

Again.

Separate observations.

No theory yet.

Good.

We ate at stone shed halfway.

Bread.

Cold meat.

Pickled something purple.

I disliked it.

Dask loved it.

Suspicious.

Maro inspected my shoulder wrap.

"You swelling?"

"Little."

"Pain worse?"

"Same."

"Hand?"

"Sore."

"Good."

"Why is everyone saying good to pain?"

"Because worse is bad."

Fair.

He handed me flask.

I drank.

Sori sat beside me.

Quiet for maybe minute.

Then:

"You scared me yesterday."

There.

Not accusation exactly.

I looked at her.

"Hounds?"

"No. You."

Fairer.

I waited.

She rubbed thumb along cup rim.

"Not because you were violent. We were all violent. Vessa hit one hard enough I heard ribs."

Vessa from other side said, "I can hear you."

"Good."

Sori continued.

"You changed."

"Yes."

"Like you stopped spending words."

That was accurate.

"Probably."

"And people became pieces."

There.

Harder.

"Variables," I said.

"That's not better."

"No."

I looked at hands.

"That's why I don't live there."

She nodded.

"I figured after."

"You were still people."

"I know."

"Important distinction."

"Still unsettling."

"Also fair."

She looked toward Vessa.

"When she said down, you looked annoyed."

"I was."

"At her?"

"At everything getting slow again."

There.

Truth.

Locked mode made world simple.

Normal world had nuance.

People.

Feelings.

Pain.

Purple pickles.

Harder in different way.

Sori said, "That sounds awful."

"Sometimes useful."

"Not same thing."

"No."

She nodded.

Conversation done.

Not fixed.

Better.

We left shed.

Bog bridge came near fourth mile.

Not bridge exactly.

Series of floating timber rectangles chained between stone anchors.

Wonderful.

My favorite.

Vessa looked at me.

"Can you?"

I tested first panel with crutch.

Moved two inches.

Shoulder said no enthusiasm.

"Yes. Slow."

Kel went ahead without being asked.

Good.

He positioned one panel at a time with boot before I transferred.

No pride issue.

Yesterday I fought hounds.

Today Kel stabilized floating wood for me.

Both true.

Halfway across, a small silver fish jumped onto panel.

Flopped.

I stopped.

Dask behind said, "Don't kick it."

"Why would I kick it?"

"Reflex."

"Whose?"

"Unknown."

Sori said, "Dask wants fish incident."

I nudged fish with crutch tip edge toward water.

It flipped itself off.

Victory.

No ledger entry.

Progress.

On far side, ground rose.

Marsh thinned.

First real trees in hours.

Thin white-barked trunks with dark leaves.

Maro called them **glass birch**.

Dask said they were not actually glass.

We all said, "We know."

He looked betrayed.

Lark Spur appeared before evening.

More than station.

Less than town.

Road junction on dry ridge.

Stable.

Two inns pretending to be sheds.

Repair yard.

Signal tower.

Small row of market tables under patched awnings.

There.

Market.

Greg lived.

Vessa saw my face.

"One hour after station check."

I stopped.

"Really?"

"If bunks secured and route board clean."

"I love you."

"Don't."

Sori laughed.

Station clerk **Elden Marr** did act like landlord.

Thin man with silver spectacles and vest too nice for ridge dust.

"Survey priority gets four bunks."

Vessa said, "We're six."

"Then two floor."

"Assignment covers six beds."

"It covers six sleeping places."

Sori stepped forward.

Oh no.

Twenty minutes later we had six bunks.

I don't know what she did.

I did not ask.

Some wars belonged experts.

Route board north showed next day open to **Greyhook Rise**, then upper return beds beyond.

No closure.

Manual signal confirmation still active.

Good.

Vessa gave us hour.

"No buying anything that affects load without asking."

She looked at me.

"Still targeted."

"Yes."

I walked market with shoulder wrapped and one crutch carrying slightly less weight.

Tiny market.

Food.

Boot repair.

Two baskets of old brass fittings.

A woman selling weather charms that looked like tied wire.

One table of retired station hardware.

There.

Of course.

I stopped.

Seller was boy maybe sixteen with scar across chin and expression of ancient disappointment.

"Don't touch black tags."

"Wasn't."

"Everyone says that after."

On table:

Old signal cups.

Cracked relay plates.

Ceramic caps.

One palm-sized brass **line quietener** with three notches and faded black-slash retirement mark.

Label handwritten:

`DAMPENS FALSE / ECHO TONE ON SHORT COPPER RUNS. RETIRED. 8c.`

Eight.

Interesting.

Very interesting.

Visible utility.

Current regional false-tone problem.

Price reflected it.

Rational.

I asked, "Why retired?"

Boy shrugged.

"Makes true weak tones quieter too. Stations stopped using after missed flood warning twelve years ago."

There.

Downside.

Real.

"Documented?"

He produced stamped old maintenance card.

Lark district.

Short copper runs only.

Not universal.

Not useless.

Potentially dangerous if used stupidly.

Eight copper.

I had twenty-three.

Could buy.

Did not.

Not yet.

I asked if he'd hold until morning.

"One bit."

There.

Bits unspecified.

Continuity trap.

I smiled.

"No."

"Then maybe here tomorrow."

Fair.

I walked away.

Patient.

Not because injured.

Because waiting could also be competence.

Wait for shoulder.

Wait for evidence.

Wait before buying tool just because current problem made it sexy.

Wait before deciding crew now saw me differently forever.

Wait before opening reserve again.

I could do that.

Probably.

At food stall I bought nothing because dinner covered.

Growth continued.

Back at bunkhouse, Dask was asleep already.

Early.

He looked dead.

Sori whispered, "No grinding yet."

Kel said, "Give him time."

Maro had taken top bunk.

Vessa was writing route notes.

I lay down carefully.

Shoulder complained.

Hand complained.

Good.

Not good.

Stable.

Today no fight.

No magic.

No crisis.

We moved six miles north anyway.

Sometimes adventure was surviving enough yesterday to still have road today.

Patient.

I could hate it tomorrow.

# CHAPTER 397

## THE CUSTOMER

I woke up because my shoulder had opinions.

Not loud ones.

Persistent.

Roll wrong.

Pain.

Reach wrong.

Pain.

Exist ambitiously.

Pain.

Good morning.

Lark Spur bunks were narrow enough that turning over required planning anyway.

Across room, Dask slept on his back with mouth open.

Teeth quiet.

Either cured or plotting.

Sori had blanket over face.

Kel already gone.

Maro sat by window eating something from paper.

"What?" I asked.

"Breakfast."

"Looks like insulation."

"Oat cake."

"Same thing."

He held out half.

I took it with left hand.

Current shoulder wanted less work.

Patient.

Still.

Annoying.

Outside, Lark Spur had morning color.

Dust.

White bark.

Canvas awnings snapping.

Stable smell.

Signal mast with three arms all behaving themselves for once.

Market tables reopened before road crews finished breakfast.

Of course they did.

People liked money more than sleep.

Relatable.

I checked shoulder before doing anything stupid.

Forward lift better than yesterday.

Side lift still unpleasant.

Grip sore but functional.

No numbness.

No new swelling I could tell.

Right crutch still had tooth marks.

I ran thumb over deepest groove.

Ugly.

No crack.

Fine.

Vessa found me outside.

"How bad?"

"Less bad."

"Useful number."

"Seven bad yesterday. Five bad now."

"That is not useful."

"You started it."

She looked at range once.

"Low load again today."

"Yes."

"No practice."

"Yes."

"No fighting."

"Planning not to."

"Good plan."

There.

Authority preserved.

Then she looked past me toward market.

"You going back to brass thing?"

"Maybe."

"Why?"

Good question.

Not accusation.

So I answered.

"Because eight copper is enough that I should decide instead of pretending I'm not interested."

She nodded.

"Fair. We leave second bell."

One hour.

Customer.

Apparently that was today.

I went back to hardware table.

Young seller was there.

Same chin scar.

Same expression suggesting civilization had disappointed them personally.

The line quietener still sat under little square of glass.

Palm-sized brass.

Three notches.

Black-slash retirement mark.

Eight copper.

I did not touch.

Seller looked at me.

"You came back."

"Against everyone's advice."

"Good customers do."

"That sounds predatory."

"Market."

Fair.

I pointed at quietener.

"Show me maintenance card again."

Seller slid it over.

Stamped old district works card.

Short copper run.

Tone suppression tested under echo-return conditions.

Retired after weak true tone suppression contributed to missed warning.

No magic poetry.

Good.

"You said district stopped using them twelve years ago."

"This style. Some newer ones have split threshold plates."

"And cost?"

"More than you want."

"Try me."

"Forty-two copper new, if you can get order accepted."

I stared.

"I didn't want."

Seller shrugged.

"Told you."

Good market.

Eight for obsolete dangerous version.

Forty-two for modern safer one.

Rational.

I asked, "What exactly counts as short run?"

Seller leaned back.

"Card says under three relay lengths."

"Local length?"

"Yes."

"Which means nothing to me."

"Then don't install it."

"Not planning to."

"Then what are you buying?"

There.

Good seller.

I thought.

Not a solution.

Not crew equipment.

Not something I could legally slap onto station line.

Personal artifact-ish hardware with known rule: suppresses some false/echo tones on short copper, but risks suppressing weak true tone.

Potentially useful for experiments later.

Potentially useful around weird tone systems.

Potentially dangerous enough to be interesting.

Eight copper.

I had twenty-three.

Vale twenty-three.

Buying would leave fifteen.

Not poverty.

Not trivial.

Night Rest had been twenty-eight.

Step Keeper six.

Three Reeds five.

This fit current artifact tier.

Question wasn't can.

Worth.

I asked, "Can it be used disconnected?"

Seller's expression changed half degree.

Better question.

"Test bench, yes. Needs copper line in and out. Doesn't need active station."

"Minimum?"

"Handspan each side works for bench. But that's not same as field behavior."

"Good. Any residual response without tone source?"

"Not that card says."

"Any known interaction with reflective field cases?"

"No claim."

"Return-light?"

"No claim."

"Mana load?"

"Passive plate."

"Can it be opened?"

"Physically."

"Safely?"

"Depends who."

"Helpful."

"Accurate."

I liked seller more.

Bad sign.

"Name?"

"Tirren."

"Greg."

"I know."

I stared.

"How?"

"You fought hounds at Three Posts."

There.

Fuck.

"That was yesterday."

"Road's faster than you."

Of course.

Reputation.

Uneven.

Regional.

Not fame.

Still annoying.

"What did you hear?"

Tirren counted on fingers.

"One-legged survey hand. Crutches. Hit dog. Three people alive. Somebody said magic. Somebody else said mud."

"Last person smarter."

"Probably."

"And you know my name?"

"Medical cart stopped here. Pellin talks."

Human network.

Not prophecy.

Good.

I looked at quietener again.

"Seven."

Tirren laughed.

Actually laughed.

"No."

"It's retired."

"Yes."

"Known dangerous failure mode."

"Yes."

"Nobody sane installs it."

"Correct."

"Seven."

"Eight."

"You like it too much."

"You came back."

Damn.

I tried different angle.

"Maintenance card included?"

"At eight."

"Copper bench leads?"

"No."

"Two handspans scrap lead?"

Tirren thought.

"Nine with leads."

"Eight with leads."

"Eight and one bit."

Bits.

Unknown cursed economy lane.

No.

"Eight flat. You keep the bit."

Tirren narrowed eyes.

"That's not how bargaining works."

"It is now."

Long pause.

"Fine."

There.

I bought it.

Eight copper.

Greg twenty-three to fifteen.

Vale twenty-three.

Symmetry broken.

Good.

Sori would enjoy that less than me.

Tirren wrapped quietener in wax cloth, maintenance card separate, two short copper leads coiled around outside.

"Do not use it on live signal line unless someone who owns line says yes."

"Good rule."

"Do not decide false tone is safer than weak true tone."

"Better rule."

"Do not open plate with knife."

"Why?"

"Because people do."

"That wasn't answer."

"Spring leaf. Cuts fingers."

"There."

I paid.

Customer.

Actual artifact-ish acquisition.

Not hidden miracle.

Not bargain of century.

Dangerous obsolete tool with known use and known reason world stopped trusting it.

Perfect.

I put it in shoulder bag carefully.

Then immediately moved shoulder bag to left-side carry because right shoulder complained.

Perfect less.

At station, Sori saw purchase wrap.

"You bought it."

"Yes."

"Eight?"

"Eight."

"So fifteen."

"Do not say numbers like judgment."

"I am literally custody."

"Of Survey things."

"Emotionally broader."

Dask appeared.

"Can I see?"

"Later."

"Why?"

"Because we leave."

He looked injured.

"You bought experimental hardware and won't let me inspect?"

"Correct."

"Cruel."

"Growth."

Vessa checked wrap.

"Personal. Not field equipment."

"Yes."

"No live-line use under my assignment without local owner authorization."

"Yes."

"No testing while moving."

"Yes."

"Good."

Maro said, "I hate it already."

"You haven't seen it."

"You like it."

Fair.

Second bell.

We left Lark Spur.

Road toward Greyhook Rise changed fast.

Dry ridge climbed.

Marsh fell away behind.

White-barked trees thinned into low dark scrub.

Stone underfoot became red-brown and sharp-edged.

Wind stronger.

No reeds.

No water smell.

After days of wet country, dryness felt wrong.

My crutch tips clicked instead of sank.

Good.

Shoulder less good.

Kel carried one shared case again.

I carried only bag and self.

Greyhook road had old iron hooks hammered into stone every fifty paces.

Huge.

Rust-black.

Some empty.

Some held chains disappearing downslope.

I waited almost ten minutes before asking.

Personal best.

"What are hooks?"

Maro said, "Old freight anchors."

Dask added, "And storm line points."

"Same hooks?"

"Often."

"For wagons?"

Kel answered.

"Heavy freight on crosswind days. Chain wagon to next hook, move chain, repeat."

I looked down slope.

Steep enough.

"That sounds miserable."

"It is."

Good.

World had infrastructure for weather.

We climbed.

At first rise, wind hit hard enough to shove coat against body.

My balance hated lateral force.

Vessa saw.

"Need route adjustment?"

"Not yet. Shorter plants."

Right shoulder protested if I planted too far.

So shorter.

Current body again.

No pride.

At one iron hook, strips of cloth tied around base.

Not shrine.

Maybe warning.

Maro stopped.

"Wind cut ahead."

Kel looked sky.

"How bad?"

"Normal bad."

Dask said, "That is not category."

Everyone ignored him.

Wind cut was narrow saddle between two stone shoulders.

Road crossed exposed for maybe sixty yards.

Black chain ran waist-high along uphill side between hooks.

Local sign:

HIGH WIND
ONE HAND / LINE OR RAIL
NO LOOSE LOAD
WAIT ON RED FLAG

Flag green.

Fine.

One hand line.

Problem.

I had two crutches.

Maro said, "There is lower route. Adds half mile."

I looked at saddle.

Could do it.

Probably.

Right shoulder still compromised.

One-hand line meant one crutch and one foot, ugly.

Harness maybe.

No.

Vessa asked, "What do you want?"

There.

Not can.

Want.

I wanted upper route.

I also wanted not to be idiot two days after shoulder strain.

"Lower."

Dask looked at me.

No comment.

Good man.

We took lower.

Longer path cut behind stone shoulder through little ravine.

Protected wind.

More distance.

Less stupid.

Patient becoming annoying identity.

Halfway through ravine, we found wagon.

Not ours.

Small two-wheel freight cart tipped against wall.

One wheel off.

Driver sitting on rock with bandaged hand.

Young woman standing over broken axle pin swearing continuously.

Not short Greg swearing.

Long narrative swearing.

"I told him, I told him if that pin went twice in wet season it was going to go again because wet season isn't fucking magic that resets iron, and he says no no, Mora, you grease it and it forgives you, like iron has religion, and now look, now fucking look at it."

There.

Dialogue variance alive.

Kel stopped.

"Need help?"

Mora said, "Need new pin. Unless one of you is secretly blacksmith."

Dask almost raised hand.

Sori physically put hand on his sleeve.

"No."

Driver said, "We have spare blank. Need fit."

Kel checked.

"I can shave wood, not iron."

Mora pointed north.

"Greyhook yard has fitter. Two miles. Cart blocks half path but people can pass. We'll sit."

No emergency.

No Greg.

Except something under cart caught my eye.

Small metal disk in dirt.

Not theirs maybe.

I pointed.

"Yours?"

Mora looked.

"No."

Driver leaned.

"Old hook token."

Maro said, "Don't pick yet."

There.

Interesting.

Disk iron-gray, maybe two inches across, square hole center, one edge polished bright.

Half buried near old anchor socket in ravine wall.

Mora said, "Those wash out sometimes. Freight tally thing."

Driver corrected, "Storm tally."

"Whatever."

Dask crouched.

"Not same."

Sori said, "Of course not."

Maro looked at socket.

"Could belong old anchor system."

"Useful?" I asked.

"Maybe historically."

"Magical?"

"Maybe not."

Dask: "Could be reactive iron."

Mora: "Could be trash."

Best answer.

I did not pick it.

Not because scared.

Because local road had owner system.

Vessa asked Mora, "Road authority claim salvage?"

"Marked hardware goes Greyhook yard. Loose unmarked usually whoever, but old anchor pieces maybe Roads wants."

There.

No free loot.

Vessa marked location for road notice.

We continued.

Greg wanted disk.

Greg had just bought quietener.

Greg could survive wanting two things.

Growth.

Greyhook Rise appeared near fourth bell.

Not town.

Fortified work ridge.

Stone terraces cut into hill.

Wind towers with big slatted arms.

Freight yard.

Repair sheds.

Rows of anchor chains hanging from frames.

One long public stair climbed center ridge like challenge written personally at me.

No.

Side ramp existed.

Good civilization.

At entrance, road board listed upper return beds and something new:

**NORTH REACH SURVEY HOLD - WEATHER WINDOW PENDING**

Vessa read.

"That's us."

Good.

Greyhook station lead came out before we entered.

Tall woman in red coat with hair braided tight against wind.

"River Survey?"

Vessa handed papers.

Woman scanned.

"You're late enough to be interesting but not late enough to be problem."

"Three Posts delay."

"Heard. Hounds."

Her eyes found me.

Of course.

"Crutch man?"

"Unfortunately."

"Don't fight anything here. Wind does enough."

"Strong policy."

She introduced herself **Aven Ro**.

Greyhook road/survey liaison.

Different speech.

Fast, declarative, no patience.

"North Reach weather window opens maybe tomorrow second bell, maybe not. Upper return beds walkable. Final ridge isn't. Crosswind. We hold crews here until tower strips agree for full half bell."

Vessa: "Any alternate?"

"Lower freight tunnel, closed collapse inspection. West shelf mule line, cargo only and miserable. So wait."

There.

Adventure.

Weather.

Not enemy.

Aven continued.

"You have two nights authorized if weather holds bad. After that route order recalculates. Bunks six. Food ugly. Baths nonexistent. Market tiny. Repair yard excellent."

Repair yard.

I looked at crutch.

Aven noticed.

"That thing bitten?"

"Yes."

"Bring to Rusk. He repairs staff shafts, poles, crutch wood if hardware standard."

There.

Useful.

Not artifact.

Actual road life.

We entered.

Greyhook wind made everything louder.

Chains knocked.

Tower slats clapped.

People shouted from twenty feet because otherwise conversation belonged sky.

I liked it.

Shoulder hurt.

Quietener in bag.

Fifteen copper left.

Road north temporarily held by weather.

And somewhere behind us, an old iron token waited in dirt for Road authority to decide whether it was trash.

Customer.

I had bought one thing.

Not every thing.

That felt more dangerous than buying nothing.

Because now I had started.

# CHAPTER 398

## THE REPAIRMAN

Rusk looked at my crutch and said, "Dog?"

"Reed hound."

"Dog with wet opinions."

Fair.

Greyhook repair yard occupied half a terrace below freight anchors.

Three open-sided sheds.

Two saw benches.

Iron brazier.

Rows of staff shafts, wagon poles, chain hooks, replacement slats, and enough clamps to restrain a small god.

Wind moved through everything.

Not gusts.

Constant pressure.

Every loose strap had somewhere to slap.

Every hanging tool had been tied twice.

Rusk was maybe sixty.

Maybe forty-five with weather.

Hard to tell here.

Broad shoulders.

White beard cut short.

Two missing fingertips on left hand.

He took my right crutch only after I handed it over.

Good start.

Rolled shaft.

Pressed thumb around tooth grooves.

Held it near ear and flexed gently.

Then looked at me.

"You hit dog with this too?"

"Yes."

"After it bit?"

"Before and after."

"Mm."

That sound contained judgment and professional interest.

He set crutch on padded bench.

"Wood's sound. Deepest tooth got through finish, not through working depth. Hand grip fine. Upper cuff fine. Tip fine."

I relaxed more than expected.

Rusk noticed.

"You like these."

"I need these."

"Different."

"Both."

He nodded.

"Also different."

There.

Repair people.

He proposed shallow resin fill in grooves, wrap reinforcement around deepest mark, re-seal shaft.

Not structural splice.

Not replacement.

"Cost?"

"Assignment?"

Vessa from behind me said, "Personal equipment damaged during field incident under assignment. Reasonable repair covered if standard."

Rusk looked at her.

"Reasonable is ugly word."

"Use cheaper one."

"Covered."

Excellent.

No copper.

Economy liked paperwork sometimes.

"How long?" I asked.

"Hour if wind behaves. Two if it doesn't."

I looked outside.

Wind stole a rag off peg despite being tied.

Rusk watched it snap at cord.

"Two."

Fair.

He pointed at my shoulder.

"You favoring right?"

"Little."

"Then sit somewhere. Don't practice being heroic in my yard."

Everyone had same advice now.

I was becoming predictable.

I sat on low crate beside shed wall.

Left crutch across knees.

Right shoulder dull ache.

Better than yesterday.

Still there.

Greyhook weather hold had turned whole ridge into temporary city of people waiting professionally.

Freight crews played cards under chain awning.

Two mule handlers argued about salt blocks.

Wind tower operators walked terrace lines checking cloth strips, vanes, and weighted cords.

One woman spent ten minutes retying same canvas cover because wind wanted job too.

There.

New ecology.

Work shaped by air instead of water.

Dask arrived carrying my wrapped line quietener like sacred relic.

I frowned.

"Why do you have that?"

He stopped.

"Sori gave it to me."

Sori from doorway said, "I gave him the bag because you left it on bunk. I did not give him permission to unwrap anything."

Dask held package farther from body.

"It remains wrapped."

"Good."

He handed it over.

I took with left hand.

Shoulder complained less.

"Bench?" he asked.

"Maybe."

"Here would be ideal."

Rusk from bench said, "For what?"

Dask began.

"Retired short-run line quietener, passive brass plate, likely split leaf internal but older type, intended to suppress echo or false tone on short copper signaling runs while unfortunately reducing weak true tones as well."

Rusk kept working.

"So bad bell helper."

Dask looked pained.

"That is incomplete."

"Still understood."

I liked Rusk.

Greyhook had its own test bench for retired signal hardware.

Of course.

Wind towers used short copper runs from weighted strip frames to local click boxes.

Not magic maybe.

Not only magic.

Mostly mechanics and line resonance.

Rusk said bench belonged yard foreman, not him.

Foreman **Tala Venn** was currently up tower three swearing at west vane.

"If she comes down not mad, ask."

"If mad?"

"Ask tomorrow."

Good system.

Weather hold board changed at first evening bell.

Aven Ro crossed terrace shouting before sign even got chalk.

"North Reach still held! West strip disagrees! Tower one green, tower two green, tower three red twice in quarter bell! No one gets creative!"

A freight man shouted, "What if we're already creative?"

"Then be creative indoors!"

She kept walking.

Excellent management.

Vessa checked route board.

"Morning earliest."

No frustration in voice.

Just fact.

I had learned this crew traveled by fact more than desire.

Useful.

Rusk opened resin tin.

Smelled like pine and hot stone.

Filled tooth marks with narrow bone spatula.

Wrapped deepest section in thin dark fiber tape soaked with resin.

Not pretty.

Strong-looking.

"Will wrap change flex?" Dask asked.

Rusk: "Tiny."

"How tiny?"

"Tiny enough I'm not measuring for you."

Dask inhaled through nose.

Sori sat beside me.

"He's growing."

"Painfully."

"Like fungus."

Dask looked over.

"I can hear you."

"Good."

Crew normal.

That helped.

No one had asked me to demonstrate hound technique.

No one wanted locked Greg back out for entertainment.

Good people.

Maybe.

Tala Venn came down tower three near second bell.

Small woman.

Maybe fifty.

Hair braided under leather cap.

One eye watering from wind.

She was absolutely mad.

She kicked tower stair bottom.

"West vane is lying."

Rusk said, "Vane can't lie."

"Then whoever set lower weight is lying through vane."

She saw us.

"Survey?"

Vessa nodded.

"Held north."

"I know. Everyone knows because Aven has lungs."

Aven somewhere above shouted something unintelligible.

Tala pointed upward.

"See."

I waited.

Dask did not.

"We have retired short-run line quietener and would like bench-only comparison if yard permits."

Tala stared.

Then at package.

Then at me.

"Yours?"

"Yes."

"Live line?"

"No."

"Good, because I would hit you with it."

Reasonable.

She took maintenance card.

Read.

"Old West Locks pattern."

"Likely," Dask said.

"Not likely. Stamp says West Locks."

Dask leaned.

There was tiny district mark under grease stain.

He looked betrayed by own eyesight.

Tala enjoyed this.

"Bench three. Short copper. Mechanical tone source. No field light. No active relay. We can see if plate still does basic bad-bell-helper job."

Rusk smiled without looking up.

Dask whispered, "It is not called that."

I said, "It is now."

Bench three sat inside second shed.

Simple enough.

Two upright pegs.

Copper line between.

Small striker box on one end.

Listening plate on other.

A second branch with adjustable slack and old echo loop.

Tala explained.

Normal strike made one clean click at plate.

Echo loop could create weaker delayed second click.

Quietener inserted between two short leads.

No magic.

No live signaling.

No stakes beyond curiosity.

Exactly right.

I unwrapped brass plate.

First time as owner.

Heavy for size.

Three notches.

Black retirement slash faint.

Spring leaf inside slit visible when tilted.

Dask wanted to touch.

Did not ask.

I looked at him.

He sighed.

"May I?"

"After first run."

"Fine."

Growth under oppression.

Tala ran baseline.

CLACK.

Then weaker tick.

Clear enough.

She adjusted slack.

Again.

CLACK.

Tick.

"Echo deliberately exaggerated," she said. "Real line messier."

Greg understood.

We inserted quietener.

No drama.

No glow.

No hum.

Tala struck.

Clack.

Nothing obvious after.

Dask leaned toward plate.

"Again."

Tala: "You say please to my bench."

He blinked.

"Please."

Again.

Clack.

Tiny delayed sound maybe.

Much less.

There.

It worked.

At least on toy problem designed to show exact use.

Useful.

Known.

Then Tala reduced main strike strength.

"Now downside."

Baseline weak strike without quietener.

Click.

Echo softer.

With quietener:

Tick.

Barely.

Dask frowned.

I did too.

Tala nodded.

"There. It doesn't know truth. It knows shape and strength."

Good line.

Not magic wisdom.

Engineering.

That was why thing was dangerous.

It could not distinguish false from important.

Neither could lots of people.

I liked it more.

Which was bad.

"One more," I said.

Tala looked.

"Why?"

"Different echo delay. Same main strength."

She nodded.

Good question apparently.

Adjusted branch.

Longer delay.

Baseline gave clean main + later weak tick.

Quietener damped later tick less than short-delay echo.

Interesting.

Dask started speaking fast.

"Frequency or phase sensitivity maybe, or mechanical settling time, possibly leaf preload relation, if we vary line tension and delay independently we'd need grid, perhaps twelve runs minimum, preferably twenty-four."

Tala held up hand.

"No."

He stopped.

"Why?"

"Because my bench is for work. You got answer. Plate functions. Downside functions. Go away."

I wrapped quietener.

"Thank you."

"You bought a problem."

"Yes."

"Good. Most interesting tools are."

There.

Collector logic.

Not power.

Problem with shape.

Back outside, Rusk had crutch hanging under roof beam to cure.

"One hour."

Shoulder had stiffened while sitting.

I stood carefully.

Left crutch only plus wall hand.

Bad idea for travel.

Fine for three steps.

Sori offered arm.

I took it.

No speech.

Small recalibration.

Greyhook evening market was mostly workers selling worker things.

Wind goggles.

Leather tie loops.

Weighted cloak hems.

Hook gloves with reinforced palms.

Dried meat.

Tiny bottles of eye oil.

One table of bent signal vanes sold as wall decoration to people with questionable taste.

I wanted one.

Did not buy.

Fifteen copper.

Vale twenty-three.

Money not crisis.

Still money.

I bought nothing.

Look at me.

Growth.

Kel did buy a weighted scarf.

Sori mocked him until wind hit her face on upper terrace.

Then she asked where seller was.

Kel refused to tell for almost five minutes.

Justice.

At third bell, Greyhook performed wind check.

All three towers visible from yard.

Huge slatted arms angled into wind.

Weighted cloth strips hung from side booms.

Tower one green flag.

Tower two green.

Tower three red.

Then green.

Aven watched through brass tube.

"Need half bell agreement."

People groaned.

Not us.

We had bunks.

I had repaired crutch soon.

Weather could be weather.

Rusk returned right crutch after cure.

Resin dark around tooth grooves.

Thin reinforcement wrap maybe four inches long.

He handed it to me.

"Test here."

I did.

Plant.

Weight.

Forward.

Back.

Small twist.

No creak.

No give.

Grip same.

Flex maybe different by amount Dask would ruin evening trying to name.

"Feels good."

Rusk: "Feels sound?"

I corrected.

"Feels sound."

"Good. Don't hit dogs for two days."

"Planning not to."

"Plans attract dogs."

Maybe true.

Vessa appeared with route update.

"Window possibly dawn. Aven wants everyone ready first bell. If towers disagree, we wait. If they agree half bell, we cross immediately."

"What is cross?"

"Greyhook crown to North Reach shelf. About one and three-quarter miles exposed. Anchor chain every thirty paces. Two wind shelters. One stair descent. No wagons. Mule line separate lower shelf."

I looked shoulder.

She saw.

"Can you do it if window opens?"

Good question.

Not can you force it.

Can you do it.

"Probably. Need right shoulder reassess morning. Chain requirement?"

"No hand-on-chain if green window. Chain is emergency only."

Better.

"Then probably yes."

"If probably becomes no, say no."

"Yes."

There.

Normal Greg.

No performance.

Night bunks were in long stone room built into ridge.

Wind thumped roof anyway.

Dask wanted to write bench results into personal notes.

I let him copy maintenance card observations but kept ownership notes myself.

New artifact entry mentally:

Line quietener.

Works on short copper bench.

Reduces short-delay echo strongly.

Reduces weak true strike too.

Longer-delay echo damped less in one bench condition.

No claim beyond bench.

No live use.

No magic interaction known.

Useful.

Dangerous.

Mine.

That last word felt good.

Collection growing.

Not because I needed to fill slots.

Because objects had stories and constraints and ways they could change decisions.

Holdfast waited Carrow.

Retreat oval waited Merecross.

Rest stone.

Shade unit.

Step Keeper.

Now this.

Most of them I barely understood.

Excellent.

Sori lay on bunk above me.

"You smiling at brass?"

"No."

"You're smiling."

"Shoulder pain."

"Looks romantic."

"Go to sleep."

"Customer."

"Don't start."

She laughed.

Wind hit wall.

Stone room shuddered tiny amount.

Nobody cared.

I did.

New place.

New rules.

Tomorrow maybe we crossed exposed crown where wind itself decided whether road existed.

Tonight I had one repaired crutch, one sore shoulder, one dangerous little brass plate, fifteen copper, twenty-three copper of Vale, and no reason to become more than that.

Repairman.

Not because I fixed crutch.

Rusk did.

Not because I fixed quietener.

Didn't need fixing.

Because day had been about keeping useful things useful.

Body.

Equipment.

Judgment.

Even restraint.

Turns out maintenance was adventure too.

Annoying.

# CHAPTER 399

## THE CROSSER

Tower three went green before breakfast.

Nobody trusted it.

Reasonable.

Aven Ro stood under the route board with a cup in one hand and her coat snapping around her knees.

"One green. Two green. Three green. Clock starts now. Nobody celebrates."

Dask looked offended.

"I wasn't going to celebrate."

"Good. Keep doing that."

Greyhook morning had apparently begun angry.

I liked consistency.

My shoulder liked nothing.

Better than yesterday.

Not good.
