I tested it beside the bunkhouse while the tower crews watched their weighted strips.

Forward lift.

Fine until high.

Side lift.

Still complained.

Grip.

Sore, functional.

Right crutch.

Plant.

Weight.

Twist.

The dark resin marks and narrow reinforcement wrap held.

No creak.

No give.

Good.

Vessa watched from three paces away.

"Probably?"

"Still probably."

"Any worse?"

"No."

"Any numbness?"

"No."

"Then probably can go."

There.

Not heroic.

Medical science.

Probably.

Aven shouted from board.

"Quarter bell!"

The three strips stayed green.

Wind moved through Greyhook hard enough to make every hanging chain talk.

Not loud exactly.

Constant.

Iron against iron.

Cloth snapping.

Wood knocking.

People raising voices because the ridge stole half of every sentence.

Kel came out wearing the weighted scarf he'd bought yesterday.

Sori pointed.

"Still stupid."

A gust hit her sideways.

She caught the wall.

"Where was the seller?"

Kel smiled.

"Market closed."

"I hate you."

"No you don't."

"Currently flexible."

Dask had tied his pencils to his coat.

Individually.

Three little cords.

I stared.

He noticed.

"Wind."

"I know what wind is."

"Then why are you looking?"

"I'm learning what fear looks like in a pencil."

Maro laughed into his sleeve.

Vessa said, "Load check."

We did.

My bag sat close against body.

Quietener wrapped inside with maintenance card and two short copper leads.

Tavi's striped rock farther down.

Knife.

Water.

Food.

Nothing loose.

No stupid decorative vane.

Regret.

Aven shouted again.

"Half bell! Window open!"

Everything changed quickly.

Not panic.

Work.

A gate on the north stair opened.

Two freight crews moved first toward the lower mule shelf.

Our cases had already been transferred to that line before dawn.

We carried only personal load and one compact record case Sori refused to surrender.

Vessa checked each of us.

"Crown to shelter one. Shelter one to stair. Stair to shelter two. Shelter two to north gate. Nobody races the window. If red call comes, nearest shelter. If chain bell rings, clip if you have clip, hold if you don't. Greg?"

"No clip."

"Emergency chain with both hands if stopped."

I looked at crutches.

"That would be exciting."

"Avoid excitement."

"Plan already established."

Aven handed us six green cloth tags.

"Tie visible. North gate counts tags, not faces. If you lose one, tell them before they decide the wind ate you."

I tied mine to bag strap.

Sori tied Dask's because his knot was apparently insulting.

"It was secure," he said.

"It was a measurement knot."

"That's not a thing."

"It looked like one."

We climbed to crown.

Then Greyhook ended.

Not literally.

Emotionally.

The ridge ahead had no interest in people.

North Reach ran like a narrow back of red-black stone between two enormous drops.

West side fell into pale broken country full of dry gullies and low cloud shadow.

East side dropped sharper into a green-gray basin I couldn't see the bottom of.

The maintained path was maybe eight feet wide at first.

Sometimes twelve.

Sometimes five.

Anchor posts stood every thirty paces or so, iron sunk into stone, heavy chain sagging between them at knee to waist height depending ground.

Not a railing.

Too low and too far from path in places.

Emergency infrastructure.

Something to reach if walking stopped being walking.

Wind shelters looked tiny from crown.

Stone boxes with one side open away from prevailing gust.

One maybe half mile out.

Second beyond stair.

North gate invisible.

I had crossed worse.

First life.

Different body.

Different world.

That fact used to annoy me.

Now it was just accounting.

Vessa said, "Maro first. Kel second. Greg center. Sori. Dask. Me rear. Ten paces spacing until shelter one."

Nobody argued.

We crossed.

First twenty paces were easy.

Of course.

The wind wanted confidence before collecting tax.

Crutch tips clicked against stone.

Right.

Left.

Foot.

Not actually right-left-foot.

My body knew sequence better than language did.

Two points ahead.

One behind.

Weight centered low.

Do not let upper body chase gust.

Let shoulders move a little.

Keep pelvis boring.

Boring pelvis survived weather.

That was probably not a proverb.

Could be.

At forty paces first real gust hit from west.

Not push.

Impact.

My coat flattened against side.

Bag tugged.

Right crutch unloaded for half heartbeat.

I let it.

Didn't snatch for ground.

Left crutch and right foot held triangle.

Then right tip came down again.

There.

Current body.

Current competence.

Nobody said anything.

Good.

Maro raised fist ahead.

Stop.

We stopped.

Not because danger.

Because a freight line below was moving.

Far under west lip, three mules walked narrow shelf with linked cases between handlers.

Wind carried no hoof sound up.

Made them look fake.

Tiny animals moving without noise.

Dask behind me said something.

Wind stole all of it.

I turned.

"What?"

He shouted.

Still nothing.

Sori yelled, "HE SAID THE WEST VANE MOVED!"

I looked back toward Greyhook.

One tower arm shifted.

Green strips stayed green.

Fine.

Dask shouted again.

Sori shouted, "HE KNOWS!"

This system was excellent.

We moved.

Path narrowed.

Six feet.

Then five.

Chain closer on west side.

I hated chain being west side.

If gust pushed east, chain was behind direction of travel.

Probably intentional for prevailing wind.

Today wind mostly west.

Fine.

Mostly was a dangerous word.

Shelter one came after enough walking that shoulder had started warming rather than hurting.

Good sign.

We entered one at a time.

Inside, silence hit almost as hard as wind outside.

Not true silence.

Stone hummed.

But conversation returned.

Kel said, "I can't hear myself think out there."

Dask said, "I can."

Sori: "Tragic."

Vessa checked time strip nailed inside shelter.

Simple weighted ribbon protected by glass.

"Window still green. Five minutes water."

I drank.

Shoulder assessment.

Warm.

Not worse.

Palm okay.

Right crutch wrap still sound.

Maro leaned against back wall.

"Next section worse."

"Encouraging," I said.

"You want lie?"

"Depends quality."

"Next section has pastries."

"Terrible."

"I don't practice."

Outside, chain bell rang once.

Everyone looked.

Vessa was already at opening.

Second ring.

Not alarm pattern.

Aven had explained.

Two isolated rings meant gust front passing Greyhook crown.

Three fast meant stop and shelter.

Continuous meant clip or hold.

We waited.

No third.

Vessa watched dust farther west.

A brown sheet moved across lower gullies.

Not toward us exactly.

Parallel.

"Two minutes," she said.

Nobody complained.

There.

Weather wasn't plot.

Weather was schedule.

We left.

Next section had no pastries.

Maro had lied.

Path angled down along east face.

Wind changed because ridge changed it.

Instead of steady west pressure, gusts curled upward.

That was worse.

Crutch tips got light without warning.

Not sliding.

Unweighting.

I shortened stride.

Crutch.

Crutch.

Foot.

Pause when needed.

Kel ahead turned once.

I gave thumbs-up.

He kept going.

No babysitting.

Good.

At first stair, Vessa changed order.

"Greg behind Kel. Sori behind Greg. Maro takes bottom."

Stone stair cut twenty-three steps down across exposed face.

Chain at outer edge.

Inner wall shoulder-high first half, knee-high second.

Wonderful.

I looked.

Old Greg brain offered six ways to descend quickly.

Current shoulder rejected four.

Current body rejected another.

One remained.

Fine.

"Backward?" Kel asked.

"Sideways."

I turned toward wall.

Right foot down.

Left crutch lower step.

Right crutch same level as foot until weight transferred.

Then down.

Slow.

Stable.

Wind shoved coat against wall.

Useful for once.

Step nine.

Ten.

Eleven.

Shoulder complained.

Not dangerous.

Twelve.

Thirteen.

A gust hit upward.

Right crutch tip skipped half inch.

Stopped against stair lip.

My body froze exactly long enough.

No more.

Sori behind said nothing.

Good.

I reset.

Continued.

At bottom Maro waited.

"Fine?"

"Yes."

"Actually?"

I rolled shoulder once.

"Six breaths."

Vessa heard from above.

"Take ten."

"Abuse of authority."

"Nine now."

I shut up.

Ten breaths.

There.

Patient survived becoming crosser.

We moved.

Second shelter was smaller.

Also occupied.

Two Roads workers sat inside with a coil of bright orange rope between them and a broken wind-strip frame on floor.

One woman had nose packed with cloth from a bleed.

The other man was eating something that smelled aggressively of onion.

"Room," woman said.

We squeezed.

Eight people in stone box built for maybe six.

Dask immediately looked at broken frame.

Then looked at owner.

Progress.

"May I?"

Woman followed his eyes.

"No."

"Okay."

More progress.

She pointed at my repaired crutch.

"Dog?"

Regional gossip again.

"Yes."

"Three Posts?"

"Unfortunately."

Onion man said, "Heard you killed six."

"Killed none."

"Heard eight."

"Still none."

"That's less interesting."

"For hounds, better."

Woman snorted and immediately regretted it because nose.

"Fuck."

Sori handed her clean cloth.

"Thanks."

Vessa asked, "North section?"

Woman became work again.

"Crosswind stronger after marker forty. Not red. Tower call still green. One loose chain between fifty-two and fifty-three, flagged blue. Don't use it. North gate knows."

"Any debris?"

"Small grit. Nothing with ambition."

Good phrase.

I kept it.

A bell rang outside.

One.

Pause.

One.

Not pattern.

Dask counted anyway.

Of course.

Road woman said, "Greyhook crown settling. You're fine."

Vessa waited another minute.

Then we went.

North section widened briefly.

That was trap emotionally.

Because wider path meant stronger wind.

Nothing broke it.

Sound disappeared almost completely.

Not quiet.

Pressure.

Air roaring so evenly it became absence.

I could see Kel's coat snap and hear almost none of it.

Sori's mouth moved once.

No sound.

She stopped trying.

Good.

We communicated by hands.

Maro fist.

Stop.

Palm low.

Crouch slightly.

Point west.

Gust.

We waited.

Dust and tiny stone grains skittered across path.

One hit cheek.

Then another.

Nothing with ambition.

Accurate.

Maro dropped hand.

Move.

At marker forty-seven, wind shifted east for three breaths.

That was interesting.

Not magical necessarily.

Terrain.

Air.

Ridge.

Dask's head turned hard.

I saw question happen.

He didn't stop.

Also progress.

Marker fifty-two had blue cloth tied around chain.

Loose section sagged lower than others.

We stayed inner path.

No touch.

Marker fifty-four.

Fifty-five.

Then the wind stopped.

Completely.

Everyone stopped too.

Not because order.

Because wrong.

My coat fell against body.

Kel's weighted scarf hung dead.

Dust dropped.

For maybe two breaths North Reach became still.

Not sheltered.

Still.

I felt skin tighten.

Dask looked at Vessa.

Maro looked at sky.

Vessa raised fist.

Hold.

No bells.

No tower signal visible this far.

Three breaths.

Four.

Then wind returned from west with a slap.

We leaned.

Normal.

Vessa pointed forward.

Move.

Good.

No investigation in middle of exposed ridge.

That was how people died while being curious.

North gate appeared after marker sixty.

Stone wall across widened shelf.

Low building.

Two wind masts.

Three people waving us in.

We crossed final stretch.

My shoulder hurt now.

Not injured-new.

Used.

Palm sore.

Breathing harder.

Still controlled.

North gate worker counted green tags before faces.

"Six!"

Vessa answered, "Six!"

Gate shut behind us.

Not dramatic.

Wood and iron.

Then I was inside lee wall and suddenly heard everyone again.

Dask said, "At marker fifty-five the wind velocity approached zero for approximately four breaths."

Sori said, "Hello to you too."

"It did."

"We survived. Say hello first."

He looked at me.

"Hello."

"Wrong person."

I sat on first available bench.

"Acceptable effort."

Vessa checked me.

"Shoulder?"

"Used. Not worse in a new way."

"Hand?"

"Sore."

"Crutch?"

I planted right one twice.

Solid.

"Rusk wins."

Kel pulled off weighted scarf.

Sori held hand out.

He stared.

"What?"

"I want to try it."

"You called it stupid."

"I contain growth."

He gave it to her.

North gate worker came over.

Older man, broad face, missing half an eyebrow.

"Survey?"

Vessa showed papers.

He read.

"Merecross. Long way."

"Getting longer."

"That's roads."

He pointed north.

"Your cases come mule shelf in maybe an hour. North Reach Station is down slope, not up. Don't follow the tower road unless you want weather people yelling at you."

"Route beyond?"

"Station will brief."

Dask said, "Was the still-air interval at marker fifty-five expected?"

Worker's face changed a little.

Not fear.

Recognition.

"You got one?"

"Approximately four breaths."

"Huh."

"What is it?"

"Usually nothing."

I hated usually.

Pella had taught me that.

Worker scratched missing eyebrow side.

"Reach gets dead pockets. Wind folds wrong around stone. Most are one breath, two. Four's longer. Tower crews record them."

"Magical?" Dask asked.

"Weather."

"That doesn't answer."

"It answers enough for breakfast."

I liked him.

Vessa said, "Name?"

"Holl Renn."

Dask turned.

Holl looked at him.

"No relation that I know."

"I hadn't asked yet."

"You turned around."

Dask closed mouth.

Excellent.

We waited for cases.

North side of Reach was not what I'd expected.

I hadn't expected anything specific.

Still.

Greyhook's dry red ridge fell away into a high basin full of dark grass and white stone ribs.

Not marsh.

Not forest.

Open rolling country.

Wind moved across grass in huge visible sheets.

Far north, something silver cut horizontally across basin.

River maybe.

Road definitely.

And beyond that, low blue hills layered into distance.

More world.

There it was again.

That feeling.

Carrow existed behind me.

Merecross behind that.

Bell Ford.

Alder Basin.

West Locks.

Three Posts.

Lark Spur.

Greyhook.

Names accumulating.

Not replacing home.

Adding to map inside me.

I had wanted a door that didn't lead home before dinner.

Apparently the world had taken that personally.

The mule cases arrived forty-eight minutes later.

Dask knew because of course he did.

No damage.

One seal dusty.

Sori checked custody.

Kel checked straps.

Vessa got station directions.

We descended.

North Reach Station sat half mile below gate behind a long white stone windbreak.

Bigger than Three Posts.

Smaller than Lark Spur.

Two bunkhouses.

Stable.

Signal mast.

Survey office.

Public cookhouse.

And something I had not seen in days.

A proper bath sign.

I stopped.

Maro nearly walked into me.

"What?"

I pointed.

He followed finger.

"Bath?"

"Bath."

"You've seen bath."

"Not recently enough."

Sori came beside us.

"Assignment covers?"

Vessa, ahead, didn't turn.

"Basic wash only. Hot soak personal."

There.

Economy returned.

I looked at sign.

**BASIC WASH - ASSIGNMENT / ROAD CREW**

**HOT BASIN - 1C**

One copper.

I had fifteen.

Vale had twenty-three of my future.

My shoulder hurt.

My hand hurt.

I had crossed a wind ridge on crutches.

This was not existential.

This was value.

I said, "I'm buying the bath."

Sori looked at me.

"Immediately?"

"Before anyone invents work."

Vessa finally turned.

"Briefing first."

"Cruel."

"Then bath."

Acceptable.

Inside survey office, station lead laid map on table.

Her name was **Ira Fen**.

Thin woman with silver hair cut to chin and ink on three fingers.

She spoke like every sentence had already been edited once before leaving her mouth.

"You crossed during a clean window?"

Vessa said, "Mostly. Four-breath dead pocket near fifty-five."

Ira wrote that without reaction.

"Common enough. Long enough to log."

Dask visibly relaxed because someone had finally used acceptable wording.

Ira continued.

"Your north assignment changes here. Merecross requested observational continuity through North Reach, then discretion for extension if regional conditions justify. They do."

There.

Something moved.

Not wind.

Story.

Vessa leaned over map.

"What conditions?"

Ira tapped three places.

"False tones at West Locks. Three Posts arm drift. Greyhook dead pockets longer than seasonal average. North basin has a different problem."

Dask inhaled.

Ira looked at him.

"Don't connect them yet."

He exhaled.

Good woman.

She tapped silver line I'd seen from gate.

"This is the **Lume River**. North road crosses at **White Span**. For four mornings, the river has reflected sunrise from the wrong direction."

Silence.

Ira added, "Only for several minutes. Physical sun remains east. Reflection appears west-facing on calm water. No heat change. No known navigation effect. Local ferrymen hate it."

I looked at map.

New problem.

Different grammar.

Good.

"How far?" Vessa asked.

"White Span twelve miles by road. Halfway settlement at **Kettle Vale**. You can make Kettle Vale tomorrow if you leave first bell. White Span day after."

There.

Not home.

Farther.

Ira said, "You have two choices. Remain here one day for North Reach observations, or continue under extension and take the basin route."

Vessa looked at crew.

Not just me.

Good.

Kel shrugged first.

"Road's open."

Maro said, "I haven't seen Lume in years."

Sori looked at Vessa.

"Custody okay if station stamps extension."

Dask said nothing.

That was suspicious.

Then: "I would like to observe the river."

Of course.

Vessa looked at me.

Shoulder sore.

Hand sore.

Fifteen copper.

One bath waiting.

Home farther south than it had ever been in this life.

"Greg?"

I looked at map.

Kettle Vale.

White Span.

Lume River.

Wrong sunrise.

Then north beyond it.

"Bath first," I said.

Vessa's mouth twitched.

"Then?"

"Then north."

There.

Crosser.

Apparently one ridge wasn't enough.

# CHAPTER 400

## THE SOJOURNER

I paid one copper for hot water.

Best purchase of expedition.

Possibly best artifact too.

Nobody liked that comparison.

North Reach bathhouse had four stone basins separated by waist-high walls, one cold channel, one hot channel, and a woman at desk who looked capable of denying civilization to anyone who annoyed her.

I gave her copper.

She bit it.

Normal.

"Half bell."

"Can I buy another half?"

"After first half."

"Excellent system."

She stared.

I stopped talking.

Also excellent system.

Hot water reached shoulder and every muscle in body immediately filed complaint.

There.

That was the problem with being busy.

Body waited until safe before becoming dramatic.

Right shoulder loosened slowly.

Palm stopped feeling like somebody had packed it with gravel.

Forearm still sore.

Nothing healed.

Everything became less stupid.

I sank lower.

First life had baths too.

Better ones sometimes.

Worse ones often.

Hot spring under ruined keep.

Copper tub in mountain inn.

One terrible adventurer bath where three men argued about whose blood clogged drain.

This was better because nobody was dying.

Also I was nineteen.

Again.

Still weird.

Less weird than before.

I thought of Carrow.

Cala's kitchen.

Tavi's noise.

Lyssa.

Theatre.

Vale.

My chest did small thing.

Not pain.

Pull.

Home.

Then I thought of map upstairs.

Kettle Vale.

White Span.

Lume River.

Wrong sunrise.

Pull opposite direction.

Interesting.

Maybe belonging was not rope.

Maybe it was weight.

Home could pull without preventing movement.

I liked that better.

Bath woman knocked wood against stone partition.

"Five minutes."

Cruel.

I stood carefully.

Shoulder better.

Still there.

Good.

I did not buy second half.

One copper had done job.

There.

Restraint.

Cash fourteen.

Vale twenty-three.

Still not dying.

I dressed and returned to Survey office smelling less like hound, marsh, wind, and bad decisions.

Sori noticed first.

"You look human."

"Expensive."

"One copper."

"Exactly."

Dask looked at shoulder.

"Range improved?"

"Do not turn bath into test protocol."

"I wasn't."

"You were halfway there."

He considered.

"Maybe."

Growth.

Ira Fen had extension papers ready.

Three sheets.

One station stamp.

One regional seal.

One line I actually cared about.

**OBSERVATIONAL CONTINUITY AUTHORIZED THROUGH WHITE SPAN / LUME RIVER.**

Vessa read everything.

Sori read everything again because that was Sori.

Dask tried to read over shoulder.

Sori moved page three inches away.

He moved with it.

She moved again.

He stopped.

"I'm not touching."

"You're breathing on custody."

"Air is not custody interference."

"Keep arguing and it becomes one."

Ira stamped page.

"Food through Kettle Vale covered. Public lodging covered if station bunks unavailable. White Span local boats are not automatically covered unless survey use is authorized there. Personal purchases remain personal."

Greg economy loved sentences like that.

Vessa asked, "Route conditions?"

Ira tapped map.

"North basin road dry. Wind moderate. Two long grades. One white-stone causeway across lower grass floodplain. No weather hold. Kettle Vale has well, station desk, inn, repair stable, market on alternating days. Today is not market day."

Damn.

Ira continued.

"White Span road beyond Vale is open. River crews want outside witnesses at dawn if reflection repeats. That means you need Kettle Vale tonight. Leave within half bell."

There.

Bath then north.

Promise kept.

We packed.

Nothing dramatic.

That was becoming its own pleasure.

Kel checked straps.

Sori sealed papers.

Dask tied pencils again even though wind inside basin looked less violent.

Maro stood outside looking north like person who had done this before and forgotten enough to enjoy it again.

Vessa counted people.

Six.

Always six.

I checked bag.

Quietener.

Tavi rock.

Knife.

Food.

Water.

Paper.

Fourteen copper.

No oval.

Artifacts had geography now.

Some home.

Some Merecross.

Some with me.

Collection becoming map too.

We left North Reach Station before second bell.

High basin swallowed us differently than ridge.

Wind still existed, but no longer had teeth.

Dark grass bent in huge sheets from west to east.

White stone ribs rose from ground every few hundred paces like backs of buried animals.

Road was pale hardpack with black drainage cuts.

Long sightlines.

Nothing could hide unless it tried very hard.

I liked it immediately.

Then disliked it after forty minutes because long sightlines made distance rude.

You could see hill forever before reaching it.

Maro said, "That one is first grade."

I looked at hill.

"That one?"

"Yes."

"We've been walking toward it since birth."

"Maybe longer."

Dask said, "Approximately forty-three minutes."

Sori groaned.

"Nobody asked."

"Greg implied duration."

"Greg implied suffering."

"Those can overlap."

Hard to argue.

First grade climbed steadily.

Not steep.

Worse.

Steady meant no excuse to stop.

Shoulder warmed, then complained.

Palm okay.

Right crutch repair held.

At top Vessa gave five breaths without me asking.

I took eight.

Nobody counted except Dask.

He did not say number.

Actual growth.

From ridge crest we saw Lume River clearly for first time.

Silver line far north.

Too far for water detail.

Wide enough to matter.

White Span itself invisible.

Kettle Vale somewhere lower, hidden behind rolling grass and stone.

I stood there longer than needed.

Not because magic.

Scale.

Carrow had become small in imagination behind me.

Not less important.

Just geographically small.

Different.

In first life I had crossed kingdoms.

This should not impress me.

It did.

Second life made distance personal again.

Maybe because there were people waiting behind it now.

First life at nineteen had mostly wanted away.

Current nineteen had something to return to.

That made north feel farther.

And better.

Maro said, "You okay?"

"Yes."

"You're staring."

"River."

"Still there."

"Good."

We descended.

At lower grass floodplain road became causeway.

White stone blocks, three feet above surrounding dark grass.

Grass looked solid until wind moved it and revealed water shining beneath.

Floodplain.

Not marsh exactly.

Seasonal sheet water hidden under grass roots.

Causeway had gaps every fifty paces with narrow bridges over flow cuts.

No rails.

Because apparently this region hated decorative safety.

Kel said, "Watch right edge. Some stones undercut."

Local sign agreed.

**KEEP CENTER / WHITE MARKS SAFE / BLUE MARKS UNDER REVIEW**

Blue paint appeared every so often.

Dask loved system.

"Clear visual coding."

Sori said, "Please marry road."

"Road has no legal personhood."

"Coward."

Halfway across, grass to east moved wrong.

Not wind.

Something long traveled beneath.

We all saw.

Maro stopped.

Shape maybe ten feet.

Could be water channel.

Then head surfaced.

Pale.

Flat.

Two dark side fins.

Mud sailer maybe.

There.

The bridge animal again? Not same animal. Too far.

Species perhaps.

Creature surfaced once, breathed with ugly wet cough, then vanished.

Dask whispered, "That was likely a mud sailer."

Maro: "Likely."

"Could we stop?"

Vessa: "No."

"For thirty seconds?"

"No."

"Ten?"

"Walk."

He walked.

Greg understood pain.

I looked anyway while moving.

Nothing returned.

Good.

No monster fight.

Just animal.

Road after causeway climbed second grade.

Shorter.

Steeper.

My shoulder disliked more.

I stopped once.

Ten breaths.

Asked before world asked for me.

There.

Smart.

At top Kel offered to take bag.

I almost said no.

Then checked shoulder.

"Twenty minutes."

He took it.

No ceremony.

Sori saw.

Said nothing.

Good.

Locked Greg had turned people into variables.

Normal Greg could let variable carry bag.

Maybe that was healthy.

Kel wore bag badly.

Too high.

I endured for three minutes.

"Lower strap."

He adjusted.

"More."

Adjusted.

"There."

"You're difficult."

"I'm injured, not dead."

"Debatable."

Normal crew.

Road crossed little stone marker field near afternoon.

Dozens of waist-high white pillars on both sides.

Each had shallow bowl carved top.

Most empty.

Some held water despite no rain.

Dask slowed.

Maro said, "Old wind wells."

"What do they do?" I asked.

"Collect condensation when nights cool. Travelers used to drink."

"Safe?"

"Not now. Birds shit in them."

Magic defeated by bird.

Excellent.

One bowl had tiny silver shimmer around edge.

I looked.

Kept walking.

Not everything needed investigation.

This was becoming real skill.

Late afternoon road finally dropped.

Kettle Vale appeared all at once.

Not valley exactly.

Bowl between white stone ridges.

Maybe forty buildings.

More than Lark Spur.

Less than Carrow.

Roofs dark slate.

Several round steam vents.

One tall black chimney.

Fields in terraces.

Roads converging from east and north.

And kettles.

Actually kettles.

Huge iron ones outside buildings, some big enough to bathe horse.

I stopped.

"Why?"

Maro smiled.

"You'll like this."

"That worries me."

We entered under stone arch painted:

**KETTLE VALE / HOT SPRINGS, BREWERS, ROAD REST**

There.

Of course.

Natural hot water.

Brewer town.

Road stop.

Name literal enough.

Smell hit next.

Mineral water.

Malt.

Smoke.

Bread.

I forgave region immediately.

Station desk occupied front room of inn called **The Copper Kettle**.

Too obvious.

Perfect.

Desk clerk was broad man named **Orren Pike**, beard braided in two little ropes, voice too formal for room.

"Good afternoon. Survey travelers under regional extension?"

Vessa showed papers.

"Yes. Six."

"We have been informed of your likely arrival. Accommodations are available in rear annex. Evening meal covered under road assignment. Bathing is not included because our communal spring is separately operated."

I looked at Vessa.

She said, "You already bought one."

Cruel again.

Orren continued like tax record.

"White Span departure recommended before first bell. Dawn reflection, if it repeats, begins shortly after first direct light upon western bank. Ferrymaster requests observers positioned before sunrise. Road distance approximately six miles."

Dask asked, "Duration of prior reflections?"

Orren consulted note.

"Morning one: about six minutes. Morning two: eight or nine. Morning three: seven. Morning four: five. Times are local estimates except morning four, which was clocked at five minutes and twenty breaths by bridge assistant."

Good.

Better evidence already.

"Any effect on people?" Vessa asked.

"None reported."

"Boats?"

"None reported."

"Fish?"

Orren paused.

"One ferryman claims fish rise toward false light. Second ferryman says first ferryman lies when sober. I cannot classify."

I liked Orren too.

Sori said, "Market?"

Orren looked at her.

"Tomorrow afternoon."

I suffered quietly.

Vessa said, "Tomorrow is not today."

"I know."

"Good."

We got rear annex.

Six bunks.

Again.

No argument this time.

Luxury.

Kel returned my bag after exactly twenty-three minutes.

I did not ask how he knew.

Dask did.

Of course.

Shoulder felt better after unload.

Not healed.

Maybe fifty percent angry instead of seventy.

Useful scale invented entirely from mood.

Evening meal was thick barley stew, dark bread, pickled onions.

Good.

Dask ate onions individually because apparently food had measurement protocol too.

Sori watched him for one minute.

"Why?"

"I like them separately."

There.

Not technical.

Human.

She accepted.

After food Vessa assigned dawn plan.

Leave Kettle Vale before first bell.

Reach White Span before sunrise.

Maro river/terrain.

Dask instruments.

Sori records/custody.

Kel transport/logistics.

Greg witness log with Ira's observation framing.

No special Greg role.

Good.

I asked, "Can I see bridge before dawn without standing on bridge?"

Maro said, "West bank has rise above span. Best view."

"Good."

Dask asked, "Could quietener have any relevance?"

There.

Vessa looked at me.

I looked at wrapped object.

"No evidence. Different system. Leave packed unless local owner asks about it for separate reason."

Dask nodded.

No fight.

Growth.

We had one hour before sleep.

I walked outside.

Kettle Vale at night steamed.

Literally.

Warm vapor rose from drainage channels along road.

People sat on stone benches with cups.

Brewers rolled barrels.

Someone sang badly in building nearby.

Children chased each other between kettles until woman yelled at them not to die in boiling water.

Lived in.

Far from home.

And ordinary.

There.

That was the thing.

Adventure was not constant danger.

It was breakfast somewhere you didn't know yesterday.

A bath purchased because shoulder hurt.

A road with strange grass.

A town that smelled like malt.

A river tomorrow reflecting wrong sun.

And people back home continuing without me.

I wondered what Tavi had done today.

Probably something loud.

Cala probably worked.

Lyssa probably had own day entirely independent of my dramatic wind ridge.

Good.

I wanted that.

Home not frozen.

Me not frozen either.

I sat on low wall beside steaming drain.

Maro found me after few minutes.

He held two cups.

"Tea."

"Free?"

"Station kitchen."

"Best price."

He sat.

We watched steam.

After while he said, "You look happier."

"Bath."

"I meant trip."

I thought.

"Yes."

"Good."

"That's all?"

"You want speech?"

"No. Last one was upsetting."

"Good."

We drank.

No revelation.

Didn't need one.

I was nineteen.

Again.

Four hundred chapters worth of life would have sounded absurd if chapters existed.

They did not.

Days did.

Road did.

Tomorrow did.

White Span waited six miles north.

Home waited much farther south.

For first time since leaving Carrow, neither direction felt like mistake.

Sojourner.

Temporary resident of everywhere between.

# CHAPTER 401

## THE WITNESS

We left Kettle Vale in the dark.

Good dark.

Cold dark.

The kind that made every warm doorway behind us feel criminal.

Orren Pike stood under Copper Kettle's awning with a lantern and the expression of a man personally offended by predawn logistics.

"Six miles. West-bank rise. Do not take lower ford road. It floods without dignity."

Kel adjusted mule lead.

"Road marked?"

"Yes. Poorly, but yes."

"Comforting."

"I do not promise comfort. I promise direction."

Formal bastard.

I liked him.

Steam from Kettle Vale's spring drains drifted low across road behind us.

For first half mile town existed mostly as smell.

Malt.

Wet stone.

Smoke.

Then even that faded.

High basin took over.

Dark grass silvered by moon.

White stone ribs holding cold.

Road pale enough to follow without lantern once eyes adjusted.

Wind weaker than yesterday.

Good.

My shoulder had opinions anyway.

Better opinions.

Not silence.

I had slept.

Hot basin had helped.

Not healed.

Right hand still tender when I put too much palm into crutch grip.

So don't.

Simple.

Current body liked simple when I listened before it shouted.

Vessa walked near front with Maro.

Kel had mule.

Sori carried compact custody case.

Dask carried instruments like they were infant princes.

I carried witness board and my own bag.

Quietener stayed wrapped.

Good quietener.

Stay irrelevant.

We passed first white milestone after maybe twenty minutes.

No one commented.

Crew habit.

Earlier somebody would have asked what district cut stone, why mark shape changed, whether white mineral was local.

Now we knew when road furniture was just road furniture.

Growth.

Dask eventually ruined it.

"The marker face is rotated three degrees east from road normal."

Sori said, "There he is."

"What?"

"I was worried you died."

"I have been walking beside you."

"Physically."

Maro made little sound that might have been laugh.

Pre-dawn cold sharpened everything.

Crutch clicks.

Mule tack.

Breathing.

Grass moving in long shallow waves.

Then water sound arrived.

Before river.

Low.

Broad.

Not rushing.

Moving weight.

I noticed first because first life had trained ears around rivers.

Maro noticed maybe same time.

He turned head north.

"Lume."

Dask checked sky.

"Still forty minutes, maybe."

Sori said, "Maybe?"

He sighed.

"Thirty-eight to forty-two by current light."

"Much better."

Road descended gradually.

White ribs got lower.

Grass gave way to short gray scrub and bare gravel patches.

We crossed one shallow steam-cut where warm water from some buried spring ran toward river.

Not enough to fog.

Just enough that stone near it didn't frost.

I stepped over carefully.

No problem.

Shoulder warmed.

Hand okay.

Ahead, first bridge lantern appeared.

Then second.

Then line of pale structure across dark water.

White Span.

Name lacked imagination.

Bridge deserved better.

Long stone span over broad river with seven arches I could count before dawn fully separated them.

Pale limestone or something close.

Low parapets.

One raised center section with little signal hut.

West-bank rise stood maybe hundred yards upstream, grassy hump reinforced with stone steps and observation posts.

Two ferry docks below bridge despite bridge existing.

Of course.

Infrastructure accumulated like grudges.

A man on west dock waved lantern.

"Survey?"

Vessa raised hand.

"Merecross extension."

"Up rise. Fenner's there. Don't stand bridge center until he says."

"Why?"

"Because he's been awake five mornings and needs authority to survive."

Good enough.

We climbed rise.

My shoulder complained by top.

I stopped six breaths without being told.

No one said anything.

Better.

Observation rise had three fixed posts facing river, one small weather shelter, bench, instrument table, and a brass sight frame bolted into stone.

Local lead **Fenner Vale** stood beside it.

No relation to Vale debt, probably.

I refused universe joke.

Fenner was maybe thirty-five, hair tied back badly, beard not tied at all, coat buttoned wrong by one hole.

Five mornings awake indeed.

He shook Vessa's hand.

Then looked at us.

"Who keeps clean witness?"

Vessa pointed at me.

"Greg."

Fenner looked at crutches.

Looked at my face.

"You're dog man."

There.

Regional reputation ruining everything.

"Apparently."

"Killed twelve?"

"Zero."

"That's disappointing."

"Mostly to dogs."

He smiled once.

"Good. You're left post."

No ceremony.

Excellent.

Assignments happened fast.

Greg left witness post.

Dask sight frame + timing slate.

Maro water behavior + bank/weather.

Sori master record.

Kel bridge/ferry movement and any practical traffic effect.

Vessa oversight, local coordination.

Fenner local sequence.

Bridge assistant **Lessa Quill** came up from span carrying two mugs and a folded towel around neck.

Young woman.

Freckles.

Hair shoved under cap badly.

She handed one mug to Fenner.

"You forgot this again."

"I remembered eventually."

"After I remembered for you."

"Still counts."

She looked at us.

"Outside witnesses? Thank every saint and every accountant."

Dask said, "Why accountants?"

"Because saints don't sign overtime."

Good.

Messy normal people.

Fenner briefed while sky lightened.

"Reflection appears only if water's calm enough to hold sky clean. Choppy morning yesterday would have ruined it, except wind dropped right before sunrise. First edge shows downstream of arch four. Then spreads west, not east. Looks like sun coming from over low hills there."

He pointed west.

No sun west.

Obviously.

"Does bridge shadow affect?" Maro asked.

"Some. Reflection crosses shadow. That's part I hate."

"Surface only?"

"As far as we know. Pole into water looks normal. Bucket looks normal if you carry it away."

That mattered.

Dask said, "Bucket tests documented?"

Lessa said, "Yes, because after morning two Fenner nearly fell in trying to look underneath light."

Fenner: "I did not nearly fall."

"Your boot went in."

"One boot."

"That's how falling starts."

I liked her too.

Sori asked, "Any physical temperature?"

"None measured beyond normal sunrise change," Fenner said. "No burns. No frost. No smell. No sound."

Kel: "Traffic?"

"We hold ferries during appearance because people are idiots. Bridge stays open one lane unless visibility changes. Hasn't."

"Why hold ferries if no effect?"

Fenner looked toward dock.

"Because first morning ferryman stopped rowing to stare and drifted under bridge."

There.

Human consequence.

Not magical consequence.

Excellent.

Sky east went gray-blue.

Then pale.

Dask stopped talking.

Everyone did.

We took positions.

My witness board sat across lap.

Left post gave clear view upstream and bridge face.

Real sunrise would break east behind low ridge.

Wrong reflection supposedly west-facing.

I watched water.

Broad dark band.

Current slow enough surface looked like stretched metal.

Small ripples near bridge piers.

Nothing.

Bird called somewhere downstream.

Mule sneezed.

Lessa whispered, "Every time."

Fenner whispered back, "What?"

"Mule."

"Different mule."

"Still every time."

Not useful.

Good.

First physical sun touched east ridge.

There.

Thin gold line.

I logged time mark from Dask's board call.

Real light hit tops of white bridge arches.

River east edge brightened normally.

Then west water lit.

Not gradual.

Not flash either.

A shape appeared.

Long pale-gold path on water downstream of arch four.

Wrong angle.

I stared.

There are things knowledge makes less impressive.

This wasn't one.

Because it looked normal.

That was the problem.

Perfect ordinary sunrise reflection.

Placed where sunrise could not be.

Gold path ran from western bank toward mid-river as if invisible sun sat low over west hills.

Real sun east still visible.

Two suns in reflection.

One actual.

One lying.

I wrote.

**Wrong-direction reflection first visible downstream arch four. West-facing apparent source. Real sun east visible simultaneously.**

No poetry.

Later.

Dask called, "Start."

Sori marked.

Maro said, "Wind near zero. Surface calm."

Kel: "Ferries held. Bridge open one lane."

Fenner didn't speak.

He'd seen it four times.

Still watched like first.

Reflection spread.

Not across whole river.

Maybe forty-yard band at widest.

Edges soft.

Followed surface texture.

When ripple crossed, false light rippled normally.

That mattered.

It behaved like reflection once present.

Source impossible.

Behavior ordinary.

Good nightmare.

A fish broke surface inside false band.

Dask inhaled.

I heard it.

I did not write fish seeking false sun.

Fish surfaced.

That's all.

Another fish broke outside band thirty seconds later.

Better.

Fuck ferryman theory.

Maro pointed.

One bridge pier shadow crossed gold path.

False reflection continued through shadowed water.

Not visually through solid shadow exactly.

It dimmed less than real sunlight should.

I wrote carefully.

**False band remains visible within bridge-cast surface shadow; apparent brightness reduced slightly, not extinguished.**

Dask shouted, "Measure!"

Fenner said, "With what?"

Dask looked wounded.

No calibrated light instrument.

So no number.

Good.

Lessa took pole from post.

"Bucket?"

Fenner nodded.

She descended west-bank steps, tied rope, lowered white enamel bucket into false band.

Everyone watched.

Bucket hit water.

Filled.

Surface around rope glowed gold.

She hauled.

Water inside bucket looked like water.

No false west sun.

Real east sky reflected weakly in bucket depending angle.

There.

Specific.

Phenomenon attached to river surface/context, not portable water in simple bucket test.

I wrote.

Sori repeated aloud for clean record.

Kel crossed first bridge lane with local worker while phenomenon active.

Not because thrill.

Traffic observation.

He walked span west to east and back.

Returned.

"No footing change. No visibility loss from deck. Looking down is distracting."

Lessa said, "That's official wording for stupid pretty."

Kel: "Yes."

Reflection strengthened as real sun climbed.

Then something else happened.

Small wake moved upstream through false band.

No boat.

No animal visible.

Maybe current shear.

Maybe submerged thing.

Gold path bent around wake normally.

Again.

Phenomenon obeyed water once there.

I wrote wake, not cause.

Dask said, "Three minutes forty."

Sori: "Observer timing?"

"Board timing."

"Good."

Look at us.

Institutional romance.

At four minutes, west-facing gold began narrowing.

Not fading evenly.

Outer edges contracted toward arch four/downstream center.

Maro said, "Wind increasing east side."

A tiny breeze touched actual grass.

False band edge broke where ripples increased.

Maybe calm requirement.

Could be.

Not claim yet.

Then Dask said, "Five minutes."

Still visible.

Fenner muttered, "Longer than yesterday."

Lessa: "Don't jinx."

"Not magic."

"Still don't."

At five minutes fifty-ish by Dask board, reflection thinned to bright streak.

At six minutes twelve seconds equivalent by local board marks, it vanished.

Not flash.

Just became ordinary water.

Real sunrise remained east.

Everyone kept watching another full minute.

Good discipline.

Dask called end.

Sori marked.

Fenner sat down hard on bench.

"Six twelve."

Dask said, "Six eleven and approximately half."

Lessa threw towel at him.

He caught it badly.

"We don't have half-second instrument," she said.

Dask looked at towel.

Then at her.

"Correct."

Growth through assault.

We did first-pass debrief before anyone invented theory.

Observations:

Fifth morning recurrence.

False west-facing sunrise reflection appears despite real sun east.

First visible downstream arch four.

Spreads within calm band.

Behaves like normal reflection under ripples/wake.

Persists somewhat under bridge-cast shadow.

Bucketed water does not retain wrong reflection.

No heat/smell/sound change observed.

Bridge footing/visibility unchanged.

Ferries held administratively, not by physical effect.

Fish surfaced both inside and outside band.

Duration about six minutes twelve by board timing.

Wind picked up as phenomenon contracted, possible correlation only.

No connection to West Locks tones.

No connection to arm drift.

No connection to Greyhook dead pockets.

No connection to my quietener.

Good.

Dask looked physically pained by final four.

I enjoyed that.

Fenner asked, "You brought a quietener?"

There.

I frowned.

"How do you know?"

"Road gossip. Dog man bought bad bell helper at Lark Spur."

Regional economy of privacy excellent.

"It's packed."

"Keep it packed. River doesn't have copper line."

"Agreed."

Dask said, "There could be analogous filtering behavior."

Fenner stared.

"There could be a goose inside moon."

"That's not analogous."

"Exactly."

I loved river people.

We moved down to bridge after phenomenon ended.

White Span in daylight looked less haunted.

Still beautiful.

Seven arches.

Center signal hut.

Two ferry docks.

River broad enough that opposite bank had its own weather personality.

Local ferryman **Berrit Cole** was furious nobody cared about fish.

"They rise west. I'm telling you. Every morning. They turn their damn heads."

Maro asked, "Fish heads?"

"Yes."

"You can see head direction under reflected light?"

"I've fished forty years."

Lessa yelled from hut, "Thirty-two!"

Berrit yelled back, "Your mother counts wrong!"

"She's dead!"

"Still wrong!"

Excellent local evidence.

Second ferryman, old woman named **Toma Grell**, said fish rose because dawn bugs rose.

"Berrit sees religion in carp."

Berrit: "You use wrong bait."

Toma: "I catch more than you."

"Because river pities widows."

"I'm not widowed."

"Then it pities your husband."

This was useless scientifically.

Very useful socially.

Sori wrote no fish conclusion.

Good.

Vessa met Fenner and Lessa inside signal hut.

I followed because witness log.

White Span had no requirement we stop after one morning.

Regional request asked **two outside-witness mornings if recurrence continues**, unless crew/resources redirect.

There.

Extension extended.

Vessa asked lodging.

Fenner said bridge quarters had four bunks and floor space, but village **Westbank** half mile upstream had beds, food, actual walls, and people less likely to step on instruments.

Dask said, "Bridge quarters would reduce travel time."

Sori said, "Village has beds."

Dask: "Half mile."

Sori: "Beds."

Vessa looked at me.

"Shoulder?"

"Fine enough. Better after moving. Worse if I pretend it's gone."

"Hand?"

"Usable."

"Then Westbank."

Good.

We packed observation gear.

No rush.

Sun fully up.

River normal.

Wrong reflection gone like it had never happened.

That bothered me more than if it left scorch mark.

World should admit when it does weird shit.

Instead river looked innocent.

We walked upstream road.

Westbank appeared behind low willow line.

Actual willows.

Not glass.

Not magical maybe.

I didn't ask.

Village small.

Twenty buildings.

Boat repair sheds.

Smokehouses.

One long inn with blue roof.

Children skipping flat stones into side channel.

A dog sleeping under fish rack.

I looked at dog.

Dog looked at me.

We reached diplomatic agreement.

No one moved.

Good.

Inn sign read **THE TWO SUNS**.

Of course.

Fresh paint.

Opportunists.

I respected it.

Innkeeper **Mella Torr** said name used to be The Blue Heron until three days ago.

"People ask for wrong-sun rooms. I don't know what that means, but they pay for breakfast."

Sori asked if Survey lodging covered.

"Six beds if you don't mind two rooms."

"Covered rate?"

"Yes."

"Good."

No fight.

No floor.

Miracle.

Lunch came with smoked river fish, potatoes, sour greens.

Berrit would claim fish spiritually west-facing.

Tasted east enough.

Dask spread notes.

Vessa stopped him.

"Eat first."

"I can do both."

"You've proven that. Poorly."

He ate.

I sat by window facing river.

Shoulder ached low.

Not bad.

Four hundred chapters ago, apparently, I had no map for any of this.

No.

Wrong thought.

Not chapters.

Life.

Not long ago in this life, Carrow had been enough world.

Then roads happened.

People happened.

Work happened.

A job became route.

Route became places.

Places became names I could miss later.

Kettle Vale already behind us.

Greyhook farther.

Merecross much farther.

Carrow farther than all of them emotionally because home measured differently.

And tomorrow before dawn I would stand beside impossible sunrise again and try not to make it mean more than it did.

Witness.

That was harder than hero sometimes.

Hero got to decide what things meant.

Witness had to keep looking after wanting answer.

# CHAPTER 402

## THE SHOPPER

Westbank had a market.

This was dangerous.

Not because of thieves.

Probably.

Because Mella Torr mentioned it while I was eating my second potato.

"Boat yard row sets tables after noon," she said. "Mostly hooks, cord, smoked things, old river junk. Since the second sun started, everyone's selling anything shiny for twice what it's worth."

I stopped chewing.

Sori saw.

"Finish lunch before you shop."

"I haven't said anything."

"You stopped chewing."

"Temporary condition."

Mella pointed spoon at me.

"You the artifact boy?"

"No."

"Dog boy?"

"Also no."

"Crutch boy?"

I looked down.

"Unfortunately evidence supports that one."

Mella nodded.

"There's a woman two doors past cooper who sells retired ferry hardware. Don't buy anything she calls lucky."

"Why?"

"Because she calls everything lucky."

Sori closed eyes.

"Mella."

"What?"

"You just gave him directions."

"He looked sad."

I had not.

Probably.

Vessa finished bread.

"We have the afternoon. Second observation before sunrise. Greg can look at junk if he remains available."

There.

Authorization.

Sori pointed at Vessa.

"You're worse."

"I'm not his mother."

"Neither am I."

"Then stop parenting his purse."

I liked Vessa more every day.

Maro drank tea.

"Fourteen copper."

I looked at him.

"Why do you know?"

"You talk."

"Apparently too much."

Dask had already unfolded yesterday's observation sheet beside his plate.

Vessa tapped it once.

"Eat first."

He folded it again.

Progress remained terrifying.

We spent the first part of afternoon doing actual work.

Unfortunate.

Fenner sent copies of local logs upstream with a boy who ran like roads were optional.

Sori compared five mornings of timing.

Dask drew the bridge and false-band boundaries until his paper looked diseased.

Maro walked Westbank shore with Toma Grell to ask about current, wind, insects, fish, ferry behavior, and every other thing fishermen could lie about with confidence.

Kel checked our cases and arranged pre-dawn mule hold.

Vessa spoke with Westbank road clerk about what happened after second outside-witness morning.

I got the clean-copy witness ledger.

My job was to turn six people's observations into something a stranger could read without accidentally inheriting our guesses.

Harder than fighting dogs.

Less biting.

Mostly.

I wrote:

**OBSERVED: FALSE WEST-FACING SUNRISE REFLECTION PRESENT WHILE PHYSICAL SUN VISIBLE EAST.**

Then:

**OBSERVED: PHENOMENON APPEARS TO REQUIRE CONTINUOUS RIVER-SURFACE CONTEXT UNDER CURRENT SIMPLE TESTS. COLLECTED BUCKET WATER DID NOT RETAIN FALSE REFLECTION.**

I stared at second sentence.

Too strong.

Crossed out **require**.

Changed to:

**OBSERVED: PHENOMENON WAS NOT RETAINED IN COLLECTED BUCKET WATER UNDER CURRENT SIMPLE TEST.**

Better.

Dask leaned over shoulder.

"Continuous surface context is still likely."

"Likely isn't observed."

"We could write probable."

"Could."

"Will we?"

"No."

He considered.

"Okay."

There.

I had broken him.

Or improved him.

Same silhouette from distance.

By midday my shoulder had gone from ache to low complaint.

Good.

Hand still tender where hound bite-force had traveled through wood into palm.

Also good.

Pain shrinking into memory.

Not gone.

I tested crutch wrap once outside.

Solid.

Then Mella yelled from inn door.

"Tables are up!"

Sori looked at ceiling.

"Why are you helping him?"

"Commerce."

Excellent woman.

Westbank market was not a market by Carrow standards.

It was twelve tables, three blankets, two carts, and one man selling eels from a barrel while loudly denying they were yesterday's eels.

"Fresh!"

Toma Grell, passing behind him, said, "They know your name."

The eel man ignored her.

Boat-yard row smelled like tar, wet rope, smoked fish, sawdust, and river mud.

Useful things everywhere.

Hooks.

Nails.

Copper rivets.

Waxed cord.

Net needles.

Pulley cheeks.

Float corks.

Old ferry tokens.

Bent brass plates.

A box labeled **SUN GLASS** containing ordinary green bottle shards.

I picked one up.

Seller said, "Caught wrong dawn in it yesterday."

"Did it stay?"

"No."

"Then what am I buying?"

"Memory."

"I have one."

He lowered price from one copper to three bits without me asking.

I put shard down.

No universal bit conversion.

Also bottle.

Two victories.

Kel found me near cooper.

"Vessa says stay in village."

"I am."

"She means buildings, not the road."

I looked at him.

"Oppressive leadership."

"You want coffee?"

"Yes."

He handed me cup.

Free.

Best price.

The retired ferry-hardware woman sat exactly where Mella said.

Two doors past cooper.

Table under blue cloth.

Woman maybe sixty, maybe forty after river weather. Hair white at temples, arms brown and ropey, left little finger missing first joint.

Sign:

**RIVA SORN**

**FERRY HARDWARE / OLD SIGNAL / RIVER SALVAGE**

Under it, smaller:

**NOT RESPONSIBLE FOR PREVIOUS OWNERS**

Promising.

Riva watched me approach.

"Dog boy."

"No."

"Twelve dogs."

"Zero."

"Less impressive."

"Safer for dogs."

She looked at crutches.

"Those kill twelve?"

"They participated in zero deaths."

"Shame."

Kel drank coffee beside me and refused to help.

Riva's table was better than shiny-bottle man.

Not because things looked magical.

Because they looked used.

A brass ferry bell clapper with one side flattened.

Three ceramic current tags.

A spool of black waxed cord.

Two palm-sized slate plates etched with parallel grooves.

An old route token with center hole worn oval.

A little silvered mirror in leather frame, cracked corner.

A copper cup with no handle.

A fist-sized cage made from six thin brass ribs around empty center.

That one caught eye.

Of course.

Riva saw.

"Lucky."

Mella had warned me.

"What does it do?"

"Keeps ferry lantern from going out in bad crosswind."

"How?"

"By being lucky."

"Actual answer."

Riva smiled.

"Old wick cage. Air enters low, leaves high. Brass warms, keeps draft from biting flame directly. Mundane. Two copper."

There.

Good seller.

"Then why lucky?"

"Because people pay more when scared."

Kel coughed into coffee.

I picked up one grooved slate plate.

"This?"

"Current reader."

Interesting.

"Meaning?"

"Old ferry type. Set flat at waterline. Wet it. Grooves show cross-current by how film pulls."

"Magic?"

"Barely, depending who you ask. Slate's treated. River men call everything magic when they don't want to explain surface tension."

"Does it work?"

"Yes."

"Why retired?"

"Because painted floats are easier to see from boat."

I turned plate.

Back had stamped marks.

**WS-F/9**

White Span Ferry maybe.

Riva said, "One copper."

One copper.

I had fourteen.

I did not immediately buy it.

Growth.

"Can you demonstrate?"

She looked offended.

"Yes."

Better.

She took shallow tray from under table.

Poured water from bucket.

Set plate at one edge.

Then dragged spoon through water sideways.

Film across grooves pulled visibly toward spoon wake, leaving darker wet lines on downstream groove edges.

Simple.

Useful.

Not magical enough to ruin day.

"Treatment wears?"

"Eventually."

"How know?"

"Water stops beading clean."

"Can renew?"

"River shops. Wax-mineral rub. Cheap."

"Any false readings?"

Riva looked at me properly now.

"Rain. Oil. Soap. Mud. Bad angle. Person who already decided what current should do."

Excellent.

"Last one expensive."

"Always."

I wanted it.

Problem.

Not because one copper hurt.

Because wanting cheap useful thing was how bags became heavy.

First-life me had owned rooms full of equipment.

Some useful.

Some theoretically useful.

Some purchased because I had once thought, **I could imagine a situation.**

A situation was not a reason.

I looked toward river.

Tomorrow we were literally observing surface behavior.

That was a reason.

Maybe.

But local crew already had current ribbons and floats.

That weakened it.

"White Span has these?"

Riva snorted.

"Had. That's from White Span."

"Why'd they retire them?"

"New float boards eight years ago. Easier from bridge. Plates still good close-bank."

"Why are you selling station property?"

Kel finally became interested.

Riva pointed at stamped slash beside code.

Tiny diagonal punch.

"Retirement mark. Bought lot legal. Ask Fenner if you enjoy paperwork."

I did not.

Sori would.

"One copper?"

"One."

"With renewal rub?"

"No."

"How much rub?"

"Two bits."

There was bits again.

I still didn't know conversion universally because world hated accounting.

"One copper with enough rub for one renewal."

Riva stared.

"You bargain badly."

"Why?"

"Because I would have given two renewals."

I stared back.

Kel laughed so hard he spilled coffee.

"Fuck you."

Riva smiled.

"One copper. Two renewals."

"Fine."

I paid.

Fourteen to thirteen.

There.

New object.

Small.

Useful.

Not destiny.

Riva wrapped plate in old cloth and added thumb-sized tin of gray wax-mineral paste.

"Don't use paste until beading fails. Too much makes grooves sluggish."

"How much?"

"Rice grain each edge. Rub thin. Warm with palm."

"Good."

She tapped cracked mirror.

"Lucky river mirror. Three copper. Shows drowned men before they drown."

"No."

"Two."

"No."

"One."

"You said three because scared people pay more."

"You're learning."

I left.

Kel said, "You bought one thing."

"Yes."

"I'm proud."

"Don't make it weird."

"Could've bought dead-man mirror."

"It was cracked."

"That the problem?"

"Among several."

We brought plate back to inn.

Sori inspected retirement punch before I even asked.

"Legal enough."

"Thank you, mother."

She looked at me.

"I will throw it in river."

"Then we can test current."

"I hate that answer."

Vessa examined plate.

"Personal hardware."

"Yes."

"You don't add it to official test tomorrow unless Fenner agrees."

"Yes."

"You don't use it as excuse to stand somewhere stupid."

"Define stupid."

"No."

Fair.

Dask appeared.

"Is that treated slate?"

I put it back in cloth.

"No."

"I can see it."

"Then why ask?"

"May I inspect it?"

There.

Progress again.

I handed it over.

He checked grooves without touching treatment surface first.

Even more progress.

"Old F-series ferry reader."

"Riva said."

"These are good."

"Retired."

"Good and retired can coexist."

There.

Artifact philosophy.

Or old people.

Same thing eventually.

Maro returned from river with Toma around third bell after noon.

He looked at plate.

"Had one as kid."

"Of course."

"Grandfather used it checking side channels."

"Did it work?"

"If he agreed with it."

Perfect.

Toma pointed at me.

"You bought Sorn's junk?"

"One junk."

"Good plate."

"You just called it junk."

"Both."

River culture understood artifacts better than scholars.

We spent rest of afternoon not doing anything important enough to become crisis.

That mattered.

I walked Westbank slowly.

Boat repair sheds had hulls upside down on blocks.

Children scraped old pitch from planks for coins.

A woman smoked tiny silver fish over alder wood.

Two men argued whether a boat named **MOTHER'S PATIENCE** had ever possessed either.

A shrine near upstream dock held river stones stacked in pairs.

No one explained it.

I didn't ask.

The Two Suns had already added a second painted yellow disk beside old heron sign.

Badly.

Mella was delighted.

"Tourists like bad paint. Feels authentic."

"Authentic to what?"

"Paying me."

Respect.

Late afternoon, messenger arrived from White Span.

Not emergency.

Lessa.

She walked in carrying a board and immediately stole bread from our table.

"Fenner says tomorrow's surface won't be as calm. North wind expected before dawn."

Dask sat up.

"How strong?"

"Enough to make little chop. Not enough to close ferry."

There.

New variable.

Vessa said, "Does local request still want observation?"

"More. Fenner says if wrong light needs calm water, maybe tomorrow tells us something."

Dask looked almost happy.

Lessa pointed bread at him.

"Don't."

He controlled face.

"I am not doing anything."

"You're thinking loudly."

Sori pulled clean sheet.

"Expected wind direction?"

"North to northeast. Starts weak before dawn, strengthens around sunrise if weather office isn't lying."

Maro said, "River runs east-southeast there."

Lessa nodded.

"So cross-ripple."

Greg brain moved.

Not locked.

Just interested.

If phenomenon required mirror-like surface, chop should break it.

If it appeared anyway and followed each small face of water, different evidence.

If it appeared only in sheltered patches behind piers, useful.

If nothing appeared, still useful but not proof.

One morning absence meant one morning absence.

Dask said, "We should establish chop before first light."

"Yes," Vessa said.

"And compare sheltered water under west bank."

"Ask Fenner."

"And perhaps place a controlled flat pan adjacent to river surface to test sky geometry without river continuity."

That was actually good.

I looked at him.

"That's better than bucket after."

He looked pleased.

"Yes."

Sori said, "Don't encourage him."

Lessa swallowed bread.

"Fenner has wash basin."

"Clean?" Dask asked.

"No."

"Can it be cleaned?"

"Probably."

"Material?"

"Metal."

"Reflective?"

"It's a basin, Dask."

"That does not answer."

"Then come look at stupid basin."

He did.

Of course.

Vessa sent Maro too.

I stayed.

That was growth.

Or shoulder.

Both.

Evening came slow over Lume.

From Westbank, physical sunset happened west like respectable celestial behavior.

No second sun.

No wrong reflection.

Just orange river and smoke.

I sat on inn step with new slate plate in lap.

Thirteen copper now.

Vale twenty-three.

Quietener eight.

Plate one.

Bath one.

Money moving.

Not disappearing.

Becoming things.

Some temporary.

Hot water.

Some durable.

Tools.

Some future obligation.

Vale.

That distinction mattered more than purse number.

First life, eventually, I had enough money that small purchases stopped being decisions.

I had thought that was freedom.

It was.

Also made it easier to accumulate stupid shit.

Second life had taught me something poverty had not.

Not **never spend**.

That was fear.

Spend where object changed options.

The quietener did.

Maybe.

The slate plate did in smaller way.

Hot bath changed one evening and next morning's shoulder.

Also worth money.

Different durations.

Different value.

Sori came outside.

"You're counting."

"Thinking."

"Same face."

"Thirteen."

"I know."

"Stop knowing my money."

"You announce purchases like military victories."

"One copper."

"Historic."

I held plate up.

"Useful."

"Probably."

"Rude."

She sat beside me.

For a while we watched river.

Then she said, "You know tomorrow might show nothing."

"Yes."

"Dask will be unbearable."

"Yes."

"Fenner will look personally betrayed by water."

"Probably."

"You?"

I thought.

"Write nothing."

She nodded.

"Good."

That was witness job.

But shopper job had been better.

More immediate.

Pay copper.

Get thing.

Cause and effect.

Beautiful.

River did not respect commerce.

Before bed, Vessa gathered us for ten minutes.

Tomorrow plan changed slightly.

Leave Westbank later than Kettle Vale departure because White Span only half mile away.

Arrive while still dark.

Measure wind/chop before first light.

Use same witness positions as morning where possible.

Fenner would place one cleaned metal basin on west-bank rise, filled from river before sunrise, left undisturbed beside sight frame.

Not to prove mechanism.

Just compare isolated surface with river surface during same sky.

No one would shade it, touch it, move it, or add objects during event.

Good.

Current slate plate could be offered as **supplemental local-approved observation only**, not core test.

I asked, "Worth it?"

Maro said, "If Fenner wants."

Vessa said, "Ask him."

Dask said, "Yes."

Nobody had asked Dask.

Sori wrote that down emotionally.

Not literally.

Probably.

Then Vessa looked at me.

"Shoulder?"

"Better."

"Hand?"

"Better."

"Sleep."

"Yes."

No argument.

There.

Definitely growth.

Upstairs, my room faced river.

Maro already asleep in other bed by time I finished checking bag.

Quietener wrapped.

Tavi's rock.

New slate current reader.

Renewal tin.

Knife.

Clothes.

Small pieces of a life temporarily carried instead of stored.

I put bag under bed.

Crutches against wall.

The reinforced bite marks caught moonlight from window.

Ugly.

Good.

Evidence.

Outside, Lume moved in dark.

Tomorrow it might reflect wrong sun again.

Might not.

Wind might break it.

Might reveal it differently.

Metal basin might show nothing.

Might show something.

Plate might be useful.

Might just become another object in bag.

I liked that.

Not uncertainty exactly.

Options.

I had thirteen copper.

A debt.

A repaired crutch.

A strange brass quietener I did not fully understand.

A one-copper river plate old ferries no longer wanted.

A home far south.

A river outside.

And tomorrow morning still unspent.

Not bad.

# CHAPTER 403

## THE OBSERVER

White Span looked different in wind.

Good.

If it had looked exactly the same, I would have been annoyed on principle.

We left Westbank while the inn sign still had both fake suns painted black against night.

Half mile.

Easy.

Cold enough to make shoulder complain before movement warmed it.

Not injured.

Just reminding me it existed.

Lessa waited at west rise with two lanterns, one broom, one dented wash basin, and no coffee.

"Fenner forgot coffee."

"Again?"

"He remembered basin."

"Tradeoffs."

She pointed toward bridge.

"Wind's here."

It was.

North to northeast.

Not strong.

Enough.

Lume's surface carried small diagonal ridges where yesterday had been broad calm sheet.

No mirror.

Not yet.

Dask was already happy.

I could tell because he had become quieter.

Dangerous sign.

Fenner came up hill carrying timing board under one arm and coffee under other.

Lessa looked betrayed.

"You remembered?"

"I contain multitudes."

"You contain one cup."

"Mine."

She took it.

"Now none."

Local command structure.

Efficient.

Vessa checked positions.

Same as yesterday where possible.

I took witness post.

Dask sight/timing.

Maro bank/weather.

Sori master record.

Kel bridge traffic and practical effects.

Fenner local sequence.

Lessa utility and insulting everyone.

Good team.

The basin sat on a waist-high stone table beside sight frame.

Clean enough.

Metal dull, not polished.

Filled from Lume before first light.

Water inside trembled slightly when gusts hit rim.

Not ideal isolated still surface.

Still useful.

Dask wanted to shield it.

Fenner said no.

"Same sky. Same wind. Different container. Don't improve test until after we see test."

Dask looked at me.

"He's right."

"I know."

"You looked upset."

"I am capable of being right and upset simultaneously."

There.

I liked him again.

My slate plate stayed wrapped in bag.

For now.

Fenner had approved supplemental use later if needed, but not before first observation pass.

Good.

No new toy contaminating baseline.

The east brightened.

Real sunrise came like yesterday.

Slow.

Normal.

Respectable.

Physical sun broke ridge.

Gold touched upper bridge.

We watched downstream of arch four.

Nothing.

One breath.

Ten.

Thirty.

Wind moved chop diagonally across river.

No false band.

Dask marked.

Fenner said nothing.

Lessa whispered, "Don't sulk at water."

"I'm not."

"You are."

Minute one.

Nothing broad.

Then Maro said, "There."

Not center.

Closer to west bank.

A narrow patch behind one projecting stone shoulder where surface chop weakened.

Pale gold.

Wrong direction.

Small.

Maybe six feet across.

It appeared in protected water while exposed water beside it stayed ordinary gray-gold from real east sun.

There.

New.

Not answer.

Dask's pencil moved.

My brain moved too.

Calm patch.

Shelter.

False reflection present locally.

Exposed chop no broad band.

Do not say requires calm.

Not yet.

The patch widened along sheltered edge maybe another three feet.

Stopped.

Wind gust hit.

Surface broke.

False light fragmented.

Not vanished.

Split into little flashes between ripples.

Then rejoined when water flattened for two breaths.

Good.

Very good.

Not because solved.

Because behavior.

I wrote:

**FALSE REFLECTION PRESENT IN PARTIALLY SHELTERED WEST-BANK WATER WHILE EXPOSED ADJACENT WATER REMAINED CHOPPED AND DID NOT FORM PRIOR BROAD BAND.**

Then:

**WITHIN SHELTERED PATCH, STRONGER RIPPLE FRAGMENTED VISIBLE BAND INTO DISCONTINUOUS FLASHES. LESS RIPPLE RESTORED MORE CONTINUOUS APPEARANCE.**

Sori read over once.

Nodded.

Dask said, "That suggests surface coherence threshold."

"Suggests," I said.

"Yes."

He actually used word correctly.

Progress was disgusting.

Basin.

I looked.

Nothing false.

It reflected east sky normally.

Metal rim vibrated slightly in wind.

Water inside had small ripples.

No west-facing gold.

Fenner saw too.

"Basin's boring."

"Useful boring," Sori said.

"Still boring."

Then Lessa said, "Wait."

We waited.

She crouched beside basin without touching.

One corner of basin sat behind sight-frame post.

Tiny sheltered wedge.

Water there was calmer than rest.

For maybe one second I thought pale gold flashed.

Maybe.

Then gone.

Dask inhaled.

I said, "No claim."

He exhaled.

Lessa said, "I saw something."

"So did I."

Sori: "What?"

"Possible pale flash in sheltered basin corner. Less than one breath. Could be real-sun glint, observer angle, rim reflection, or false phenomenon."

Dask said, "Record ambiguous."

"Yes."

There.

We did.

No miracle.

No clean lab answer.

Good.

False patch in river lasted longer than I expected.

Not six minutes broad.

Closer to four, depending whether you counted fragmented flashes as continuous event.

That became argument.

Dask wanted two timings.

Fenner wanted one operational time.

Sori solved it.

"Primary visible interval and continuous-band interval separately."

There.

Primary false-light presence: about four minutes fifty breaths by board.

Continuous sheltered band: about three minutes twenty breaths before stronger chop broke it mostly into intermittent fragments.

Then fragments continued perhaps another minute and change before nothing distinguishable remained.

No one liked approximate wording.

Reality remained rude.

Kel crossed bridge during event again.

No footing issue.

No visibility loss.

He reported false patch harder to notice from bridge deck today because wind broke surface.

Practical consequence.

Fenner reopened ferries sooner than yesterday because broad distracting band never formed.

That mattered.

Administrative hold shortened.

Not because danger lower proven.

Because distraction lower observed.

Toma Grell took first ferry.

Berrit complained he should have.

Toma told him spiritual carp could wait.

Good.

After false light vanished, Fenner approved my slate plate.

Not for anomaly.

Current.

We went down west bank to safe shelf.

Riva's plate set flat at waterline.

Maro helped choose position.

Wet.

Film pulled along grooves southeast with slight cross-bias eastward under north wind.

Matched local float board generally.

Good.

Useful object remained useful object.

No glowing.

No secret reaction.

Dask looked almost disappointed.

I was pleased.

"Current reader reads current," I said.

"Revolutionary."

"Write paper."

Sori said, "Don't."

We packed.

Then route changed.

Fenner had message from Ira Fen by noon rider.

Outside witness obligation complete after two mornings.

Continue extension **east along Lume** to **Bellweather Crossing** if crew willing and Vessa approves.

Reason was not another miracle waiting on schedule.

It was infrastructure.

Bellweather maintained a line of old river mirrors and ferry sight posts used for dawn navigation before modern markers.

Regional office wanted comparison because White Span phenomenon might depend on local geometry, local river bend, or broader river reach.

Not proof.

Comparison.

Bellweather was roughly nine road miles east.

One overnight stop possible at **Low Fen Orchard** halfway.

There.

World opened again.

Vessa read message twice.

"Optional extension through Bellweather, return routing after unless new operational reason."

Sori checked stamp.

"Valid."

Kel said, "Road?"

Fenner said, "East bank road dry. One old ferry stair washed out, detour marked."

Maro looked interested.

"Bellweather mirrors still there?"

Fenner shrugged.

"Some. Half used for hanging laundry last I saw."

Dask looked offended on behalf of mirrors.

Excellent.

Vessa looked around crew.

"We can stop here. Obligation met. Or continue."

No pressure.

Good.

I looked east.

Lume bent broad and bright through grasslands.

Nine miles.

Another place.

Another comparison.

Another bed not mine.

Home remained south.

Still there.

I wanted it.

Also wanted east.

Apparently this was my problem now.

"Continue," I said.

Maro nodded.

Kel: "Fine."

Sori: "If paperwork stays clean."

Dask said, "Bellweather has historical mirror infrastructure."

Vessa: "That is not a vote."

"Continue."

"Better."

We left White Span after midday meal.

No ceremony.

Fenner shook Vessa's hand.

Lessa gave Dask his own pencil cord back because he had left one yesterday.

Berrit told me fish had definitely been confused this morning.

Toma told him fish had definitely been fish.

I recorded neither.

Westbank faded behind.

The Two Suns sign visible longer than village because yellow paint was offensively bright.

Then bend took it.

East road followed Lume but stayed thirty to a hundred yards inland depending bank.

New grammar.

River always nearby.

Sometimes visible.

Sometimes hidden behind willow-like scrub and low white bluffs.

Sound came and went.

Flat boats moved downstream with square dark sails.

Road traffic more commercial than north basin.

Barrels.

Fruit carts.

Net bundles.

One wagon carrying six enormous ceramic jars tied individually in straw.

Dask wanted to know contents.

Driver shouted, "VINEGAR!"

Dask asked, "Why six jars?"

Driver shouted, "BECAUSE SEVEN BROKE THE AXLE!"

Good answer.

Shoulder did well first two miles.

Then began low ache.

I shifted bag.

Kel noticed.

Didn't ask.

At next shade stone he stopped mule for water.

Coincidence.

Probably not.

I took ten breaths.

Nobody counted aloud.

Crew had learned me.

That was strange.

Nice.

The road climbed away from Lume near afternoon.

Below, river surface flashed real sun correctly.

East afternoon light.

No second reflection.

Normal river could still be pretty after becoming evidence.

Important.

Around fourth mile we found first old river mirror.

Not at Bellweather.

A roadside remnant.

Tall stone post with rusted iron frame holding oval silvered plate tilted toward river.

Clouded almost black.

One corner cracked.

Laundry not present.

Dask stopped.

Vessa said, "Thirty seconds."

He approached without touching.

Stamp at base:

**BW-R3 / SIGHT 12**

Bellweather Reach 3 maybe.

Maro said, "Old dawn sight marker. Boatmen lined reflection with notch to read approach in fog."

"Magic?" I asked.

"Mirror."

Dask said, "Silver treatment sometimes directional."

Maro: "Mirror."

I liked Maro.

The plate reflected us badly.

Six stretched people.

One mule.

My head looked long.

Unacceptable.

No false sun.

No anomaly.

We moved.

Low Fen Orchard appeared near dusk.

Not much settlement.

An orchard on slightly raised island of dry ground, rows of low black-barked trees with pale green fruit, one long farmhouse, two worker sheds, road shelter, hitch rail.

Sign:

**LOW FEN ORCHARD / ROAD BEDS / PEAR BRANDY / NO CREDIT**

Promising.

Owner **Hedda Morn** met us with pruning knife still in hand.

Broad woman, gray braid, left knee stiff.

"Survey?"

Vessa showed stamp.

"Beds six?"

"Four beds. Two hammocks."

Sori opened mouth.

Hedda said, "Hammocks count if you sleep."

Sori closed mouth.

Interesting.

Vessa said, "Assignment coverage?"

"Road rate, yes. Dinner extra if not listed."

It was listed.

Good.

We had stew with pears in it.

I expected terrible.

It wasn't.

Dask hated it.

This made meal better.

After dinner Hedda poured tiny finger of pear brandy for herself, none for us unless paid.

I did not buy alcohol.

Mostly because shoulder and dawn schedule.

Also because one copper for bath had been smarter.

Maybe later.

Outside orchard, Lume glimmered through trees half mile away.

Tomorrow Bellweather.

Old mirrors.

Different river geometry.

Comparison rather than answer.

That felt right.

I checked bag before hammock.

Quietener.

Slate reader.

Tavi rock.

Renewal paste.

Thirteen copper.

No magic attempts.

No fights.

No solution.

But we knew more.

Wind chop mattered to visible form.

Shelter mattered enough to produce local patch.

Isolated basin did not clearly reproduce effect, though one ambiguous flash existed.

Current stayed current.

Ferries changed procedure based on observed distraction.

And route moved east.

Observer.

Not solver.

For now, better.

# CHAPTER 404

## THE COMPARER

Hedda Morn woke us with a spoon against a pot.

Not bell.

Spoon.

Three hard strikes from farmhouse kitchen.

Dask sat upright in hammock and hit head on beam.

"Fuck."

Hedda shouted through wall.

"Breakfast!"

Sori opened one eye.

"Efficient system."

I had slept in bed.

Rank had privileges.

Or disability.

Same result sometimes.

My shoulder was stiff when I first moved it.

Not sharp.

Not new.

I rolled it slowly.

Forward.

Side.

Better than Greyhook.

Hand closed around crutch grip without complaint until last bit of squeeze.

Also better.

Right crutch wrap still dark where Rusk's resin had filled tooth marks.

Still ugly.

Still mine.

I stood.

No drama.

Breakfast was oats with pear.

Of course.

Pear bread.

Pear preserve.

And something Hedda called orchard tea that tasted like hot bark remembering fruit.

Kel drank twice.

"Good."

Hedda looked at him.

"No."

He drank third time anyway.

Maro asked road condition.

"Dry," Hedda said. "East ditch soft after marker six. Don't step in it."

Dask said, "Why marker six specifically?"

"Because that's where ditch is soft."

"I mean what caused it?"

"Water."

"From where?"

Hedda pointed spoon toward ceiling.

"Sky."

Dask looked at me.

I ate pear.

No help.

We left first bell.

Low Fen Orchard disappeared behind black-barked trees in twenty minutes.

Bellweather road stayed close to Lume now.

Closer than yesterday.

Sometimes river only thirty yards away beyond willow scrub.

Sometimes road climbed white shelf and water dropped below us, broad and brown-green with silver where morning touched it.

No wrong sun.

Physical sun east.

Reflection where geometry expected.

Good.

Or boring.

Boring was data.

Dask looked at river every opening.

Sori finally said, "If you turn your head any farther, it comes off."

"I am observing."

"You're walking into Maro."

He corrected course.

Maro said, "Observed."

I liked route crew.

At second mile, old sight posts became frequent.

Not every hundred yards.

Maybe every half mile where road and river aligned.

Stone uprights.

Iron frames.

Some empty.

Some held blackened oval plates.

One had been repurposed to hang laundry.

Dask stopped.

Vessa did not.

He caught up.

Growth.

Maro explained while walking.
