"Bellweather used mirrors before bank lamps got cheap. Fog sits low. Boatmen couldn't always see far shore. Sight plates caught dawn or lamp and gave line."

"Directional silvering?" Dask asked.

"Mirror."

"Treatment could have directional response."

"Mirror."

"Those statements aren't mutually exclusive."

"Still mirror."

Sori said, "I'm putting that on your grave."

Maro considered.

"Fine."

At marker six, ditch was soft.

We didn't step in it.

Revolutionary.

Bellweather announced itself by sound.

Hammers.

Bells.

Boat horns.

People yelling numbers.

Then smell.

Tar.

Coal smoke.

Wet wood.

Fish.

Something sweet frying.

Then town.

Bellweather Crossing was bigger than Westbank and Kettle Vale combined.

Not Carrow-big.

Different.

Long instead of dense.

Town stretched along both banks with bridge at center and ferry docks above and below.

Main bridge was iron-and-stone, three broad arches, newer than White Span.

Upstream stood older crossing remains.

Two massive stone piers with no deck between them.

Each pier carried a tall frame of silvered plates arranged like vertical fan.

There.

River mirrors.

Not one.

Dozens.

Most blackened.

Some bright.

One missing.

Dask made noise.

Not word.

Noise.

Sori said, "Control yourself."

"I am."

"Worse."

A road clerk met us at west checkpoint.

Woman named **Pera Lonn**.

Short.

Round glasses.

Blue ink stripe down left sleeve.

She read Ira's extension paper twice.

"White Span wrong dawn."

Vessa said, "Observed six mornings total, two outside-witness mornings."

"And you brought Renn."

Dask brightened.

Pera pointed at me.

"Him."

I sighed.

"Why?"

"Dog story."

"Zero dead."

"Heard fifteen."

"Inflation."

Pera looked at crutches.

"You fought fifteen dogs on those?"

"I did not fight fifteen dogs."

"How many?"

I thought about actual moving hounds, threats, contacts, numbers in reeds.

"Enough."

Vessa said, "We're here for mirrors."

Pera nodded.

Work replaced gossip.

"Good. Because if you're here for sunrise, you missed it."

"We know."

"Better. I hate visitors who think dawn waits for paperwork."

She stamped extension.

"Bellweather archive keeps mirror logs. Old north pier still has six serviceable plates. South pier nine. We do not use them for navigation anymore. Maintenance checks twice yearly because heritage office pays us to pretend rust has feelings."

Dask inhaled.

Pera held up finger.

"No touching without keeper."

He exhaled.

"Understood."

"Archive first. Keeper after lunch."

Good.

No repeated dawn.

Comparison by records and infrastructure.

Bellweather archive occupied second floor above ferry office.

Stairs.

Of course.

Twenty-eight.

I counted because body did.

Sori noticed because she notices everything annoying.

"Need stop?"

"No."

At top shoulder warm.

Fine.

Archive smelled like dust, river damp, and old leather.

Pera handed us to **Olden Marr**, archivist.

He was maybe seventy, narrow as ruler, white hair tied with red string.

He wore fingerless gloves despite warmth.

First thing he said:

"Do not call them magic mirrors."

Dask looked personally attacked.

"I haven't."

"Good. Keep it that way."

I liked Bellweather.

Olden pulled four ledgers.

"Dawn sights. Forty-two years surviving. Earlier burned in ferry-office fire."

"Any wrong-direction reflections?" Vessa asked.

Olden looked over glasses.

"If there were, I assume that's why you're here."

"Yes."

"Then no."

Simple.

Dask said, "None?"

"None recorded."

"That isn't same as none occurring."

Olden's expression softened slightly.

"Correct. You may stay."

There.

Dask passed exam.

We split ledgers.

Not read every page.

Thank gods.

Olden had indexes.

Season.

Visibility.

Mirror faults.

Fog.

Glare.

False lamp.

Sun alignment.

Boat incidents.

We searched terms around reflection anomalies.

There were many normal problems.

Morning glare blinding eastbound pilot.

Fog doubling lamps.

Ice on plates creating split highlights.

Bird shit.

More bird shit.

A note from twenty-three years ago:

**SOUTH ARRAY SHOWED TWO DAWNS. CLEANED GULL RESIDUE. RESOLVED.**

Dask stared.

"Technically two dawns."

Olden said, "Technically gull shit."

Sori laughed loud enough to get shushed by another clerk.

Useful absence accumulated.

Forty-two years indexed.

No persistent west-facing sunrise on river.

No multi-minute wrong-direction surface reflection matching White Span description.

No record of bridge shadow failing to extinguish a false river reflection.

No recurring calm-water-only west band.

That did not prove Bellweather couldn't do it tomorrow.

But it mattered.

White Span looked more local.

Or newer.

Or unnoticed elsewhere.

Or dependent on geometry Bellweather lacked.

Many options.

Good.

Olden brought a map.

Lume bent differently here.

At White Span river ran east-southeast.

Bellweather crossing sat on long north-south bend before river turned east again.

Old mirror arrays faced southeast and southwest from old piers.

"Why two directions?" I asked.

Olden tapped map.

"Morning fog. One array caught dawn. Other caught bank lamps. Later both caught lamps depending season."

"So not symmetrical use."

"No."

Dask said, "Were plates differently treated?"

"Yes."

Maro looked betrayed.

Olden continued.

"Morning plates had blue-black backing. Lamp plates red-brown. Silver wash same family. Different backing reduced glare."

Dask looked at Maro.

Maro said, "Mirror."

Fine.

We went to lunch.

Ferry office fed assignment crew lentil soup and fried river cakes.

I bought nothing.

Thirteen copper survived noon.

Pera joined us halfway through.

"Keeper available. North pier only. South access closed because someone dropped wrench through ladder yesterday and decided gravity was management's problem."

Kel said, "Was it?"

"Eventually."

North pier access was ground-level walkway from bank to old stone base.

No climbing.

Good.

Keeper **Jori Pell** met us there.

Maro looked at him.

Jori looked at Maro.

"No."

Maro blinked.

"I didn't ask."

"Everyone asks."

Dask said, "Are you related?"

Both men said, "No."

Excellent.

Jori was younger, maybe twenty-seven, thick black hair and one gold tooth.

He carried maintenance satchel full of cloths, oil, gauges, and a little square viewing hood.

Six serviceable north plates remained on old fan array.

Each oval roughly forearm long.

Silver face slightly smoked.

Back dark blue-black.

Frames could rotate limited arc and tilt.

Stops prevented full turn.

Not mystical.

Engineering.

Old engineering was still engineering.

Jori demonstrated without changing stored calibration.

He opened one viewing hood around lower plate.

"Cuts side glare. Look from mark."

Brass foot mark embedded in stone.

I looked.

Plate reflected bright patch of southern sky and thin line of river.

Move head two inches.

Reflection changed sharply.

Directional by geometry and hood.

Not magic necessarily.

Dask looked after permission.

Sori after him.

Maro.

Kel.

Vessa.

Then Jori said, "White Span people describe light staying under bridge shadow?"

"Yes," Vessa said.

"Mirrors can't tell you about that."

Dask said, "Not directly."

"Not indirectly either unless you invent story."

I liked him too.

Jori showed backing.

Blue-black layer had fine mineral flecks.

"Glare cut. Helps dawn edge show in fog."

"Magical treatment?" Dask asked.

Jori shrugged.

"Silver wash takes charge during making. Backing takes some. Once made, plate mostly plate. You want guild answer, ask mirror guild. You want ferry answer, it reflects things."

Maro smiled.

Vindicated.

I asked, "Any plate ever show source not actually in front of it?"

Jori scratched chin.

"Cracked ones can double. Bent ones shift. Backing peel makes ghosts. Fog makes idiots."

"Persistent wrong direction?"

"Not if plate healthy."

"River surface?"

"River does what river wants."

Not answer.

Accurate.

Dask asked to inspect maintenance records.

Jori pointed back toward archive.

"Olden has them."

Dask visibly suffered.

We had just left archive.

Vessa said, "Later if needed."

There.

Restraint.

Then something useful happened.

Not anomaly.

Money.

Pera found us at pier.

"Regional office sent pay clarification. Extension days count as field days."

Sori immediately said, "Starting day four."

Pera nodded.

"Yes. Your original route language. Two copper per completed field day after third. Merecross already paid departure six. North extension didn't void terms."

I stopped looking at mirror.

There.

Economy.

"How many completed paid days do you have logged?" Sori asked.

Pera checked slip.

"Three additional since threshold, through yesterday. Today pays on completion."

I did arithmetic.

Six copper accrued.

Not paid yet.

Today would make eight accrued after workday complete.

Sori looked at me.

"Don't spend it before paid."

"I wasn't."

"You stopped looking at the mirror when she said eight copper."

Fuck everyone knowing my face.

Pera said, "Bellweather can settle regional extension pay tomorrow morning if Vessa signs tonight's completion. Or carry ledger onward."

Vessa said, "Settle here."

Good.

Tomorrow potentially eight copper.

Thirteen to twenty-one.

Vale still twenty-three.

Interesting.

Not rich.

But closer to something.

Artifact markets got different when purse crossed twenty.

I did not say that.

Sori would hear anyway.

She narrowed eyes.

I looked innocent.

Poorly.

We spent late afternoon comparing Bellweather's present river surface to White Span notes.

Not formal anomaly watch.

Just ordinary observation.

Wind light southwest.

Water moderate ripple.

Real sun west now because afternoon.

Reflections normal.

WS-F/9 plate stayed in bag.

No reason.

Quietener stayed in bag.

Even less reason.

Good tools did not demand use.

That was maybe most important thing about owning them.

At fifth bell, Vessa closed workday.

Pera signed local completion.

Sori copied pay note.

Dask asked one final question about backing mineral composition.

Olden sent him to mirror guild three streets uphill.

He looked at Vessa.

"Tomorrow," she said.

"Before departure?"

"Maybe."

He accepted maybe like man swallowing bone.

Bellweather lodging was above old ferry tavern.

Six beds.

No hammocks.

Luxury.

Dinner not included.

Assignment rate covered room, not food.

There.

Economy again.

Tavern board:

**STEW + BREAD - 2 BITS**

**FRIED RIVER CAKES - 3 BITS**

**MEAT PIE - 5 BITS**

Bits.

Still no universal conversion.

I had bits.

Unspecified amount.

Annoying even internally.

I bought stew and bread.

Paid in bits.

Copper remained thirteen.

No need to invent conversion because cashier knew and I didn't care enough.

Freedom.

Sori sat across from me.

"You're pleased."

"Food."

"Pay."

"Also pay."

"Eight tomorrow if signed."

"Apparently."

"Twenty-one."

"Stop."

"Vale twenty-three."

"I know."

"Almost symmetry."

"Debt is not collectible set."

"You collect worse things."

She had point.

Kel joined with meat pie.

"What's next after Bellweather?"

Vessa sat down at end table with Pera's route note.

"Regional office gave two options."

Of course.

She unfolded paper.

"One: turn south along old ferry road toward Merecross network. Not Carrow directly. Two: continue east to **Sable Quay**, where Lume widens into trade basin."

Dask appeared from nowhere.

"Sable Quay has mirror guild."

Vessa stared.

"How do you know?"

"Olden."

"Of course."

Maro sat.

"How far?"

"Eleven miles. Road good. One night likely at **Rill House** or camp depending pace."

Sori asked, "Operational reason?"

Vessa read.

"Bellweather comparison suggests White Span behavior not historically common here. Regional office wants records delivered to Sable Quay survey annex because Quay handles lower-Lume traffic and has broader sight-system archive. Extension authorized if crew willing."

There.

Again.

A legitimate door.

Not quest.

Paperwork.

Best kind.

Kel said, "East."

Maro nodded.

Dask was already spiritually in mirror guild.

Sori looked at me.

"Don't say market."

I hadn't.

Now I was thinking it.

Sable Quay.

Trade basin.

Eleven miles farther from home.

Twenty-one copper tomorrow if pay settled.

Dangerous combination.

Vessa said, "Greg?"

I ate last bread.

Shoulder low ache.

Hand almost normal.

Crutch sound.

Bag slightly heavier than when I left Carrow.

Map much heavier.

"East," I said.

Sori sighed.

"He means market."

"I mean east."

"Same face."

Fuck my face.

Outside, old Bellweather mirrors caught evening lamps one by one.

Not wrong light.

Just light.

That mattered.

White Span had shown us something strange.

Bellweather showed us what forty-two years of ordinary strange looked like when people bothered to write it down.

Glare.

Fog.

Cracks.

Ice.

Bird shit.

Normal failures.

Normal explanations.

Comparison did not solve the wrong sunrise.

It did something better.

It made the mystery smaller without making it less interesting.

Local.

Maybe.

New.

Maybe.

Geometry-dependent.

Maybe.

At least not obviously everywhere.

That was enough.

Tomorrow I would collect eight copper.

Then we would go east.

Apparently that was what my life did now.

Compare one place to another until map became too large to pretend I was only visiting.

# CHAPTER 405

## THE EARNER

Bellweather paid me in the morning.

That was satisfying.

Not poetic.

Better.

Pera Lonn had three papers, two stamps, one clerk beside her, and the expression of a woman who believed money should only move after paperwork had suffered enough.

"Vessa Harn signed completion. Local extension verified. Four payable field days. Two copper each. Eight copper."

She pushed a small cloth pouch across desk.

I picked it up.

Weight.

Real.

Not theoretical money.

Sori said, "Count it."

"I trust bureaucracy."

Pera looked offended.

"Don't."

Fair.

I counted.

Eight.

Thirteen became twenty-one.

Vale remained twenty-three.

Close enough to annoy me.

Not close enough to matter.

Sori watched me put the pouch away.

"Don't."

"Again, haven't said anything."

"Trade basin. Twenty-one copper. Finish the road first."

"Purchases."

"Same posture."

Pera handed Vessa route packet.

"Sable Quay annex closes fourth bell. If you don't make it today, Rill House can stamp lodging. Road is clear through mile eight. Mile nine has freight-control rotation because east embankment work."

Kel asked, "Delay?"

"Depends carts. Foot crews pass faster."

"Mules?"

"Controlled batches."

Dask asked, "Mirror guild hours?"

Pera looked at him.

"I regret mentioning mirror guild."

Olden Marr, passing behind us with a ledger, said, "I mentioned it."

Pera pointed at him.

"Then I regret you."

He kept walking.

Bellweather remained excellent.

We left before second bell.

Twenty-one copper felt different in purse.

Not because weight.

Because choices.

At thirteen, some prices ended conversation.

At twenty-one, they became questions.

Still not yes.

Question.

Could afford did not mean should buy.

This was adulthood.

Terrible system.

East road out of Bellweather widened quickly.

Commercial road.

Two carts abreast in places.

Packed gravel instead of hard dirt.

Stone drainage.

Mile markers maintained enough to deserve numbers.

The Lume widened too.

At Bellweather it had been broad river.

East of Bellweather it started behaving like infrastructure.

Docks every mile or two.

Short loading ramps.

Tether posts.

Warehouses standing alone beside road for no obvious town.

Flatboats moved downstream under dark square sails, heavy enough that water climbed their sides.

One carried stacked blue ceramic jars.

Another carried timber.

Another something under canvas that smelled like pepper even from road.

I liked trade roads.

Things moved because someone wanted them somewhere else badly enough to pay.

Simple motivation.

Complicated world.

Around mile three we passed a roadside exchange board.

Not market.

Prices.

Freight listings nailed under awning.

**BELLWEATHER -> SABLE QUAY**

**BARREL SPACE**

**HALF CRATE**

**LIVE BIRDS EXTRA**

**NO OPEN OIL**

Kel stopped long enough to read transport rates.

Vessa said, "Considering cart?"

He nodded.

"Shoulder?" she asked me.

"Fine."

Not perfectly.

Fine.

Low ache only after first hour.

Hand mostly normal.

Current body was coming back online.

Kel said, "Walking keeps us flexible. Cart batch could save two hours if timing good."

Sori said, "Costs?"

"Assignment transport only if route need documented."

Vessa looked at road.

"No need yet."

Good.

We walked.

At mile five, trade road split around a low quarry cut.

Main carts took lower stone lane.

Foot traffic climbed upper shelf.

Sable Quay first appeared from there.

Not town.

Shape.

Far east, river opened wide enough to become silver-gray basin.

Buildings clustered on both banks and on low artificial-looking islands or piers reaching into water.

Masts.

Cranes.

Smoke.

Three bridges visible from miles away.

One high and narrow.

One broad.

One maybe rail or freight-only.

Dask stopped walking.

Sori said, "Walk."

"I am."

"Not currently."

Maro shaded eyes.

"Bigger than last time."

"When?" I asked.

"Nine years."

"How much bigger?"

"That east crane field wasn't there."

There.

World changing without us.

Good.

Sable Quay became destination instead of concept.

And then road made us pay for looking.

Mile nine freight-control rotation.

Pera had not lied.

East embankment work narrowed road to one reinforced lane between river drop and active stonework.

A red-cloth control post held maybe thirty carts.

Foot traffic could pass in side corridor.

Mules could not.

Our shared cases rode two pack mules.

There.

Delay.

Not crisis.

A control woman named **Tess Arlo** explained.

"Mule batch in thirty-five minutes if no westbound priority. Forty-five if stone train comes. Foot crew can go now, animals stay here with registered handler."

Vessa looked at Kel.

Kel looked at cases.

"I stay."

Sori said, "Custody?"

"I'll sign hold."

Vessa considered.

"We split?"

Normally I disliked split parties.

Stories punished them.

Life sometimes rewarded logistics.

Sori said, "Annex paperwork can start without physical cases if seal numbers logged."

Dask said, "Mirror guild is in Quay."

Nobody acknowledged him.

Vessa decided.

"Kel stays with mules. Sori stays for custody?"

Sori shook head.

"Kel can hold under transfer if control post countersigns. I should take records forward."

Tess said, "We countersign."

There.

Vessa assigned.

Kel with mules/cases.

Rest of us foot corridor into Quay.

We'd regroup at annex or Rill House depending timing.

Kel said, "Bring food."

"Specific?" Maro asked.

"Anything not pear."

Reasonable.

Foot corridor ran narrow against river side.

Guard rope on one side.

Stonework on other.

Workers set dressed blocks into embankment using tripod hoists and shouted cadence.

Not magical spectacle.

Labor.

One foreman used a little brass level that flashed green when stone sat true.

I noticed.

Kept walking.

Twenty-one copper did not require purchasing every useful thing visible.

Important reminder.

A worker shouted, "One-leg, watch rope!"

I did.

Loose hauling line crossed path knee-high for two breaths.

I stopped.

They lifted.

I passed.

No pride.

No performance.

Normal accommodation between strangers doing work.

Good.

Sable Quay west gate wasn't gate.

It was tax arch.

Two stone pylons framing road with hanging boards listing fees by wagon, beast, barrel, dock class, and probably phases of moon.

Foot entry free.

Excellent.

Inside, sound changed.

Carrow had crowded streets.

Sable Quay had systems colliding.

Dock bells.

Auction calls.

Cart wheels.

Boat horns.

Saw mills.

Metal striking metal.

People yelling berth numbers.

People yelling prices.

People yelling because everyone else was yelling.

Buildings leaned commercial.

Tall warehouses, narrow offices, inns stacked over shops, guild signs, weighing houses, rope halls, chandlers, repair yards.

The river basin occupied center of everything.

Water wide enough that opposite bank looked like another district instead of across street.

Floating docks formed lanes.

Cargo cranes swung over barges.

One barge carried an entire small house.

Dask said, "Mirror guild."

I said, "Survey annex."

He looked at me.

"Yes."

Pain.

We found annex first.

Good people.

Three-story stone building beside west basin office.

Sign:

**LOWER LUME SURVEY ANNEX**

Below:

**TRAFFIC / SIGHT / FLOW / INCIDENT RECORDS**

Inside smelled like ink and wet coats.

A clerk named **Nema Vos** took Vessa's packet and immediately became alarmingly competent.

She was maybe thirty, dark curls tied high, sleeves rolled, six pencils behind one ear.

Six.

Dask looked at pencils.

She noticed.

"Don't."

He looked impressed.

Vessa summarized White Span and Bellweather in five minutes.

Nema asked good questions.

"White Span six consecutive mornings?"

"Yes."

"Two outside-witness?"

"Yes."

"Broad band calm morning, sheltered patch choppy morning?"

"Yes."

"Bellweather archive comparable record?"

"None in forty-two surviving indexed years."

"Cause?"

"Unknown."

"Connection to signal anomalies upstream?"

"Not established."

Nema nodded.

"Good. Keep it that way until reason."

Dask and I both liked her.

Dangerous.

She read Ira's extension authorization.

"Your crew can close regional extension here if you want. Or annex can issue lower-Lume continuation if we find reason."

There.

Choice existed.

Not automatic.

Vessa asked, "Any reason yet?"

Nema looked toward ceiling like records lived above it.

"Maybe. Not the exciting kind."

Best kind.

She pulled one thin folder.

"Three ferry captains reported unusual dawn glare this week near **Glassmouth Reach**, two miles east of Quay basin. Not wrong-direction sunrise. Not multi-minute band. Just glare strong enough to obscure channel marks for seconds at a time. Could be ordinary angle because water level dropped four inches."

Dask inhaled.

Nema held up hand.

"Could be ordinary angle."

He closed mouth.

"We're checking tomorrow anyway. If you're still here, outside eyes useful. If not, your White Span packet stands alone."

Good.

No railroad.

Nema stamped receipt.

"Your cases?"

"Delayed at mile nine," Vessa said.

"Fine. Bring by third bell."

Sori handed seal numbers.

Nema checked.

"Lodging?"

"Not arranged."

"Survey rate at Dockhouse Seven if rooms open. If full, Rill House west road or private inns. Food not covered unless assigned overnight work."

Economy.

Excellent.

Then she said dangerous thing.

"Mirror guild is two streets north."

Dask smiled.

Sori looked at her.

"Why would you tell him?"

Nema looked at Dask.

"Because I want to see what happens."

Chaos clerk.

We had maybe one bell before Kel arrived.

Vessa said, "One bell. No purchases for assignment. Personal time. Stay in west basin district."

There.

Freedom.

I did not immediately go to market.

Important.

I went with Dask to mirror guild.

Different.

Guild occupied narrow brick building with black glass windows.

Sign showed silver oval crossed by blue line.

Inside, front room displayed mirrors.

Not household mirrors.

Signal plates.

Sight plates.

Inspection mirrors.

Heat-reading dark glass.

Tiny convex mirrors for machine housings.

Long narrow pieces mounted in brass.

Prices.

Oh.

There were prices.

**FOG SIGHT PLATE, NEW - 18C**

**LAMPSIDE GHOST CHECKER - 11C**

**SILVER-BACK FIELD MIRROR - 9C**

**SPLIT-ANGLE CALIBRATION PAIR - 27C**

**RIVER DAWN PLATE, BLUE-BACK - 34C**

There.

Twenty-one copper suddenly became medium money.

Useful.

Not enough.

Exactly right.

A guild woman approached.

**Elya Senn**, badge said.

Forties.

Shaved head.

Silver dust embedded in cuffs.

"Survey?"

Dask said, "Yes."

I said, "Personal browsing."

She looked at my crutches, then bag, then Dask's face.

"Which one is dangerous?"

"Him," I said.

Dask said, "Probably him."

Fair.

Elya asked what we wanted.

Dask launched into Bellweather plate backing.

I wandered.

Not far.

Still listened.

Elya explained blue-black dawn backing reduced off-axis glare and improved contrast, yes, but no, plates did not create light.

Normal.

Then I found used shelf.

Danger.

Not junk.

Guild-inspected used.

Tags had condition grades and reason retired.

Better danger.

A palm-sized folding inspection mirror with edge chipped.

4c.

A two-piece sight bracket missing one clamp.

3c.

A narrow smoked plate retired for backing peel.

6c.

A little brass housing containing three rotating glass slits.

Tag:

**PARALLAX FINDER, PF-3**

**USED / GRADE C**

**10C**

**FAULT: CENTER DETENT SLIPS UNDER VIBRATION**

I picked it up only after checking sign said handling allowed.

Heavy for size.

Three slits aligned different angles when ring turned.

Old field tool for checking whether apparent offset came from observer position or target displacement.

Interesting.

Very interesting.

Ten copper.

I had twenty-one.

Vale twenty-three.

Half my liquid almost.

No.

Not immediate.

Good.

Elya appeared beside me.

"That one bites people who love clever tools."

"Because detent slips?"

"Because ten copper feels cheap compared to thirty-six new."

"How much repair?"

"If only spring, two to four. If ring channel worn, six to nine. If axle bent, throw it at enemy."

"Tested?"

"Bench stable. Vibration fault confirmed. Axle appears straight. No guarantee under field abuse."

There.

Rational price.

"Use?"

"Line-of-sight comparison. Surveyors. Machine aligners. Mirror fitters. People who think their eyes are trustworthy until tool insults them."

I wanted it.

Problem.

Could it change options?

Yes.

Would I use it beyond current river mystery?

Probably.

Could I afford ten?

Yes.

Should I spend nearly half current liquid on another bounded problem while owing Vale twenty-three?

Different question.

I put it back.

Elya nodded.

"Good."

"What?"

"Most people who touch that one buy it because they already imagined saving thirty-six."

"I imagined twelve different uses."

"Worse."

"Agreed."

Dask arrived.

Saw PF-3.

Made noise.

I said, "No."

"I didn't say anything."

"Your face."

There.

Justice.

We left without buying.

Twenty-one survived mirror guild.

This deserved medal.

Outside, Sable Quay market district spilled north.

I could see rows of awnings.

Hear auction bell.

Smell spice, hot metal, fried dough.

I did not go.

Not yet.

One bell meant one bell.

Crew before market.

Painful maturity.

Kel arrived at annex near third bell with mules and cases.

Delay had been forty-two minutes.

He looked annoyed.

"Stone train."

"Food?" he asked.

Maro handed him fried bread he'd bought in street.

Kel immediately forgave civilization.

Cases checked in.

Seal intact.

Nema countersigned delivery.

Vessa closed route segment.

Then asked crew what we wanted.

Not work assignment yet.

Today could end here.

Tomorrow optional Glassmouth observation if we remained.

Or extension could close and we could start south/west return network.

Or wait for new orders.

Sori said, "We should stay one night at least. Cases just arrived."

Kel said, "Agreed."

Maro said, "Glassmouth is two miles. Might as well look if annex wants outside eyes."

Dask said, "Mirror guild opens first bell."

Everyone ignored him.

Vessa looked at me.

Sable Quay outside.

Twenty-one copper.

A ten-copper parallax finder sitting on used shelf.

Market I hadn't entered.

Home much farther west than I'd planned weeks ago.

Shoulder almost normal.

Purse healthier because I had worked.

This mattered.

Not luck.

Not found money.

Not inheritance.

Labor.

Field days.

I had been useful long enough that road paid me for remaining useful.

I liked that more than expected.

"Stay," I said.

Vessa nodded.

"One night. Glassmouth dawn if annex confirms. Then reassess."

Good.

We got Dockhouse Seven rooms.

Survey rate.

Two rooms, three beds each.

Food personal.

I bought a hot meat bun from street for one bit.

Bits remained unspecified.

Copper stayed twenty-one.

Then, finally, I walked market.

Not to buy.

To look.

Mostly.

Sori came with me because she did not trust "mostly."

Sable Quay market was not twelve tables.

It was streets.

Tool row.

River row.

Salvage row.

Textile court.

Spice lane.

Auction yard.

Used magical hardware behind wire screens with licensed sellers.

Prices that made twenty-one copper look like childhood allowance.

Forty.

Sixty.

One hundred twenty.

Silver marks.

Gold once, on a locked case I did not even approach.

There.

Economic ceiling moved upward.

Not because world got more expensive.

Because I had reached market where expensive things lived.

Important distinction.

Sori watched me realize.

"Happy?"

"Extremely."

"You're not buying anything."

"Also true."

"Growth."

"Temporary."

She laughed.

We stopped at one licensed salvage window.

Inside sat a squat iron ring the size of dinner plate with six engraved notches and a tag:

**LOAD-SHARE COLLAR / RETIRED DOCK CRANE / 48C**

Known use notes listed.

Known failure notes listed.

Price forty-eight.

I read everything.

Could not afford it.

Not remotely with Vale.

And that was fine.

Better than fine.

There were things worth wanting beyond current reach.

Good.

Money had direction now.

Not hoard.

Access.

Option.

Future yes.

We walked back at dusk.

Sable basin lights came on one dock at a time.

Mirrored in water normally.

Hundreds of them.

No wrong sun.

Just commerce.

I had twenty-one copper.

Twenty-three owed.

A bag of small tools.

A body mostly recovered.

A crew that had somehow become people I expected beside me at dinner.

And a market big enough to remind me I was still poor in interesting ways.

Excellent.

Tomorrow, maybe Glassmouth.

After that, who knew.

For once, earning money had not made me want to go home and protect it.

It made road look longer.

# CHAPTER 406

## THE CONTRACTOR

Sable Quay woke before I did.

That was offensive.

By first gray light, carts were already rattling over stone below Dockhouse Seven.

Somewhere close, someone hammered metal in steady groups of four.

A crane bell rang twice.

Then once.

Then three times.

Probably meaningful.

To someone.

I lay still another ten breaths.

Luxury.

Shoulder felt mostly normal until I lifted arm high.

Then low reminder.

Hand closed clean around crutch grip.

Good.

Body debt shrinking.

Money debt not.

Twenty-one copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Near symmetry remained annoying.

I got dressed.

Maro was already awake, sitting at window with tea.

"Glassmouth?" I asked.

"Messenger came."

"And?"

"Annex says no need."

Excellent.

"Ordinary?"

"Probably."

"Probably isn't observed."

Maro looked at me.

"You've been around Dask too long."

"Terrible illness."

He handed me folded annex note.

Nema Vos had written in fast slanted hand:

**GLASSMOUTH DAWN GLARE REPRODUCED UNDER LOW-WATER CHANNEL ANGLE. EAST MARKER VISIBLE AFTER SHADING PANEL RESET. NO OUTSIDE WITNESS NEEDED. REPORT CLOSED LOCAL.**

Good.

Boring solved itself.

Even better.

At bottom:

**IF YOUR CREW IS AVAILABLE, COME ANNEX AFTER BREAKFAST. POSSIBLE SHORT CONTRACT. NOT MIRRORS.**

There.

Dangerous words.

Not mirrors.

I liked them immediately.

Breakfast downstairs was two eggs, dark bread, and something advertised as river sausage.

I bought bread and eggs.

Bits.

Copper untouched.

Sori watched me pay.

"You keeping ledger now?"

"No."

"You looked."

"At money."

"That's ledger with anxiety."

"I have no anxiety."

"Twenty-one."

"Stop."

Kel arrived carrying coffee and no visible pear products.

He looked spiritually repaired.

Dask appeared last.

He had silver dust on one cuff.

Vessa saw it.

"You went guild before breakfast."

"No."

Sori looked at cuff.

Dask looked at cuff.

"Briefly."

"Did you buy anything?" Vessa asked.

"No."

I said, "Good."

Dask looked at me.

"You have no authority."

"Correct. Still good."

We reached Lower Lume Survey Annex after first bell.

Quay looked different in morning.

Not prettier.

More honest.

Yesterday, scale had been visual.

Cranes.

Bridges.

Warehouse roofs.

Markets.

This morning it was throughput.

Carts entering tax arch in two lanes.

Cargo slings moving every minute.

Flatboats waiting three deep at one dock.

Porters carrying numbered boards.

Clerks shouting weights.

Everything moving because someone expected something to arrive somewhere else.

Carrow had work.

Sable Quay had systems for work becoming volume.

Nema Vos stood behind annex counter with seven pencils today.

"One migrated," Dask said.

She pointed at him.

"Don't count them."

"I wasn't."

"You were."

Good.

Nema waved us into side room.

No archive.

Thank gods.

Table.

Map.

Three forms.

Better.

"Glassmouth closed local," she said. "Water dropped, eastern channel marker sat against direct glare angle, shading panel was two notches off because hinge pin wore. Fixed."

Dask said, "So captains were correct about unusual glare."

"Yes."

"And wrong about unusual cause."

"Mostly."

"Good."

Nema looked at him.

"You enjoyed that too much."

"No."

"That was an enthusiastic good."

Sori made sound like victory.

Nema tapped map.

"This is unrelated to your river-light work. Different ask."

Map showed south edge of Quay basin and a narrow industrial spur called **Blackglass Cut**.

"Dock Eight transfer yard has three retired load frames being moved to salvage auction tomorrow. One failed inspection last week. The other two were taken out with it because same production lot and nobody trusts cousins after one bites."

Kel said, "What failed?"

"One collar released late under lateral swing. No injury. Damaged crate. Yard shut frame."

There.

Load-share collars.

My brain remembered forty-eight copper one from market.

Nema continued.

"Dock wants independent pre-salvage function record so auction sheet distinguishes dead iron from restricted-use parts. Survey annex can provide two people. Dock wants four. Your crew has relevant field/load experience and records discipline."

Vessa asked, "Duration?"

"Half day. Maybe less if weather holds."

"Pay?"

Nema smiled.

There.

Contract.

"Separate from route pay. Dock Eight pays three copper per external hand for inspection witness and handling. Four if you accept controlled unloaded-motion test on designated frame."

Kel said, "Per person?"

"Per approved external hand. Not everyone needs to take it."

Sori immediately asked, "Liability?"

Nema slid form.

"Dock retains equipment liability. External witnesses follow yard lead. No live cargo. No suspended human load. No climbing frame. No magical intervention unless yard lead requests and procedure written."

I liked Sable Quay more.

Specific paperwork was attractive.

Vessa read form.

"Who do they want?"

"Kel for transport/load handling. Sori for record/custody. Greg because Dock heard he solved overloaded mule distribution on approach route and because he apparently killed eighteen hounds."

"Zero."

Nema nodded.

"I wrote zero in margin."

Good woman.

"Fourth could be Vessa or Maro. Dask only if yard wants technical witness."

Dask looked offended.

Nema said, "You ask too many questions around moving machinery."

"That is unfair."

"It's accurate."

Vessa studied map.

"If we take contract, regional route status?"

"Paused personal/local work day. Does not count toward field-day extension pay unless annex separately assigns. Your Sable Quay records delivery is complete. You are technically free crew until next route order."

There.

Important.

We had become available.

Not unemployed.

Available.

Different feeling.

Vessa looked at us.

"I don't choose your side work."

Kel said, "I'll take it."

Sori said, "Three copper for half day to watch paperwork and stop Greg touching things. Yes."

"I don't touch things."

Everyone looked at me.

"Without permission."

Better.

Nema said, "Greg?"

Three copper guaranteed.

Four if controlled unloaded-motion test.

Twenty-one to twenty-four or twenty-five.

Vale twenty-three.

There was symmetry again, but better.

Also dock hardware.

Real systems.

Real failure.

Potential salvage auction tomorrow.

"Yes."

Vessa said, "I'll go as fourth."

Maro looked relieved.

Dask did not.

"What do I do?"

Nema said, "Anything that doesn't involve Dock Eight."

He looked at me.

I said, "Mirror guild."

"I was going to say annex archive."

"Liar."

He had face.

Contract signed.

Three copper base each for Kel, Sori, Vessa, me.

Possible fourth with designated test.

No money yet.

Sori looked directly at me.

"Unpaid."

"I know."

"Then don't count it yet."

"I wasn't."

Blackglass Cut was two bridges south and one freight tram east.

We could walk.

Dock Eight sent wagon.

I accepted wagon.

Growth.

Quay industrial south edge felt different from market district.

Less polished.

Bigger.

Warehouses with black brick bases.

Iron rails sunk into street.

Chain hoists hanging between buildings.

Steam venting from roof pipes.

Painted warning signs everywhere.

**KEEP CLEAR OF SWING**

**RED LINE ACTIVE LOAD**

**NO OPEN FLAME EAST SHED**

**IF BELL CONTINUES, LEAVE**

That last one was good advice generally.

Dock Eight transfer yard occupied half a block.

Three retired load frames sat on low stone pads.

Each was basically a heavy iron rectangle taller than person, with upper crossbar, two side arms, pulley clusters, and a central brass-and-steel collar assembly designed to distribute suspended load across paired lines.

Not portable.

Not wearable.

Infrastructure.

Yard lead **Bran Tey** met us.

Big man.

Shaved beard except jaw stripe.

Left ear folded from old injury.

He spoke like every extra word cost him.

"Kel."

Kel raised hand.

"Sori."

Sori.

"Vessa."

"Here."

"Greg."

I raised crutch slightly.

Bran looked at it.

"No climbing."

"Wasn't planning."

"Good."

Then he pointed at frames.

"A, B, C. B failed. C same lot. A older replacement lot. Auction wants condition sheet. We verify unloaded movement, latch delay, visible wear, line symmetry. No cargo."

Sori asked, "Failed how?"

Bran walked to B.

"Lateral swing test. Collar should re-center within two counts after side release. Took five. Then one jaw held half count after opposite line unloaded. Crate twisted."

Kel crouched near base without crossing red paint.

"Maintenance?"

"Regular."

"Last collar rebuild?"

"Nine months."

"Lot?"

"BK-17. C also BK-17. A BK-12."

There.

Real failure family.

Bran handed Sori prior sheet.

"You write independent. Don't copy mine until after."

Good.

We inspected visually first.

No touching.

Frame A:

Surface rust at lower left foot.

Lines removed.

Collar clean.

Jaw faces polished.

One stop pin newer than others.

Frame B:

More wear.

Right jaw inner face had narrow bright crescent where metal contacted unevenly.

Center spring housing scratched around cap.

Not proof.

Interesting.

Frame C:

Looked better than B.

Same lot stamp.

One side arm had old yellow inspection paint.

Kel said, "C's left guide sits low."

Bran looked.

"By how much?"

Kel didn't answer immediately.

He took small folding gauge from Bran after permission.

Measured.

"Hair over one line."

Bran checked.

"Yes."

Not huge.

Recorded.

My job became sequence and observation.

Normal mode.

No need locked.

Frame A unloaded movement test first.

Two dock workers installed test ropes and weighted wooden blocks, not suspended high, just enough tension to move collar through designed range.

Bran controlled.

"Left pull."

Worker pulled.

Collar shifted.

"Hold. Release."

Release.

One.

Two.

Centered.

Opposite.

One.

Centered before two.

Jaw open-close.

Clean.

Kel watched line travel.

Sori wrote.

Vessa watched safety boundary.

I watched what changed after release.

Frame B.

Left pull.

Release.

One.

Two.

Three.

Four.

Centered near four.

Again.

Three and half.

Opposite line unload.

Right jaw hesitated.

Not dramatic.

Half count maybe.

Enough.

Bran said, "Matches failure tendency unloaded."

I said, "Less severe than loaded event."

"Yes."

"So don't write same."

Sori looked at me.

"I know."

"For audience."

"What audience?"

"Me."

She wrote harder.

Frame C.

This was reason contract existed.

Same lot.

No known failure.

Visual guide low.

Bran ran same sequence.

Left pull.

Release.

One.

Two.

Centered.

Again.

Two.

Opposite.

One.

Two.

Clean.

Jaw release clean.

Kel frowned.

"Again with side arm at midpoint."

Bran looked at him.

"Why?"

"Low guide may only matter when travel crosses center under changing angle."

Good.

Bran considered.

"Allowed."

They reset.

Midpoint side arm.

Left pull.

Release.

One.

Two.

Collar reached near center.

Then tiny catch.

Not stop.

A hitch.

Then seated.

Kel looked at me.

I had seen.

Sori too.

Bran said, "Again."

Second run.

Same place.

Smaller hitch.

Third.

Nothing obvious.

There.

Intermittent.

Not failure.

Not clean.

Bran crouched beside guide.

"No touching until isolated."

Workers released all test tension.

Bell once.

Red line cleared.

Then Bran opened guard.

Inside, left guide roller had thin gray buildup along lower edge.

Grease and grit.

Maybe.

Kel said, "Could drag."

Bran said, "Could."

Not claim.

They cleaned roller.

Reran midpoint three times.

No hitch.

Again three.

No hitch.

Bran grunted.

"Auction sheet: serviceable unloaded after cleaning, restricted pending loaded shop recertification. Same lot note retained."

Good.

Not miracle.

Maintenance.

Then Bran looked at me.

"Controlled motion add-on. Want fourth copper?"

I looked at setup.

"What changes?"

"You operate hand-control lever for A through three cycles under me. No load beyond test blocks. Need external operator because auction buyer complained dock workers know feel and compensate unconsciously."

Interesting.

That was actually reasonable.

"Why me?"

"You have hands."

Fair.

Then he looked at crutch.

"And you won't climb."

Also fair.

Vessa asked, "Risk?"

"Ground station. Lever waist high. Emergency stop beside hand. I stand at shoulder."

I checked body.

Shoulder mostly fine.

Right hand mostly normal.

Lever likely right-hand work.

"Resistance?"

Bran demonstrated with system isolated.

Moderate.

No jerk.

"Fine."

Sori said, "One copper."

"Professional development."

"One copper."

Both.

They reset A.

I stood at control.

Two crutches planted behind painted operator box.

Right foot centered.

Left crutch slightly forward.

Right crutch slightly back because lever pull would shift torso.

Current-body mechanics.

Bran noticed.

"Stable?"

"Yes."

"Cycle one. Pull to mark. Hold. Release on bell."

I pulled.

Resistance smooth.

Stopped at mark.

Bell.

Release.

Collar traveled.

Centered.

Second cycle opposite.

Third mixed midpoint.

All clean.

No magic.

No cleverness.

Just doing thing correctly.

Bran signed add-on.

"Four copper."

Good.

Contract work ended before third bell.

Not exciting.

Very satisfying.

Nema had been right.

Not mirrors.

Better.

Dock cashier paid immediately against Bran's sheet.

Four copper each to Kel, Sori, Vessa, me because all four had participated in designated controlled sequence/witness add-on? No.

Careful.

Kel, Sori, and Vessa had base three.

I had operated add-on.

Only I got four.

Sori watched coins land in my palm.

"Twenty-five."

"You got paid too."

"This isn't about me."

"It could be."

"Twenty-five."

"Stop."

Bran said, "You two married?"

"No," both of us said.

He shrugged.

"Sounds exhausting."

Back outside, Quay noon traffic hit like wall.

Twenty-five copper.

Vale twenty-three.

There.

Liquid cash now exceeded debt principal-equivalent.

That was not debt freedom.

Vale wasn't payable simply by handing over purse and walking away without terms.

But psychologically?

Interesting.

First time in long while I could look at twenty-three and know purse had more.

Not counting bits.

Not counting tools.

Just copper.

I didn't feel rich.

I felt less cornered.

Different.

Kel nudged shoulder lightly.

"Food?"

"Yes."

We bought dock noodles from street cart.

Bits.

Again.

Copper stayed twenty-five.

Sori said, "You're celebrating."

"Noodles."

"You always hide behind food."

"Reliable shield."

Vessa said, "Annex at fourth bell. Then we're free unless new route comes."

"Free for how long?" Kel asked.

"Until they decide they need us or we decide we're done."

There.

That was new too.

Away arc had started because work pulled us.

Now work had delivered us somewhere large enough that being temporarily free became its own pressure.

What do you do when road stops giving instructions?

Apparently eat noodles.

At annex, Nema took contract sheet copy and laughed when she saw Bran had written:

**EXTERNAL OPERATOR: STABLE, DOES NOT ANTICIPATE LEVER RETURN.**

"High praise."

"I try not to anticipate machinery."

"Good policy."

Then she gave Vessa new regional status.

No mandatory task.

No immediate continuation.

White Span packet accepted.

Bellweather comparison accepted.

Glassmouth closed local.

Crew could:

- remain Quay up to two nights under reduced survey lodging rate, personal food;
- take westbound courier cart back toward Bellweather/Merecross network tomorrow;
- accept new work if posted;
- continue independently downriver with no assignment coverage.

No quest.

No glowing door.

Choice.

That felt bigger.

Dask returned during status briefing carrying three pamphlets and no purchases.

Probably.

I checked hands.

Empty.

He saw.

"Your face."

"Fair."

Nema added, "One thing. Dock salvage auction tomorrow morning includes the three load frames and stripped parts. Public viewing before bell two."

My attention shifted.

Sori said, "You heard auction."

"Yes."

"That's all I needed."

Nema continued deliberately, evil now.

"Frame B collar assembly likely sold as repair-only lot. C may remain whole. A probably operational restricted. Individual parts sometimes go cheap if yard doesn't want storage."

"How cheap?" I asked.

Sori closed eyes.

Nema said, "Could be five copper. Could be fifty. Auction."

There.

Artifact pressure.

Not purchase.

Not yet.

Real market.

Real downside.

Real information now because I had seen exact frames.

Twenty-five copper.

Dangerous.

Vessa said, "Personal time after fifth bell. No one commits crew resources to auction."

"Understood."

Sori looked unconvinced.

We left annex.

Dask immediately said, "Frame B collar could be educational."

"No," Sori said.

"I did not say buy."

"You and Greg together are financial event."

I said, "I have twenty-five."

She turned.

"Why would you announce that?"

"You already know."

"Still worse."

We walked toward Dockhouse Seven through market crowd.

PF-3 finder remained two streets north.

Ten copper.

Tomorrow load-frame salvage auction.

Unknown prices.

Vale twenty-three.

Quietener.

Slate reader.

Twenty-five liquid.

And for first time since leaving Carrow, no one had told me where I had to go next.

That was almost more dangerous than market.

At corner, road board listed destinations:

**BELLWEATHER WEST**

**SOUTH LOCKS**

**SABLE LOWER DOCKS**

**EAST MARSH ROAD**

**RIVER PACKET TO DUSKPORT - DAILY**

Duskport.

New name.

Sori saw me reading.

"Don't."

"It's a sign."

"You're doing it."

"Doing what?"

"Getting farther away."

I looked at river.

Then market.

Then sign.

Home south and west somewhere beyond too many places now.

No assignment pushing.

No emergency.

No reason I had to continue.

Which meant continuing would actually be mine.

That changed weight.

I tapped crutch once on stone.

"Maybe."

Sori groaned.

Good.

Contract had been simple.

Do work.

Get paid.

Four copper.

Cause and effect.

Choice afterward was harder.

Twenty-five copper in purse.

Twenty-three Vale.

Auction tomorrow.

River packet daily.

Duskport on sign.

Road no longer asking anything from me.

Apparently that was when adventure became expensive.

# CHAPTER 407

## THE BIDDER

Auctions were dangerous because they turned not buying something into losing.

That was bullshit.

Very effective bullshit.

I knew this before breakfast.

Knowing did not help.

Sori found me in Dockhouse Seven common room with the Dock Eight auction notice flattened beside my bowl.

"No."

"Good morning."

"You're studying it."

"Reading."

"Same thing when you want something."

"I don't know if I want anything."

She sat across from me.

"Worse."

The notice listed twelve lots.

Frames A, B, and C were not twelve separate lots because auctioneers understood temptation better than engineers.

**LOT 4: BK-12 TRANSFER FRAME A, RESTRICTED OPERATIONAL, COMPLETE**

**LOT 5: BK-17 TRANSFER FRAME B, REPAIR / PARTS**

**LOT 6: BK-17 TRANSFER FRAME C, SERVICEABLE UNLOADED, LOADED RECERTIFICATION REQUIRED**

Then smaller component lots.

Guide rollers.

Jaw assemblies.

Stop pins.

Spring housings.

Two old lateral collars from unrelated frames.

One box labeled **MIXED BK HARDWARE, UNVERIFIED**.

There.

Box.

Always box.

Sori followed eyes.

"No."

"You don't know what I'm looking at."

"Mixed hardware."

"How?"

"You like boxes where seller admits ignorance."

That was unfair.

Accurate.

Still unfair.

Kel came down carrying bread.

"Auction?"

"Apparently."

"I'm going."

Sori looked at him.

"Why?"

"Frame C."

"You're buying a transfer frame?"

"No. Want to see what it sells for."

There.

Normal person.

I could learn.

Vessa entered behind him.

No field coat.

That changed her more than expected.

Still Vessa.

Just woman having breakfast instead of route authority.

"Annex has nothing for us," she said. "We're off assignment unless we accept something new."

Dask appeared from stairs.

"Mirror guild opens at second bell."

Sori pointed at him.

"Also no."

"I didn't ask you."

"Practicing."

Maro came last.

He looked rested.

Annoying.

"Duskport packet leaves fourth bell," he said.

I looked at him.

Sori closed eyes.

"Of course you asked."

Maro shrugged.

"Route board was there."

"Cost?" Kel asked.

"Three copper passenger deck. Five with bunk allotment if space. Freight separate."

There.

Duskport had price now.

Three copper.

Not much.

Which made it more dangerous.

"How long?" I asked.

"One day and one night if river behaves. Arrives next morning or midday."

"How far?"

"Packet clerk said about thirty river miles. Road longer."

Thirty.

Carrow got farther away without moving.

Sori watched me look at the route board.

"Duskport?"

"Maybe."

"Market?"

"Probably."

"Idiot."

"Sometimes."

Vessa poured tea.

"We decide after auction. No rush. Daily packet means daily."

Good.

No artificial deadline.

That made choice more real.

I hated it.

Dock Eight public viewing started before bell two.

Auction yard looked different with buyers.

Yesterday it had been work.

Today everything had prices waiting to happen.

Dealers wore chalk boards on belts.

Repair shops sent apprentices with measuring sticks.

Two freight companies had men in clean coats pretending not to know each other.

A woman with three gold rings inspected frame feet by tapping them with knuckle.

Bran Tey stood near rail with arms folded.

Same jaw stripe.

Same folded ear.

He saw us.

"Morning."

Longest thing he'd ever said.

Kel nodded toward C.

"Viewing open?"

"Yes. Don't cycle."

"Wouldn't."

Bran looked at me.

"You bidding?"

"Maybe."

"Don't buy B."

I laughed.

He didn't.

"I wasn't planning to."

"Good."

That was probably illegal auction advice.

Or friendship.

Hard to tell with Bran.

Frame A drew serious attention.

Complete operational hardware had value even restricted.

Frame C too.

B attracted scavengers.

People cared less about whole failed frame than which pieces could be certified separately.

I cared about pieces too.

That was problem.

I walked lots once without touching.

Then again with Kel.

Lot 8 held two lateral collars.

Neither from B.

Different lots.

One had a stop tooth chipped.

Other looked intact but retirement reason said **UNSTABLE RETURN UNDER COLD LOAD, NOT REPRODUCED WARM**.

Interesting.

Not useful enough.

Lot 9: jaw assemblies.

Heavy.

No.

Lot 10: guide rollers and mounts.

Maybe useful if I owned transfer frame.

I did not.

Lot 11: stop pins and springs.

Generic enough to be useful to someone.

Not me.

Lot 12.

Mixed BK hardware.

Wood crate maybe two feet long.

Contents tagged individually where known.

Four worn guide shims.

Three spring seats.

A brass center-index plate.

Two small counterweight pucks.

One intact hand-sized **load-memory tongue** from older BK-9 series.

There.

I stopped.

Kel noticed.

"That?"

"Maybe."

Load-memory tongue was not memory like thoughts.

Old industrial term.

On certain transfer frames, tongue held last neutral bias long enough for operator to see whether frame returned to same resting center after unload.

Diagnostic aid.

Not active control.

Not power source.

Not valuable alone to most people because modern frames built index directly into collar housing.

Old-life me had used similar things.

Not this make.

Same idea.

I crouched as much as current geometry allowed.

Tag:

**BK-9 INDEX TONGUE / RETIRED / RESPONSE UNKNOWN / NO MATCHING FRAME IN LOT**

No price.

Auction.

Sori appeared at shoulder.

"What does it do?"

"Shows whether old frame returns to same neutral bias after unload."

"Do you own old frame?"

"No."

"Then no."

"Could be bench useful."

"For what?"

There.

Good question.

I looked again.

Tongue was brass strip with weighted dark tip and three tiny witness marks along base.

Mounting holes.

Mechanical bias.

Could possibly be adapted as repeatable displacement indicator.

Possibly.

That word had cost me money before.

"I don't know yet."

Sori smiled.

"Then no."

"Maybe cheap."

"Still no."

Kel crouched.

"Counterweight pucks useful."

"For what?"

He pointed at me.

"See?"

Fuck.

We moved on.

Public auction began second bell exactly.

Auctioneer **Ressa Vale** stood on low platform.

Sori froze.

Ressa looked at Sori.

"No."

Sori's mouth opened.

Ressa grinned.

"Everyone asks."

I nearly fell over.

Sori pointed at me.

"Not one word."

"I have several."

"Die quietly."

Ressa Vale was maybe fifty, broad shoulders, red scarf, voice capable of moving freight without crane.

"Terms! Copper unless announced silver. Deposit immediate. Full payment before lot leaves yard. Restricted hardware stays restricted. Certification does not transfer because you feel lucky. If you buy something stupid, congratulations on your property."

Excellent.

Lot 1 went fast.

Old dock winch housing.

Seven copper.

Lot 2, chain guides.

Four.

Lot 3, mixed iron feet.

Three.

Then A.

Frame A opened twelve copper.

Jumped fifteen.

Eighteen.

Twenty-two.

Twenty-six.

I stopped thinking about owning whole frame around twelve.

Repair shop and freight company fought to thirty-one.

Sold thirty-two copper.

Kel whistled.

"More than I thought."

"Complete hardware."

"Still."

Frame B opened two.

Nobody.

One parts dealer offered two.

Another three.

Gold-ring woman four.

Dealer five.

Sold five.

There.

Failed frame still worth five as metal and components.

Rational.

Frame C opened eight.

Quickly twelve.

Fifteen.

Eighteen.

Then slowed.

Same-lot note and loaded recertification mattered.

Gold-ring woman took it for twenty-one.

Exactly my purse.

I could have bought it.

Technically.

And then owned twenty-one copper of transfer frame I had nowhere to put, no reason to use, and no money.

Beautiful demonstration of difference between affordability and sense.

Sori leaned close.

"You could've."

"Yes."

"You didn't."

"Yes."

"Growth."

"Stop."

Lots 7 through 11 went mostly to shops.

Prices low enough to tempt, high enough to remind me other people also understood hardware.

Then mixed box.

Lot 12.

Ressa lifted inventory sheet.

"Mixed BK hardware. Unverified. No matching frame. Scrap and bench use. Opening one copper."

One.

A man behind me said, "One."

Ressa pointed.

"One. Two?"

I knew man.

Not personally.

He'd bought B.

Parts dealer.

He wanted box because parts.

Reasonable.

I should let him.

"Two," I said.

Sori inhaled through nose.

Dealer looked at me.

"Three."

Three.

Box probably worth three in metal/components alone.

Maybe more.

Load-memory tongue alone might be useful to repair shop with BK-9 work.

I had no matching frame.

No immediate use.

But diagnostic indicator could be adapted.

Could.

Not would.

Auction brain whispered losing.

Fuck auction brain.

I set ceiling before next bid.

Four.

Not because I had twenty-five.

Because four was price where mixed box remained tolerable experiment and salvage, even if tongue useless to me.

"Four," I said.

Dealer said, "Five."

Immediately.

There.

Done.

I shut mouth.

Ressa looked around.

"Six?"

Silence.

She looked at me.

I did nothing.

"Five once. Five twice. Sold."

Dealer got box.

My body felt stupid little loss.

Like I'd failed.

I had not.

I had succeeded at not paying above my number.

Harder than it sounded.

Sori said nothing for several seconds.

Then:

"Good."

"Don't."

"You stopped."

"I had ceiling."

"You had ceiling before bidding?"

"Yes."

She looked genuinely impressed.

Rude.

Kel said, "I would've gone six."

"Why?"

"Pucks."

"You don't know what you'd use them for."

He stared at me.

"Fuck you."

Excellent.

Auction ended before third bell.

I spent zero copper.

Twenty-five survived.

That should have felt boring.

It did not.

I had bid.

Lost.

On purpose.

Different skill.

Bran caught us leaving.

"Good ceiling."

I looked at him.

"You heard?"

"Everyone heard."

"Auction. Right."

He nodded toward dealer loading B.

"He'll make money."

"Probably."

"You wouldn't."

"Probably not."

"Good."

Bran walked away.

Five words of approval.

Basically speech.

Outside Dock Eight, Sable Quay was fully awake.

Cranes moving.

Bells.

Carts.

Packet horns.

River traffic stacking into lanes.

We had no assignment.

That still felt strange.

Vessa gathered us near public board.

"Decisions."

No clipboard.

No authority voice.

Almost.

"I'm staying Quay one more night," she said. "Annex asked if I'd review a route form this afternoon. Personal choice, not crew order."

Kel said, "I'll stay. Dock work tomorrow maybe."

Dask said, "Mirror guild."

Everyone ignored him.

Maro looked at river board.

"I might take packet east."

There.

I turned.

"Duskport?"

"Never been."

"You said you'd seen Lume years ago."

"Not lower than Quay."

Interesting.

Sori folded arms.

"I have to decide whether my records duty ended when annex accepted packet or whether I am voluntarily following idiots."

"Which idiots?" I asked.

"Plural was deliberate."

Dask said, "If Duskport has lower-river optical guilds, I could compare fabrication methods."

Sori looked at him.

"Three idiots."

Kel said, "I'm staying."

"One sensible."

Vessa looked at me.

No order.

No job.

No route reason.

Twenty-five copper.

Three-copper deck passage.

One day and one night.

Thirty river miles.

Daily packet, so not now-or-never.

Home south and west.

Duskport east/downriver.

I thought about Cala.

Tavi.

Lyssa.

Theatre.

Vale office.

Carrow streets.

Not because I wanted to leave them.

Because I could feel exact direction they were from here.

Home had become compass point.

That was new.

I could go back.

Not today perhaps, but deliberately.

Route existed.

Money existed.

I wasn't trapped.

That mattered.

Maybe more than distance.

"Tomorrow packet," I said.

Sori stared.

"You decided?"

"Yes."

"Why?"

Good question.

Not assignment.

Not mystery.

Not emergency.

Not money.

"Because I want to see it."

There.

Simple.

Maro smiled.

Dask said, "Good."

Sori looked offended by lack of argument.

"That's it?"

"That's it."

"No elaborate justification?"

"Would you prefer one?"

"No."

"Then excellent meeting."

Vessa nodded once.

"Packet office before close. Buy tomorrow passage if you mean it."

There.

Commitment should cost something.

Good.

We walked to lower packet office.

Maro came.

Dask came.

Sori came while claiming she had not decided.

Packet clerk sat behind brass grille.

Sign:

**DUSKPORT DAILY**

**DEPART 4TH BELL**

**PASSENGER DECK 3C**

**BUNK ALLOTMENT 5C, SPACE LIMITED**

**MEALS SEPARATE**

**PERSONAL BAG INCLUDED**

**LARGE FREIGHT WEIGHED**

I asked, "Crutches count personal?"

Clerk looked at me.

"Are they attached to you emotionally?"

"Deeply."

"Personal."

"Good."

"Deck has benches. Covered middle section. Two stairs to lower washroom. Crew can store crutches during sleep if you want."

"I don't."

"Then don't."

Excellent transport policy.

"Tomorrow deck," I said.

"Three copper."

I paid.

Twenty-five to twenty-two.

Clerk stamped small blue ticket.

**SABLE QUAY -> DUSKPORT**

There.

Choice became object.

Maro bought deck too.

Dask hesitated.

Not money, I thought.

Guild.

"Tomorrow?" I asked.

"Mirror guild opens second bell. Packet fourth."

"You can do both."

He looked relieved.

"Yes."

Bought ticket.

Sori stood at grille.

Clerk said, "Travel?"

She looked at me.

"I hate all of you."

"Three copper," clerk said.

She paid.

Four idiots.

Kel and Vessa stayed Quay.

That changed crew.

Not breakup.

Not drama.

Just people choosing differently.

Good.

We told them outside.

Kel shrugged.

"Bring back something stupid."

"No."

"You will."

"Probably."

Vessa looked at four tickets.

Then at me.

"You're not my crew tomorrow."

That landed.

"I know."

"If something goes wrong, you make your own call."

"I know."

"Don't lock in because you think that's what I'd ask."

There.

Interesting.

"Okay."

She held gaze.

"Greg."

"I heard you."

"Good."

Then she offered hand.

I took it.

No ceremony.

"You were useful," she said.

"High praise."

"It is."

Kel clapped my good shoulder carefully.

"If you come back missing other leg, I'm charging repair labor."

"Fair."

Sori said, "Why is that the standard?"

"He has pattern."

"One is not pattern."

Dask said, "Statistically, sample size is inadequate."

Everyone told him to shut up.

Perfect.

Afternoon opened.

No work.

No auction left.

Ticket in pocket.

Twenty-two copper.

Vale twenty-three.

One copper difference again.

Funny.

I could have paid five for bunk.

Didn't.

Deck was fine.

I could have bought PF-3.

Didn't.

Still maybe wanted it.

That was okay.

Wanting did not create obligation.

Neither did bidding.

Neither did money.

Neither did road.

I spent rest of afternoon doing something radical.

Nothing strategic.

Walked Quay.

Ate fried dough for bits.

Watched crane crew move six barrels without dropping any.

Bought paper and charcoal for bits because I was low.

Sat on river wall.

Tavi's striped rock came out of bag.

I rolled it in palm.

Carrow object.

Duskport ticket beside it.

Two directions.

Not enemies.

I wrote home.

Not full route report.

Cala would hate that.

Short.

**Still safe. Work ended at Sable Quay. I chose to keep going downriver tomorrow with Maro, Sori, and Dask. Duskport, one day and one night by packet. This part is my choice. Shoulder almost normal. Crutch repaired. I have money. I miss you. Tell Tavi his rock is now farther from home than he is. Tell Lyssa I am telling her because I chose something.**

I stopped.

Read last line.

Kept it.

There was public post west at evening bell.

Not fast.

Good enough.

I paid postage in bits.

No copper.

Then sat until sun lowered.

Lume turned gold.

Correct direction.

Respectable river.

Tomorrow I'd ride it thirty miles farther away.

Not because bridge failed.

Not because Survey extended contract.

Not because strange light needed witness.

Not because anyone needed me.

Because I bought ticket.

That was different.

Maybe stupid.

Maybe good.

Usually same ingredients.

Ticket stayed in shirt pocket.

I checked twice.

Then third time because growth had limits.

# CHAPTER 408

## THE MEDIATOR

Dask nearly missed the boat because of mirrors.

Of course he did.

Fourth bell had not rung yet.

It had only threatened to.

But packet crew had already started pulling the last freight net tight when Dask came running down Sable Quay with one hand on his coat, one on a narrow paper tube, and absolutely no dignity.

Sori stood beside me under the covered middle deck.

"Leave him."

"Cruel."

"Educational."

Maro leaned against rail.

"He bought something?"

"If he did, I'm throwing it overboard."

Dask reached gangplank.

Deckhand blocked him with arm.

"Ticket."

Dask bent over, breathing hard.

"Pocket. Left. No, other left."

Deckhand looked at us.

I pointed.

"His left."

Ticket appeared.

Blue.

Stamped.

Valid.

Dask boarded three breaths before gangplank lifted.

Sori said, "You look terrible."

"Guild opened late."

"Did you buy the finder?"

"No."

There.

Growth everywhere.

"What is tube?" I asked.

"Comparative backing samples. Paper only."

Sori narrowed eyes.

"Free?"

"Yes."

"Suspicious."

Packet horn sounded.

Sable Quay began moving backward.

No.

We did.

That remained one of river travel's little lies.

The packet was called **Bright Gull**, painted on stern in blue letters over older red letters someone had failed to sand completely away.

Long low hull.

Two decks.

Cargo middle and forward.

Passenger deck aft and center.

Covered roof over maybe half.

Open benches along rails.

Small galley window.

Lower washroom exactly two stairs down and four more inside, which clerk had technically described without mentioning how narrow everything was.

I managed.

Crutches fit if turned sideways.

Bag stayed under bench between feet.

Quietener wrapped.

WS-F/9 wrapped separately.

Tavi rock in side pocket.

Twenty-two copper.

Vale twenty-three.

No job.

No Vessa.

That last one felt stranger than expected.

Not bad.

Just space where instruction usually lived.

Captain was **Iven Sarr**, woman maybe forty-five, dark hair cut at jaw, scar under lower lip, voice made for being heard through rain.

She gave passenger rules from center deck.

"Three scheduled stops before Duskport. We do not leave channel without cause. We do not wait for private arguments. If you fall overboard, yell before you become difficult to find. Meals cost bits unless ticket says otherwise. Night deck lights out one bell after dark except marked rail lamps. Anyone touching cargo seals will spend rest of trip discussing regret with my mate."

Good captain.

Mate was **Rell Koss**, thin man with enormous mustache and no visible sense of humor.

Probably hidden under mustache.

Passenger mix maybe thirty.

Traders.

Families.

Two soldiers off duty.

A woman with cages of white river birds.

Three apprentices sharing one blanket despite not being cold.

Old man asleep before Quay disappeared.

One child carrying wooden wheel everywhere.

And a woman named **Tessa Morn**.

I noticed her because she wanted something too loudly.

Not at first.

At first she just watched west bank.

Late thirties maybe.

Brown coat too warm for day.

Hair braided tight.

One small leather case under both hands like it might run away.

She asked Rell twice how long to **Mile House Landing**.

He answered both times.

"About three bells."

Second time she asked, "If river's good?"

"If river's river."

Not helpful.

Accurate.

Bright Gull cleared Sable basin by fifth bell.

Quay noise fell behind.

Lower Lume widened.

Banks flatter.

More reed islands.

Channel markers taller and painted in pairs.

Freight barges moved in slow lines downstream.

One carried timber stacked high enough to offend balance.

Dask read paper samples.

Sori pretended not to nap.

Maro watched shore like someone seeing childhood story he had missed.

I enjoyed doing nothing.

For maybe an hour.

Then Tessa Morn began asking everybody.

Not us first.

Crew.

A merchant.

Bird woman.

One soldier.

Rell again.

Finally Sori heard enough to become curious.

Dangerous.

"What's wrong?" she asked.

Tessa looked at her.

Then at our group.

"My son is at Reedbank."

There.

Not Mile House.

Different.

Sori said, "Reedbank isn't scheduled?"

Tessa shook head.

"Private landing. Half mile beyond Mile House on north bank."

Rell, walking past, said without slowing, "We are not stopping there."

Tessa turned.

"You don't know what I'm asking."

"You've asked four crew. I know."

"Then stop."

Rell stopped.

Mustache remained in motion from momentum.

"Captain decides channel deviations. Private landing is outside route."

"It's not outside channel."

"Landing is shallow side cut."

"At this water it isn't."

"You a pilot?"

"I grew up there."

That changed texture.

Rell's face did not.

"Captain decides."

Tessa looked at us like crew had become wall.

Sori said, "What happened to your son?"

Tessa gripped leather case.

"He cut his leg five days ago. Mill blade. Not deep enough to take it. Deep enough to get dirty. Reedbank healer wrote me yesterday. Fever started. I have feverglass and redroot tincture from Sable Quay. If I wait Duskport, then packet back, that's two days. Maybe more."

Human problem.

There it was.

"Why not get off Mile House and go overland?" Maro asked.

"Road washed two weeks ago. Foot path exists. Three miles through reed flats."

She looked at my crutches then away quickly.

"For me, maybe two bells if dry. With case. At night if we arrive late."

"Could hire cart?" Sori asked.

"Mile House has one cart when axle isn't broken. I don't know."

Dask said, "How old is your son?"

"Fourteen."

Tessa's voice changed on number.

Not dramatic.

Worse.

Held.

"His name is Oren."

Nobody asked.

She needed us to know.

Captain Iven eventually came because Rell told her passenger was recruiting opinions.

I liked Rell slightly more for phrasing.

Iven listened.

All of it.

Then said, "No Reedbank stop."

Tessa went white with anger.

"You didn't even look."

"I know Reedbank cut."

"At summer low?"

"At this draft."

"You carry less than full cargo."

"Still too much to promise turn room if north wind pushes stern."

"Then put me in skiff."

That was new.

Iven's jaw tightened.

"Skiff is emergency equipment."

"This is an emergency."

"Not aboard."

There.

Procedure.

Clean line.

Ugly result.

Tessa looked around like she wanted somebody to say what all of us were thinking.

Fourteen-year-old with fever.

Medicine in mother's hands.

Skiff hanging twenty feet away.

"What would make it emergency enough?" she asked.

Iven said nothing for one breath.

Then:

"Aboard emergency. Collision. Person over water. Fire. Hull failure. Medical crisis on packet. Skiff launch into side cut for private delivery risks crew, schedule, passengers, and equipment."

"Schedule."

Tessa spat word.

Iven's eyes hardened.

"Yes. Schedule. There are people aboard with reasons too."

And there it was.

Other half.

Bird woman raised hand slightly.

"My birds have to reach Duskport quarantine before morning inspection. Miss it, I hold them another day and lose contract."

One merchant said, "I have auction papers that close tomorrow second bell."

Soldier near rail said, "My sister's wedding."

Other soldier looked at him.

"You said funeral."

"Wedding feels longer."

Not helping.

A thin man in gray coat stood from bench near stern.

"My daughter is being sentenced tomorrow."

Silence.

There.

He had not spoken all morning.

"Appeal hearing," he corrected. "If I am late, she goes without family witness."

Tessa stared at him.

He stared back.

Neither apologized.

Good.

Because neither should.

Iven said, "Mile House is scheduled. We reach before dusk. Anyone leaving there accepts own onward travel. Reedbank is not scheduled."

Tessa said, "So correct procedure is my son waits because your skiff belongs to emergencies that happen on your deck."

Iven's face changed slightly.

Not because accusation false.

Because it was true enough to hurt.

"Correct procedure keeps thirty passengers and crew from making every private emergency the packet's emergency," she said.

Also true.

Fuck.

Everyone looked uncomfortable.

I was not involved.

Beautiful.

Then Tessa looked at me.

Of course.

"You were Survey?"

Road gossip.

Maybe crutches.

Maybe Sori records case.

"For a while."

"You know routes. Risk."

"Some."

"Tell her."

No.

Absolutely not.

"Tell her what?"

"That half mile isn't going to kill thirty people."

There.

She wanted a claim.

Not truth.

A weapon shaped like truth.

My normal skill finally became problem.

I could not bound that into clean answer because I did not know Reedbank cut, boat draft, wind, current, skiff handling, captain's liability, Oren's fever severity, Mile House cart status, path condition, or how much delay mattered to everyone else.

Correct thing was say I didn't know.

I did.

"I don't know enough to tell her that."

Tessa flinched like I had struck her.

"Of course."

There.

Procedural honesty.

Human cost.

I hated feeling of it.

She stood.

"Everybody on this boat knows how to explain why they can't help."

Then walked forward.

Sori watched her go.

"That was fair."

"I know."

"Also not fair."

"I know."

Maro said, "Both can happen."

Dask looked miserable.

Good.

He had no instrument for this.

Neither did I.

For next half bell, nothing happened.

Which meant everybody thought about it.

Worse.

I went to captain.

Not to tell her what to do.

Iven stood near wheelhouse with route slate.

"You're here to argue?"

"Maybe ask."

"Ask."

"What's actual Reedbank risk?"

She studied me.

"You believe her?"

"I believe she has medicine and wants to get it to son. I don't know fever."

"Good answer."

"Annoying."

"Useful."

Vessa would like her.

"Actual risk?"

Iven pointed river chart.

"Main channel here. Mile House dock south bend. Reedbank cut enters north side after bend. Bright Gull can enter mouth if water holds. Turning inside is problem. We would need back out or continue shallow loop that rejoins main river two miles later."

"Can skiff do it?"

"Yes."

"Then why hard no?"

She looked at me long enough.

"Because launching skiff needs two crew. Skiff returns maybe half bell, maybe bell if landing bad. Packet drifts or anchors. We miss Gate Nine traffic slot."

"How much delay?"

"Could be one bell. Could be three. Gate Nine after dark can hold us until morning depending freight priority."

There.

Not fifteen minutes.

"So thin man's appeal?"

"Potentially missed. Bird quarantine definitely. Auction likely."

"Could Tessa ride skiff and skiff continue to Reedbank then not return?"

"Emergency skiff isn't passenger taxi and Reedbank has no packet-certified return crew."

"Could one crew stay?"

"Then I'm short one trained hand overnight through Gate Nine."

All bad.

"Could another boat take her from Mile House?"

"Maybe."

"Maybe meaning?"

"Local fishers sometimes run Reedbank. No guarantee."

"Can we know before Mile House?"

Iven paused.

There.

Question.

Not answer.

"Signal tower at Mile House can see private-dock flag board if clear."

"Does Reedbank have flag?"

"Yes."

"Can Mile House signal ahead to see if boat is available?"

"They can ask."

"Can we ask now?"

Iven looked upriver toward next relay post.

"At Marker Seventy-two. Fifteen minutes."

There.

Not solution.

Information.

My favorite drug.

But user wanted human problem, not procedure magically fixing it.

So I asked next.

"If no local boat?"

"Then she's at Mile House with foot path. Or I break route."

"Would you?"

Iven looked at passengers behind us.

"I don't know."

There.

Captain finally had no clean answer either.

Good.

Bad.

We reached Marker Seventy-two.

Iven signaled tower.

Flags went up.

Questions relayed.

Mile House answered ten minutes later.

No cart.

Axle broken.

Of course.

Private boat?

One Reedbank fisher at Mile House currently.

Good.

Boat leaving north before dusk.

Better.

Capacity?

Two passengers plus light bag.

There.

Answer.

Almost too clean.

Then final flag.

Fisher would not take north cut after sunset because channel stake missing near Willow Tooth.

Bright Gull expected Mile House about half bell before sunset if no delays.

Window tight.

If packet lost twenty minutes, Tessa misses fisher.

And river already had freight queue ahead.

Human problem remained.

Iven told Tessa.

Hope made her worse.

"Then don't lose twenty minutes."

Iven said, "I'll do my job."

"Your job keeps getting between me and mine."

"My job is carrying you there alive and on published route."

"My son is already there alive. Maybe."

There.

Knife.

Iven turned away because captain could not afford reply.

Freight queue appeared around next bend.

Three barges.

One timber raft.

Gate-control tug moving upstream.

Bright Gull slowed.

Tessa stood at rail counting minutes without clock.

Everyone felt it.

Bird woman too.

Gray-coat father too.

Nobody became villain.

That was worse.

Dask asked me quietly, "Can packet overtake?"

"Ask captain."

He did not.

Good.

We lost twelve minutes.

Maybe fourteen.

Tessa started crying without sound.

Just tears.

I looked away.

Then freight tug signaled our lane open earlier than expected.

Bright Gull moved.

Not fast.

Packets didn't become racehorses because mother cried.

But Iven took every legal opening.

I could tell because Rell changed deck rhythm.

Lines ready before turns.

Cargo checks early.

Meal service delayed.

No one announced favor.

They just worked.

That mattered.

Mile House dock came into view with sun low.

Small stone landing.

Three houses.

Signal tower.

One broken cart visibly missing wheel.

And a narrow fishing skiff tied outside pier.

Tessa made sound.

Not word.

Relief breaking.

Bright Gull docked.

Fisher was **Noll Renn**.

Of course.

Dask started.

Noll held up hand.

"No."

Dask looked wounded.

Tessa barely heard any of it.

She was down gangplank before Rell finished saying wait.

Iven snapped, "Passenger Morn!"

Tessa froze.

Captain walked to rail.

"Your ticket continues Duskport. If you leave here, packet owes no onward transport."

"I know."

"Reedbank fisher says north cut dark unsafe after sunset. You have maybe half bell."

"I know."

"If he refuses midway, you do not order him through."

Tessa looked at captain.

Anger still there.

Gratitude too.

Neither canceled other.

"I know."

Iven nodded.

Tessa looked at me.

I expected thanks.

She said, "You could've helped sooner."

There.

Good.

Because she was right from her side.

"Maybe."

She shook head.

"You people love maybe."

Then left.

Noll shoved off with Tessa and medicine case.

Skiff angled north.

We watched until reeds swallowed them.

No confirmation.

No success yet.

No clean closure.

Bright Gull departed on schedule plus some minutes.

Bird woman still had chance at quarantine.

Gray-coat father still had chance at hearing.

Tessa had chance at son.

Captain had not broken route.

Procedurally, excellent.

Emotionally, nobody felt excellent.

Sori sat beside me after dark.

Deck lamps low.

River black.

Dask and Maro somewhere forward buying soup.

"You found workaround," she said.

"Captain did."

"You asked question."

"That's not same as helping."

"Tessa thinks so."

"She thinks I should've made captain stop."

"Would you?"

I looked at river.

There.

Question.

All options hurt somebody.

If I had authority and no fisher existed.

Stop and maybe cost father hearing.

Bird woman's contract.

Crew safety.

Thirty people schedule.

Or don't stop and maybe cost boy leg.

Maybe life.

No clean answer.

"I don't know," I said.

Sori nodded.

Not disappointed.

That helped less than expected.

"You usually know what not to claim," she said.

"Yes."

"Different problem."

"Yes."

"Eventually you have to choose anyway."

There.

Fuck.

She went for soup.

I stayed.

Bright Gull moved downriver.
