Somewhere behind us, Tessa was trying to reach Oren before dark.

Somewhere ahead, gray-coat man was counting distance to daughter's hearing.

Bird woman checked cages for fourth time.

Captain watched channel.

Everybody wanted something badly.

River did not care.

Procedure cared only enough to keep system from becoming thirty private wars.

Necessary.

Insufficient.

That was new.

I had spent months learning not to pretend uncertainty was certainty.

Useful skill.

Not enough skill.

Sometimes facts narrowed choices until all remaining choices were cruel in different directions.

Then somebody still had to choose.

No Vessa tomorrow to say which.

No captain for every problem.

No protocol that could carry blame away.

I looked at dark water and felt Duskport pulling east.

Chosen road.

Apparently choice included this too.

Shit.

# CHAPTER 409

## THE COMPANION

Sleeping on packet deck was less romantic than traveling stories implied.

Mostly because traveling stories cut the benches.

And the snoring.

And the child who had been kicking the same wall for twenty minutes.

Not hard.

Just enough.

Tap.

Tap.

Tap.

Sori opened one eye.

"I will throw him into river."

"You don't mean that."

"I mean adjacent to river."

"Better."

Maro had somehow folded himself into corner bench and looked asleep.

Dask was not asleep.

Of course.

He sat under covered middle deck with guild pamphlet open against one knee and lantern hooded low.

I said, "If you blind yourself before Duskport, I'm not reading signs for you."

"This is not bright."

"It's brighter than closed eyes."

"Most things are."

There.

Packet wisdom.

Bright Gull moved through dark water with steady engine thrum under floorboards and occasional chain-rattle from cargo restraint.

Banks had become shapes.

A few lamps.

Black reeds.

Farmhouses rare now.

More docks.

More industrial lights.

Lower Lume smelled different.

Wider water.

Mud flatter.

Smoke farther away but more constant.

I lay on bench with shoulder bag under head because trusting thirty strangers with twenty-two copper and several weird tools felt optimistic.

Crutches rested against chest and rail.

Not comfortable.

Secure.

Good enough.

Tessa's seat was empty.

That was annoying.

Not because I wanted her back.

Because empty seats made absences visible.

Her medicine case had gone with her.

Good.

Oren maybe got it.

Maybe didn't.

No way to know tonight.

I hated not knowing less than I hated pretending.

Progress.

Still hated it.

Somewhere near first night bell, packet slowed.

Not stop.

Just changed rhythm.

Passengers noticed because everyone notices when thing carrying them changes mind.

Rell Koss came through covered deck.

"Gate Nine queue. Twenty minutes if honest. Forty if not. Stay aboard. Galley open."

Gray-coat father stood immediately.

The one with daughter's hearing.

He had name now because Sori had asked earlier.

**Harven Coil.**

Mid-fifties.

Gray coat too warm for weather.

Hands that kept folding and unfolding same handkerchief.

"Forty?"

Rell looked at him.

"Could be less."

"Could be more."

"Yes."

Harven's jaw worked.

"You said Duskport morning."

"Captain said morning or midday if river and traffic cooperate."

"My daughter's hearing is second bell."

"I know."

"Do you?"

Rell did not take bait.

"Yes."

Harven looked past him toward forward deck.

"Can we pass?"

"Not without slot."

"Can you buy priority?"

There.

Interesting.

Rell's face changed one degree.

"Sometimes."

"Then buy it."

"Packet doesn't carry discretionary gate-priority budget."

Harven laughed once.

Not humor.

"Of course."

Then bird trader stood.

Her name was **Mira Sol**.

She had three reed cages under cargo awning, each holding long-necked gray birds that looked personally offended by existence.

"Do not buy priority," she said.

Harven turned.

"Excuse me?"

"If packet pays emergency rate, captain will pass cost to manifest surcharge. I have twelve birds under commercial ticket and quarantine fee already paid."

"My daughter may be sentenced tomorrow."

"And my birds may be refused if inspection closes before arrival."

"They're birds."

Mira went still.

Bad choice.

Harven knew it after saying it.

Too late.

"They are six months of breeding," Mira said. "And contract deposit. And my sister's farm debt."

"I didn't mean..."

"You meant exactly enough."

There.

Human problem again.

Apparently river had curriculum.

Sori sat up fully.

Dask closed pamphlet.

Maro opened eyes without moving head.

Rell said, "No gate priority purchase tonight. Captain's decision."

Harven looked toward forward stairs.

"Then I want to speak to her."

"She is navigating queue."

"I don't care."

"She does."

Rell moved on.

Harven followed three steps, stopped because crewman blocked forward access politely and completely.

Then he turned back.

Looked at me.

Of course.

Why did everyone keep deciding I had face for this?

"You helped that woman."

I stayed on bench.

"A little."

"Help me."

Sori looked at ceiling.

I said, "With what?"

"Tell captain to buy priority."

There it was.

Again.

Same shape.

Different people.

I could say I didn't know enough.

True.

Useful maybe.

Also starting to sound like shield.

"How much is priority?" I asked.

Harven blinked.

"I don't know."

"Then first question."

Mira said, "Gate Nine after-hours priority isn't fixed. Depends freight queue."

Harven turned on her.

"You know?"

"I ship birds."

"Then help."

She laughed without warmth.

"You called them birds."

"They are birds."

"And she is your daughter. Both can be true while you remain an ass."

Maro coughed into sleeve.

Not hiding laugh well.

Harven rubbed face.

"Fine. I'm sorry."

Mira looked surprised.

So was I.

"Fine," she said.

Not forgiven.

Enough.

Sori said, "What actually happens if you miss second bell?"

Harven sat opposite us.

That was first time he looked old instead of angry.

"Hearing starts second bell. Appeals clerk said family witness statement can be accepted before decision if I am present. If I'm not, counsel can read written statement."

"You have written statement?"

"Yes."

"Then your daughter isn't unrepresented."

"No."

"But you want to be there."

He looked at her.

"She's nineteen."

There.

Not answer.

All answer.

Sori nodded.

Mira's face softened despite herself.

Harven continued.

"She hit a dock foreman."

Dask said, "Why?"

Sori elbowed him.

Harven didn't mind.

"Foreman grabbed her apprentice by throat. She broke his nose. He fell. Hit edge. Lost two teeth. Foreman says assault. Yard says insubordination. Apprentice says foreman started it. Three workers say they didn't see."

There.

Mess.

"What is she appealing?" I asked.

"Work-ban order and six months labor levy."

"Not prison?"

"No."

Some tension left room.

Harven saw it.

"That still destroys her trade."

"I know."

"Do you?"

He stopped himself.

"Sorry."

Mira said, "Learning."

He ignored her.

Sori asked, "What does your being there change?"

Harven looked down at handkerchief.

"Maybe nothing."

There.

He laughed once.

"That's the worst part. Maybe nothing. But if she looks up and I'm not there..."

He did not finish.

That was wanting something badly.

Not legal leverage.

Presence.

Hard to price.

Hard to procedure.

Mira said, quieter, "My inspection closes third bell, not second."

Harven looked at her.

"So?"

"So if priority costs too much, don't buy it for me."

That was not same as consent.

Not quite.

She stared at cages.

"But if delay goes past first bell, I need to know. I can send runner from first dock if we arrive south quay. Quarantine office might hold clerk if warned. Maybe."

There.

Another maybe.

But hers.

Harven said, "Thank you."

Mira shrugged.

"Still birds."

One bird hissed.

"See?" she said. "Assholes."

Humanity restored.

I got up.

Shoulder fine.

Right hand fine.

Crutches planted against moving deck without much thought now.

"I'm going to ask gate cost."

Sori looked at me.

"Captain said no priority."

"I'm not asking her to buy."

"Good."

There.

Different problem.

Facts still useful.

Just not verdict.

Rell was near aft rail watching signal lamps.

I asked, "Priority cost?"

"Why?"

"Because people are arguing about thing with no number."

He considered.

"Fair."

Forward signal tower showed three red lamps and one amber.

Gate structure ahead looked like black wall with lit slots.

Rell said, "Current after-hours pass for packet our class likely two to four copper. Could spike six if stone convoy keeps slot."

"Per packet?"

"Yes."

"Who pays if captain chooses?"

"Company account if operational need. Passenger-originated discretionary priority gets surcharge by manifest class."

"Meaning?"

"Split across eligible tickets. Freight heavier share. Passenger lighter."

"How light?"

"Depends final cost. Maybe bit or two each passenger. Freight account takes most."

There.

Small individually.

Not nothing commercially.

"Why captain refused?"

Rell looked at me.

"Because if every personal deadline buys gate slot, packet timetable becomes auction. Also company reviews discretionary passes. Too many and captain loses route."

There.

Another person with something to lose.

Captain's job.

Not just budget.

I nodded.

"Can passengers voluntarily cover priority without forcing others?"

Rell's mustache moved.

"Technically? Maybe."

"Allowed?"

"Private payment to gate office can be accepted if packet master approves and payment doesn't alter freight contract."

"Would Iven approve?"

"Ask her."

"She's navigating."

"Yes."

Fair.

Rell looked forward.

"Queue just moved. Might be irrelevant in five minutes."

Of course.

We waited.

Five minutes became eight.

No move.

Harven came aft.

"Well?"

"Two to four copper likely, maybe six. Passenger surcharge usually small because freight carries most. Captain also risks route review if she uses discretionary priority too often."

Harven stared.

"I'll pay six."

Mira had followed.

"You have six?"

"Yes."

"Then pay it yourself."

"I said I would."

"You said buy priority."

"Fine. I will pay."

Sori arrived behind them.

"Hold."

Harven's face tightened.

"What now?"

"Can you pay six and still afford Duskport transport, hearing fees, food?"

He looked angry again.

Then tired.

"Barely."

"Barely is answer."

"I don't care."

"Your daughter might."

"She can yell at me after."

There.

Decision.

Maybe bad.

His.

I said, "Captain still has to approve."

Harven looked at me.

"Then ask her."

I did.

Not because I knew right answer.

Because he had one.

Iven met us at stair after another ten minutes when queue stabilized enough to spare attention.

Harven spoke first.

"I will personally pay up to six copper for discretionary priority if Gate Nine accepts it. No passenger surcharge. No freight surcharge."

Iven looked at him.

Then me.

"This your idea?"

"Question was mine. Choice is his."

Good sentence.

Keep.

She looked back at Harven.

"You understand priority does not guarantee hearing arrival. We can still be delayed downstream."

"Yes."

"You understand payment may be nonrefundable once gate slot changes."

"Yes."

"You understand I can still refuse if passage unsafe or traffic command says no."

"Yes."

Mira said, "And if he pays, my birds don't become reason."

Iven looked at her.

"Correct."

Harven said, "Fine."

Iven considered longer than comfortable.

Then shook head.

"No."

Harven went white.

"Why?"

"Stone convoy is emergency bank repair material. Priority would move us ahead of it if accepted now. Gate office already flagged convoy. I'm not asking them."

There.

Fuck.

Correct procedure had found another person not even aboard.

Somewhere downstream bank needed stone.

Delay that and who knew what happened.

Harven laughed.

"Of course."

Not at her.

At world.

Iven said, "Queue estimate now seventeen minutes. We hold."

"And if it becomes forty?"

"Then we reassess."

"My daughter..."

"I know."

Harven snapped.

"Stop saying that."

Iven did.

Good.

Nobody won.

Seventeen minutes became nineteen.

Then gate lamp went green.

Bright Gull moved.

No bought priority.

No heroic exception.

Harven sat with face in hands until gate doors closed behind us.

Mira put cup of tea beside him without speaking.

He drank it.

People were irritating.

Also good.

Night deepened after Gate Nine.

Some passengers finally slept.

I didn't immediately.

Sori sat beside me on rail bench.

"You asked different question."

"Apparently still didn't fix it."

"That's point."

"Annoying point."

"Yes."

Harven's decision had been clear.

Pay six.

Captain's decision had been clear.

Don't delay emergency bank stone.

Both could be right enough.

One still lost.

Maybe only nineteen minutes.

Maybe those nineteen mattered.

Maybe downstream delays erased everything anyway.

Could not know.

Sori said, "You keep trying to find option where nobody has to be villain."

"Usually exists."

"Does it?"

I thought of first life.

War.

Triage.

Retreats.

Contracts.

People left behind.

Orders that saved hundred and killed ten.

Of course it didn't always exist.

I had known that.

Maybe second life had let me forget because smaller problems usually had smaller exits.

"No," I said.

"Good."

"Why is admitting misery good?"

"Because otherwise you'll keep treating it like missing information."

There.

She was annoying when correct.

"Sometimes it is missing information."

"Yes."

"Sometimes better question changes options."

"Yes."

"And sometimes every option still sucks."

"Congratulations."

"I hate this lesson."

"Good lessons usually rude."

Maro spoke from bench across aisle.

Eyes closed.

"Bad lessons too."

Sori looked over.

"Were you awake?"

"Unfortunately."

Dask said from floor near cargo wall, "I was also awake."

"Nobody asked."

"I know."

Progress.

Eventually I slept.

Not well.

Packet sleeping came in pieces.

Engine vibration.

Cold rail against shoulder.

Someone stepping around crutch tip.

A baby crying once then stopping.

Bird hissing in dream.

At some point river widened enough that packet movement changed.

Less bank echo.

More open-water roll.

I woke before dawn.

Maro was already awake.

Of course.

He stood at rail with cup.

"Duskport?" I asked.

"Not yet."

East ahead was gray.

Lower Lume had broadened into something almost lake-like between low banks.

Freight silhouettes moved in distance.

Tall masts.

Long barges.

A line of lights far downstream that could have been town or industrial docks.

Maybe both.

Harven stood forward near passenger boundary.

Still awake too.

I joined him.

He didn't look at me.

"How late are we?"

"Rell says maybe twelve minutes off schedule now."

"Could make it."

"Yes."

He nodded.

Then:

"I shouldn't have said birds."

"Probably not."

"I don't care about birds."

"I know."

He looked at me sharply.

I almost apologized for phrase.

Then didn't.

"That's problem," he said. "Everyone cares about one thing most. Then boat acts like all things weigh same."

There.

"Boat has to carry all of them," I said.

"I know."

He smiled without humor.

"See?"

Fair.

We stood quiet.

After a while he said, "If I miss hearing, she'll know I tried."

"Probably."

"You people love probably too?"

"Deeply."

That got tiny laugh.

Good.

Not forgiveness.

Not needed.

When physical sun began lifting east, river reflected it correctly.

No wrong dawn.

Nobody cared.

Good.

For once light could just be light.

Then packet horn sounded twice.

Rell walked deck.

"Duskport outer basin in one bell. South quay first. North quay after. Passengers with hearings, inspections, and other reasons to ruin my morning should gather bags now."

Harven moved immediately.

Mira too.

They looked at each other.

Harven said, "Quarantine third bell?"

"Yes."

"If we dock south first, your office north?"

"East quarantine pier."

He thought.

"I know court runner near south gate. If I make hearing, I can send boy east with arrival notice."

Mira stared.

"Why?"

"Because they're birds."

She looked offended.

Then laughed.

"Asshole."

"Apparently."

There.

People disagreeing did not mean people stayed enemies.

Useful.

Bright Gull rounded long mud point.

Duskport appeared.

Not as city all at once.

First cranes.

Then towers.

Then roofs stacked behind water.

Then ships.

Actual ships.

Not river packets.

Deep-hulled coastal vessels moored beyond lower quays.

Masts like stripped forest.

Warehouses five stories high.

Smoke stacks.

Temple roof with green tiles.

A market canopy running nearly quarter mile along water.

And farther east, beyond basin mouth, something gray-blue and enormous.

Sea.

I stopped breathing for one second.

Not because never seen sea.

First life had.

Because second life had not.

Carrow felt impossibly inland suddenly.

Sori came beside me.

"Your face."

"Shut up."

"That's sea."

"I know."

"You look surprised."

"I am allowed."

Dask pushed between us.

"Duskport mirror district is near east quay."

We both looked at him.

"What?"

Maro laughed.

Bright Gull sounded horn again.

South quay workers waved signal flags.

Harven held handkerchief so tightly knuckles went white.

Mira checked bird cage straps.

Everyone wanted something.

Duskport didn't care.

Good city already.

I had twenty-two copper.

A debt.

A bag of tools.

Three companions who had chosen same boat for different reasons.

A home west.

An unknown boy behind us with fever.

A father beside me trying to reach daughter before second bell.

A bird trader trying not to lose farm contract.

And sea ahead.

Adventure, apparently, was not one big problem.

It was thirty small lives all insisting theirs mattered most.

Hard to argue with any of them.

# CHAPTER 410

## THE PROSPECT

Duskport had stairs.

Of course it did.

The sea had apparently not improved civilization.

Bright Gull tied south quay eight minutes before second bell.

Harven Coil was standing before gangplank touched stone.

Captain Iven pointed at him.

"You fall in, hearing gets worse."

"Understood."

"Wait."

He waited.

Barely.

Mira Sol had both bird cages ready.

One bird made noise like old hinge.

"Shut up," she told it.

It did not.

Rell Koss got freight lane clear.

Gangplank down.

Harven went first after crew.

Not running.

Fast enough to make running jealous.

Mira followed with porter and cages.

At bottom Harven turned.

"Quarantine runner. South gate?"

"Blue arch," Mira said.

"I remember."

"You better."

Then he disappeared into Duskport.

No ending.

Good.

Or terrible.

Same thing sometimes.

Mira looked at me before leaving.

"If you want word about the boy, packet office posts notices from upriver."

"Good to know."

Fucking face had traveled thirty miles and developed reputation.

Then she left.

Iven stood by rail while passengers emptied.

I offered hand.

"Thanks."

"For what?"

"Not killing us."

"Low standard."

"Reliable one."

She shook once.

"Duskport packet office is Pier Four if you need westbound. Bright Gull returns tomorrow afternoon unless freight changes."

There.

Return route.

I liked knowing.

Didn't mean taking.

Maro came down behind me.

Dask had already stopped halfway down gangplank because he could see something reflective across basin.

Sori shoved him gently between shoulders.

"Move."

"Those are signal mirrors."

"Move while knowing that."

We entered Duskport.

First thing city did was almost run me over with fish.

Cart.

Fish cart.

Important distinction.

Driver yelled.

I moved.

Current body could move fast enough for fish.

Progress.

South quay was not a street.

It was six streets arguing over same stone.

Porters.

Carts.

Rope sellers.

Customs runners.

Fishmongers.

Sailors carrying things that should have required three sailors.

Children with message tubes.

Three languages in first hundred yards, one I knew poorly, one not at all.

Salt on everything.

Not river salt.

Sea.

I could smell it under tar and fish and smoke.

I wanted to go see water.

Dask wanted mirrors.

Sori wanted lodging before we became four idiots paying emergency rates after dark.

Maro wanted food.

Democracy failed immediately.

Sori won.

"Beds first."

"Sea is large," Maro said. "Will probably remain."

"Probably?" Dask asked.

Maro looked at him.

"For Greg."

I hated all of them.

Cheap lodging near south quay existed if cheap meant relative to Duskport.

First place wanted two copper each.

No.

Second wanted one copper plus bits, shared loft, wash basin extra.

Maybe.

Third had sign:

**ANCHOR'S END**

**BEDS 1C**

**FLOOR 4 BITS**

**NO FIGHTING INSIDE**

Inside.

Important qualifier.

Sori inspected loft.

I inspected stairs.

Twelve.

Wide.

Rail both sides.

Acceptable.

Innkeeper **Pell Varo** looked at crutches.

"Loft bed near stair?"

"Yes."

"Same price."

"Good."

"If you fall, scream after landing. Wakes fewer people."

"Thoughtful."

We took four beds.

I paid one copper.

Twenty-two to twenty-one.

There.

Vale twenty-three.

Back under debt by two.

Still fine.

Bag locked in foot chest with my small brass lock.

Quietener.

WS-F/9.

Tavi rock.

All there.

Crutches stayed with me.

Obviously.

Then sea.

We walked west through market lane because Dask said mirror district was west and Sori said if he vanished alone she would sell his coat.

"You cannot."

"I can sell coat."

"It's mine."

"Market decides."

Duskport widened as we moved.

River basin on right.

Warehouses gave way to chandlers and workshops.

Then shipyards.

Masts everywhere.

Not decorative.

Working forest.

Ropes thicker than wrist.

Anchor chains.

Sails stretched for repair across frames like giant skins.

Magic lived here too.

Not fireworks.

Blue seam-lines along one drydock wall that pushed seep water toward drains.

Wind vanes with tiny charged fins correcting dock cranes.

A warehouse door whose iron threshold flashed dull green when sealed cargo crossed.

Dask pointed at all of it.

I told him to conserve finger.

Then we reached seawall.

There.

Ocean.

No river bank opposite.

No far wall.

Gray-blue under pale morning.

Coastal ships small against it.

First-life me knew oceans.

Second-life me stood on two crutches and looked anyway.

Body did not care what memory knew.

Chest opened a little.

Wind hit face.

Salt.

Gulls.

Distance.

Maro stood beside me.

"Worth three copper?"

"Yes."

Sori said, "Don't tell packet office."

Dask was looking backward.

Of course.

"There's a mobility shop."

I turned.

Across seawall road, between sailmaker and rope-repair yard, hung wooden sign shaped like boot.

Except boot ended at shin.

Under it:

**HARK & VENN**

**BRACES / ARTICULATED SUPPORTS / WORK LIMBS / MARINE FITTINGS**

I looked longer than intended.

Sori noticed.

Of course.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"That's a lie."

Maro read sign.

Then looked at left leg.

Not rude.

Direct.

"Have you ever tried one?"

"Work limb?"

"Peg."

There.

Word.

Simple.

I looked down.

Left knee.

Nothing below.

Crutches had become normal enough I sometimes forgot they were equipment until city gave me twelve stairs and fish cart.

"Not current life."

Sori frowned.

"You had one first life?"

"No. Had leg."

"Right."

Maro said, "Could help."

I knew.

Obviously.

But knowing abstractly and shop existing across street were different things.

Dask said, "Depends socket, stump tolerance, alignment, terrain, gait training, whether knee control is good, whether they use passive foot or rigid end, weight, load transfer, skin shear, and probably six other things I don't know."

Sori stared.

"That was almost useful."

"I read."

"Terrifying."

I looked at shop again.

A man came out carrying wooden lower leg.

Not pirate peg.

More complex.

Leather socket above.

Shaped shin.

Broad split foot with dark rubber-like sole.

Brass side stay.

He handed it to woman in dock-worker trousers.

She sat on bench, rolled trouser leg, fitted it over right below-knee stump, tightened straps, stood between parallel rails outside shop.

Then walked.

Not perfectly.

Better than crutches for carrying crate with both hands.

That mattered.

Very much.

I had two hands.

Crutches usually rented both.

Peg could buy one or both back depending task.

Mobility not replacement.

Option.

Options were expensive.

Sori said, "Go ask."

"We just got here."

"Shop is here now."

"I can ask later."

"You can."

She paused.

"You won't."

Rude.

Maybe true.

Maro said, "Ask price. Doesn't buy it."

Auction lesson returned.

Wanting did not create obligation.

Neither asking.

Fine.

We crossed.

Hark & Venn smelled like leather, cedar shavings, oil, and hot metal.

Walls held braces.

Splints.

Work hooks.

Artificial feet.

Jointed knees for above-knee loss.

Rigid pegs.

Marine legs with wide soles and drainage holes.

Some beautiful.

Some ugly.

All practical.

Woman behind bench looked up.

Maybe sixty.

Dark skin.

White hair cut short.

Three fingers missing from left hand.

She looked at me once.

Not crutches first.

Gait.

Shoulders.

Left knee.

Hands.

Then crutch tips.

Professional.

"Below knee."

"Yes."

"How long?"

"Current body?"

She paused.

"Interesting answer."

Sori covered face.

"Long enough healed," I said.

"Pain?"

"Not usually."

"Skin breakdown?"

"No."

"Knee range?"

I flexed.

She watched.

"Good. Sit."

"I'm only asking."

"Then sit while asking."

I sat.

Her name, according brass plate:

**MASTA VENN - LIMBWRIGHT**

Venn.

No.

I did not ask.

Growth.

She pulled stool.

"What do you want?"

There.

Not what happened.

What do you want.

I looked at doorway.

Dock worker outside walking rails.

"More hands."

Masta nodded.

"Good answer."

"Not speed."

"Speed comes later if it comes."

"I want to carry something without using mouth."

"Also good."

Sori said, "He does use mouth."

"Sometimes."

Masta ignored her.

"You travel?"

"Apparently."

"Work?"

"Mixed. Field. Theatre. Carrying. Survey recently."

"Fight?"

I looked at crutch tooth repair.

She followed eyes.

"Yes," I said.

"Don't fight on first fitting."

"Wasn't plan."

"People say that."

She asked permission before touching knee/stump.

I said yes.

Quick assessment.

No drama.

Pressure points.

Scar position.

Knee control.

Hip strength.

Standing balance between rails.

She had me put crutches aside and stand on right leg with hands on rails.

Then shift pelvis left into padded temporary support block under stump without loading full.

Body understood possibility immediately.

Not walking.

Just line.

Weight could go somewhere that was not right foot or hands.

Interesting.

Very.

Masta stepped back.

"You could use work leg."

Sori leaned forward.

"How much?"

I glared.

Masta did not.

"Depends."

Of course.

"Basic rigid dock peg, leather socket, no articulated ankle, indoor/flat-yard use, three fittings and gait rail sessions. Eighty-four copper if wood ordinary and no rush."

Silence.

Dask said, "Oh."

Good contribution.

Masta continued.

"Travel work limb with split foot, replaceable sole, better wet grip, side stay, adjustable socket because bodies change. One silver and eighteen copper."

There.

Silver.

Actual silver.

No conversion stated.

Did not need one.

More than twenty-one copper.

Much more.

"Marine drainage foot and salt-resistant fittings starts one silver thirty-six. Articulated ankle higher. Charged balance assist higher than that. I don't recommend charged assist first limb unless you enjoy paying me to watch you fall creatively."

I liked her.

Hated prices.

"How much higher?" Dask asked.

Masta looked at him.

"Are you buying?"

"No."

"Then enough."

Sori smiled.

I asked, "Basic peg would help carrying."

"Yes. Also teach bad gait if you pretend it's travel leg. Flat work. Short durations first. Skin checks. You keep crutches."

"Travel one?"

"Better for what you're describing. Still keep crutches. Limb is tool, not miracle. Some days stump says no. Some ground says no. Some stairs say go fuck yourself."

There.

Perfect.

"Timeline?"

"If I take you."

I blinked.

"If?"

"I have eleven active fittings. Three shipyard injuries this month. Two children outgrowing braces. One sailor who keeps breaking same foot because he believes instructions are personal attack."

"Fair."

"Deposit gets slot."

"How much?"

"Travel leg, twenty copper deposit. Balance due before final hardware leaves shop. Fittings over maybe eight to twelve days if you're local and stump behaves. Longer if not."

Twenty.

I had twenty-one.

Technically could place deposit.

Then one copper.

Food.

Lodging.

Travel.

Vale.

No.

Not impossible.

Worse.

Possible enough to hurt.

Sori knew arithmetic.

Her face changed.

No teasing.

"You could reserve it," she said.

"And have one copper."

"Yes."

"No."

Masta nodded.

"Good."

I looked at her.

"You don't want deposit?"

"I want clients who can eat between fittings."

There.

Again.

Human being.

"Any cheaper used?"

"Sockets are body-specific. Feet and stays sometimes reused. I have two salvage feet. Neither fits your load well. Could make something cheaper and worse. Won't."

"Payment plan?"

"For locals with guild guarantor. You're not local."

"Work trade?"

Masta looked at me again.

Different.

"What work?"

Good question.

I had no answer ready.

"Depends what you need."

"I need money."

"Same."

Maro coughed laugh.

Masta leaned back.

"You said Survey."

"Temporary regional route crew. Glasswake prep, field observation, load work, records."

"Records?"

"Yes."

"Mechanical?"

"Some."

"You know load frames?"

"Enough to be dangerous if unsupervised."

"Better answer than expert."

She tapped bench with three-fingered hand.

"My brother runs **Venn Marine Recovery** on East Hook. Salvage diving, wreck lift, quay recovery. They lost condition clerk two weeks ago. Not dead. Married someone in Gullhaven and left with no notice, which my brother considers worse."

Dask said, "Condition clerk?"

"Recovered hardware gets logged before yard tears it apart. Salt exposure. Charge residue. deformation. ownership marks. whether object should be touched. whether object should be left in fucking bucket until someone qualified arrives."

I looked at her.

She looked at me.

There.

"Pay?"

"Ask him."

"Would it cover leg?"

"Not in afternoon."

"Few days?"

"Depends whether you're useful."

Good.

"Name?"

"Hark Venn."

I looked at shop sign.

Hark & Venn.

"You're Venn."

"Yes."

"He's Hark?"

"No. Hark died twelve years ago. My brother is also Venn. Shop name stayed because changing sign costs money."

Sori laughed.

Masta pointed at her.

"You understand business."

"Unfortunately."

I asked, "You'd refer me?"

"I said ask him. Referral is expensive word."

"Fair."

"Tell him Masta said you know enough to answer questions without pretending. He'll hate that less than most things."

That was not referral.

It was something.

I looked at travel leg hanging wall.

One silver eighteen copper.

No.

Not yet.

But now price existed.

Worker existed.

Work existed.

Path existed.

Different from wanting impossible thing.

Dangerous.

I asked one more question.

"If I earn enough, you can make me one that actually fits how I move? Not generic dock peg."

Masta's expression sharpened.

"If you do fittings. If you listen. If you stop when skin says stop. If you don't ask me to build weapon because you fought dogs once."

"How did you know about dogs?"

"You have repaired bite marks on crutch and face of man waiting to tell story."

Fuck face.

"Then yes," she said. "I can build you work leg. Maybe travel leg. We find out what body tolerates before we decide what ego wants."

There.

I wanted it.

Badly.

Not because title.

Not because pirate joke.

Because I had spent nineteen years of second life adapting around missing lower leg and months making crutches into extensions of hands.

I was good on them.

Very good.

That did not mean I had to stay on them forever.

A peg would not give leg back.

It would give option.

Hands.

Standing.

Carrying.

Different fights maybe.

Different work.

Different city grammar.

Stairs still assholes.

But fewer things rented both arms.

Twenty-one copper in purse.

One silver eighteen copper on wall.

I did not know exact conversion here.

Didn't matter.

Not enough.

For first time in a while, that felt useful.

Not enough meant target.

Sori saw it happen.

"Oh no."

"What?"

"You have work face."

"Apparently I have many faces."

"This one's worse."

Maro said, "East Hook?"

Masta pointed through back wall.

"Follow seawall east past drydock bells. Big red recovery crane. Yard sign missing R because storm. Says VEN MARINE RECOVE Y. Can't miss it."

Dask said, "That seems missable."

"Then get lost."

We left.

Sea still there.

Duskport still huge.

Mirror district still waiting for Dask.

Harven's daughter somewhere in court.

Mira's birds somewhere in quarantine line.

Tessa's son upriver with fever, maybe.

World did not reorganize around my leg.

Good.

But my day did.

I stood on seawall with both crutches under arms and looked east.

Red recovery crane visible above roofs.

Missing-letter sign probably below it.

Twenty-one copper.

One silver eighteen.

No job.

Maybe job.

I could go sightseeing.

Could shop.

Could spend day at sea.

Could turn west tomorrow.

Instead I adjusted shoulder bag.

"East Hook," I said.

Sori groaned.

"You just arrived."

"Yes."

"You were supposed to wander."

"I am wandering toward employment."

"That's not wandering."

Maro started walking.

"Depends how bad interview goes."

Dask looked west toward mirrors.

Then east toward us.

Pain.

Actual pain.

"Go," Sori told him. "Mirror guild. Meet Anchor's End tonight."

"You're sure?"

"Yes."

He vanished west.

Three of us went east.

I looked once more at shop sign behind us.

Work limbs.

Not miracle.

Tool.

Expensive tool.

Good.

I knew what to do with expensive.

Become worth more.

# CHAPTER 411

## THE HAND

East Hook smelled worse than south quay.

Achievement.

Less fish.

More rope rot.

Hot pitch.

Wet iron.

Old bilge.

Something chemical enough that Sori stopped breathing through nose.

"Charming."

Maro said, "Working harbor."

"That's excuse people use for smell."

Red recovery crane rose above roofs exactly where Masta said.

Big enough that I saw pulley block before yard.

Sign below:

**VENN MARINE RECOVE Y**

Missing R.

Masta had not exaggerated.

Dask would've enjoyed this.

Good he was elsewhere.

Yard occupied wedge between seawall and drydock cut.

Two recovery barges.

One narrow diving tender.

Stacks of chain.

Winches.

Floats.

Hooks.

Three open-sided sheds.

A fenced quarantine pen with warning boards.

And people.

A lot of people missing pieces.

Not everyone.

Not even most.

Enough that my eye stopped catching it as exception.

Man hauling line with left hand and iron work hook where right should be.

Woman on short rigid peg, thigh intact, moving crate labels with both hands.

Older diver with two natural legs but brace from hip to ankle.

Boy maybe seventeen with three fingers on one hand and no apparent concern.

Foreman on articulated below-knee limb, broad marine foot dark with wet grit.

There.

Not specialty shop because story wanted Greg to find one.

Specialty shop because harbor ate people slowly.

Sometimes quickly.

Winch cable.

Crane load.

Propeller.

Dock crush.

Gangrene after bad cuts.

Cold-water damage.

Wreck collapse.

Sea did not care whether body came home complete.

And Duskport was big enough to turn recurring disaster into industry.

Repair bodies.

Repair braces.

Repair tools.

Keep people working.

Maro followed my eyes.

"Makes sense."

"Yeah."

Sori said nothing.

Maybe because there wasn't joke.

At gate, clerk stopped us.

"Business?"

"Work. Maybe. Masta Venn sent me to ask."

"Sent or mentioned?"

Good harbor question.

"Mentioned. Specifically refused word referral."

Clerk smiled.

"That's Masta."

He shouted across yard.

"TAV! MASTA SENT A MAYBE!"

Man near recovery cradle shouted back.

"I'M BUSY!"

"HE HAS ONE LEG!"

"SO DO HALF OUR CUSTOMERS!"

Not half.

Point stood.

Clerk looked at me.

"He'll come."

"Tav?"

"Tavren Venn. Masta's brother."

There.

Name.

Tavren came three minutes later.

Mid-fifties.

Broad chest.

Dark skin like Masta.

Hair mostly gray and tied low.

Right ear missing top third.

Both hands present.

He wiped them on cloth already dirtier than hands.

Looked at me.

Crutches.

Bag.

Sori.

Maro.

Then back me.

"What'd she promise?"

"Nothing."

"Good."

"Said your condition clerk left."

His face changed.

"Married."

"Yes."

"Gullhaven."

"Also yes."

"No notice."

"Apparently."

"Bastard."

"She said similar."

"What can you do?"

There.

No résumé.

Good.

"Records. Field condition. Load systems enough to ask right specialist. Survey work. Some salvage-adjacent hardware. I don't know your yard."

"Magic?"

"Bronze. Not enough to sell you magic expertise."

One eyebrow.

"Good. Diving?"

"No."

"Swimming?"

"Yes. Not with this setup in working water."

"Good. Rigging?"

"Basic load sense. Not harbor-qualified."

"Ownership marks?"

"Can record. Don't know Duskport codes."

"Charge residue?"

"Observe with proper tools. Not universal appraiser."

"Useful answer."

Masta family trait.

Tavren pointed to fenced pen.

"Trial. Half day. Two copper if you finish records clean enough I don't have to redo them. One copper if I keep any useful portion but have to rebuild most. Nothing if you lie, touch quarantined hardware, or decide being clever is substitute for asking."

Two copper.

Not peg money.

Not nothing.

"What am I recording?"

"Today's shallow haul. Four pieces. Yard diver already cleared biological hazard. Charge status mixed. Ownership unresolved on two. You work under Nali."

"Nali?"

"Condition lead until I replace clerk."

"Then why need clerk?"

"Because Nali is also diver, rigging inspector, and only person here who can read old south-coast wreck marks without inventing letters. I prefer she does those jobs."

Reasonable.

"Two copper. Half day."

"If useful."

"Done."

Sori said, "Do you need records witness?"

Tavren looked at her.

"What are you?"

"Records/legal. Not applying."

"Then no."

Sori smiled.

"Excellent."

Maro asked, "Visitors allowed?"

"From rail. Don't cross yellow rope."

They left me to become employed or embarrassed.

Both educational.

Nali was woman I'd seen on articulated marine leg.

Forty maybe.

Short black curls.

Left BKA.

Her work limb had broad split foot, salt-dark fittings, leather socket covered in waxed canvas, side stay.

Basically Masta's expensive category in motion.

She walked on wet planks without drama.

Not natural gait.

Good gait.

Different.

She carried clipboard and pry bar.

Both hands.

Again.

I wanted one.

Badly.

Nali saw me looking at leg.

"Masta?"

"Yes."

"How much did she quote?"

"One silver eighteen."

Nali laughed.

"Cheap mood."

"That's cheap?"

"Mine cost more. Marine foot. Reinforced salt stay. Employer paid part after wreck."

"Recovery yard does that?"

"Sometimes. Harbor guilds too. Depends how you lost it, where you work, whether someone owes you, whether forms survive accident."

There.

Infrastructure.

"Duskport has enough demand?"

Nali looked around yard.

"You serious?"

"I'm new."

She leaned clipboard against rail.

"Port has three limbwright shops I trust, two I wouldn't let fit stool, one brace guild, shipyard clinic, sailors' mutual fund, dockers' injury pool, divers' burial fund, and a charity that mostly buys children braces and argues with adults about drinking."

"Three shops?"

"Hark & Venn does working limbs. South Arch does fancy articulated city legs and noble children. Brine Street does cheap pegs and repairs while-you-wait. Masta gets sailors, dockers, salvagers, mill hands, anyone who needs body to survive wet work."

"So not unusual."

"Losing limb? Still bad day. Getting fitted? Tuesday."

Good line.

She picked clipboard up.

"Harbor machines don't forgive sleeves, hands, feet, or timing. Sea adds infection. Before clinics got better, more people lost limbs from cuts that should've stayed cuts. Now fewer. Still enough."

"And sailors?"

"Rope crush. capstan. cargo. wrecks. boarding if they're idiots or unlucky. Storm damage. Frostbite north routes. Some come here already missing pieces because Duskport has specialists and work that will still hire them."

That last part mattered.

Not just harbor creating amputees.

Harbor attracting them.

Because adaptations existed.

Work hook.

Marine peg.

Modified ladders.

Harness jobs.

Dock unions that knew forms.

An ecosystem.

Nali tapped clipboard against my chest lightly.

"History lesson costs time. Trial started."

Right.

Work.

Yellow-rope pen held four recovered objects on draining racks.

All tagged temporary numbers.

A: iron cargo latch assembly, barnacle scars, twisted hinge.

B: brass-and-wood signal housing with cracked faceplate.

C: compact black ceramic cylinder wrapped in copper cage.

D: length of articulated chain with three pale glass inserts.

I looked at C longest.

Nali noticed.

"Don't."

"I wasn't touching."

"Good."

"What is it?"

"Your job says what it is physically, what marks are visible, what condition can be observed, what tests are authorized. Not what your imagination wants."

I liked Nali too.

Duskport problem.

Nali gave me forms.

Sections:

Recovery location.

Water depth.

Diver clearance.

Biological clearance.

Visible ownership marks.

Material.

Deformation.

Corrosion.

Charge-screen result.

Leak/heat/motion.

Handling class.

Disposition pending.

Lots of blank space.

"Do I fill recovery data?"

"Already entered from dive slate. Copy exact. Don't improve wording."

Good.

A came from old quay collapse at East Hook Three, six feet water.

B and C from same wreck pocket near **Merrin's Jaw**, twelve feet.

D snagged independently on harbor sweep line.

Nali demonstrated charge screen.

Portable forked brass frame placed near object without contact.

Three positions.

Green dull.

Amber pulse.

Red sustained.

"Green means safe?" I asked.

"No."

Good.

"Green means this screen did not detect threshold charge in this position."

"Better."

"Amber?"

"Some response. Stop and call me. Red?"

"Stop faster."

"Excellent."

A was easy.

Iron latch.

Ownership stamp partly visible under corrosion.

I copied characters I could see and bracketed uncertain segment.

Nali checked.

"Why not complete likely yard code?"

"Because likely isn't visible."

"Good."

B looked simple until faceplate shifted when rack vibrated from crane outside.

I stopped.

Nali looked.

"Why?"

"Movement not from me. Faceplate loose. Could expose interior if rack shifts again."

She nodded.

Moved rack wedge.

Authorized visual from side only.

Inside wood darkened with water.

Copper contact strips green-white at edges.

Charge screen green from front, green left, amber faint right.

I stopped.

Nali came.

Ran screen herself.

Amber again.

"Good. Mark localized residual response. No opening. Specialist later."

Not mystery.

Not my job to solve.

C black ceramic cylinder.

Copper cage.

No obvious corrosion except one dark seam.

Ownership marks none visible.

Charge screen from first position gave nothing.

Second nothing.

Third...

Fork hummed.

Not amber light.

Mechanical hum.

I froze.

Nali's hand came up immediately.

"Back."

I backed.

She looked at fork.

No light.

Hum stopped.

"Did screen touch?"

"No."

"Distance?"

"About two fingers from cage."

"Movement?"

"None observed."

"Heat?"

"Didn't touch. No visible vapor."

"Sound source?"

"Could be fork. Could be cylinder. I can't separate."

Nali looked at me.

"Good."

She put red isolation tag on rack.

Not because red screen.

Because unknown response.

Called **Torrin Glass**, charge specialist.

He arrived annoyed.

Older man with missing left eye.

Not limb.

Harbor still diversified injuries.

"What?"

Nali pointed.

"Screen hum position three, no lamp. Greg observed."

Torrin looked at me.

"Touch it?"

"No."

"Good."

He used different instrument.

Long insulated probe held near cage.

Waited.

Nothing.

Moved.

Tiny vibration.

Again.

"Resonance," he said. "Cage responding to screening fork frequency. Not stored charge. Could still have stored charge below probe threshold. Leave isolated until dry."

There.

Explanation partial.

Not secret treasure.

I wrote exact.

D chain was more annoying.

Three glass inserts, one cracked.

Ownership mark on every fourth link but two different stamps appeared.

I almost assumed repair splice.

Stopped.

Nali asked why.

"Two stamps. Could be repair, reused chain, or mixed assembly."

"Good. What do you write?"

"Both stamps, positions, no ownership conclusion."

"Good."

This work fit brain.

Not glamorous.

Useful.

By midday my forms were done.

Nali took them away without praise.

Excellent sign.

I ate dock bread and smoked fish in bits while waiting.

Maro had left rail at some point.

Sori remained because apparently watching employment interview counted entertainment.

She sat beside me.

"How bad?"

"I like it."

"That's bad."

"Very."

She looked toward Nali's leg.

"I get shop now."

"Yeah."

"Three trusted shops."

"Apparently."

"Harbor destroys people."

"And employs them after."

Sori chewed bread.

"That's darker."

"Also better."

"Both."

There.

Human problem without needing speech.

System hurts.

System adapts.

People keep working because rent exists.

Some because they want work.

Some because sea is only thing they know.

Some because body changed, not person.

Nali came over.

"Tavren wants you."

"Good wants or bad wants?"

"He always looks like bad wants."

Tavren was in shed office with my forms.

He had marked three spots in charcoal.

"A. You wrote ownership stamp partial. Why not infer full?"

"Couldn't see full."

"B. Amber right. Why no probable source?"

"Didn't know."

"D. Two ownership stamps. Why no likely repair note?"

"Could be repair. Could be reused links. I didn't inspect joins under authority."

He stared.

Then nodded once.

"Nali says same."

He put two copper on desk.

There.

Pay.

"Useful."

I picked up copper.

Twenty-one to twenty-three.

Equal Vale again.

"More work?"

Tavren leaned chair back.

"Maybe."

Of course.

"Tomorrow we have wreck lift outside breakwater. Old coastal courier **Merry Wake**. Sank eight years ago. Owner finally sold salvage rights after insurance fight. We recover two cargo-room safes, steering hardware, whatever loose fittings remain. Divers bring up. Yard records."

"Condition clerk work?"

"Partly. More crowded. Ownership tags matter because salvage contract excludes personal effects and temple cargo. Anything marked passenger cabin goes separate custody. Anything with temple seal stays underwater until temple observer arrives."

There.

Human conflict seed already.

"Pay?"

"Three copper full day if you're records only and useful. Four if you also do shore-side load tally and don't slow riggers. Five if Nali signs you for both plus evening custody inventory."

Better.

Not silver tomorrow.

Several days could matter.

Living costs matter too.

"How many days work?"

"Merry Wake maybe three if weather holds. Then East Hook quay collapse. Then whatever sea breaks next."

"I want several."

He looked at my crutches.

Then at me.

"For leg?"

Masta told him.

Or obvious.

"Yes."

"Masta prices mean you're short."

"Very."

"Good. Desperate workers lie. Don't."

There.

"I won't."

"Also don't work hungry to save faster. Nali will notice and send you home."

Masta family.

Annoying.

"Understood."

Tavren pushed form across desk.

Temporary yard hand.

Three-day trial.

No guaranteed hours beyond posted recovery.

Base 3c full useful records day.

Add-ons only assigned and signed.

Personal gear liability mine.

Yard covers assigned protective equipment.

No diving.

No live-load riding.

No unsupervised charge handling.

No salvage claim simply because I noticed value.

Excellent clause.

I signed.

Sori read before I did because she was Sori.

"Fine," she said.

Tavren looked at her.

"You his lawyer?"

"No."

"Good. Lawyers expensive."

"So are mistakes."

He considered.

"You want work?"

"No."

"Good."

We left yard with two new copper and tomorrow's start before first bell.

East Hook looked different now.

Still smelled terrible.

But I could see network.

Limbwright shop west.

Recovery yard east.

Dock clinic behind crane row.

A man with work hook buying noodles.

Nali crossing wet plank on marine foot while carrying clipboard and pry bar.

No one applauded.

No one stared.

Duskport had enough broken bodies that adaptation had become ordinary commerce.

Sad reason.

Useful result.

I thought about Carrow.

Could I have gotten fitted there eventually?

Probably some version.

Maybe imported.

Maybe general brace maker.

But here, harbor generated enough need for people to get very good at specific problem.

Wet grip.

Salt fittings.

Drainage.

Deck angle.

Rope work.

Ladders.

Sockets that had to survive sweat and rain and twelve-hour shifts.

Specialization followed repeated pain.

Civilization did that sometimes.

Turned injury into profession.

Not beautiful.

Still better than pretending injury was rare because it made people uncomfortable.

Sori nudged me.

"You're doing face again."

"Which?"

"Math."

I counted.

Twenty-three copper.

Travel limb one silver eighteen.

Tomorrow three to five if useful.

Bed one copper if Anchor's End still same.

Food bits mostly.

Vale twenty-three waiting back home.

No conversion.

No shortcut.

Just days.

"Three-day trial," I said.

"I heard."

"Maybe more after."

"I heard."

"Could get there."

Sori looked at me carefully.

"Eventually."

"Yes."

Not soon.

Good.

Soon would've been cheap.

We walked back toward city center.

Sea wind behind us.

Recovery crane ahead of memory.

Tomorrow wreck.

Tomorrow other people's property under eight years of water.

Tomorrow workers who knew things I didn't.

I had two copper more than morning.

And a job for three days if I stayed useful.

Peg still impossible today.

Less impossible than yesterday.

That was enough.

# CHAPTER 412

## THE TALLYMAN

Sea work started before sunrise because apparently oceans also hated reasonable hours.

I reached East Hook in dark blue light with Maro carrying breakfast and Sori carrying opinions.

Dask was asleep.

Coward.

Venn Marine Recovery already looked awake.

Lanterns along quay.

Recovery barge loaded.

Divers checking lines.

Riggers testing winch brake.

Nali stood on wet planks with one foot planted on broad split sole of her marine limb and other on crate.

Clipboard under arm.

Coffee in hand.

She looked at me.

"Early."

"You said before first bell."

"Most people hear 'before' and negotiate emotionally."

"I can complain while punctual."

"Good."

Tavren came from office.

"Greg."

"Yes."

"Today you tally."

Four copper possibility.

There.

He pointed to long board hanging beside launch ramp.

Columns.

**ITEM**

**LIFT POINT**

**EST. WET WEIGHT**

**ACTUAL CRANE SCALE**

**CONDITION CLASS**

**CUSTODY**

**YARD DESTINATION**

"You keep shore-side load tally and condition intake," Tavren said. "Nali owns condition calls. Jerr owns rigging. You do not tell divers how to dive. You do not tell crane how to crane."

"Understood."

"If counts disagree, stop handoff before object leaves marked lane."

"Yes."

"If temple mark appears, nobody lifts unless observer present."

"Yes."

"Personal effects separate."

"Yes."

"If you get clever, do it quietly enough that nobody dies."

"Reasonable."

He walked away.

Nali said, "That was basically affection."

"I'm learning dialect."

Merry Wake lay outside east breakwater.

I never saw whole wreck.

Only marker buoy and recovery lines disappearing into gray water.

That was more interesting somehow.

Ship had sunk eight years ago after hull breach in winter storm.

No heroic legend.

No famous treasure.

Courier ship.

Cargo.

Mail.

People.

Insurance fight afterward lasted longer than vessel had sailed.

Now salvage rights finally sold.

Venn had contract for specified hardware and cargo-room safes.

Families still held claim windows on identified personal effects.

Temple cargo excluded pending observer.

Nothing clean.

Good.

First lift came up as steering quadrant segment.

Heavy bronze arc crusted green.

Divers had tagged **MW-SH-1**.

Jerr's crane crew landed it inside yellow intake lane.

Scale called eighty-six wet.

I wrote eighty-six.

Not eighty-five because it looked like eighty-five.

Growth.

Nali checked ownership stamp under growth.

Courier company mark.

Condition class three.

Mechanical salvage.

Yard rack B.

Simple.

Second lift was loose fittings basket.

Third was one safe.

Safe mattered.

Small iron box, maybe knee high, two lifting eyes, one bent.

Chain came tight.

Jerr swore.

"Hold."

Crane stopped.

Diver signal line twitched twice.

Runner shouted from rail.

"Bottom snag."

Jerr adjusted angle.

I watched.

Not my craft.

After three minutes safe rose another foot.

Then something came with it.

Canvas.

Dark bundle snagged around lower corner.

Nali saw before me.

"Stop."

Crane stopped.

Bundle broke surface.

Cloth wrapped around long narrow shape.

Not cargo packing.

Too careful.

Jerr said, "Personal?"

Nali did not answer.

"Lane C," she said. "No unwrap."

Safe and bundle landed together.

Ownership problem immediately.

Safe belonged salvage contract if cargo-room manifest matched.

Bundle might not.

I wrote both as one lift but two intake objects.

Nali glanced.

"Good."

Little things.

Paid little things.

A yard clerk named **Hessa Brin** came over from custody table.

Not my Hessa.

Probably.

Did not ask.

She wore blue cord around sleeve meaning family-claim custody.

"Bundle goes sealed rack."

Nali nodded.

A man outside yellow rope shouted.

"That's mine."

There.

Human problem arrived before breakfast finished.

Man maybe forty.

Weathered coat.

Red eyes.

Not drunk.

Or not only drunk.

He pushed to rope.

Worker blocked him.

"Back."

"That is my brother's."

Nali looked at him.

"Name?"

"Toren Vale."

Of course Vale.

Sori, farther back, physically turned away.

I almost laughed.

Wrong moment.

Toren pointed at bundle.

"That wrap. My mother stitched those blue corners. Eren carried charts in it."

Hessa Brin said, "Claim registry?"

"Filed six years ago."

"Number?"

"I don't carry dead brother's paperwork in pocket."

"Then we verify before release."

"You have it right there."

"And if you're wrong?"

He slapped chest.

"I'm not."

Could be true.

Could be grief making certainty.

Could be both.

Nali said, "Nothing leaves wet intake until cataloged."

Toren looked at her.

"I've waited eight years."

There it was.

Procedure versus somebody's life again.

Different shape.

Not emergency.

Still sharp.

"I know," Nali said.

"No, you don't."

Her face did not change.

"No. I know policy."

That answer was honest.

Also bad.

Toren's eyes went to bundle.

"You tear that open and dump his things on table, I swear to every god in this harbor..."

Hessa Brin stiffened.

"Nobody said dump."

"That's what yards do. Inventory. Photograph. Assign lot."

"Family effects don't become auction lot."

"Unless you decide it isn't his."

There.

He wanted one thing badly.

Not value.

Not money.

He wanted brother's bag back intact.

Custody process wanted identity established before release.

Both reasonable.

Nali looked at me.

Not because I was authority.

Because I was holding tally.

"Record dispute. Do not move bundle until custody review."

I wrote exactly that.

Toren saw.

"You're writing?"

"Yes."

"Write it's his."

"I can't."

"Of course."

Tessa echo.

Everybody knows how to explain why they can't help.

I hated that line now.

Useful line.

Hated it.

I looked at bundle.

Blue corners visible.

Canvas water-dark.

One brass strap buckle under growth.

Maybe ownership mark without opening.

"Can we examine exterior only?" I asked Hessa.

She looked at Nali.

Nali looked at custody line.

"No opening," Hessa said. "No cutting. No contents exposure. Exterior handling permitted with witness if crane lane clears."

Better question.

Not solution.

We moved bundle separately onto slatted table.

Toren stayed behind rope.

Hessa present.

Nali present.

I did not touch until told.

Exterior had blue cross-stitch corners.

Four.

One mostly rotted.

Buckle greened over.

Leather strap stamped faintly.

I wet cloth and cleaned only stamp face.

Letters:

**E. VA...**

Could be Vale.

Could be Varn.

Could be half a dozen names.

Toren saw from rope.

"Eren Vale."

Hessa said, "Maybe."

He flinched like she'd hit him.

I understood why.

Maybe was accurate.

Still cruel word after eight years.

I looked at stitch pattern.

"Claim file might include description?"

Hessa nodded slowly.

"If family filed identifying details."

"Can runner pull file while object stays sealed?"

"Yes."

"Do that."

Toren snapped, "I told you."

"I know."

"Then why file?"

"Because if it matches your claim, nobody has to open it to prove enough."

He stopped.

There.

Not perfect.

Possible.

Hessa sent runner.

We resumed recovery.

Work did not pause for grief.

That felt wrong.

Also necessary.

Second safe came up clean.

Then steering linkage.

Then two sealed brass cylinders from cargo locker.

Portable charge screen showed no active response.

Temple observer had not arrived yet.

No temple seal.

Standard cargo.

Condition three.

Rack C.

My board filled.

Wet weight.

Dry estimate blank until later.

Custody marks.

Dispositions.

Then diver bell rang hard.

Three fast.

Every worker changed.

No shouting first.

Just movement.

Jerr locked crane.

Tender crew hauled signal.

Nali was already toward ladder.

I did not know code.

Maro did not either.

Sori went still.

A diver surfaced wrong.

Too fast.

One arm over line.

Helmet half flooded.

Tender grabbed harness.

Second diver came up behind helping.

They got first onto platform.

Worker yelled, "Leg cut!"

Blood in water.

Not much visible.

Enough.

Tavren appeared from nowhere.

"Clinic. Now."

No Greg.

No hero.

Qualified people moved.

I backed lane clear.

That was useful.

Then I saw problem on my board.

Current lift line still attached below.

Crane load status uncertain.

If emergency crew forgot, somebody might move equipment with live line.

I wrote **HOLD - DIVER INCIDENT / LINE STATUS UNKNOWN** in giant letters and clipped red tag to crane tally hook.

Jerr saw.

Nodded.

"Good."

Tiny.

Useful.

Diver's name was **Pella Ro**.

Cut from torn hull edge through suit at calf.

Not amputation.

Not yet anything dramatic.

Clinic cart took her.

Nali returned five minutes later with wet hands.

"Stable enough. Deep cut. They think tendon spared."

She flexed her own left knee absent-mindedly.

Not symbolism.

Worker knowledge.

"Recovery pauses until line inspected," she said.

Jerr agreed.

Tavren looked at sky.

Money disappearing by minute.

Nobody argued.

Procedure clean because danger clear.

Human cost still there.

Pella would lose work days at minimum.

Maybe more.

Dock injury pool covered some.

Nali said it without speech.

Another reason limbwrights existed.

Another reason funds existed.

Not because harbor romantic.

Because harbor hurt people often enough to build institutions around damage.

Toren Vale was still waiting.

Runner returned during pause with claim file.

Hessa read.

"Blue cross-stitch corners, four. Brass buckle stamped E. VALE. One leather chart wrap, narrow. Mother listed as Sela Vale."

Toren stopped breathing.

Hessa looked at bundle.

Then at file.

"Exterior match sufficient for provisional family identity."

"Provisional?"

"We still need signature and release witness."

"But no opening?"

"No opening required for identification if you accept sealed contents as-is."

He nodded too fast.

"Yes."

Hessa said, "If later there's someone else's property inside, you inherit reporting obligation."

"Yes."

"If there is hazardous material, you report."

"Yes."

"If seal breaks before release stamp..."

"I won't touch it."

His voice cracked.

There.

He did not thank us.

Good.

He shouldn't have to.

He had waited eight years for people doing their jobs.

Hessa moved bundle to family rack under seal.

Toren walked beside rope like it might disappear again.

I watched too long.

Nali tapped board.

"Tallyman."

"Right."

Work resumed after line inspection.

No hidden monster.

No ancient curse.

Just ship broken under water and humans trying to extract pieces without adding bodies.

By midday I understood why recovery paid better than simple records.

Every object crossed three systems at once.

Weight.

Condition.

Ownership.

Sometimes hazard.

Sometimes memory.

Sometimes all of them.

A corroded hinge was hinge until stamp made it evidence.

A sealed bag was canvas until man behind rope recognized mother's stitching.

A bronze cylinder was scrap until temple seal said absolutely not.

That one arrived after lunch.

Temple observer still twenty minutes away.

Diver sent up slate first:

**MARK FOUND / LOWER LOCKER / TEMPLE SUN-THREE**

Tavren immediately canceled lift.

Client representative objected.

First time I met him.

**Sel Marr**, salvage-rights broker.

Clean boots.

Bad sign in recovery yard.

"Contract says cargo-room recovery."

Tavren said, "Temple exclusion says mark stays submerged pending observer."

"It's already rigged."

"Then it can stay rigged."

"We're paying diver time."

"Yes."

"Every minute costs."

"Yes."

"You could bring it to surface and leave sealed."

"No."

Sel looked at Nali.

"Your view?"

"No."

Then at me.

Why me?

Because new face sometimes looked flexible.

"You did records on this contract?"

"Today."

"Does bringing sealed cargo up alter custody?"

I knew enough now.

Not enough to answer broad.

"Contract says stays underwater until observer. Surface violates literal condition."

"Literal."

There was disdain in word.

"Yes."

"Do you think it matters?"

Human question disguised as technical.

"I don't know why temple required it."

"Exactly."

He smiled like I'd helped.

"So maybe it doesn't matter."

"Or maybe that's why it does."

Smile gone.

Good.

Sel said, "You people love uncertainty when somebody else pays for it."

Toren echo in different clothes.

Everybody had line.

Tavren said, "Observer is twelve minutes out now."

"Twenty became twelve."

"Time works."

Sel swore and walked away.

No grand conflict.

Just money versus custody rule whose reason wasn't visible.

Correct procedure won this one.

Maybe rightly.

Still cost somebody.

Observer arrived eleven minutes later.

Temple woman named **Iri San**.

She checked mark underwater through diver slate and surface lens.

Then authorized lift into separate blue-rope lane.

Reason for underwater rule turned out boring and sensible.

Certain old temple shipping seals used water-pressure fracture paint. If broken cargo was lifted dry before observer saw pattern wet, evidence could vanish.

There.

Unknown reason had actual reason.

Sel looked annoyed.

Not embarrassed.

People rarely rewarded universe for proving them wrong.

Object came up.

Ceramic chest with sun-three seal, one corner cracked.

Fracture paint feathered around crack underwater-blue.

Iri photographed/sketched before drying.

Then custody transfer.

I wrote what I was told.

Not story of gods.

Condition.

Custody.

Weight.

By late afternoon my right hand cramped from writing.

Shoulder held.

Crutches were annoying on wet planks because every tally move meant board, pencil, crutch, body.

More hands.

Again.

Nali moved through same yard with clipboard in one hand and pry bar in other.

Her marine limb clicked softly on wet boards.

Not magic.

Not effortless.

Twice she stopped to retighten socket strap.

Once she sat and rubbed stump through canvas because day was long.

Still had two hands.

I wanted one more than morning.

Badly.

Tavren reviewed tally at sunset.

He checked weights against crane sheet.

One mismatch.

Mine said forty-two.

Crane sheet forty-seven.

I traced item.

Loose fitting basket had drained before my second notation.

Wet scale initial forty-seven.

I had copied later rack weight forty-two into wrong column.

Mistake.

There.

Not hypercompetent perfection.

I corrected with strike-through, initial, note.

Tavren watched.

"Why?"

"Wrong column. Rack weight after drain."

"Could've made crane sheet wrong."

"Could have. It wasn't."

He nodded.

Next.

No shame ceremony.

Good workplace.

Nali checked condition entries.

"Useful."

Tavren checked red hold tag after diver incident.

"Who put that?"

"Me."

"Good."

Then family-dispute note.

"You solved?"

"No. Claim file matched exterior enough for custody to release sealed later."

"Better answer."

He counted coins.

Four copper.

Assigned load tally and useful records.

No evening inventory.

Exactly contract.

Twenty-three to twenty-seven.

There.

Four copper closer to silver I could not yet count toward because conversion remained unstated.

Still closer.

Maybe.

Definitely more money.

Tavren said, "Tomorrow same wreck. Weather good. Pella out. Crew short. You do same unless Nali changes."

"Okay."

"Third day maybe quay collapse if divers finish."

"Okay."

He looked at my crutches.

"Masta fitting you?"

"Not yet."

"Saving?"

"Trying."

"Don't rush deposit."

Family trait.

"She said same."

"She usually right when expensive."

"Good to know."

He left.

Nali sat on crate loosening side stay on her limb.

"First full day?"

"Yes."

"Feet hurt?"

"One foot. Hands more."

She laughed.

"Work leg won't fix work."

"I've been lied to."

"Masta definitely didn't lie."

"No."

She rubbed scar line through socket edge.

"You still want one?"

I looked at board.

Wet yard.

Two crutches leaning against crate.

My hands sore from moving everything around them all day.

"More."

"Good."

"Why good?"

"Means you saw bad parts too."

There.

That mattered.

She strapped limb back.

Stood.

"Tomorrow bring gloves. Yard issue pair sucks."

"I don't own recovery gloves."

"Then use yard pair and complain. Harbor tradition."

I could do that.

At Anchor's End that night, Dask had three mirror pamphlets and no new mirror.

Sori looked at four copper.

"Twenty-seven."

"Yes."

"Still not enough."

"Yes."

"You look happy."

"Annoying, isn't it?"

Maro asked, "Good day?"

I thought about Pella bleeding into sea.

Toren waiting beside rope.

Temple chest staying underwater while money burned.

One wrong number on my tally.

Four copper.

Nali rubbing stump at end of shift.

"Useful day," I said.

That was more accurate.

Tomorrow before first bell again.

Merry Wake still under water.

My leg still missing.

Money still not enough.

Fine.

All three were things I could work with.

# CHAPTER 413

## THE DRINKER

Duskport was famous for beer.

Apparently.

Nobody had mentioned this until I was already drinking it.

That felt dishonest.

"Why does it taste like rope?"

Sori lowered her cup.

"Rope tastes better."

Maro swallowed with face of man accepting medical procedure.

"Smoke."

"There's smoke," I agreed.

"And salt."

"Beer should not have salt."

Dask sniffed his.

"I think that's mineral."

"You think everything is mineral when you don't know."

"That's not true."

"Name three things."

He looked at beer.

"This may be mineral."

Good.

We had been told to come here by six different people.

Not one recommendation.

Six.

Pell Varo at Anchor's End.

Nali.

A fish seller.

A porter who had never spoken to us before.

Dask's mirror-guild woman.

And an old sailor who overheard Sori ask where people went after work and shouted, "Black Keel!" from across street like city had appointed him.

The Black Keel sat three streets inland from East Hook.

Low ceiling.

Black beams.

Long tables scarred by knives, mugs, hooks, and one circular burn mark large enough to have story.

A ship's bell hung over bar.

Every wall carried brewery plaques.

**DUSKPORT BLACK**

**OLD BREAKWATER STOUT**

**SALT HOUSE NINE**

**THREE ANCHORS STRONG**

**GULL'S REGRET**

That last one should have warned me.

Bartender asked if first time.

Sori said yes.

He smiled.

Not kind smile.

Then brought four squat mugs of **Old Breakwater Strong**.

"Local," he said.

"Good?" I asked.

"Local."

Second warning.

I drank anyway.

Now here we were.

"Why is Duskport famous for this?" I asked.

Woman beside us turned around.

"Because it survives ships."

She had one eye, both arms, and enough shoulders for three people.

"What does that mean?"

"Strong. Keeps. Doesn't turn on long runs. Water goes bad, weak beer goes sad, Breakwater stays angry."

"It tastes angry."

"That's how you know it's working."

She raised mug.

I looked at mine.

"How strong?"

Bartender, somehow listening from fifteen feet away, called:

"Strong enough."

Everyone nearby laughed.

Fucking city.

This was after second Merry Wake day.

Which had started before sunrise with rain.

Not storm.

Harbor rain.

Cold enough to make wood slick and everyone mean.

Pella was out.

Jerr had one fewer diver than planned.

Tavren looked at sea, looked at crew, and cut target list in half.

Good employer.

Annoying for money.

I reported with right hand still stiff from yesterday's writing.

Nali handed me waxed slate instead of paper.

"Rain."

"I noticed."

"You'd be surprised."

She had different foot on today.

Same socket and side stay.

Narrower sole with deeper cross-grooves.

I noticed.

She noticed me noticing.

"Wet deck foot."

"You change feet?"

"Sometimes."

"How long?"

"Two minutes if clean. Ten if sand gets in lock. Forty if angry."

"The foot?"

"Me."

Good.

Then work.

Merry Wake gave us steering housing first.

Then a copper-bound locker with no personal mark.

Then half a deck winch that weighed enough to make crane complain.

Greg did not become exciting.

I tallied.

Nali conditioned.

Jerr swore at rain.

Tavren swore at insurance paperwork.

Sori had come to watch first lift and somehow ended up helping Hessa Brin copy custody numbers because Duskport apparently converted visitors into labor if they stood still too long.

Maro left after breakfast to see coastal herb market.

Dask returned from mirror guild with a pamphlet nobody asked for.

Normal.

Then near third bell, diver slate came up with:

**LOWER CABIN / BRASS CASE / BLUE MARK / FIXED UNDER BENCH / OWNER UNKNOWN**

Tavren read it.

"Blue mark shape?"

Runner shrugged.

"Slate says blue."

"Useful."

Second slate ten minutes later.

**OVAL BLUE / THREE WHITE CUTS / CASE BOLTED / CAN REMOVE BENCH OR CASE**

Nali stopped.

"Three cuts?"

Hessa looked up from custody table.

"Courier medical."

Tavren said, "Maybe."

Hessa nodded.

"Maybe."

There.

People learning.

Old courier emergency medical cases used blue oval with three white slash marks in some routes.

Not all.

Merry Wake had carried passengers and dispatch cargo.

Eight years underwater.

Case could contain nothing.

Could contain spoiled supplies.

Could contain charged medical hardware.

Could belong to ship.

Could have been personal physician kit.

Salvage contract did not specifically list medical equipment.

Sel Marr appeared within twelve minutes because money sensed hesitation.

"If it's bolted to ship, fixture."

Hessa said, "Not necessarily."

"Bolted."

"So are private lockboxes."

"Under bench."

"People bolt things under benches."

Sel looked at Tavren.

"Lift it. Determine after."

Tavren looked at Hessa.

Hessa looked at contract.

Then me.

Why me?

I was tally.

"Any old manifest category?" I asked.

She flipped pages.

"Merry Wake manifest lists one ship medical cabinet under operating equipment. No serial. One passenger physician, Dr. Alen Marr, declared professional kit at embarkation. No description."

Sel's face changed.

Small.

Marr.

There.

"Relation?" Sori asked from custody table.

Sel looked at her.

"Grandfather."

Oh.

Good.

Human problem had arrived for work.

"He died on Wake?" I asked.

"Yes."

Sel folded arms.

"And before anyone gets sentimental, salvage company bought rights. My family claim lapsed decades ago."

"Eight years," Hessa said.

"Feels decades."

"Claim window closed six years ago."

"Exactly."

Sori watched him.

"You don't want it?"

Sel's jaw moved.

"I want contract executed."

Not answer.

Nobody pushed.

Tavren asked, "Can divers identify mount without disturbing case?"

Jerr said yes.

Next slate:

**CASE HAS OWN BASE PLATE. BASE PLATE BOLTED TO BENCH. QUICK RELEASE CORRODED CLOSED.**

That changed things.

Ship fixture could be base plate.

Case could be removable equipment.

Or ship equipment designed removable.

Still not answer.

Sel said, "Cut release. Lift case."

Hessa said, "Custody class first."

"How? Underwater?"

"We can hold."

"Holding costs me."

There.

Again.

Tavren looked at rain.

Divers had limited bottom time.

Weather window narrowing.

If we spent twenty minutes debating one case, second planned winch lift might die for day.

That cost yard.

Cost Sel.

Cost crew opportunity.

If we lifted uncertain case into wrong custody lane, could compromise claim.

Correct procedure did not hand us clean answer.

Sori asked, "Is there a neutral action?"

Hessa said, "Photographic slate if diver has plate."

Jerr shook head.

"Plate topside."

"Send down?"

"Ten minutes rig, maybe."

Sel laughed once.

No humor.

"Ten minutes for picture of box."

I looked at him.

"You care."

He looked at me.

"Don't."

"Okay."

I stopped.

Not my story to pry open because surname appeared.

Tavren made call.

"Send plate. We lose second winch if we lose it."

Sel said, "That's my salvage day."

"Yes."

"I'm paying for it."

"Yes."

"And you're choosing picture over recoverable hardware."

"Yes."

Tavren did not explain himself into innocence.

That mattered.

Plate went down.

We waited.

Rain tapped crane hood.

Nali adjusted left socket once.

I held slate under coat.

Picture plate returned with case image.

Blue oval.

Three white cuts.

And along lower corner, almost gone:

**A. MARR / PHYS.**

Sel stared.

Nobody spoke.

Hessa said, "Personal professional property. Family claim expired. Under salvage contract, unclaimed personal effects transfer to insurer estate, not salvage buyer."

Sel closed eyes.

"Of course they do."

Sori said softly, "You can file descendant petition."

"I know."

"Different from salvage claim."

"I know."

There was anger in second one.

Not at her.

Maybe everyone.

Tavren said, "Lift?"

Hessa considered.

"Authorized to family-custody rack under insurer estate. Sealed. No opening."

Sel laughed again.

"So my company pays divers to recover my grandfather's case so insurer can hold it while I petition them to give it to me."

Nobody had useful response.

Because yes.

That was exactly what happened.

"Fuck me," he said.

Nali said, "Probably separate form."

He looked at her.

Then laughed for real.

Small.

Needed.

Case came up.

I recorded wet weight.

Ownership mark.

Custody destination.

No condition opening.

Sel stood six feet away and did not touch it.

When Hessa sealed family rack, he said:

"My mother said he carried silver instruments."

Not to anyone.

Then walked off.

We lost second winch.

Tavren paid crew anyway.

Not full extra work because not performed.

Fair.

Late afternoon we did custody inventory because rain shut further lift.

Nali assigned me.

There.

Potential fifth copper.

Inventory was less exciting than wreck.

Which meant dangerous.

Excitement kept brain awake.

Inventory made numbers become furniture.

I checked tags.

Rack positions.

Seal counts.

Weight sheet references.

One mismatch.

Not dramatic.

Temple chest listed blue lane rack 4 on intake sheet, physically rack 5 because rack 4 had drainage leak.

Move had been authorized.

Record not updated.

I found move chit clipped under Hessa's board.

Corrected cross-reference.

Second mismatch was mine.

Yesterday's loose fitting basket had two sub-bags, not one.

I had recorded combined disposition without sub-bag count.

Nali made me fix it.

Good.

Third was not mismatch.

I thought it was.

Safe A seal number looked 7716.

Custody sheet 771G.

I stared too long.

Hessa said, "Old stamp. G has flat tail."

"That's terrible."

"Yes."

"Why use it?"

"Because city bought nine thousand seals before anyone complained."

Economy.

Beautiful enemy.

At end, Nali signed both day records and custody inventory.

Tavren counted five copper into hand.

"Useful."

Twenty-seven to thirty-two.

There.

Five copper.

Largest single day's ordinary earned pay in current life, I thought.

Maybe.

Would need records.

Did not claim.

Thirty-two copper.

Still not enough.

Much closer to eighty-four.

Still silver problem beyond that.

Good.

Then Nali said, "Beer."

"What?"

"You worked two days at East Hook. Duskport requires beer."

"Law?"

"Worse. Culture."

Tavren, passing behind her, said, "Black Keel."

"Why?"

"Beer."

"Good?"

He kept walking.

"Strong."

Third warning.

At Anchor's End, Pell Varo asked how yard went.

"Useful."

"Then Black Keel."

"Why does everyone say that?"

"Beer."

"Good?"

"Duskport."

Fourth warning.

Maro returned smelling like herbs.

Dask returned carrying pamphlet titled **REFLECTIVE FABRICATION STANDARDS OF LOWER COASTAL GUILDS, ABRIDGED**.

Sori had ink on fingers from custody forms.

I said, "Apparently we have to drink beer."

Maro said, "Good."

Dask said, "Duskport beer is famous."

"How do you know?"

"Mirror guild told me."

Of course.

Sori said, "One."

"One what?"

"Beer. I am not carrying any of you up twelve stairs."

"I can climb."

"Sober."

"Also drunk."

"Not experiment."

So Black Keel.

Old Breakwater Strong.

Rope beer.

The one-eyed woman introduced herself as **Hena Quill**, deck boss from north-coast grain ship.

She explained Duskport beer while insulting us for not knowing.

Port had been brewing strong dark beer for generations because grain came downriver, salt ships needed something that kept, and harbor water historically tasted like metal in summer.

Brewers roasted grain hard because damp storage spoiled lighter malt more easily.

Then sailors developed taste for it.

Or claimed they did.

Then breweries competed over strength.

Then Duskport started exporting barrels.

Then reputation became fact because enough people repeated it.

"So it's famous because it doesn't spoil?" Sori asked.

Hena shrugged.

"Started there. Now famous because Duskport says so louder than everyone else."

That I believed.

"And people like taste?"

Hena took long drink.

"Some."

"Do you?"

"No."

I stared.

She grinned.

"But I'm from Duskport."

There.

City explained.

First mug tasted terrible.

Halfway through, tasted terrible warmer.

This was not improvement.

It was strong though.

Very.

I felt it behind eyes before mug empty.

Current body had different tolerance than first life.

Important data.

Sori's cheeks went red.

Maro became more talkative.

Not loud.

Just sentences longer than normal.

Dask became exactly same except faster.

Terrifying.

"The bitterness isn't actually primary problem," he said. "It's finish. There is a mineral persistence after swallow which interacts with smoke character and perhaps salt air perception. If consumed inland it might be different. We would need comparison."

Sori pointed at him.

"No experiments."

"That's not experiment. That's tasting."

"You are not taking beer inland."

"I didn't say I was."

"You just proposed inland comparison."

I laughed.

Too much.

Beer.

Hena slapped table.

"Second round."

Sori said, "No."

Bartender already pouring.

"No," Sori repeated.

Hena said, "Visitors get two."

"Why?"

"First teaches. Second forgives."
