"Does it taste better?"

"No."

Honest city.

Second mugs arrived.

I should not.

Work before first bell tomorrow.

Current body.

Crutches.

Stairs.

Money target.

Responsible.

I took sip.

Still fucking horrible.

"This is worse," I said.

Maro said, "You know what's coming now."

"That's worse."

Sori drank hers and shuddered.

"I hate Duskport."

"You bought second."

"I did not buy this."

Hena raised hand.

"I bought."

Sori pointed at her.

"I hate you."

"Good. Drink."

People around us sang eventually.

Not coordinated.

Several songs at once.

One table sang ship-launch song.

Another answered with rude version.

Someone rang bell.

Bartender yelled because bell apparently had rules.

Nobody listened.

Maro told story about being twelve and falling through greenhouse roof because he tried to retrieve kite for girl who did not like him.

That was new.

"Did she like you after?" I asked.

"No."

"Did kite survive?"

"Also no."

"Successful operation."

"I broke two ribs."

Sori laughed hard enough beer came out nose.

Worth Duskport.

Dask admitted he once spent three days proving his uncle's kitchen table was tilted.

"Was it?" I asked.

"Yes."

"How much?"

"Three millimeters over length."

Sori put head on table.

"Why three days?"

"He kept moving it."

"Because you were measuring his table."

"That affected method."

I loved him a little.

Dangerous beer.

Then Sori looked at me.

"Your turn."

"No."

"Story."

"I have no stories."

Three people stared.

"Fine."

I considered first life.

Too many.

Some not funny anymore.

Some never were.

I chose one where I tried to impress a mercenary captain by jumping from stable roof at twenty-three and landed in manure because I misjudged darkness.

I left out S-class.

Did not matter.

Maro laughed before ending because he knew where story went.

Sori asked, "Did captain hire you?"

"Yes."

"Why?"

"Said anyone willing to commit that hard to bad idea could be pointed at enemy."

Dask nodded seriously.

"Reasonable."

"No," Sori said.

"Historically successful."

"Still no."

Second mug became half.

Then less.

I stopped there.

Not discipline exactly.

Stomach had vote.

Beer sat like smoked brick.

Strong beer plus horrible taste had natural braking system.

Maybe design.

Hena approved.

"You'll live."

"Was that uncertain?"

"First-timers sometimes try third because pride."

"No."

"Smart."

"I've been called that."

"By who?"

"Me mostly."

She laughed.

At some point work left shoulders.

That was point.

Not beer.

Not Duskport reputation.

We had been moving for days.

Survey.

Road.

White Span.

Bellweather.

Sable Quay.

Packet.

People wanting things.

Duskport.

Job.

Peg.

Money.

Wreck.

For one evening, none needed solving.

Sori stopped tracking purse.

Maro stopped watching room exits.

Dask stopped taking notes.

Actually.

He put pamphlet away.

I stopped calculating silver I did not have.

Thirty-two copper stayed thirty-two whether I stared at it or not.

We were merry.

There.

Stupid word.

Accurate.

When we finally left, Duskport air felt excellent because it did not taste like beer.

Sori walked straight.

Mostly.

Maro sang one line of launch song and forgot next.

Dask explained that forgetting lyric did not mean song lacked lyric.

I used crutches carefully.

Very carefully.

Wet cobble plus alcohol plus one leg was not place for pride.

I was not drunk enough to be stupid.

I was drunk enough to know I could become stupid.

Useful boundary.

At Anchor's End, twelve stairs waited.

Sori pointed.

"Fuck."

"Correct."

Pell Varo looked up from desk.

"Black Keel?"

"Yes."

"Breakwater?"

"Yes."

"Good?"

Four of us said, "No."

He smiled.

"Duskport."

I climbed stairs one at time.

Crutch.

Right foot.

Lift.

Crutch.

Right foot.

Lift.

Slow.

Not elegant.

Safe.

Halfway, I thought about work leg.

Then told brain to shut up.

Tomorrow.

Tonight I had earned five copper, lost argument with beer, learned Maro had once fallen through greenhouse for girl, and discovered Dask could make drunkenness sound peer reviewed.

Useful day.

Maybe good one too.

Beer remained unforgivable.

# CHAPTER 414

## THE RETAINER

Duskport beer did not improve with sleep.

That was first finding.

Second finding: head mostly fine.

Stomach annoyed.

Mouth tasted like burned rope had filed complaint overnight.

Useful.

I drank water.

A lot.

Sori was already downstairs at Anchor's End with tea and face of person who had won by suffering less.

"Morning."

"No."

"You look fine."

"I am fine. Beer is not."

Maro sat across from her eating eggs.

"I slept well."

"Traitor."

Dask arrived last carrying mirror pamphlet and looking exactly normal.

"I think bitterness may be preservative-adjacent rather than flavor-forward."

Sori pointed spoon at him.

"You are banned from describing breakfast."

Good.

I ate bread.

Kept it.

Then East Hook.

Final trial day.

That mattered more than beer.

Weather had changed.

Rain gone.

Wind up.

Not dangerous yet.

Enough to wrinkle harbor water and slap loose canvas against frames.

Tavren stood at yard gate with slate.

"Merry Wake delayed."

Nali looked past him toward sea.

"Outside chop too ugly?"

"Divers can work. Crane barge can't hold clean enough for safe heavy lift until turn."

He looked at me.

"Quay collapse."

There.

Different job.

Good.

East Hook lower service quay had lost twelve feet of retaining face overnight.

Not whole quay.

Section.

Old timber crib behind stone had rotted, one tide seam opened, then yesterday's rain and overnight wash took enough fill that front edge slumped.

No one injured.

Two handcarts lost.

One storage cage tilted into water.

And three businesses immediately disagreed over what counted as emergency recovery versus private property preservation.

Excellent morning.

Tavren pointed at map.

"We stabilize retrieval lane only. Harbor Works owns quay. We recover objects if authorized. We do not repair wall."

Nali added, "No one steps beyond red chalk. Ground under intact stone may not be intact."

Jerr had riggers there already.

Pella still absent.

Different diver today, **Oss Renn**, no relation to Dask unless universe hated me.

I did not ask.

Growth.

My assignment:

shore tally.

condition intake.

load sequence board.

Not crane command.

Not structural decision.

Tavren said, "If Works changes boundary, you rewrite board before line moves."

"Yes."

"If owner screams, you still rewrite board."

"Likely yes."

"Good."

We went.

Collapse looked worse up close.

Stone face had folded inward like broken teeth.

Water sucked through gaps.

Mud under surface moved brown with each surge.

A green-painted storage cage leaned half-submerged against remaining wall.

One handcart wheel stuck above water.

Second cart gone.

Three owners stood behind rope arguing before crew arrived.

**Lena Vos**, rope chandlery.

**Perrin Holt**, lamp-oil warehouse.

**Serr Arlo**, courier storage.

Lena wanted cage first because it held imported dry rope.

Perrin wanted submerged oil lockbox before water compromised seals.

Serr wanted courier dispatch chest because it contained route documents and customer deposits.

Harbor Works inspector **Toma Kess** wanted none moved until void edge was sounded.

Everybody had reason.

No obvious villain.

Again.

Toma put iron sounding bar against stone three feet behind red chalk.

Hollow.

Moved back another foot.

Solid.

Red line shifted.

I erased board.

Rewrote staging.

Lena said, "Every minute rope sits wet costs me."

Toma said, "Every minute worker stands on hollow quay costs leg."

That shut conversation for almost three seconds.

Then Perrin said, "My oil box is already underwater."

Serr said, "My chest has names and deposits."

Tavren looked at water.

"Recovery order follows access and hazard, not who shouts loudest."

Lena opened mouth.

"Shout if you want. Doesn't change line."

She closed it.

I liked him.

First problem was green cage.

Visible.

Accessible from water.

Heavy enough that crane needed clean angle.

Rigging team set sling from stable side.

Nali had me prepare condition lane because cage might split under lift.

I set tarps.

Tags.

Two empty baskets.

Crutches annoyed me immediately.

One hand for slate.

One hand unavailable because crutch.

Then switch.

Then brace slate against chest.

Then move.

Slow.

Nali walked past carrying pry bar and tags in both hands.

Wet-deck foot today again.

She noticed eyes.

"Work."

"Working."

"Stare after."

Fair.

Lift began.

Green cage came up crooked.

One lower corner caught broken stone.

Jerr stopped.

No drama.

Line held.

Oss reached from skiff, shifted sling angle.

Second lift.

Cage cleared.

Then bottom seam opened.

Rope coils started dropping.

Not expensive magic.

Just thirty years of gravity waiting.

Jerr yelled, "Catch basket!"

This was not my lane.

But catch basket was beside me.

I could move it.

I hooked one crutch over shoulder, grabbed basket with free hand and dragged, ugly and fast, into tarp lane while Nali caught second side.

Two coils landed in.

Third hit mud.

Fourth stayed cage.

Good enough.

No hero.

Just hands shortage.

Again.

With peg I could have carried basket instead of dragging.

Noted.

Later.

Cage owner Lena crossed arms.

"How much ruined?"

Nali said, "Not my decision. Condition first."

I recorded:

four rope coils visible.

Two basket-caught, wet.

One mud-contact.

One cage-retained.

No assumption about sale loss.

Lena hated that.

"They're wet."

"Yes."

"Write ruined."

"I can't establish ruined."

"You can see them."

"I can see wet."

She looked ready to murder epistemology.

Nali said, "He writes wet. You argue value with insurer."

Lena swore.

Fair.

Then oil lockbox.

Perrin's.

Smaller.

Submerged under collapsed cart frame.

Oss found it.

But before lift, charge screen from skiff gave faint amber on latch.

Perrin said, "It's lamp oil. No charge."

Nali said, "Box disagrees."

"It has anti-spark seal."

There.

Useful.

Not dangerous magic necessarily.

Anti-spark seal could retain tiny charge.

Nali changed handling class to low residual known-use if mark matched.

Mark did.

Lift approved with no opening.

I learned local hazard vocabulary without pretending surprise.

Then Serr's courier chest.

Divers couldn't find it.

That became problem.

Serr had seen it at midnight before collapse.

Said chained to inner post.

Oss found post.

Chain cut.

Chest absent.

Current could have moved it.

Could be under debris.

Could have been removed before collapse.

Could have drifted into basin.

Serr's face changed.

"It was there."

Toma said, "I believe you. Doesn't make it here now."

"There are deposits in it."

"How much?" Sori asked.

She had come after noon with Maro, mostly to see if I still had head.

Serr looked at her.

"Eleven customers. Fourteen copper total. Documents more important."

Not huge by Duskport market.

Huge if customers thought courier stole money.

He wanted full search immediately.

Tavren had contracted stabilization recovery until third bell.

Full basin search would mean extra diver time, likely push Merry Wake turn window, cost crew, maybe no result.

Again.

Human wants.

Correct procedure had no clean answer.

Serr said, "I'll pay."

Tavren said, "Not about only pay."

"Then what?"

"Diver limit. Current. Other booked work."

"So my customers lose deposits because you're busy?"

Tavren did not flinch.

"Maybe."

Serr laughed bitter.

"You all love maybe."

That hit.

Tessa.

Same line shape.

Different person.

I hated it.

Sori saw me notice.

I looked at collapse map.

Not for clever answer.

For actual options.

"Can Harbor Works sweep downstream grates?" I asked Toma.

"Already scheduled after wall secure."

"Today?"

"Likely before dusk."

"Can recovery mark chest description to them?"

"Yes."

"Can East Basin watch post?"

Tavren said, "Harbor watch can circulate missing property notice."

Serr said, "That's not search."

"No," I said.

I did not pretend.

"It's not."

He looked at me.

"Then don't sell it like one."

"I'm not."

Silence.

Better.

Tavren chose:

No extended diver search.

Current too poor near collapsed face.

One additional ten-minute sweep within safe lane only.

Then stop.

Harbor Works grate sweep + watch notice + recovery description.

Serr hated it.

Accepted because what else.

Ten minutes found nothing.

That stayed unresolved.

Good.

Around second bell, wind shifted.

Crane barge outside breakwater signaled Merry Wake window opening early.

Tavren had choice.

Keep all crew on quay collapse through third bell.

Or split and send recovery barge team to wreck now.

Short crew because Pella out.

Jerr said he could take three riggers plus Oss.

Nali said condition intake at yard could handle delayed returns.

Tavren looked at me.

"You can hold quay board with Toma?"

There.

More responsibility.

Not structural authority.

Not crane.

Board.

Records.

Owner sequence.

"Yes."

He looked at Nali.

She nodded.

"He can."

That meant something.

Tavren split crew.

He went Merry Wake with Jerr.

Nali stayed half bell, then returned yard for incoming.

I stayed quay with Toma and two recovery hands.

First time Tavren was not physically there.

No Vessa.

No Tavren.

Toma owned safety boundary.

I owned recovery board/tally.

Easy.

Until wall moved.

Not collapse.

Sound.

Deep wooden crack under stone.

Toma's head snapped.

"Back."

Everybody moved.

One owner didn't.

Perrin stepped toward oil box staging because he thought box was slipping.

I said, "Back."

He said, "It's moving."

"Back."

He took another step.

I did not lock in fully.

Didn't need retirement fund.

Just louder.

"Perrin. Back now."

Something in voice worked.

He stopped.

Toma grabbed his coat and pulled him behind second line.

Three seconds later one intact-looking paving slab dropped six inches into void.

Not dramatic explosion.

Worse.

Proof.

Perrin went white.

His oil box had not moved.

Shadow had.

Toma re-marked boundary another six feet.

Recovery halted.

That killed last cart attempt.

Perrin said nothing.

Then, quietly:

"Thank you."

I said, "Toma heard it."

"You yelled."

"Also true."

No sermon.

Work resumed only after Toma approved alternate lane.

We finished what could finish.

At third bell, Tavren returned wet and tired.

Merry Wake had produced second safe and one steering linkage.

No injuries.

Good.

He checked quay board.

Read every correction.

Serr missing chest notice.

Boundary shifts.

Canceled cart lift.

Perrin incident.

Green cage partial loss.

Then looked at me.

"You change safety line yourself?"

"No. Toma did. I rewrote board after."

"Tell owner ruined rope?"

"No. Wet/mud contact only."

"Tell courier chest stolen?"

"No. Missing after collapse."

"Direct Perrin?"

"Told him back after Toma called back."

Toma said, "He listened second time."

"Eventually," I said.

Tavren nodded.

That was review.

Then pay.

No custody inventory tonight.

Four copper.

Thirty-two to thirty-six.

There.

Trial total had become:

Two half-day.

Four first full.

Five second.

Four third.

Fifteen copper earned at Venn Marine Recovery.

Not fortune.

Real.

Tavren put coin pouch away.

"Trial done."

There.

I waited.

Didn't sell myself.

He hated that probably less.

"You staying Duskport?"

"For now."

"How long?"

"At least long enough to make bad financial decisions about leg."

Nali, beside him, snorted.

Tavren looked at left knee.

"Masta?"

"Yes."

"Travel?"

"Probably."

"Expensive."

"Yes."

"Good."

I blinked.

"Good?"

"Cheap limb that fails under load costs more."

Fair.

Then offer.

Not permanent.

Not guild.

Not charity.

**Open temporary recovery hand, condition/tally class.**

Five-day block.

Minimum three full assigned days if weather/work supports.

Base 3c useful records day.

4c when load tally assigned.

5c with signed evening custody inventory.

Same restrictions.

No diving.

No rigging authority.

No salvage claim rights.

If yard has no work, no pay.

If Greg leaves for fitting appointment, tell them before morning board if possible.

Tavren said, "You're useful."

Vessa phrase.

Different person.

Same high praise.

"I'll take it."

"Starts tomorrow second bell. Merry Wake if sea holds, East Hook inventory if not."

"Second bell?"

"You worked three early days. Sleep."

Beautiful employer.

Nali said, "Also Masta wants you tomorrow before first bell."

I looked at her.

"Why?"

"She sent runner. Said if you're serious, she wants proper measurements before swelling patterns change with more yard work."

There.

Not deposit.

Not fitting start.

Measurements.

"Cost?"

"Assessment included if you eventually buy. One bit if you waste her morning."

"That's fair."

Sori appeared from behind stacked cage.

"You're going."

"Apparently."

"Good."

I counted current money mentally.

Thirty-six copper.

Vale twenty-three.

Travel leg one silver plus eighteen.

Still far.

But now there was open work block.

And Masta wanted real measurements.

That changed target from fantasy shopping to project.

Dangerous.

Good.

We walked back toward Anchor's End.

Duskport smelled like salt, tar, and brewery smoke.

Somewhere Black Keel existed.

I did not go.

Growth.

Maro asked, "Beer?"

"No."

Dask said, "Could compare another label."

"No."

Sori said, "Miracle."

I looked down at crutches.

Then at hands.

Both occupied.

Tomorrow morning Masta would measure what could replace some of that occupation.

Not leg.

Tool.

Option.

Then second bell, back to work.

I liked shape of it.

Earn.

Measure.

Work.

Save.

Maybe fit.

Not dramatic.

Serious.

For first time since Duskport, I had something close to plan.

Naturally, that made me suspicious.

# CHAPTER 415

## THE FITTING

Masta Venn measured everything.

That included things I had not known could be measured.

"Stand straight."

"I am."

"No. You're standing crutch-straight."

"Different?"

"Very."

She tapped left hip.

"You load away from this side before you think about it."

"Reasonable."

"Useful. Also problem."

There.

Morning.

Before first bell.

Hark & Venn smelled like leather and hot glue and whatever oil Masta used on brass fittings.

I had eaten.

She asked.

Apparently that mattered.

I had also not had beer.

She did not ask.

Probably because Duskport could smell its own crimes.

Masta had cleared long bench and brought out strips of cloth, wax pencil, calipers, leather tape, a wooden measuring fork, two temporary support blocks, and one expression that suggested I was furniture with opinions.

"Pants up."

I rolled left leg cloth above knee.

She looked at stump.

"Any soreness from yard?"

"No skin breakdown. Mild pressure after long crutch day."

"Where?"

I pointed.

Lateral upper edge.

She marked it.

"Scar sensitivity?"

"Low."

"End pressure?"

"Depends."

"Good answer."

She pressed gently.

Different places.

Asked each time.

No ceremony.

No sympathy face.

Good.

Circumference at several heights.

Length from knee line.

Knee flexion.

Extension.

Hip line.

Pelvis level.

Right leg length.

Crutch height.

Even shoe sole thickness.

"Why shoe?"

"Because if I build left side to imaginary floor and right boot adds half inch, you walk like roof."

"Fair."

She had me stand between rails.

Right foot centered.

Hands lightly on rails.

Then she placed padded block under left stump and raised it by thin wooden plates.

One.

Two.

Three.

"Tell me when pelvis feels level."

"That's not reliable."

"Exactly. Tell me anyway."

I hated craft people.

"There."

She looked.

"You're wrong."

"Excellent test."

"You think level is low left because that's your normal."

There.

That landed.

She raised one more plate.

"Now?"

"Too high."

"Good."

Dropped half.

"Now?"

I waited.

Body argued.

Then settled.

"Strange."

"That means close."

She checked hips with measuring bar.

"Close."

Interesting.

Current body had spent years building a map around absence.

A fitted leg would not simply add support.

It would force map to change.

Masta wrote numbers.

"You overprotect left side."

"I have no foot there."

"Yes. That's why."

"Insightful."

"Pay me silver and I have more."

I liked her less.

She moved on.

Temporary socket shell next.

Not finished socket.

Open leather-and-canvas cup with straps.

"This is not fitting."

"You keep saying that."

"Because people hear 'measurement' and think they're walking by lunch."

"I'm not people."

"Worse."

She wrapped stump in thin sock, seated temporary cup, tightened straps one at time.

Pressure.

Odd.

Not pain.

Contained.

That was best word.

I had spent years with air below knee.

Now something held shape around it.

Body disliked novelty.

Not exactly fear.

Attention.

Every nerve suddenly had meeting.

"Pain?"

"No."

"Numbness?"

"No."

"Burning?"

"No."

"Pressure?"

"Yes."

"Where?"

I pointed.

She marked shell.

Loosened.

Shifted pad.

Retightened.

"Better?"

"Different."

"Useful word."

Then support post.

Just plain adjustable wooden shaft under socket.

No foot.

No finished hardware.

She locked it to rail fixture so it could not slide.

"Load ten percent."

"How do I know ten?"

"You don't. That's why I watch."

Great.

I shifted.

Right leg immediately tried to keep almost everything.

"More left."

I shifted.

"Too much shoulder."

"Shoulders aren't on leg."

"Your whole body is on leg."

There.

Again.

I relaxed arms.

Shifted pelvis.

Pressure moved down temporary socket.

Not bad.

Not comfortable.

Real.

"Hold."

I held.

Five breaths.

Ten.

Fifteen.

Muscles I used constantly still found new complaint.

Left hip working differently.

Lower back adjusting.

Right calf relaxing slightly.

Hands doing almost nothing.

That was the thing.

Hands.

Both free on rails rather than bearing body.

I lifted right hand completely.

Then left.

Masta immediately said, "Don't perform."

"I wasn't."

"You were about to."

Probably.

I put hands back.

She had seen enough.

"Travel leg, yes."

There.

Simple.

"Not basic peg?"

"You could use one. Wrong tool if you plan roads, wet yards, ships, stairs, carrying, fighting, whatever nonsense face says."

"Face says nothing."

"Face says expensive mistakes."

Rude city.

She pulled stool.

"Your main problems aren't stump length or knee. Those are good. Your problem is habit. You unload left side before danger exists. Pelvis sits a little low left when tired. Right side overworks. Crutches reinforce some of that because they make it functional."

"So leg fixes it."

"No."

Of course.

"Leg gives you chance to train different pattern. If you wear badly, you can build worse pattern faster."

"Good."

"Also socket volume will change during day. Yard swelling. Heat. Salt. Long standing. First month means adjustments."

"How many?"

"Enough."

"Helpful."

"Could be three. Could be nine."

There.

Not product.

Process.

I asked, "Would you still quote one silver eighteen?"

She looked at measurements.

"Yes for travel work build as described. Maybe plus small material difference if you choose reinforced side stay after trial shell. I don't know yet."

"Deposit still twenty?"

"Yes."

Thirty-six copper in purse.

Could do it.

Still no.

Not yet.

"How much do I need liquid before you think I'm not being stupid?"

She smiled slightly.

First time.

"That's not my job."

"You had opinion last time."

"Last time you'd have one copper. Easy opinion."

Fair.

I thought.

Lodging.

Food.

Return route.

Vale.

Uncertain workdays.

Silver balance unknown in copper terms because denomination structure here still not mine to invent.

Twenty-copper deposit would leave sixteen.

Better than one.

Still maybe thin if work stopped.

"Not today," I said.

Masta nodded.

No disappointment.

"Good. Measurements stay six weeks before I remeasure everything. Major body change, injury, skin problem, come sooner."

"No charge?"

"Not if you eventually buy. One bit if you disappear forever and wasted morning."

"Threatening."

"Business."

She handed me small card.

**GREG / LEFT BKA / TRAVEL-WORK CANDIDATE / BASELINE TAKEN**

Below that numbers I did not understand yet.

Real.

Not mine.

Not leg.

Still real.

I put it in shoulder bag.

"Second bell," she said.

"Work."

"Then stop standing here."

Good morning.

East Hook at second bell had no wreck lift.

Wind still wrong outside breakwater.

Instead Tavren pointed inland.

"North Pier warehouse strike."

I looked at him.

"Strike?"

"Not labor strike. Crane strike."

"Ah."

"Hook hit upper beam. Part of loading gallery collapsed. No injuries. Cargo trapped. Harbor inspector cleared one lane."

Different again.

Good.

North Pier warehouse belonged to **Kerrin & Sons Provisioning**.

Three floors.

Stone lower.

Timber upper loading gallery over quay.

One crane boom had swung in gust during morning transfer and smashed through gallery corner.

No one hurt because warning bell sounded before secondary collapse.

Now half upper platform hung at angle.

Sacks, crates, two copper-bound ice cabinets, and one long white case remained inside cleared zone.

Harbor inspector owned structure.

Kerrin owner wanted perishables first.

Insurance assessor wanted nothing moved until damage plates finished.

Dock master wanted quay lane reopened before evening tide because two provisioning ships were due.

Of course.

People.

Tavren said, "We remove what inspector authorizes. Greg, board and tally. Nali condition. Sesh rigging."

New rigger **Sesh Om**.

Short woman, enormous forearms, braided beard ornaments despite having no beard.

Actually hair braids tied under chin because wind.

Looked beard-like.

I did not comment.

Growth.

Inspector **Meren Cole** had chalked green retrieval corridor across lower floor.

Anything outside green stayed.

Owner **Jase Kerrin** hated green.

"Ice cabinets first."

Meren said, "One is outside corridor."

"Contents are worth thirty copper by tonight."

"Floor under it is cracked."

"Can rig from above."

Sesh said, "No."

Jase looked at her.

"You haven't looked."

"Looked. Still no."

Efficient.

Second cabinet was inside green.

We took that first.

Then food crates.

Then long white case.

White case had no obvious ownership mark.

Just three blue dots and brass end caps.

Nali stopped intake.

"Cold?"

I held hand near without touching.

"Slightly."

She screened.

Green.

Then amber at center.

"Not open here."

Jase said, "That's mine."

Nali said, "Mark?"

"Provisioning inventory."

"Paper?"

He called clerk.

Clerk brought manifest.

Long white case listed as **LEASED PRESERVATION ROD / NORTH COAST COLDWORKS / RETURN DUE 17TH**.

Not Jase's property.

Lease equipment.

Interesting.

"Need Coldworks custody notice," I said.

Sori would be proud.

Nali nodded.

Jase did not.

"I need it working."

"Maybe," I said.

He glared.

"You people love maybe?"

Tessa line hit memory.

There.

Different person.

Same frustration.

I did not get defensive.

"Yes," I said. "Because I don't know if it works."

He stared.

Then looked away.

No clever fix.

We tagged leased equipment and sent runner.

Meanwhile second ice cabinet remained outside green.

Jase kept watching it.

Thirty copper by tonight.

Real loss.

Meren kept line.

Real collapse risk.

No one became wrong.

At third bell, upper platform creaked.

Not loud.

Enough.

Meren raised fist.

Everyone stopped.

Then another crack.

Sesh said, "Out."

We moved.

I backed on crutches.

Bad direction for speed.

Current body knew.

Turn.

Right foot.

Crutches.

Move.

Nali was ahead carrying board and pry bar.

Both hands occupied.

Her left work leg planted and pushed clean.

Mine did not exist.

I moved anyway.

Fast enough.

Gallery corner dropped another foot after we cleared.

Dust.

Boards.

One sack burst.

No injury.

Meren closed green corridor entirely.

Jase swore.

Then stood very still.

His second ice cabinet was still inside.

Thirty copper maybe dying behind unsafe floor.

Nobody told him procedure was good news.

Tavren said, "We stop."

Jase said, "Can you recover from quay side?"

Sesh looked.

"Maybe after brace crew. Not now."

There it was again.

Maybe.

Human word.

Not cowardice.

Not answer.

Sometimes only honest bridge between no and yes.

Jase rubbed face.

"Fine."

He did not mean fine.

We packed.

Short day because structure closed.

Tavren still had another yard job after fourth bell, but only two hands needed.

He picked Nali and Sesh.

I expected no pay or maybe partial.

Temporary block said assigned days, not guaranteed full.

Tavren looked at board.

"Records useful. Half day plus stop not your fault. Three copper."

I blinked.

"Full base?"

"You were assigned full day. Harbor closure ended it. Yard absorbs closure."

Good employer again.

"Thanks."

"Don't thank payroll."

Thirty-six to thirty-nine.

There.

Thirty-nine copper.

Still no deposit.

Still target.

But card in bag now.

Baseline.

Candidate.

That word did something.

Not ownership.

Not promise.

Direction.

At Anchor's End, Dask was drawing three mirror diagrams on napkin.

Sori had letter.

Not home.

Harbor court runner.

She looked up when I entered.

"Harven made hearing."

There.

One thread.

"Outcome?"

"Appeal partially granted. Labor levy reduced from six months to two. Work ban stayed, review in ninety days."

Not clean.

Of course.

"He saw her?"

Sori nodded.

"Yes."

That mattered more than ruling for him maybe.

"Mira?"

"Runner note says quarantine accepted before third bell. Birds held observation overnight, contract not automatically void."

Two threads.

Not miracles.

Consequences moved.

"Tessa?"

Sori shook head.

No.

Still nothing.

Good.

Not good.

Honest.

Maro came in behind me with food.

"Work?"

"Three copper. Half day. Warehouse tried to become floor."

"Successful?"

"Partly."

He nodded like that was normal answer now.

I pulled Masta card from bag.

Set it on table.

Sori read.

**TRAVEL-WORK CANDIDATE / BASELINE TAKEN**

Her face changed.

"Oh."

"Yeah."

Dask immediately leaned.

"What are measurements?"

I covered card.

"No."

"Why?"

"Because you'll graph me."

"I wasn't going to."

"You absolutely were."

Maro looked at card.

"Deposit?"

"No."

Sori nodded.

"Good."

"Still good?"

"At thirty-nine? Better. Still not enough for me to stop judging."

"Comforting."

"You have lodging, food, Vale, uncertain work, and silver balance you still can't casually solve."

"I know."

"Good."

She pushed card back.

"But now it's real."

There.

Yes.

Real enough to measure.

Not real enough to own.

That was fine.

Probably.

I put card away.

Tomorrow board might have work.

Might not.

Five-day block had four more days.

Thirty-nine copper.

One silver plus eighteen.

Left side measured.

Body mapped wrong on purpose for years.

New map possible.

I wanted to see what it felt like.

Badly.

Which meant I had to keep doing boring thing.

Earn.

Save.

Listen.

Avoid falling through warehouse.

Reasonable plan.

Suspiciously reasonable.

# CHAPTER 416

## THE BREAKER

Sea got angry around noon.

Not storm angry.

Local angry.

Enough.

Venn Marine board had three jobs written when I arrived.

Merry Wake.

North Pier brace assist.

South Chain inspection.

Tavren looked at sky.

Crossed out first two.

"South Chain."

Nali looked east.

"Why?"

"Outer float came loose."

That sounded smaller than it was.

Duskport had two breakwater arms protecting harbor mouth.

South arm longer.

Black stone.

Iron teeth.

Signal towers.

And a line of floating chain buoys outside inner bend that marked where deep-draft ships were absolutely not supposed to cut.

One float loose meant chain could sag.

Sagging chain meant ship could cross lane it should not.

Or prop could catch.

Or buoy could drift into surf.

Or nothing.

Harbors specialized in turning one loose thing into seven expensive things.

"Inspection only?" I asked.

"Recovery if safe," Tavren said.

"My role?"

"Tally. Shore line. Equipment condition."

"No heroics?"

"You specifically? None."

Nali said, "He'll hear that as challenge."

"I heard instruction."

Both stared.

Rude.

South Chain sat beyond main warehouses where Duskport became stone, spray, and wind.

Different city.

Less shouting.

More bells.

Workers wore heavier coats.

Sea wall curved outward until buildings fell behind and only harbor towers remained.

I could see ships waiting offshore.

Real ships.

Deep hulls.

Three masts.

One broad coastal trader with blue stern.

Another narrow black ship with sails furled tight.

Outside harbor, ocean moved bigger than river ever did.

Not waves exactly.

Long shoulders.

Each one lifted horizon slightly then put it back.

I liked it.

Then one hit wall and threw water twenty feet.

Less poetic up close.

South Chain crew:

Tavren.

Nali.

Jerr.

Two riggers.

Harbor signalman **Perr Vale**.

No relation to everyone else probably.

One small recovery skiff.

And me.

Loose float was thirty yards outside breakwater face.

Yellow iron drum.

Supposed to ride at fixed angle on chain bridle.

Instead it rolled wrong and slapped sideways every third swell.

Perr said, "Chain angle's low."

Jerr said, "Maybe bridle."

Tavren said, "Maybe lower shackle."

Nali said nothing.

Good group.

Nobody married first explanation.

My board had:

**SC-4 SOUTH FLOAT**

last service: 41 days

chain class: harbor heavy

float mass dry: 63

expected bridle: 3-leg iron

weather: cross swell, west wind

traffic: outer lane hold active

Harbor had already stopped deep-draft entry through south approach.

That cost money every minute.

Signal tower belled twice.

One waiting ship answered with horn.

Impatient.

Same language everywhere.

Jerr and one rigger took skiff.

Not Tavren.

Not me.

Good.

They crossed inside face, timed opening between swells, then ran short line toward float.

I watched through brass sight tube from wall.

Nali watched chain behavior.

Perr handled signal flags.

Tavren watched everything.

First pass.

Jerr hooked inspection line.

Float rolled.

Skiff dipped.

Rigger swore loud enough wind delivered half of it.

Then slate came back by line basket.

**TOP BRIDLE LEG INTACT**

**EAST LEG INTACT**

**WEST LEG MISSING OR BELOW WATER**

"Missing," Perr said.

Tavren looked at him.

"Or below water."

"Right."

Everyone learning.

Second pass required closer look.

Jerr did not like it.

Swell had increased.

Not much.

Enough to change body rhythm.

Harbor tower showed yellow pennant.

Outer lane hold remained.

A cargo captain sent runner down wall asking estimate.

Perr said, "When fixed."

Runner said, "Helpful."

Perr said, "Accurate."

I respected him.

Second pass found west leg had not simply snapped.

Its lower eye had torn away from float plate.

Metal itself ripped around fastener.

Bad.

Meaning attaching replacement line to same plate might fail.

Tavren asked Nali, "Body sound?"

"Need inspect lower shell."

Jerr shouted from skiff, "Not from here."

Tavren nodded.

Nobody argued with man currently bouncing beside broken iron drum.

Option one: tow float in using remaining two legs.

Risk: uneven load tears second plate.

Option two: secure temporary belly sling under float.

Requires close handling in swell.

Option three: leave float, isolate lane, replace later in calmer window.

Costs harbor hours.

Maybe day.

Perr said, "Port master wants lane by fourth bell."

Tavren said, "Port master can want."

Good.

Then float did something new.

Rolled farther.

Not third-wave slap.

Full quarter turn.

Chain beneath it rose near surface.

Nali said, "Back them off."

Tavren already waving.

Perr threw red flag.

Jerr pulled skiff away.

Chain surfaced another foot.

Then snapped sideways.

Not break.

Tension shift.

Whole float jumped toward wall.

Skiff stern swung.

Rigger lost pole.

Jerr grabbed transom.

One wave hit them broadside.

For two breaths, skiff was more angle than boat.

Then recovered.

My body went quiet.

Not locked.

Close.

Attention narrowed.

Chain moving left to right.

Float drift inward.

Skiff retreat path crossing loose line.

Perr saw it too.

"Line!"

Jerr turned.

Too late.

Inspection line had fallen across skiff bow.

If it tightened while chain moved, could pull bow under.

Rigger reached for knife.

Wrong side.

Tavren shouted, "Cut bow line!"

Knife out.

Wave.

Missed.

Second try.

Line started tightening.

I was thirty yards away.

No useful physical reach.

No authority.

No clean magic confidence.

Could maybe nudge line.

Maybe.

External attempt at moving wet rope thirty yards?

Stupid.

Not enough.

Different option.

"Perr! Drop inner slack!"

He looked at me.

One breath.

Then understood.

Signal-control winch held shore inspection line through guide post.

Not chain.

Inspection rope.

He spun brake.

Wrong direction first quarter turn.

I pointed.

"Free! Free!"

He released.

Shore reel dumped slack.

Line went soft just before it fully loaded skiff bow.

Rigger cut it.

Skiff pulled clear.

Nobody died.

Excellent standard.

Tavren looked at me once.

Not praise.

Later.

Now problem.

Float continued drifting toward inner rocks.

Broken west mount plus some lower chain movement meant something below had changed too.

Nali said, "Could be chain riding debris."

Perr said, "Could be anchor shift."

Tavren said, "Could be."

Jerr shouted from safer water, "I'm not going under that."

"Correct," Tavren called.

There.

Harbor lane stayed closed.

Port master runner returned.

Bad timing.

"Fourth bell matters."

Tavren pointed at moving float.

"So does that."

Runner looked.

"Can you secure it?"

"Maybe."

"By fourth?"

"No estimate."

"Port master won't like that."

"He can come swim."

Runner left.

Nali laughed once.

Then float hit inner rock.

Hard.

Metal boomed.

One remaining bridle leg bent.

Now we had choice removed for us.

Tow by original bridle no longer sensible.

Belly sling or abandon until calmer water.

Jerr came back inside wall.

Wet.

Angry.

"Sling possible if we can pin float against lee notch."

Tavren said, "Can skiff do it?"

"Not safely alone. Need shore control."

There.

Work.

Breakwater had recessed maintenance notch twenty yards downwall.

If float drifted there, two shore lines could hold it against stone while riggers passed sling from protected side.

Problem: float would not politely drift there.

Current pushed slightly north.

Wind pushed south.

Net changed every swell.

We could use tag line from wall.

No one could stand too close edge because spray.

Tavren started assigning.

Perr winch.

Nali load monitor.

Jerr sling team.

Me board and secondary shore line.

"Secondary line?"

I asked because that sounded like rope job.

"You don't rig. You hold brake marker and call load changes to Perr."

There.

Fine.

Line went out by weighted throw bag first.

Missed.

Second caught float cage.

Skiff crew converted to proper attachment from protected side.

Now shore had influence.

Not control.

Important distinction.

Perr took strain.

Nali watched line angle.

I watched paint marks on secondary drum.

Red.

Yellow.

Black.

Each represented measured payout interval.

Simple enough.

Until water started moving them faster.

Float surged.

"Half mark!"

Perr eased.

Returned.

"Quarter back!"

He took in.

Jerr shouted from notch.

"Hold there!"

Tavren repeated, "Hold!"

Chain under float rose again.

Nali said, "No. Ease two."

Perr looked at Tavren.

Tavren said, "Ease two."

Good hierarchy.

We eased.

Chain sank.

Float moved farther from notch.

Jerr cursed.

Nali did not care.

Correct.

Second approach.

Wave timing.

Third.

Fourth.

My shoulders started hurting because even not holding rope, crutches planted on wet stone took constant micro-adjustment.

Right foot tired.

Left hip did nothing useful.

Peg thought appeared.

Wrong time.

Shut up.

Fifth approach lined up.

Jerr got sling under one side.

Other side stuck.

Something snagged underneath.

"Stop!"

Everything stopped.

Sling line twitched.

Then pulled down.

Not heavy.

Alive.

Jerr backed away.

"Something on it."

Nali squinted.

Black shape surfaced beside float.

Long.

Flat head.

Pale underside.

About six feet.

Not fish I knew.

Perr said, "Chain ray."

Of course harbor had animal named after infrastructure.

Ray had wound itself around hanging bridle or chain weeds.

Not attacking.

Trapped or feeding.

Its tail slapped water.

Jerr said, "That explains movement."

Nali said, "Maybe some."

Good.

Tavren asked, "Protected?"

Perr nodded.

"Harbor species. Can't kill unless human danger immediate."

Jerr looked at sling.

"It is making human danger."

"Not attacking."

"Still danger."

There.

Another conflict.

This one had teeth maybe.

Port lane closed.

Animal trapped.

Workers at risk.

Could cut remaining bridle and let float/chain section sink away from ray maybe.

Could wait for harbor ecology team.

Nearest response maybe half bell.

Could try free ray from shore/skiff, risky.

Tavren did not ask me.

Thank fuck.

Perr called ecology bell from tower.

Response flag came back:

**TWENTY MINUTES ESTIMATE.**

Port master runner arrived again and saw ray.

Stopped talking.

Small mercy.

Jerr said, "We wait."

Tavren said, "We wait."

There.

No heroics.

Then ray changed mind.

Or panic.

It rolled.

Tail came over float.

Remaining bridle leg twisted.

Metal screamed.

Plate began tearing.

If final mount tore, float could drop onto ray or whip chain toward skiff.

Waiting became action.

Tavren: "Everyone clear line!"

We moved.

Perr released strain.

Jerr backed skiff.

Ray rolled again.

Final bridle tore.

Float jumped free.

Chain dropped.

Ray surfaced violently.

For one instant it was fully visible.

Broad black wings.

White mouth underside.

Tail longer than body.

One iron loop caught near base.

Not chain itself.

Broken bridle ring.

It thrashed toward wall.

Skiff nowhere near.

Good.

But weighted throw line was still attached to float cage, now free and rolling toward notch.

Perr started taking in automatically.

Nali shouted, "Leave it!"

Too late.

Line tension came on.

Float swung.

Ray crossed between float and wall.

Potential crush.

Perr froze.

Bad.

Needed either full release or controlled hold.

Tavren shouted, "Free drum!"

Perr reached.

Brake lever jammed halfway.

Salt grit maybe.

Float kept moving.

Ray thrashed.

There.

Now body did switch.

Not power.

Priority.

One: ray not human.

Two: float crush could rebound line.

Three: Perr at winch danger.

Four: brake jam.

Five: manual pin release on opposite side of drum.

I had seen it when line first ran.

Three steps from Perr.

Wet stone.

Crutches.

Could reach.

"Perr, off handle."

He did.

I moved.

Fast.

Right crutch.

Foot.

Left crutch swing.

Not pretty.

Enough.

Pin sat waist height behind drum.

Too far if I kept both crutches planted.

I hooked left crutch under arm, leaned right against rail, reached.

Pin stuck.

Of course.

Two-hand problem.

Fucking hands.

I jammed right crutch against lower brace to free right hand without dropping body fully.

Bad position.

Temporary.

Pulled pin with both hands.

Nothing.

Twist then pull.

Old mechanism.

I knew type.

Twisted.

Pulled.

Released.

Drum spun.

Line dumped.

Float stopped pulling toward wall.

Ray cleared beneath it.

Tail slapped stone close enough spray hit face.

Then animal dove.

Broken ring still on tail.

But no crush.

No taut line.

No human injury.

I stayed braced against rail until drum slowed.

Tavren said, "Down."

Not Vessa.

Same word.

I listened.

Sat.

Breathing harder than action deserved.

Because current body.

Because one foot.

Because two crutches became zero at exactly wrong second.

Perr stared at me.

"You knew release?"

"Saw it."

"That pin sticks."

"Noted."

Nali came over.

"Hands?"

"Fine."

"Shoulder?"

"Fine."

"Stump?"

"Fine."

"You sure?"

I checked.

Actually checked.

"Yes."

She nodded.

No speech.

Good.

Ecology team arrived twelve minutes later instead of twenty.

Two workers in green slickers.

One had hooked pole designed for ray rings.

They found animal fifty yards north inside calm pocket.

Still carrying broken bridle ring.

Managed to guide it into shallow cage lane and cut ring loose without killing it.

Ray left under own power.

Perr recorded wildlife incident.

Nali recorded hardware failure.

Jerr recorded rigging interruption.

I recorded float recovery.

Eventually belly sling got around free float because no chain attached now.

Iron drum came ashore scratched, plate torn, shell dented but serviceable for repair maybe.

Chain section stayed underwater marked for later dive.

South lane reopened with temporary exclusion shifted north.

Not full original capacity.

Port master got partial opening around fifth bell.

Late.

Not disaster.

Runner came back to complain about delay.

Saw torn plate.

Saw ecology crew.

Changed mind.

Excellent growth.

At yard Tavren reviewed forms.

Then looked at me.

"You took release pin."

"Yes."

"Rigging authority?"

"No."

"Why?"

Fair.

"Perr was off handle. Brake was jammed. Drum needed free release. Pin function visible from prior operation. Float line was creating immediate crush/whip risk. Tavren had ordered free drum."

He waited.

"I executed stated command because mechanism jammed and I could reach alternate release."

More wait.

"If pin had been unfamiliar?"

"Would not."

"If freeing drum could have loaded primary chain?"

"Would not."

"If you weren't sure?"

"Ask or don't touch."

Tavren nodded.

"Good."

There.

Not congratulations.

Better.

Nali signed condition/tally.

No evening custody.

Load tally assigned all day.

Four copper.

Thirty-nine to forty-three.

There.

Forty-three.

Twenty-three Vale.

Still no deposit.

I passed Hark & Venn on walk back.

Did not go in.

Good.

Window display held one unfinished wooden shin blank.

Not mine.

Could imagine mine now.

Dangerous.

Kept walking.

At Anchor's End, Dask had bought nothing from mirror guild.

Progress everywhere.

Sori asked, "How was work?"

"Ray."

She blinked.

"What?"

Maro looked up.

"Animal or light?"

"Animal."

Dask leaned forward.

"Chain ray?"

"Yes."

Of course he knew.

"They nest near warm iron sometimes. Did it have ventral stripe?"

"No idea."

"Tail ring?"

"Broken bridle ring."

"Oh."

He looked delighted.

Sori said, "Were you almost killed?"

"No."

"Were you almost injured?"

"Not really."

"Did you do something stupid?"

I considered release pin.

"Managed stupidity."

"Greg."

"Tavren approved after."

"That is not same as before."

Fair.

Maro asked, "Pay?"

"Four."

Sori immediately looked at purse.

"Forty-three."

"Yes."

"Deposit is twenty."

"Still yes."

She waited.

I waited.

She smiled.

"You're not doing it."

"Not today."

"Good."

Dask said, "Why?"

Three of us looked at him.

"What?"

Sori said, "Because he enjoys eating."

"Deposit does not prevent eating."

"It makes future eating more probabilistic."

Dask thought.

"Fair."

There.

I had forty-three copper.

Enough that twenty did not feel impossible.

Still not enough that twenty felt cheap.

Good place.

Maybe.

I flexed left knee under table.

Body remembered temporary support post.

Level pelvis had felt wrong.

That was interesting.

More interesting than price now.

A leg would make some work easier.

Today it might have made release pin easier too.

Or it might have put me in worse position on wet stone if I trusted new gait too soon.

Tool.

Not miracle.

Masta's voice without Masta present.

Annoying.

Outside, Duskport harbor bell rang evening traffic.

Ships started moving through partial south opening again.

One loose float had closed part of harbor for most of day.

One trapped ray had made problem worse.

One jammed brake had nearly made it dangerous.

Nothing alone impressive.

Together, expensive.

That was infrastructure.

That was bodies too.

Little failures adding until somebody had to carry them.

I looked at crutches against table.

Then purse.

Then beer sign across street.

Absolutely not.

Some lessons held.

# CHAPTER 417

## THE EARNER

I did not have peg leg.

This became important because Dask thought I did.

"When do you get it?"

Breakfast.

No greeting.

Sori looked over tea.

"He hasn't paid deposit."

"I know. I mean when."

"When I have money."

Dask frowned.

"You have forty-three copper."

"Yes."

"Deposit is twenty."

"Yes."

"So you have money."

"I also enjoy food."

Maro buttered bread.

"And rooms."

"Occasionally."

Dask considered this as competing technical requirement.

"Right."

Pell Varo passed table.

"Peg?"

Apparently conversation had escaped.

"Future peg."

"Shame."

"Why?"

"Would improve name."

"What name?"

He kept walking.

"Greg."

I stared after him.

Sori smiled into tea.

"Don't."

"I didn't say anything."

"You stared after him."

Fuck face.

East Hook board had no work for me.

That was first time since trial.

Merry Wake heavy lift delayed another half day.

North Pier brace crew still owned warehouse.

South Chain needed divers and ironworkers, not tally hand.

Tavren pointed at board.

"No assignment until maybe third bell."

No work, no pay.

Terms were terms.

"Anything else?"

"Not from me."

There.

Could wait.

Could wander.

Could put twenty copper on Masta's bench and feel productive.

I looked west toward Hark & Venn.

No.

Not because afraid.

Because deposit did not make balance smaller enough.

One silver eighteen remained one silver eighteen minus whatever deposit counted toward final.

Still silver.

Still Duskport costs.

Still Vale.

Still no conversion I trusted enough to pretend.

Tavren said, "Harbor labor board."

"What?"

"If you want day work. East Hook exchange under red clock. Tell them recovery condition/tally, not rigger."

"You recommending me?"

"No."

"Good."

"I'll confirm you worked here if asked."

Different.

Useful.

So I went.

Duskport labor exchange occupied covered square under enormous red-faced tide clock.

Three boards.

**SHIP**

**YARD**

**SPECIAL**

People clustered under each.

Sailors.

Porters.

Clerks.

Cooks.

Carpenters.

Riggers.

Two people with work hooks.

One man missing eye.

Woman with rigid left peg leaning against post while she ate apple.

Nobody looked twice at my crutches.

That remained strange enough to notice.

Clerk asked classification.

"Recovery condition and tally. Temporary Venn hand. Not rigging."

"Writing?"

"Yes."

"Weights?"

"Yes."

"Charge screening?"

"Record only unless supervised."

She looked up.

"Good answer."

Apparently Duskport rewarded not lying about jobs.

She stamped slate.

"Two openings. Grain lighter inventory, two copper half day. Or West Teeth storm stores, three copper if full count finishes before sixth bell."

"What's West Teeth?"

"Sea wall batteries."

"Weapons?"

"Storm hardware."

"What kind?"

"Ask them."

Three copper.

New part of city.

"West Teeth."

She handed token.

"Gate Seven. Ask for Kett."

I went west.

Duskport changed past shipyards.

Fewer taverns.

More stone.

Sea wall rose higher, thick enough for carts.

Every hundred yards sat squat towers with iron shutters facing ocean.

Not military.

Maybe once.

Now storm defense.

Gate Seven guard took token.

"Kett!"

Someone shouted back:

"Busy!"

Guard shouted:

"Labor!"

"Then send him!"

Good interview.

Kett Arlo was short, broad, gray-haired, and missing left hand above wrist.

Work hook attached today.

Not fancy.

Dark steel hook with leather cuff.

He looked at crutches.

Then token.

"Venn?"

"Temporary."

"Can count?"

"Usually."

"Bad answer."

"Yes."

"Better."

He pointed underground.

Stairs.

Of course.

West Teeth stores sat inside sea wall.

Dry chambers behind thick stone.

Rows of equipment:

storm anchors.

chain drums.

folding flood braces.

sealed lamp boxes.

blue-glass tide rods.

And long iron tubes with ceramic collars.

I stopped.

Kett said, "Don't touch those."

"Wasn't."

"Everyone says that while touching."

"What are they?"

"Breakers."

I looked at him.

"Storm breakers?"

"Wave-break charges. Not explosive. Push face off incoming surge if wall team times them right. Old ones can crack collar and push sideways. So don't touch."

Magic infrastructure.

Serious.

Not artifact market.

City equipment.

"Job?"

"Inventory before storm season. Numbers, seal condition, obvious corrosion, rack location. You see damage, mark. You do not test."

"Easy."

Kett stared.

"Leave."

"Sorry."

"Nothing in harbor easy."

Fair.

We worked.

Mostly boring.

Good boring.

Rack A storm anchors.

Rack B brace pins.

Rack C lamp boxes.

Two missing seals explained by maintenance chits.

One chain drum mislabeled east instead of west.

Three tide rods with cloudy glass, already condemned.

I counted.

Kett checked.

I learned West Teeth had twelve storm stations along outer wall.

During severe coastal surge, crews used mechanical barriers first.

Breakers only when wave timing and wall load crossed threshold.

Expensive charge cells.

Dangerous if mistimed.

Duskport had lost twenty-seven feet of old seawall in storm thirty-one years ago.

After that city stopped pretending stone alone was enough.

"How many people work this?"

"Storm day? Hundreds."

"Regular?"

"Forty-two across wall. More maintenance contracts."

He adjusted hook cuff with teeth and right hand.

I watched.

Not because missing hand.

Because technique.

He caught look.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"Bullshit."

Duskport hated privacy.

"I'm getting fitted. Maybe."

He looked at left knee.

"Hark and Venn?"

"Masta."

"Good."

"Everyone knows her?"

"Everyone missing something knows someone who knows her."

There.

Harbor ecosystem again.

"How long hook?"

"This one? Twelve years. Hand gone nineteen."

"Work easier?"

"Some."

"Worth it?"

"Some days."

I waited.

He sighed.

"You want speech?"

"No."

"Good. Hook holds rope. Carries buckets. Pulls doors. Doesn't feel. Gets cold. Catches shit. Leather rubs. I take it off when stump swells. Better than no hook for work. Worse than hand. That's all."

"That's enough."

"Should be."

Back to count.

Near fourth bell, we found problem.

Rack H.

Wave-breaker collars.

Inventory sheet said twelve.

Rack held eleven.

No maintenance chit.

No transfer mark.

Kett stopped joking.

"Count again."

I did.

Eleven.

He checked adjacent rack.

Nothing.

"Seal log."

Door seal intact from morning.

Previous night seal intact.

Last full inventory eight days ago: twelve.

Could be paperwork.

Could be moved before seal.

Could be theft.

Could be maintenance without chit.

Could be one dangerous piece of storm hardware somewhere it should not be.

Kett closed room.

"Nobody leaves section."

There.

Human problem became security problem.

Guard captain arrived.

**Lera Morn**.

Tall.

Very formal speech.

"Who discovered discrepancy?"

"He did," Kett said.

She looked at me.

"State your count and actions."

"Sheet twelve. Physical eleven. I recounted once. Kett independently checked adjacent rack. We checked maintenance/transfer chits at station. None present. We checked door seal log. Current and prior night seals recorded intact. We did not open or test remaining collars."

"Did you touch rack contents?"

"Only tag rail and inventory board."

"Did you observe any person handling Rack H before discovery?"

"No."

"Do you have any theory?"

"Several possibilities. No evidence choosing one."

She nodded.

"Thank you."

No praise.

Good.

Then everyone got searched.

Including me.

Bag opened.

Quietener.

WS-F/9.

Tavi rock.

Masta card.

Guard held striped rock.

"What is this?"

"Rock."

"Purpose?"

"Child gave it to me."

She looked at me.

"Sentimental property."

"Yes."

She returned it carefully.

Good guard.

Nothing found.

Kett was furious.

Not at me.

At missing collar.

"Those don't walk."

"People do."

"Helpful."

"Sorry."

Search expanded to maintenance cages.

Then cart records.

I kept out unless asked.

At fifth bell, maintenance runner arrived holding paperwork.

Not collar.

Paperwork.

Transfer order.

Rack H unit moved three days ago to **North Wall Station Two** after cracked ceramic found during visual check.

Order had been filed under *breaker tubes*, not *collars*.

Physical move legitimate.

Rack sheet stale.

No theft.

No missing dangerous hardware.

Kett swore for long time.

Lera Morn did not.

She said:

"Classification mismatch caused security response. Correct both indexes."

That was scarier than swearing.

Kett looked at me.

"You still get paid."

"Good."

"Why wouldn't he?" Lera asked.

"People get weird when day becomes paperwork."

"Then people are wrong."

I liked her too.

Duskport dangerous.

We finished count before sixth bell.

Barely.

Three copper.

Forty-three to forty-six.

Not Venn money.

Mine anyway.

Labor board work.

That mattered.

I could earn outside one employer.

Option.

Again.

At gate Kett said, "You come back tomorrow, maybe work."

"Maybe?"

"Storm stores don't need emotional commitment."

"Good."

"But Venn pays better when they have work."

"Also good."

"You saving for leg?"

"Apparently whole city knows."

He grinned.

"Peg-Leg Greg."

There.

I stared.

He laughed.

Not cruel.

Just delighted with himself.

"That's terrible."

"It's excellent."

"I don't even have peg."

"Future-proof."

"Fuck you."

He laughed harder.

Guard at gate heard.

"Peg-Leg Greg?"

"No."

Kett said, "Yes."

"No."

Guard smiled.

"See you, Peg-Leg."

Fuck.

I left West Teeth.

By time I reached Anchor's End, I had almost forgotten.

Then Pell Varo looked up.

"How was work, Peg-Leg Greg?"

I stopped.

"How."

He pointed at porter drinking near door.

Porter pointed at another porter.

Other porter shrugged.

Duskport information moved faster than plague.

Sori was at table.

Already laughing.

Maro looked confused.

Dask looked delighted.

"It scans," he said.

"What scans?"

"Peg-Leg Greg. Three beats."

"I know how words work."

"Also descriptive."

"Not currently."

Sori wiped eyes.

"That's what makes it better."

"I hate this city."

Pell called from desk:

"Duskport!"

Fucking Duskport.

I sat.

Sori said, "Forty-six."

"Yes."

"Getting closer."

"Yes."

"Peg-Leg Greg."

"I will throw tea at you."

"You need both hands."

Silence.

She realized.

I realized.

Then she laughed so hard table shook.

I did too.

Could not help it.

Because yes.

That was entire fucking problem.

Need both hands.

Peg-Leg Greg.

Stupid.

Good stupid.

I looked at crutches leaning against chair.

Then Masta card in bag.

Travel-work candidate.

Forty-six copper.

Still no peg.

Still not enough.

But nickname had arrived before equipment.

That felt unfair.

Also somehow right.

Later, lying in loft, I thought about what Kett had said.

Better than no hook for work.

Worse than hand.

Masta:

Tool, not miracle.

Nali changing feet.

My own pelvis reading crooked as normal.

The leg would not restore me.

That word was wrong.

Restore implied old shape.

I was not going backward.

First life had two legs.

Second life had one leg and crutches and nineteen years of adaptations built around them.

A work limb would make third arrangement.

New body map.

New options.

Maybe something else too.

Not power.

Not yet.

But I remembered being reset into this body.

How much of early life had been learning what I could not do the old way.

Then learning another way.

Then another.

Eventually limitations became furniture.

Normal.

Useful even, sometimes, because you stop wasting anger on wall and start building door.

Peg would not remove wall.

Might put door somewhere I had forgotten to look.

That thought sat differently.

Not S-class switch.

Not locked mode.

Something smaller.

Forward.

Potential.

I had been thinking about leg as mobility purchase.

More hands.

Standing.

Carrying.

Work.

All true.

But maybe body change would change what I attempted before thinking no.

That mattered.

First life me had known body could usually be trained toward problem.

Second-life me knew that too in theory.

Crutches proved it.

Magic training proved it.

Work proved it.

Still, some quiet part of me had organized world around permanent subtraction.

One leg.

Two crutches.

Plan from there.

A peg did not erase subtraction.

It changed equation.

I smiled in dark.

Peg-Leg Greg.

Terrible.

If they kept saying it, I might have to buy thing just to make them technically correct.

Financial strategy.

Very mature.

# CHAPTER 418

## THE CLIENT

I paid the deposit because Tavren had work.

This made sense in my head.

Probably bad sign.

Morning board:

**MERRY WAKE / THIRD SAFE / STEERING LINKAGE / LOWER CABIN HARDWARE**

Weather clear enough.

Crane barge cleared.

Divers available.

Three days left on my open block.

Tavren pointed at me.

"Full day. Tally and condition."

Four copper if useful.

Maybe five if custody ran late.

There.

Not certainty.

Evidence.

Venn had work today.

Labor exchange existed tomorrow if Venn did not.

West Teeth would take me again if inventory or storm work opened.

Masta measurements remained good six weeks.

Forty-six copper in purse.

Twenty deposit.

Twenty-six left.

Still not rich.

Not one copper either.

Difference mattered.

I looked west.

Tavren followed eyes.

"Go."

"What?"

"You're thinking loud."

Dask infection spreading.

"Masta opens before first bell."

"We have half bell before crane leaves."

"You don't care?"

"I care if you're late."

Fair.

I went.

Hark & Venn had shutters half-open.

Masta sat behind bench drinking something that smelled less offensive than Duskport beer.

She saw me.

Then purse.

"No."

"I didn't say anything."

"You came before work carrying your purse."

"That proves nothing."

"Before first bell?"

I put twenty copper on bench.

Not dramatic pile.

Still looked like a lot.

Because it was.

Masta did not touch it.

"Why today?"

Good question.

"Because I have forty-six. Venn assigned me full day. I found independent work yesterday when Venn had nothing. Labor board can cover gaps. I know full price still hurts. I know deposit doesn't make leg appear. I know I need lodging, food, return money, Vale, and whatever stupid thing breaks next."

She waited.

"And?"

"And I want slot."

There.

Simple.

Masta looked at money.

Then me.

"Twenty leaves twenty-six."

"Yes."

"Not enough for final."

"Obviously."

"Good."

She took copper.

Counted once.

Stamped receipt.

Then wrote my name into shop ledger.

**GREG / LEFT BKA / TRAVEL-WORK / DEPOSIT PAID / BASELINE COMPLETE**

My stomach did thing.

Not fear.

Maybe.

"First shell?" I asked.

"Three days if leather behaves and I don't hate you."

"You hate me?"

"Not yet."

"Encouraging."

"Trial shell isn't leg."

"I know."

"You will walk rails. Then complain. Then I change it. Then you complain differently."

"Process."

"Yes."

"Finished in eight to twelve days?"

"Maybe. If body cooperates. If work doesn't swell stump until measurements become fiction. If side stay stock arrives. If you don't decide to fight another dog."

"One time."

"You still have bite marks on the crutch."

Fair.

She pushed receipt to me.

"You're client now."

That landed harder than expected.

Client.

Not candidate.

Not maybe.

Still no leg.

But shop had my money.

I had place in ledger.

The equation had not changed yet.

But someone had started writing next line.

"Work," Masta said.

"Right."

"And Greg?"

I turned.

"Don't spend remaining twenty-six proving deposit was brave."

"Wasn't plan."

"Good."

Outside, boy carrying bread looked at crutches.

Then shouted toward someone across street:

"Peg-Leg Greg!"

I stopped.

Boy froze.

Maybe eleven.

His friend laughed behind barrel.

I looked at him.

"I don't have peg."

He pointed at Hark & Venn.

"Soon."

Fucking city.

"Go deliver bread."

He ran.

I was smiling.

Annoying.

Merry Wake was already offshore when I reached East Hook.

Tavren looked at clock.

"On time."

"Barely."

Nali saw receipt edge in bag.

"Paid?"

"Twenty."

She nodded once.

No celebration.

Perfect.

"Then don't wreck stump before first shell."

"Apparently theme."

"Because you're stupid in repetitive ways."

Also perfect.

We boarded recovery barge.

Merry Wake finally looked different because sea looked different.

Three days of weather had cleared enough that outer water flattened into long gray rolls.

Wreck marker buoy sat maybe forty yards off port side.

Divers went down.

My station same.

Scale.

Tally.

Condition lane.

No problem.

First lift was steering linkage.

Heavy iron-and-brass assembly.

Wet weight one hundred twelve.

Courier mark.

Deep corrosion along one joint.

No charge response.

Mechanical salvage.

Rack C.

Second lift loose cabin hardware basket.

Mostly junk.

Door plates.

Bent lamp brackets.

One ceramic handle.

Two buckles.

No personal mark.

Then third safe.

It came up crooked.

Not unusual.

One sling had caught under old deck plate attached to safe side by rusted strip.

Jerr saw it first.

"Hold."

Crane stopped.

Safe hung six feet above water.

Deck plate dangled below.

Not dangerous yet.

Then strip tore halfway.

Plate swung.

Nali said, "Clear radius."

We stepped back.

Plate ripped free and hit barge deck hard enough to jump.

No one near it.

Good.

Safe settled.

I recorded separate debris object.

Nothing heroic.

Then diver bell rang.

Not emergency.

Single-two.

Object query.

Slate came up:

**LOWER FORWARD CABIN / FIXED CHEST / COPPER BANDS / GREEN GLASS INLAY / NO MANIFEST MATCH / WALL ATTACHED**

Tavren read.

Sel Marr was there again.

Of course.

He had not become less interested in money.

"Lift if fixture."

Hessa Brin checked manifest.

"No listed fixture matching."

"Then unlisted fixture."

"Maybe."

Sel rubbed face.

"Please don't say maybe."

I kept mouth shut.

Growth.

Second slate:

**CHEST ATTACHED TO WALL BY TWO IRON STRAPS / STRAPS POST-SINK? UNKNOWN / GREEN GLASS WARM TO TOUCH THROUGH GLOVE**

That changed lane.

Nali looked at Tavren.

"Warm underwater after eight years."

"Could be active."

"Could be chemistry."

"Either way no casual lift."

Sel said, "It's salvage."

Nali said, "It's warm."

"Everything is something."

"Excellent philosophy. Still warm."

Tavren ordered diver back.

No lift.

Specialist screen cage sent down first.

Different from portable screen.

Larger passive hoop with four colored telltales.

Took twenty minutes.

No one happy.

Then diver slate returned:

**HOOP AMBER TWO / BLUE ONE / GREEN GLASS PULSES WITH SWELL / STRAPS APPEAR NEWER THAN CHEST**

Dask would have died from curiosity.

Thankfully not here.

Nali said, "Could be reactive storage."

Tavren: "Specialist."

Sel: "How long?"

"As long as qualified person takes."

"That's not time."

"Correct."

There.

We shifted to other lifts.

Merry Wake finally gave second safe fully.

Then steering housing.

Then a cargo brace.

Warm chest waited underwater.

Near noon specialist arrived from **Tideglass Guild**.

Woman named **Ossa Pell**.

Small, shaved head, three silver rings left ear, absolutely no interest in anyone's urgency.

She read slates.

Asked three questions.

Then said:

"That's probably a storm-heart locker."

Silence.

Sel said, "Probably?"

She looked at him.

"Do you want expert or prophet?"

I liked her immediately.

Storm-heart lockers were old coastal emergency storage.

Not power source exactly.

They held charged heat/pressure buffers for ships riding sudden squalls.

Green glass strips indicated stored balance state.

Older models sometimes remained active for absurdly long periods if sealed.

Warmth underwater plausible.

Pulsing with swell plausible.

Also dangerous if casing pressure changed too quickly after long submersion.

"So lift slowly," Sel said.

Ossa looked tired.

"No."

"Why?"

"Because eight years pressure equilibrium. You lift twenty feet, outside pressure drops. If internal compensator is stuck, copper bands may become decoration shortly before shrapnel."

Sel stopped talking.

Good.

Plan became decompression cage.

Special recovery.

Slow lift in stages.

Hold at depth marks.

Observe glass.

If pulse changes wrong, lower again.

This was not my specialty.

But tally mattered because depth/time/temperature/visual state all had to line up.

Ossa gave me form.

Different form.

Five columns.

Depth.

Hold time.

Glass pulse interval.

Surface temperature estimate from touch probe.

Decision.

"Can you write numbers without improving them?" she asked.

"Usually."

She frowned.

"Yes."

"Good."

Kett would approve correction.

First hold: sixteen feet.

Pulse six breaths.

Temperature probe warm-two.

Hold eight minutes.

Second: twelve feet.

Pulse five breaths.

Warm-two.

Hold ten.

Third: eight feet.

Pulse three breaths.

Ossa said lower.

Immediately.

Crane lowered back to twelve.

Pulse returned five after maybe four minutes.

There.

Real behavior.

Not magic mystery for Greg to solve.

Specialist owned it.

Ossa changed plan.

Longer twelve-foot hold.

Then ten.

Then nine.

Then eight only after stable.

Hours.

Sel watched money leak through schedule and did not argue anymore.

Because chest now had credible danger.

By fourth bell it reached surface.

Not opened.

Placed into padded pressure cradle.

Green glass still warm.

Ossa tagged:

**ACTIVE-STATE UNKNOWN / PRESSURE-SENSITIVE / NO OPENING / TIDEGLASS CUSTODY PENDING OWNER DETERMINATION**

Sel asked value.

Ossa said, "Unknown."

"Range."

"No."

"Functional equivalent?"

"Modern storm buffer? Expensive. This may be dead historical hardware that stayed warm because one subassembly hates us."

Good pricing logic.

Visible strange did not equal treasure.

Sel sighed.

"Can salvage company bid if insurer releases?"

Hessa said yes through proper process.

He nodded.

No shortcut.

I wanted object.

Not literally this one.

Too dangerous.

But category.

Old coastal hardware that survived eight years underwater and still had behavior.

Duskport had shelves full of things worth wanting.

My purse now twenty-six copper because deposit.

Soon maybe thirty.

Still poor relative interesting objects.

Good.

Need more money.

Not greed.

Access.

We finished late.

No injury.

No emergency.

Just careful work.

Nali signed tally and condition.

Evening custody inventory happened because storm-heart locker paperwork infected entire yard.

I got assigned.

There.

Five-copper day.

Twenty-six to thirty-one.

Net after morning deposit, down from forty-six to thirty-one.

But now deposit existed.

Masta had twenty.

I had thirty-one.

Vale still twenty-three.

And leg process had started.

At Anchor's End, Pell looked at me.

Then at crutches.

"Still no peg."

"Observant."

"Deposit?"

"Yes."

He rang desk bell.

Why.

Two porters looked over.

Pell announced:

"Peg-Leg Greg has purchased future tense."

I closed eyes.

Sori laughed from table.

Maro said, "Congratulations."

Normal human.

Thank you.

Dask asked, "When shell?"

"Three days maybe."

"Can I observe?"

"No."

"I didn't finish."

"Still no."

Sori slid tea toward me.

"Thirty-one left."

"Yes."

"Comfortable?"

"No."

"Regret?"

I thought.

No.

That was interesting.

"No."

She nodded.

"Good."

Not celebration.

Just fact.

Later, upstairs, I put receipt beside Masta measurement card.

Candidate.

Deposit paid.

Client.

Three statuses.

Still one leg.

Still two crutches.

Still same stairs.

Nothing physical changed today.

That mattered too.

I did not want story in my head where paying money transformed body.

It didn't.

Work would.

Craft would.

Training would.

Time would.

Maybe.

And when shell came, probably first thing I would do was walk badly.

Excellent.

Still.

For first time, waiting had date shape.

Not someday.

Three days to trial shell if materials behaved.

Then rails.

Adjustments.

More money.

More work.

Eventually street.

Eventually yard.

Maybe road.

Maybe fight.

Not yet.

I looked at crutches.

Peg-Leg Greg.

Nickname remained premature.

But less premature than yesterday.

# CHAPTER 419

## THE TOURIST

Dask bought mirror.

Not useful mirror.

Not dangerous mirror.

"No."

"How much?"

He put it in bag.

"Not relevant."

"One bit."

Dask betrayed.

"I was establishing boundary."

"Badly."

Sori had paper cone of fried dough.

I had nothing.

Apparently this made me suspicious.

"Temporarily."

"You've seen yard, limb shop, labor exchange, seawall, tavern, wreck barge, and route from all those places back to inn."

"That's city."

"That's employment."

Maro nodded.

"She's right."

Unhelpful.

"What are you doing today?" I asked.

"Good."

Of course.

"Correct."

Sori said, "I want fish market."

Three people looked at me.
