Two days left Venn block.

Maybe work.

Maybe no work.

Thirty-one copper.

Deposit paid.

Trial shell in maybe two days after today if Masta schedule held.

Money mattered.

Also I had traveled thirty miles and somehow converted Duskport into series of invoices.

Annoying.

"East Hook first," I said. "If no assignment, I'm tourist."

Sori raised fried dough.

"Growth."

"Fuck you."

Pell called from desk.

"Peg-Leg tourist."

"Also you."

Duskport.

East Hook had work.

Sort of.

Tavren stood under board with arms crossed.

**MERRY WAKE / SPECIALIST HOLD**

**SOUTH CHAIN / DIVE CREW ONLY**

**NORTH PIER / STRUCTURAL AUTHORITY**

Nothing with my name.

"Third bell maybe," he said.

"Maybe what?"

"Storm-heart custody transfer if Tideglass clears movement. One records hand."

"Full day?"

"No. Two copper if it happens."

There.

Could wait three bells for possible two copper.

Could labor board.

Could tourist.

I hated that tourist was becoming actual category.

"Can I leave word at Anchor's End?"

"Yes."

"If transfer clears?"

"Runner."

"How long to report?"

"Half bell."

Good.

Option preserved.

"Tourist," I said.

Tavren looked at me.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"Good."

I left.

First problem was deciding whose bad idea won.

We compromised by doing all three.

This was not compromise.

This was schedule.

Fish market first.

Sea caves second.

Mirror district third bell if Venn did not summon me.

Sori claimed fish market was practical because food.

Then spent fifteen minutes watching woman carve translucent blue fish into flowers.

"Practical," I said.

"Shut up."

Duskport morning market occupied three covered streets near inner basin.

Fish on ice.

Fish in salt.

Fish smoking over low fires.

Crabs tied in baskets.

Long silver things I did not recognize.

Black eels hanging from hooks.

Shellfish piled like coins.

A stall sold nothing but fish eyes.

I stopped.

"Why?"

Seller looked offended.

"Soup."

"All of them?"

"You want fewer?"

Fair.

Maro bought seaweed cake.

Dask bought dried squid and immediately regretted texture.

Sori bought actual lunch supplies because one of us had adult supervision.

I bought nothing.

Then smelled pepper smoke.

Bought skewer.

One bit.

Maybe.

Seller said one bit.

I paid one bit.

Bits remained annoying and real.

Copper unchanged.

Fish market had injury culture too.

Not foreground.

Just there.

A cutter with two missing fingers used wooden guide fixed to board.

A porter with rigid peg had leather loop sewn into apron so he could hang basket while opening door.

Nobody announced adaptation.

They worked.

I watched too long at porter.

He noticed.

"What?"

"Getting one."

He looked at my left knee.

"First?"

"Yes."

"You'll hate stairs."

"Already do."

"Different hate."

Then he left.

Good city.

Sea caves were not caves.

Maro had lied accidentally.

They were old tidal storage arches cut into black cliff south of inner harbor.

At low tide, stone walk exposed between them.

At high tide, lower arches filled.

People called them caves because tourists paid more for caves than storage vaults.

I respected business model.

Maro had learned about them from herb seller because salt moss grew in upper cracks.

He wanted to see it.

Dask wanted echo.

Sori wanted view.

I wanted to not work for twenty minutes.

We got forty.

Sea was bigger from there.

Obvious statement.

Still true.

No basin walls.

No cranes framing it.

Just gray-blue water running until distance stopped explaining itself.

First life had oceans.

Bigger ones.

Stormier ones.

Monsters.

War fleets.

Floating cities once.

Still.

Second-life sea.

Different eyes.

Maro climbed three steps down toward wet ledge.

I did not.

Not with crutches and algae.

There.

Automatic no.

Not sad.

Correct.

Future leg would not make algae wise either.

But maybe with rail.

Maybe with practice.

Maybe.

Equation waiting.

Dask shouted into arch.

"HELLO."

Echo answered.

He frowned.

"Shorter than expected."

"Please don't measure cave."

"It's not cave."

"Please don't measure storage vault."

"I don't have equipment."

Sori said, "Thank gods."

We sat on upper wall.

Maro scraped tiny sample of salt moss only after asking keeper.

Sori ate fried fish.

Dask tried dried squid again because apparently failure required replication.

I watched ships.

Not working felt strange.

Not guilty.

Just strange.

Carrow would have known where I was by now if note timing behaved.

Maybe Cala had read Duskport line.

Maybe Tavi asked if sea had monsters.

Probably.

Lyssa would notice I had chosen farther again.

Also probably.

No reply yet.

Distance had teeth that way.

You could choose road faster than people could answer choice.

I did not like that.

Still chose.

Third bell approached.

No Venn runner.

Dask became unbearable.

"We're close."

"You have said that six times."

"Because we're getting closer."

Mirror district sat east of temple quarter.

Not entire district of mirrors.

Mostly glassworkers, silverers, instrument shops, lens grinders, survey suppliers, decorative workshops, and enough reflective surfaces to make afternoon sun hostile.

Dask transformed.

Not locked in.

Worse.

Happy.

He stopped at windows.

Read every placard.

Corrected one historical date under breath.

Maro and Sori walked ahead without him twice.

I dragged him by sleeve once.

"That's Elya's shop."

"Yes."

"PF-3."

"Still ten copper?"

"Probably."

"Buying?"

He looked offended.

"No."

Growth everywhere.

Public demonstration happened in square around shallow black basin.

Maybe sixty people.

Children front.

Tourists middle.

Workers back pretending not interested.

Guild demonstrator had three standing mirrors arranged around basin.

Not magical artifact.

Technique demonstration.

Silver backing thickness.

Angle.

Water surface.

Colored lamp.

She showed how same object could appear displaced, doubled, or absent depending observer position and mirror alignment.

Dask whispered entire explanation before she gave it.

Sori elbowed him.

"Let woman have job."

"I'm not interrupting."

"You are whispering her explanation."

Good line.

Then demonstration changed.

Guild assistant brought out old harbor sight plate.

Large rectangle.

Darkened silver.

Retired.

Demonstrator said it had been used before modern channel markers to let watch stations compare two lines of approach without turning whole plate.

Two viewing zones.

One frame.

No magic.

Clever geometry.

I liked it.

Then she showed modern version.

Smaller.

Cleaner.

Expensive-looking.

Dask made sound.

Not word.

Sori looked at him.

"No."

"I didn't say anything."

"That noise did."

I laughed.

Runner found me during questions.

Venn red cord around wrist.

"Greg?"

"Yes."

"Tavren says Tideglass transfer cleared. Half bell."

There.

Tourist ended.

"Coming."

Dask looked devastated.

"Questions."

"Ask mine too."

"What is yours?"

"Why anyone lets you near reflective surfaces."

I left.

Half bell was enough if I moved.

Not rushed.

Fast.

Crutches clicked through mirror district.

Crowd made lane annoying.

I knew route now.

Temple corner.

Fish street edge.

Down crane road.

East Hook.

Body knew more Duskport than week ago.

That mattered too.

Tavren had storm-heart locker in pressure cradle on low cart.

Ossa Pell beside it.

Two Tideglass porters.

Hessa with custody packet.

"Two copper," Tavren said.

"I remember."

"Records only."

"I remember that too."

Ossa looked at me.

"Good. Then perhaps today will be miraculous and nobody will improvise."

"High standard."

"Low standard. Rare achievement."

Transfer was from Venn recovery custody to Tideglass pressure room six streets inland.

Simple.

Except moving active-state-unknown pressure-sensitive old hardware through city was apparently not simple.

Cradle had four spring dampers and hand gauge.

Ossa set limits:

No stair drops.

No wheel impact harder than curb lip.

Stop if pulse interval fell below four breaths.

Stop if warm-two became hot-one.

Do not rotate chest.

Do not open.

Obviously.

Route had been cleared for cart.

Then fruit wagon broke axle on Glass Street.

Duskport.

Cart could not pass.

Porter suggested north lane.

Ossa said north lane had three cobble rises above acceptable shock.

Hessa suggested wait.

Tavren looked at clock.

Tideglass pressure room receiving window closed sixth bell.

Not disaster.

Would mean another custody night at Venn and another specialist call tomorrow.

Cost.

Delay.

No danger if held correctly.

There.

Human problem downgraded to inconvenience.

Nice.

I asked, "South alley?"

Porter shook head.

"Steps."

"How many?"

"Four."

No.

"Can cradle be hand-carried level?"

Ossa looked at me.

"Yes."

Porters looked less enthusiastic.

"Weight?"

Hessa read sheet.

"Seventy-one pounds cradle and chest."

Four people could carry.

Except alley narrow.

Two abreast maximum.

Could use poles.

Not my specialty.

I stopped there.

Tavren asked porters.

They had carry bars designed for exactly that.

Of course.

City had solved city before I arrived.

Bars fitted.

Two porters front/back single file with suspended cradle between cross straps.

Ossa walked beside gauge.

I recorded.

Tavren stabilized rear turn at one corner.

Hessa handled doors.

I used crutches.

Both hands occupied.

Again.

At narrow doorway, Hessa needed someone to hold upper leaf while porter cleared cradle.

Tavren did it.

I could not.

Not without leaning crutch and changing position in moving transfer.

Small.

Nothing.

Everything.

Soon.

We reached Tideglass room before sixth bell.

Pulse never below five breaths.

Temperature stayed warm-two.

No rotation.

No impact above stated threshold.

Custody transferred.

Ossa signed.

"Unremarkable."

"Best kind."

"Correct."

Tavren paid two copper.

Thirty-one to thirty-three.

Half-day wasn't half day.

Maybe bell and half.

Good.

Outside, Dask was waiting.

Why.

"And?"

He held up second pamphlet.

Of course.

Maro and Sori behind him carried paper bag.

"Dinner," Sori said.

"Good."

There.

Danger.

"No."

"You don't know what."

"Practice."

"Retired survey and harbor optics."

Worse.

"No."

"Load markers too."

Fuck.

Sori smiled.

"He got you."

"Partly."

Again.

"Viewing."

"Objects don't look lonely."

Beer signs came out.

Workers changed shifts.

Two children chased gull with bread until gull won.

One joke.

Thirty-three copper.

Two days until trial shell, maybe.

One day? No.

Maybe shell day after.

If leather behaved.

If swelling behaved.

If Masta did not decide my body offended craft.

No reason to rush clock.

Sori noticed.

"No second count?"

"Tourist."

Dask unfolded auction sheet on table.

I leaned over.

Viewing.

Very different.

## THE BIDDER

Viewing only lasted nine minutes.

Good record.

Auction house sat behind Mirror District in old customs hall with arched windows and terrible chairs.

Dangerous room.

Growth.

Sori noticed.

"No."

Fair.

Lot card:

**HARBOR LOAD WITNESSES, SIX, RETIRED. FOUR VERIFIED RESPONSIVE. ONE INTERMITTENT. ONE UNTESTED. CASE AS-IS.**

Estimate:

**4c to 8c.**

There.

Not cheap.

Not insane.

I crouched as much as crutches allowed.

Glass strips each about handspan long.

Used to monitor sustained strain direction on old harbor braces.

I knew class.

Not exact models.

Old versions held colored stress line for some minutes after load released.

Useful because worker could inspect after event rather than stand beside line during event.

Modern ones probably better.

Safer.

More precise.

Still.

Portable memory of force direction.

That changed questions.

Not power.

Evidence.

"That's yes with beard."

Good.

Not answer.

I asked, "Can verified ones be demonstrated?"

Reasonable.

"Any provenance?"

"Municipal surplus. East Hook brace stores. Decommission batch fourteen years ago."

"Calibration records?"

"None."

Visible utility known.

Visible risk known.

Price fair.

No secret protagonist discount.

Good.

That was problem.

Sliding center vane.

**OLD HARBOR PARALLAX COMPARATOR. MAKER UNKNOWN. FUNCTIONAL MOVEMENT RESPONSE VERIFIED. RANGE/CALIBRATION UNKNOWN. RETIRED PRIVATE ESTATE.**

**12c to 20c.**

Dask appeared beside me like summoned demon.

"I know this family."

I looked at him.

"Family?"

"Not maker. Design family. Pre-standard dual-line comparator. Membrane differential instead of silver split."

"What does it compare?"

"Apparent shift between two sight lines. If both move together, little response. If one changes relative to other, membrane tension differs."

I understood immediately.

Not because first-life memory.

Because useful.

Set one reference on fixed point.

Other on load, doorway, branch, person, line, whatever.

Relative movement without precise distance.

Could watch from cover.

Apparently.

1c.

He accepted.

Of course.

Too large.

Every acquisition created carrying problem.

Future leg.

Again.

Not every thought needed become speech.

Outside auction hall, woman with lenses passed us carrying comparator case.

Twenty-one copper buyer.

She looked happy.

Then respected her more.

She paid market.

Maybe she needed it more.

Maybe both.

Money was capability.

Not morality.

Sori said, "You survived."

"Barely."

"You bought one thing."

"Cheap thing."

"Two copper."

"Yes."

"And walked from thing you wanted."

"Yes."

"How does that feel?"

"Bad."

"Good."

"You're terrible at comfort."

"Not trying to comfort you."

Fair.

"Two."

"Does it work?"

"Verified basic response."

"Useful?"

"Probably."

I gave tile.

"Old Harbor Tension. Last-peak marker. Series four."

There.

Confirmation.

"Any good?"

Correct question.

Visible utility.

Visible limitations.

Exactly.

No secret win.

Good.

One nominal block day could finally be used.

Also correct.

She tapped my left stump lightly with two fingers, not touching skin, just gesture.

There.

I stopped.

Tomorrow.

Not finished leg.

Shell.

Rails.

Probably bad walking.

Pressure complaints.

Still.

Tomorrow.

"I wasn't."

Masta infection too.

"Reasonable."

Rude.

Because tomorrow had changed shape.

Auction tile thumped bag with each crutch cycle.

Twenty deposit already committed.

I had not solved that.

Had not solved fitting.

Physical object.

Made for me.

Somewhere in Masta's shop right now.

"Tool."

# CHAPTER 421

## THE STUDENT

I woke up before bell.

Not because disciplined.

Because somewhere in Duskport was object shaped for body.

That was different.

Masta said after work.

So naturally morning took six years.

Anchor's End breakfast tasted normal.

Too normal.

Sori watched me finish eggs.

"You look calm."

"I am calm."

"You checked time three times."

"Clock keeps changing."

Dask looked at wall clock.

"That's function."

"Thank you."

Maro smiled into tea.

Pell passed behind me.

"Big day, Peg-Leg?"

"Maybe shell. Not leg."

"Technicality."

"Entire process is technicality."

"Good luck."

That one landed differently.

Not joke.

I nodded.

East Hook board had work.

Full day.

Of course.

**MERRY WAKE / LOWER CABIN CORRIDOR / HARDWARE + PERSONAL EFFECTS HOLD**

Tideglass had cleared storm-heart area with exclusion around former mount.

Tavren pointed at me.

"Tally and condition. Four if clean. No custody tonight. You leave after fifth bell."

"Why?"

"Because Masta will complain to me if you show late."

"She'd complain to you?"

"She complains to everyone."

Nali said, "Family trait."

Good.

Work moved.

Merry Wake had become familiar enough to be dangerous that way.

Same barge.

Same cold iron smell.

Same lift bell.

Same brown water.

Different cabin.

I kept reminding myself different.

First load was three brass bunk frames and one warped locker door.

Second was kitchen hardware basket.

Third brought up something nobody wanted.

Mattress springs.

Eight years underwater had improved them aesthetically.

Not functionally.

I recorded:

**FERROUS BED FRAME / HEAVY CORROSION / NO MARKS VISIBLE / DISPOSITION BULK SCRAP**

Then Nali slapped board.

"Greg."

"Yes."

"You're writing future leg with eyes again."

I looked down.

She was right.

My handwriting had become smaller.

"Sorry."

"Don't be sorry. Be here."

Fair.

Next lift mattered more.

Divers found narrow wall cabinet below old passenger berth row.

Not valuable-looking.

Thin cedar box with brass corner plates.

One plate stamped **M. VALE / 3C**.

Sori family name made my head turn automatically.

Nali saw.

"Know it?"

"Name. Not person."

"Then write that much."

There.

We checked manifest.

Passenger list had **Mera Vale**, seamstress, berth 3C.

No declared valuables.

No relation established to Sori.

Common enough surname maybe.

Could be.

Could not.

I did not build family tree because letters matched.

Progress.

Cabinet went personal-effects lane.

I made note to ask Sori later whether name meant anything.

Not now.

Then fourth lift nearly ate entire schedule.

Half-collapsed brass ventilation trunk came up with three loose iron rods threaded through it.

Weight wrong.

Not too heavy.

Uneven.

Rigging shifted twice.

Jerr stopped.

Reset sling.

Stopped again.

No emergency.

Just bad object.

Tavren watched.

"Leave it."

Jerr said, "Could make it."

"I know."

"Weather holding."

"I know."

"Then why leave?"

Tavren pointed at torn side seam.

"Because third adjustment made it worse. We come back with spreader frame."

Jerr stared another second.

Then nodded.

No argument.

That mattered.

Competent people did not always finish thing because technically possible.

I wrote hold reason.

Not failed recovery.

Deferred recovery pending spreader frame.

Different words.

Different truth.

By fifth bell, records clean enough.

Four copper.

Thirty-one to thirty-five.

Tavren handed coins.

"Go."

"I'm going."

"You're still standing here."

Rude.

I went.

Hark & Venn had front shutters half closed.

Shop not closed.

Private fitting time.

Masta stood inside with sleeves rolled and chalk on forearm.

On bench was leg-shaped object.

Sort of.

Not peg.

Not finished leg.

Socket shell in pale layered leather over stiff inner form.

Adjustable straps.

Temporary straight support post.

Plain flat test foot at bottom.

Ugly.

Beautiful.

Mostly ugly.

I stopped.

Masta said, "If you cry I charge extra."

"I wasn't going to cry."

"Good. Pants off."

There.

Romance ended.

She checked stump first.

Skin warm.

No open spot.

No abrasion worse than normal.

Mild puffiness after full yard day.

She measured circumference again.

Compared baseline.

"Up a little."

"Bad?"

"Expected. Better fitting test after work anyway. Shows real volume."

She pressed lateral upper edge.

"Tender?"

"No."

Lower front.

"No."

Posterior scar.

"No."

"Good."

She handed liner.

Not magical.

Soft cloth and thin padded leather sections.

"On."

I did.

Then shell.

First problem:

My stump did not know where to go.

Which sounded stupid.

It had one direction.

Still.

Socket pressure came around entire limb differently from crutch life.

Not pain.

Containment.

Masta tightened front strap.

Then upper.

Then side.

"Stand between rails."

I put crutches forward automatically.

"Rails."

"Crutches are faster."

"Rails."

I hated student voice.

I set crutches against wall.

Hands on rails.

Right foot under me.

Left test foot touching floor.

Nothing happened.

Of course nothing happened.

I was wearing temporary wooden post.

Not destiny.

"Load ten percent."

"Again with numbers I cannot feel."

"Again with me watching."

I shifted.

Right leg kept most weight.

Masta tapped left hip.

"More."

I shifted.

"Shoulders down."

I dropped shoulders.

"Pelvis level."

"Feels level."

"It isn't."

There.

Again.

My body lied.

Not maliciously.

Professionally.

Nineteen years experience lying about what centered meant.

Masta placed two fingers on iliac crest.

"Here."

Shift.

"There."

I moved until she said stop.

Felt crooked.

Apparently correct.

"Hold."

Ten breaths.

Pressure built along outer upper socket.

"Any sharp?"

"No."

"Burning?"

"No."

"Numb?"

"No."

"Pressure?"

"Yes."

"Where?"

I showed.

She chalked shell.

Then:

"Step."

Fuck.

I moved left post forward.

Too far.

Test foot slapped floor.

Right knee locked hard.

Hands crushed rails.

Masta said, "Again. Smaller."

I stepped back.

Almost caught toe.

"Again."

Third try.

Left forward.

Short.

Load.

Right through.

Awful.

No rhythm.

Not walking.

Negotiating with floor.

"Again."

Five passes.

Then ten.

My left hip started working in way it had not worked under crutches.

Right calf got angry because it did not know whether to relax.

Lower back argued.

Socket pressure changed each step.

I was sweating.

This was embarrassing.

First life I could sprint broken ground in armor.

Current life I could move through Duskport faster on crutches than many people walked.

Trial shell made me worse.

Much worse.

Good.

Masta had warned.

Still hated it.

"You're trying to be good at it," she said.

"Seems reasonable."

"No. You're trying to hide bad information from me."

I looked at her.

"I'm not hiding anything."

"You correct before I can see mistake. Stop."

That was irritating because true.

I had started compensating toe catch with shoulder.

Started shortening right step before she called it.

Started using rails harder on left load.

Old habits adapting instantly around new problem.

Which was exactly wrong for fitting.

"Walk badly on purpose?"

"Walk honestly."

Worse.

I did.

She watched.

After four lengths she stopped me.

Marked shell in three places.

Upper lateral too much pressure under fatigue.

Back edge pinching when I extended hip.

Test foot angle slightly too externally rotated because my body turned it to feel safe.

Not hardware only.

Me too.

Masta loosened shell.

Took it off.

Skin pink at outer upper edge.

One narrow pressure line behind knee.

No blister.

No broken skin.

She pressed blanching area.

Color returned.

"Good."

"Looks bad."

"Looks useful."

She heated leather edge.

Worked it over form.

Added thin pad opposite pressure line instead of directly on it.

Adjusted post rotation two degrees inward.

Then shell back on.

Second round.

Better.

Not good.

Better.

That word again.

Different.

Useful word.

I made six lengths.

On seventh, something happened.

Nothing dramatic.

I stopped at middle of rails.

Weight split maybe sixty-forty.

Maybe more right.

Did not matter.

Hands resting on rails.

Not pushing.

Resting.

I lifted right hand.

Masta opened mouth.

"I'm not performing."

"We'll see."

Left hand still rail.

I stood.

One hand free.

Then I lifted left too.

Only half second.

Maybe one.

Masta did not stop me immediately.

Then body started drifting right.

I grabbed rail.

No fall.

No miracle.

But for one second both hands had belonged to me while standing.

Not borrowed from crutches.

Mine.

I looked at them.

Stupid.

Hands looked same.

Masta said, "Don't get sentimental."

"Wasn't."

"You were."

"Maybe slightly."

"Fine. One second. Then back to work."

Rude woman.

We did more.

She shortened session before I wanted.

"Why stop?"

"Because your skin gave enough information."

"I can keep going."

"Not question."

"I feel fine."

"Also not question."

She removed shell again.

Pink upper edge stronger now.

Still no damage.

"Tomorrow no shell."

"What?"

"I adjust tonight. You work normally. Come next evening if skin stays clean."

"So every other day?"

"Maybe. Maybe not. Depends skin. Depends volume. Depends what you do."

"When do I get finished leg?"

She looked at me.

I knew answer before she said it.

"When this stops lying."

She tapped shell.

Then my hip.

"And this stops lying."

Fair.

She showed hardware choices.

Not final.

A slightly broader travel foot than test block.

Wet-grip option like Nali's but less aggressive.

Possible reinforced side stay if repeated lateral load remained.

No articulated ankle yet.

Too expensive and unnecessary for first build.

"Basic travel foot first," she said. "Learn it. Then we can talk other feet later if money and use justify."

"How much later?"

"After you stop trying to buy future body before learning current one."

Everybody in city knew me now.

I dressed.

Shell stayed.

Important.

I did not take it home.

Not fitted enough.

Not safe unsupervised.

Masta hung it on peg behind bench.

Peg.

Of course.

I pointed.

"Funny."

"What?"

"Nothing."

She handed crutches.

My body returned to familiar movement immediately.

Fast.

Efficient.

Easy.

That was almost disappointing.

Crutches felt like home.

Trial shell had felt like argument.

Outside, Sori, Maro, and Dask were waiting across street.

I stopped.

"Why are you here?"

Sori said, "Pell told us."

"Pell knows too much."

"Correct."

Dask looked behind me.

"Where is it?"

"Shop."

"You don't keep it?"

"No."

He looked betrayed by manufacturing process.

Maro asked, "How was it?"

I considered.

"Bad."

Sori smiled.

"Good bad?"

"Useful bad."

"Did you fall?"

"No."

"Did you walk?"

"Technically."

Dask said, "Define technically."

"No."

We walked back.

On crutches.

Faster than shell.

Much faster.

I told them about rails.

About pressure.

About pelvis lying.

About one second.

Sori did not joke immediately.

That was how I knew she understood.

Then:

"Both hands?"

"One second."

"Still counts."

Maybe.

At Anchor's End Pell looked up.

"Well?"

"Not peg yet."

"Terrible marketing."

"I was worse than crutches."

"Even better."

"How?"

"Now nickname has character arc."

I laughed.

Fuck him.

Night was strange.

Body tired in unfamiliar places.

Left hip.

Lower back.

Right calf less than expected.

Skin intact.

I checked twice because Masta told me to, not because anxious.

Mostly.

No mindset switch.

That mattered.

I had stood with both hands free for one second.

That mattered too.

Equation had not changed yet.

Not really.

It had been written on board.

New variable present.

No solution.

Tomorrow I would go back to crutches and work.

Day after, maybe shell again.

Then again.

And again.

That was less romantic than transformation.

Probably why it might work.

# CHAPTER 422

## THE TEMP

My left hip hurt.

Not injured.

Offended.

Different category.

I woke and moved leg stump under blanket.

No sharp pain.

No skin pull.

No heat.

Just muscles complaining that yesterday I had introduced new management.

Right calf also sore.

Interesting.

Usually right calf worked too much and complained because I made it do everything.

Today it complained differently.

Apparently even partial redistribution had opinions.

I sat up.

Checked skin.

Upper lateral redness mostly gone.

Posterior line pale.

No blister.

No broken skin.

Good.

No shell today.

Masta had said no shell.

So no shell.

Very mature.

I hated it.

Downstairs Pell looked at me.

"Walking?"

"Yes."

He looked at crutches.

"Strong evidence."

"No shell today."

"Ah. Peg-Leg Greg on administrative pause."

"Eat glass."

"Breakfast first."

Sori was already there.

Maro had tea.

Dask had auction pamphlet again.

"Why do you still have that?"

"Reference."

"For what?"

"Prices."

"You own no auction house."

"Correct."

Useful conversation ended.

Sori looked at my face.

"How bad?"

"Hip sore. Skin fine."

"Good."

Then she did not ask more.

Also good.

I ate eggs.

East Hook board had my name.

Final day of block.

**MERRY WAKE / VENT TRUNK / LOWER CABIN / SPREADER FRAME**

Tavren tapped board.

"Same assignment."

Tally and condition.

Four copper if useful.

No custody planned.

One nominal full day remaining.

Last one.

Temporary employment had expiration date.

Comforting.

Nali looked at my gait before saying hello.

"Shell?"

"No-shell day."

"Good."

"You all sound relieved when I follow instruction."

"We know you."

Rude.

Merry Wake was becoming familiar.

Not home.

Worksite.

Different.

I knew where barge boards hung.

Which corner stayed slick.

Where Jerr left spare sling tags.

Which diver liked tea too sweet.

How Nali wanted condition entries ordered.

Time leaving residue.

There.

Today spreader frame came with us.

Ventilation trunk had waited.

Good.

Nobody punished steel for not cooperating yesterday.

Jerr and crew set wider support.

Two sling points instead of one central bite.

Load came up cleaner.

Still ugly.

Warped trunk flexed but seam did not worsen.

I watched HT-LP-4 in bag.

Did not pull it out.

Not my test.

Not needed.

Growth everywhere.

Second lift was three cabin doors bundled separately.

Third was galley copper.

Then lower cabin produced problem.

Not magical.

Human.

Diver brought up sealed canvas roll from under bunk frame.

Long.

Heavy for size.

No obvious charge.

Label plate half gone.

One surviving line:

**R. SOMA / ...WAY OFFICE**

Hessa checked manifest.

Passenger **Rulen Soma**, assistant courier clerk.

Not ship employee.

No declared professional kit.

Sel Marr was not there.

Good.

Different people.

Nali said, "Personal effects lane until identified."

Tavren nodded.

Simple.

Then man on quay shouted.

"That's office property."

Of course.

He wore blue courier sash.

Mid-forties.

Thin.

Sweating despite cold wind.

**Harl Fen**.

Current Duskport branch manager for **Narrowway Courier Office**.

Not Rulen's employer exactly.

Successor office.

Same company line after merger thirty years ago.

Harl had old register copy showing Rulen issued secure dispatch roll before voyage.

No serial.

No dimensions.

No description beyond `sealed dispatch roll / route books / bonds`.

Harl wanted immediate office custody.

Hessa wanted evidence.

Reasonable.

Harl was also terrified.

Not because object worth fortune.

Because office had current audit in three days and historical bond gaps still sat on books as unresolved liability.

"If those are old bond books," he said, "I need them before audit."

Hessa said, "If they are personal effects, you don't."

"They aren't."

"You have evidence they may not be."

"He was courier clerk."

"Yes."

"The roll says Narrowway."

"Partly."

Harl looked at me like maybe I was softer.

Mistake.

"You understand records."

"Some."

"Then write office property pending opening."

"Can't."

"Why not?"

"Because we haven't established that."

He exhaled through teeth.

"You people keep treating uncertainty like virtue."

There.

Again.

Not same dispute.

Same wound.

People needing answer before evidence ready.

I said, "Uncertainty isn't virtue. It's current condition."

He hated that.

Fair.

Tavren cut in.

"Roll stays sealed. Hessa routes it. Work continues."

Harl said, "And my audit?"

Tavren looked at him.

"Still in three days."

Not helpful.

True.

We moved.

By third bell Harl was still there.

Not shouting now.

Just sitting on bollard.

That was worse.

I took water break near him.

He said, "My father ran branch before me."

"Okay."

"His father before him."

"Long office."

"Long mistakes too."

There.

He rubbed face.

"Those old bonds aren't my fault."

"Probably not."

"Audit doesn't care."

"Probably not."

"If roll has route ledger, I can close six lines. Maybe seven."

"Maybe."

He laughed once.

No humor.

"There it is."

"Sorry."

"No. You're not."

I thought.

"I'm sorry it matters before we know."

That got different look.

Not forgiveness.

Less anger.

Good enough.

Hessa eventually got answer without opening roll.

Not full answer.

She found Narrowway company seal pressed into surviving brass cap.

Then old corporate mark book from port archive confirmed cap pattern used on company-dispatch containers, not private employee cases.

That established office ownership class strongly enough for transfer pending controlled opening.

Harl stood.

"So mine."

Hessa said, "Narrowway's."

"Yes."

"Not yours."

"Fine."

She was relentless.

I admired it.

Roll transferred sealed to company custody with witness and audit notation.

Harl got what he needed operationally.

Still did not know contents.

Still did not know whether books helped.

Partial relief.

More honest than miracle.

Rest of day quiet.

Which meant tired.

By fourth bell stump felt normal from crutches.

Hip soreness less.

Right calf still aware.

I checked skin at lunch in privacy.

Still clean.

Good.

At fifth bell Tavren paid four copper.

Thirty-five to thirty-nine.

Then did not dismiss me.

Uh oh.

Nali leaned against table.

Jerr pretended not listening.

Tavren said, "Block's done."

"I know."

"You worked four full Venn days after trial plus one transfer."

"Yes."

"Useful."

"Usually."

"Don't ruin it."

I shut up.

He set slate down.

"I can put you on another block."

There.

Expected.

Still mattered.

"Same terms?"

"Not exactly."

Interesting.

"Three-day flex."

"Meaning?"

"You check board when you want. If I have condition/tally work, same rates. Three base, four load, five custody. No minimum days. No penalty if fitting takes morning or you leave city. Tell me when you know you're unavailable."

"Why no minimum?"

"Because I know what you are now."

"Temporary?"

"Useful temporary."

There.

I smiled despite self.

Tavren continued.

"I'm not hiring guild clerk. You're not becoming recovery specialist because you survived two weeks. You can read work, ask before guessing, and you're less annoying than most desperate people."

"High praise."

"Don't make it emotional."

Nali said, "He already did."

"Traitor."

I looked at slate.

Three-day flex.

No minimum.

Could work.

Could fit.

Could leave.

Could labor board.

Could go auction and become poor idiot.

Could maybe travel farther.

Option.

Again.

"Yes," I said.

Tavren nodded.

Done.

No contract ceremony.

Good.

Then Nali said, "Masta said come by."

I looked at her.

"Today?"

"Skin check only. No shell unless she changes mind."

Good.

I went.

Hark & Venn smelled like warm leather and oil.

Masta looked at me.

"Pants."

"You really should vary greeting."

"No."

Skin check.

Upper redness gone.

Posterior line gone.

No abrasion.

She pressed around stump.

"Sore?"

"Hip. Not skin."

"Expected."

"Right calf too."

"Good."

"Why good?"

"Means you actually loaded left side enough to change right-side pattern."

There.

Evidence.

Small.

Real.

She turned toward bench.

Trial shell sat there.

Adjusted.

I looked.

She caught look.

"No shell today."

"I know."

Everyone had face theory.

"Tomorrow?"

"If skin stays clean."

"Tomorrow after work?"

"After whatever you do. Come before sixth bell."

"How long?"

"Until I stop."

Good.

Then she did something rude.

She handed me shell.

Not to wear.

Just hold.

"Feel weight."

I took it.

Lighter than expected.

Not light.

Support post and test foot gave awkward balance.

Socket itself dense.

"Finished one heavier?"

"A little. Better foot. Side stay maybe. Hardware."

"How much heavier?"

"Enough you'll complain."

"Useful."

"You'll get used to some. Not all."

I turned shell in hands.

Object made for body.

Strange category.

Armor was made for body.

Weapons too.

Boots.

Braces.

But this was closer.

Not replacement.

Interface.

Masta took it back before I became philosophical.

Good woman.

"Tomorrow we walk again," she said.

"Better?"

"Maybe."

"How encouraging."

"I don't care if better. I care if data better."

Right.

Walk honestly.

I left.

Outside, dusk light across street.

I stood on crutches.

Familiar.

Fast.

Efficient.

No shame in that.

Important.

Future leg did not need to defeat crutches to justify itself.

It needed to add options.

I headed toward inn.

Halfway there, someone called my name.

Not Peg-Leg.

Greg.

Sori.

She was standing outside post office with folded paper.

My stomach changed before brain did.

Home.

She held it out.

"Carrow."

There.

Finally.

I took paper.

Cala's handwriting on outside.

Not elegant.

Fast.

I opened.

Three hands inside.

Cala first.

**You went farther than two nights. Obviously. Theatre still standing. Vale asked twice whether you're dead. I told him probably not. Work is work. Don't make us come get you because you decided sea is personality now.**

I laughed.

Then Tavi.

Big crooked letters.

**DID YOU SEE A SEA MONSTER. BRING ROCK IF SEA ROCK DIFFERENT. COME BACK BUT NOT YET IF YOU ARE DOING IMPORTANT THING.**

Chest hurt.

Small.

Good hurt.

Then Lyssa.

Neater.

**I am glad you went. I am also annoyed you went far enough that being glad takes effort. Both can be true. Cala says that is adulthood. Cala is wrong about many things but not this one. Send dates when you know them. Do not turn uncertainty into disappearance.**

There.

That one stayed.

Sori watched face.

"Bad?"

"No."

"Good?"

"Annoying."

"So good."

"Yes."

I reread Tavi line.

Come back but not yet.

Smart kid.

Dangerous.

Home did not demand immediate return.

Worse.

It gave permission with conditions.

Send dates when you know them.

I did not know dates.

But I could answer something.

Tomorrow shell.

Three-day flex work.

Maybe a few more days Duskport.

Then?

Road had widened.

Could go back.

Could go farther.

Could wait until leg usable enough for travel trials.

Could not pretend no choice existed.

There.

Different pressure.

At Anchor's End I wrote reply before dinner.

Not long.

**Alive. Duskport. Working temporary harbor recovery. Paid deposit on work leg. First trial shell happened. Worse than crutches so far, which is apparently correct. Staying at least several more days for fittings and work. After that I don't know yet. I will send another date when I do. Sea monster: one chain ray, which probably counts. Tavi, sea rocks are different.**

I stopped.

Added:

**I miss you.**

There.

Simple.

No need to be clever.

Pell said outgoing courier left first bell tomorrow.

Good.

Dinner.

Sori asked about Venn.

"Three-day flex."

"You taking it?"

"Yes."

"So staying?"

"At least few more days."

Maro nodded.

Dask asked, "Does flex overlap fittings?"

"That's point."

"Efficient."

"Don't make this about efficiency."

"It literally is."

Sori said, "Let him have emotion for six seconds."

Dask looked at me.

"Are you having emotion?"

"Not anymore."

Good friends.

I counted money once.

Thirty-nine copper.

Still huge remaining leg balance.

Still Vale.

Still food.

Still room.

Still enough to breathe.

Tomorrow shell again.

No guarantee better.

No guarantee final leg soon.

Home knew where I was.

Work knew I could leave.

Masta knew I would come back.

That felt strange.

Not trapped.

Expected.

Different.

Temporary did not mean unreal.

Maybe that was whole trick.

You could belong somewhere for a while without promising forever.

Dangerous thought.

I went upstairs on crutches.

Fast.

Familiar.

Tomorrow I would be bad at walking again.

Excellent.

# CHAPTER 423

## THE PATIENT

Morning skin looked boring.

Best possible result.

No heat.

No open spot.

No angry line.

Hip still sore if I leaned wrong.

Right calf less annoyed than yesterday.

Good.

I sent Carrow letter at first bell.

Actually watched courier pouch get tied.

Did not help.

Still felt better.

Communication apparently required ritual.

Sori stood beside me.

"You trust mail now?"

"No."

"Good."

"I trust knots."

"Worse."

We walked back toward East Hook.

I stopped halfway.

She stopped too.

"What?"

"I don't think I'm working today."

Sori looked at me.

Not surprised.

That annoyed me slightly.

"Why?"

"Second shell tonight."

"And?"

"Yesterday Masta said work normally. Today she didn't say work normally."

"She also didn't say don't."

"Correct."

"So?"

"I want clean legs."

"Plural?"

"Body."

"Better."

I hated language.

Thirty-nine copper.

Work available maybe.

Three-day flex.

No minimum.

Whole point.

Still.

Money sat in head.

Sori said, "You know not earning three or four copper today won't make leg disappear."

"Yes."

"Do you believe that?"

I looked at her.

"Less."

"Good enough."

East Hook anyway.

Tavren at board.

**NORTH PIER / BRACE REMOVAL / INTAKE**

**MERRY WAKE / LOWER CABIN / WEATHER HOLD AFTER THIRD**

**SOUTH CHAIN / DIVE REPAIR**

He looked at me.

"You working?"

"No."

No argument.

He erased nothing.

"Available tomorrow?"

"Maybe."

"Tell me tomorrow."

There.

Flex actually flexed.

I waited.

Tavren looked up.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"Then leave."

Rude consistency.

Nali came out carrying coil tag wire.

Saw me.

"No work?"

"Fitting day."

She nodded once.

"Good."

"That's it?"

"What do you want, parade?"

"No."

"Then that's it."

Excellent.

I had day.

Dangerous thing.

Could labor exchange.

Would defeat point.

Could auction.

Worse.

Could train magic.

Tempting.

Then remembered Masta wanted honest body tonight, not tired body pretending fine.

So no.

This was becoming suspiciously mature.

I walked.

Not tourist exactly.

Errand person.

Bought soap.

One bit.

Had boots checked because right sole had started wearing outer heel harder than I liked.

Cobbler said normal enough for crutch gait.

I said future leg.

He said bring it back when gait changes because shoe wear would change too.

Another invisible consequence.

Duskport kept having professionals for body problems.

Annoying and useful.

I ate lunch with Maro near fish market.

He had salt moss spread on paper.

"It survived."

"Congratulations."

"I thought it would dry too fast."

"So did it?"

"Not yet."

He smiled.

Longer smile than usual.

"There's a north-coast herbalist who buys live salt moss if it roots. Not much. But I want to try."

There.

His own little engine.

"You becoming merchant?"

"No."

"Good."

"Maybe once."

"Worse."

He laughed.

Dask joined with three pamphlets.

Sori later.

Nobody asked why I was not working.

That was almost disappointing.

By fourth bell I was restless.

Not tired.

Restless.

Body wanted task because task explains waiting.

No task.

So I checked stump again.

Still boring.

Good.

At fifth bell I went Hark & Venn early.

Masta saw me through window.

"You're early."

"I know."

"Leave."

"What?"

"Walk around block."

"Why?"

"Because you're vibrating."

"I'm not."

"You are."

Fuck.

I walked around block.

Slowly.

Crutches.

Normal.

One fruit seller called me Peg-Leg.

I ignored him.

That felt like progress too.

Returned.

Masta pointed to stool.

"Skin."

Pants off.

Again romance.

She checked everything.

"Good."

"I didn't work."

She looked at me.

"Why?"

"Wanted clean data."

"Reasonable once. Don't become precious. You'll need fit that works after work too."

"Yes."

"Today tells me rested volume. Later I need yard volume again."

There.

Not purity.

Range.

She brought shell.

Looked same.

Wasn't.

Upper lateral edge trimmed slightly.

Posterior relief deeper.

Support post rotated inward from last session.

Test foot had thin leather added to outer fore edge.

"What changed?"

"You tell me."

Student voice.

I put liner on.

Shell.

Straps.

First difference:

Less fight getting in.

Not looser.

More obvious path.

Masta watched.

"Don't help me by liking it."

"I wasn't."

"You were."

Fine.

Rails.

Stand.

Right leg took too much.

I noticed before she said it.

Shifted left.

Stopped.

Looked at Masta.

She smiled slightly.

"That one you can correct."

"Why?"

"Because I already saw it last time. Now I'm testing whether you can feel it."

Different rule.

Good.

I shifted until pelvis felt wrong.

Masta checked.

"Closer."

Closer.

Not level.

Closer.

I adjusted tiny amount.

"There."

There.

Correct still felt slightly wrong.

But less wrong than first session.

Interesting.

"Step."

Left post forward.

Too far.

Caught it.

Did not correct instantly.

Masta saw.

"Again."

Second step shorter.

Test foot still slapped.

Less.

Right knee still wanted lock.

I let it happen once.

"Why?" Masta asked.

"Habit."

"More specific."

"I don't trust left load after foot contact, so right side becomes post."

"Good. Now don't fix it yet. Do three like that."

Horrible.

I did.

Bad steps.

Right knee locked.

Left slap.

Shoulders high.

Three.

She watched.

Then changed strap tension at upper front.

"Again."

I walked.

Different.

Not better immediately.

Different.

Right knee less desperate.

Left foot landed flatter.

"Again."

Five steps.

Rail hands light.

Not off.

Light.

Turn.

Turning was awful.

Crutches had taught me pivot around right foot plus swing.

Shell wanted sequence.

Small step.

Load.

Shift.

Turn.

I tried old movement anyway.

Test foot caught.

I grabbed rail.

"Good," Masta said.

"That was bad."

"Good information."

I hated her profession.

"Again."

Second turn less stupid.

Third still stupid but different.

Then standing.

Masta said, "Hands."

I loosened grip.

One hand off.

Then other.

Two seconds.

Three.

Body drifted right.

I felt drift before rail.

Corrected left hip slightly.

Four.

Five.

Masta said, "Stop."

I put hands down.

"I had more."

"I know."

"Then why stop?"

"Because I want five clean seconds, not twelve ugly ones you survive."

Rude.

Correct.

Again.

This time four seconds.

Then wobble.

Caught rail.

Again.

Six.

Not graceful.

But standing.

Hands mine.

Six seconds.

Long enough to notice room.

That was strange.

First session one second had felt like trick.

This felt like position.

Temporary.

Fragile.

But position.

I could stand and use both hands for six seconds.

Not enough to work.

Enough to imagine work without translating through crutches first.

Masta saw face.

"Don't make religion."

"Wasn't."

"Good. Step."

Again.

We walked rails another twenty minutes.

Not continuous.

Short sets.

Rest.

Skin check.

Adjust.

Again.

By end:

Left steps shorter and more consistent.

Right knee locking less frequent if I thought about it.

Foot slap still there when tired.

Turns still bad.

Pelvis better by feel maybe half time.

Hands could hover above rails for parts of straight standing.

Walking hands-free absolutely not.

Good.

Real.

Masta removed shell.

Skin check.

Upper lateral edge light pink.

Posterior line gone.

One new pressure spot lower front.

Not painful.

She marked it.

"That's from what?"

"Probably your shorter step changing forward load. Could be strap. Could be foot angle. Need repeat."

No omniscience.

Good.

She brought out two wooden foot blanks.

One broad and nearly rectangular.

One narrower with slight split groove.

"Final foot?"

"Candidates."

"Which?"

"Broad one more stable standing and dock. Narrow one clears uneven ground better, rolls easier, less lateral catch."

"Wet?"

"Both can take wet grip. Different tread."

"Travel."

"Yes."

I touched narrower blank.

"This."

"Maybe."

"Why maybe?"

"Because you like idea. I need gait."

There.

She placed both away.

Then side stay.

Thin reinforced bar along outer side of shell.

"Do I need it?"

"Maybe."

"Helpful."

"Your lateral load is improving when alignment improves. If that continues, no reinforced stay. Cheaper. Lighter. If it returns under yard volume, stay."

"How much difference?"

"Small material difference. Not changing quote yet."

Good.

"When do I take something home?"

Masta looked at me.

"Not tonight."

"I know."

"Then why ask?"

"Timeline."

"Third session. Maybe controlled shop-to-courtyard trial with crutches if skin stays boring. After that, if you can don/doff correctly and I trust socket not hurting you, you may take trial build for one hour outside. With crutches. Flat route. Back here."

There.

Actual street.

Not today.

Soon.

"Finished leg?"

"Later."

"How later?"

"You still owe me money and your body still lies sometimes."

Fair.

I asked, "Balance when?"

"Before final hardware leaves shop."

"Not trial?"

"No. Deposit covers material commitment and fitting work to current stage. I don't hand finished build out unpaid."

Good.

Thirty-nine copper.

Still far.

Work mattered.

But less abstract now.

Masta tapped my hip.

"One more thing."

"What?"

"You skipped work today?"

"Yes."

"Fine. Don't do that every fitting. I need to know how this behaves when you're tired too."

"Nali said same thing basically."

"Nali has brain."

"Occasionally."

"She'll hear you said that."

Duskport information plague again.

I dressed.

Crutches under arms.

Immediately faster.

Familiar.

No shame.

But something had changed.

Not body yet.

Not enough.

Maybe map.

Small piece.

Before, standing without crutches was memory category.

Old life.

Or impossible category.

Now it was six seconds.

Measured.

Repeatable twice.

Could get longer.

That was dangerous thought.

Not because false.

Because once brain puts number on possibility, it starts planning around improvement.

I left shop.

Outside, fruit seller from earlier saw me.

"Peg-Leg!"

I stopped.

He pointed at crutches.

"Still no peg?"

"Six seconds."

He stared.

"What?"

"Nothing."

I kept going.

That joke was for me.

Anchor's End smelled like stew.

Pell looked up.

"How many legs today?"

"One."

"Progress slow."

"Six seconds."

He also stared.

"What?"

"Nothing."

Sori was at table with Maro and Dask.

Dask immediately asked, "How long?"

Of course he understood context.

"Six."

"Seconds?"

"Yes."

"Without rails?"

"Hands off rails. Feet still where feet belong."

"Six is substantially more than one."

"Thank you, mathematics."

Maro smiled.

Sori did not joke.

She said, "How did it feel?"

I sat.

Thought.

"Less impossible."

There.

That was answer.

Not easier.

Not natural.

Less impossible.

Sori nodded.

Good friend.

No speech.

Dask ruined it.

"Five hundred percent increase."

"I will kill you."

"Technically from one to six is five hundred percent increase over baseline."

Maro laughed.

Sori put forehead on table.

Normal restored.

Later I counted money once.

Thirty-nine.

Still thirty-nine because I deliberately earned nothing today.

Interesting sentence.

Did not hurt as much as expected.

I had spent day on body project.

Not leisure.

Not work.

Investment without coin leaving purse.

Tomorrow maybe Venn.

Maybe third shell not tomorrow depending Masta.

Maybe day after.

Maybe courtyard soon.

Then flat street.

Then maybe carrying something while standing.

Then maybe both hands while moving.

There.

Planning already.

Dangerous.

Useful.

Equation still not changed.

But I had stopped treating unknown as zero.

That was something.

# CHAPTER 424

## THE WALKER

I worked before fitting.

Masta wanted tired-body data.

Fine.

I had tired body.

Venn board gave me **NORTH PIER / BRACE RELEASE / LOAD CHECK / CONDITION**.

Three-day flex arrangement.

No minimum.

I took one.

Tavren looked at me once.

"Fitting after?"

"Yes."

"Then don't be stupid before."

"Specific."

"You know what I mean."

Unfortunately.

North Pier warehouse had finally been braced enough to reopen one recovery corridor.

Not same gallery.

Not same problem.

This time structural crew had installed temporary timber shores and iron cross-braces under lower loading deck.

Meren Cole still owned structural line.

Sesh Om owned rigging.

Nali owned condition.

I owned records and load sequence.

Good.

The trapped second ice cabinet was still there.

Jase Kerrin was also still there.

He looked more tired than angry now.

"If it's spoiled, it's spoiled," he said. "I just want to know."

Different sentence from last time.

Time did that.

Meren pointed at braces.

"Corridor is temporary. Nothing outside yellow chalk. No stacking recovered weight on gallery."

Sesh said, "Cabinet first."

Jase blinked.

"Actually?"

"Access says yes."

There.

Sometimes procedure hurt.

Sometimes procedure finally lined up with what person wanted.

No moral lesson.

We rigged.

I say we.

They rigged.

I wrote.

Growth.

Cabinet came out ugly.

Copper binding green.

Lower corner crushed.

Door seal swollen.

No dramatic magical pulse.

No hidden monster.

Just very expensive food having spent too long in wrong place.

Jase wanted it opened immediately.

Nali said, "Not on recovery line."

He looked ready to argue.

Then didn't.

"Where?"

"Condition bay."

"How long?"

"Twenty minutes if door behaves."

"Fine."

Again.

Different man than first collapse day.

Or same man with less hope to defend.

We moved it.

Next was three crates of dried citrus.

Then a brass refrigeration manifold.

Then timber rack section.

Nothing heroic.

Good work.

By third bell, Meren wanted temporary brace load checked before next heavy pull.

Normally his crew used chalk witness lines and screw gauges.

One screw gauge had cracked glass.

Replacement coming.

I had HT-LP-4 in bag.

There.

Not prophecy.

Maybe actually useful.

I asked Nali first.

"Tile I bought. Last-peak. Rough only. Could it tell us whether brace foot is taking more load during test pull?"

She held out hand.

I gave it.

She read stamp.

"HT-LP-4."

"Tavren knew it."

"Old Harbor Tension."

Meren looked over.

"Certified?"

"No," I said.

Good start.

"Reset?"

"About fifteen minutes. One peak only."

"Impact?"

"Bad."

Nali added, "Useful only as extra observation. Not structural clearance."

Meren nodded.

"Then extra observation."

There.

Tool found job because job existed.

Not because protagonist owned tool.

We set tile under secondary brace shoe where Meren wanted comparison.

Not primary load path.

Test pull happened.

Tile crescent darkened toward seaward edge.

Meren checked chalk line.

Then adjacent brace.

Then screw reading from functioning gauge.

"Consistent with slight seaward bias," he said.

Not discovery.

Confirmation.

He ordered one wedge reseated before next lift.

HT-LP-4 took fifteen minutes to reset.

We did other work.

Useful limitation.

I liked it more because it was annoying honestly.

At condition bay, Jase's ice cabinet finally opened.

Smell answered question before anyone else.

Bad.

Very bad.

Jase closed eyes.

"All of it?"

Nali looked without pretending expertise beyond condition.

"Upper trays clearly spoiled. Lower sealed tins need food inspector if you want salvage claim."

He nodded.

No shouting.

"Thirty copper," he said.

Not exact now.

Old estimate.

Maybe actual loss more.

Maybe less.

He laughed once without humor.

"I spent three days wanting door."

Nobody knew what to say.

So nobody made speech.

Good.

By fifth bell my shoulders were tired.

Left hip ordinary crutch tired.

Right calf mostly normal again.

Stump skin warm but not angry.

Exactly what Masta wanted.

Nali signed four-copper day.

Thirty-nine to forty-three.

Tavren handed coins.

"You testing shell tired?"

"Yes."

"Then go be bad at walking."

"Everyone very supportive here."

"Go."

I went.

Hark & Venn shutters open this time.

Masta had rail room cleared.

Shell sat on bench.

Same pale leather.

Same temporary post.

Test foot looked different.

Narrower than previous block.

Slight split groove down front.

Not finished travel foot.

Mockup.

"You changed foot."

"Observant. Pants off."

Tradition.

Skin check first.

She pressed upper lateral edge.

"Nothing."

Posterior.

"Nothing."

Lower front where last mark had appeared.

"Slight tender?"

I thought.

"Pressure awareness. Not tender."

"Better answer."

She measured volume.

Higher than rested session.

Lower than first full-day fitting.

"Good range," she said.

"Meaning?"

"Meaning shell must work on body you actually have, not body you present on perfect afternoon."

Fair.

She made me put liner on myself.

Then shell.

No help unless asked.

I got first strap wrong.

Not backwards.

Too loose.

She said nothing.

I stood.

Shell shifted half finger.

I sat.

Retightened.

"Good," she said.

"You could have told me."

"Then you would know because I told you."

Teacher disease.

Second attempt held.

Rails.

Weight left.

Pelvis.

Less wrong.

Still wrong.

I could find level now if I moved slowly enough.

That was new.

Step.

Left.

Shorter.

Right.

No knee lock.

Again.

Foot contact quieter.

Again.

Turn.

Bad.

Not catastrophic.

Again.

Masta said, "Stop correcting shoulder."

I froze.

"Was I?"

"Yes."

Fuck.

Walk honestly.

I repeated turn badly.

She watched.

Then moved rear strap one hole.

"Again."

Better.

Not good.

Better.

We did rails for maybe twenty minutes.

Then Masta said word I wanted.

"Courtyard."

I looked at her.

"Now?"

"No, next winter. Yes, now."

Crutches came with us.

Obviously.

Shop courtyard was twenty paces long.

Flat stone.

One drain channel.

Bench.

Wall rail on first half only.

No crowd.

Except Nali somehow appeared in doorway.

"You live here?"

"Masta sent runner."

"Why?"

"Because she wanted someone who knows when new leg user is lying."

Masta said, "He already has me."

"Second opinion."

Wonderful.

First ten paces used rail.

Slow.

Left foot.

Right foot.

Do not overstride.

Do not lock right knee.

Do not throw shoulder.

Do not swear at foot.

I failed last one.

Drain channel came.

Maybe inch deep.

Nothing.

My brain treated it like terrain.

Because new foot did.

I stopped.

Masta said nothing.

Nali said nothing.

I looked.

Set left foot before groove.

Shift.

Right over.

Then left test foot over.

Easy.

Stupidly easy.

Still required thought.

At end of rail, Masta said, "Crutches."

I took them.

Not under arms.

Light contact.

Two points instead of body suspension.

Different.

"Walk back."

I did.

Crutches barely loaded first three steps.

Fourth step, left foot slapped.

Fifth, right knee locked.

Sixth, I leaned hard into right crutch.

Masta said, "Stop."

I stopped.

"Why?"

"Pattern fell apart."

"Why?"

"Because I'm tired."

"Good."

Nali said, "Also you're trying to walk like man who already knows."

"I do know walking."

Both women stared.

"Fine."

I sat on bench.

Skin check.

Light pink upper edge.

No lower-front mark yet.

Masta waited two minutes.

Checked again.

Pink fading.

"Again. Shorter route."

Five paces out.

Five back.

Crutches light.

Better.

Then she did something strange.

"Stand there."

Middle courtyard.

No rail.

Crutches in hands.

"Now put them together in right hand."

I did.

Both crutches bundled.

Awkward.

Left hand free.

"Take this."

She handed me small wooden box.

Maybe five pounds.

Nothing.

I held it.

Standing.

One hand holding both crutches.

One hand holding box.

Both hands occupied by objects that were not keeping me upright.

That was new.

Not both free.

Better.

More useful.

"Walk three steps," she said.

I looked at her.

"With crutches in one hand?"

"If you need one, drop box and use one."

Nali said, "Don't prove point."

Good warning.

I stepped.

Left.

Right.

Left.

Three.

Ugly.

Slow.

But box came with me.

I stopped.

Masta took it.

"Enough."

"Again."

"No."

"Three steps."

"Enough."

I hated her.

Correctly.

She removed shell.

Skin check.

Upper edge pink.

Lower front faint mark.

No open skin.

No heat.

No numbness.

"Useful," she said.

"Can I take it?"

"No."

Of course.

"Tomorrow?"

"Maybe."

"Outside hour?"

"Maybe."

"You enjoy this."

"Very much."

Nali laughed.

I put pants back on.

Masta marked shell.

"Foot shape?"

I asked.

"Narrower is better for your step. Not convinced on final width."

"Side stay?"

"Less convinced you need reinforced one. Good. Saves weight. Maybe money depending final hardware. No promise yet."

"When finished?"

She looked at me.

"When you can use trial outside without making me regret craft."

Fair.

At door she said, "Tomorrow morning skin check yourself. If clean, work or don't. Come fourth bell. If clean then, one-hour flat route."

There.

Outside.

Actual city.

One hour.

Not today.

Tomorrow maybe.

I walked back to Anchor's End on crutches.

Fast.

Comfortable.

Familiar.

Still better.

That was okay.

Pell saw me enter.

"Peg-Leg?"

"Three steps."

He frowned.

"That's worse nickname."

Sori looked up.

"Three?"

I nodded.

Maro smiled.

Dask immediately said, "Unsupported?"

"Crutches in one hand. Box in other."

He thought.

"So functionally loaded bipedal transfer."

"Do not call it that."

"Why?"

"Because I want friends."

Sori laughed.

Then asked quieter:

"How did it feel?"

I thought.

Not less impossible.

Different now.

Still not walking normally.

Still slower than crutches.

Still supervised courtyard.

Still temporary shell.

But I had carried something three steps while neither hand held my body up.

That wasn't theoretical.

That wasn't standing still.

That was movement.

Tiny.

Ugly.

Real.

"Useful," I said.

She smiled.

No teasing.

Later in loft, I caught myself planning tomorrow's route.

Not distance.

Functions.

Door.

Counter.

Carry cup.

Stop and talk without parking crutches.

Maybe stand at stall and use both hands.

Maybe hold notebook while moving few steps.

My brain had changed question.

Not **Can I walk like before?**

Wrong question.

Not **Can this replace crutches?**

Also wrong.

Question was becoming:

**What can I do now that I could not do yesterday?**

There.

Small click.

Not S-class.

Not power.

Not old body returning.

Just possibility becoming inventory.

Three ugly steps.

Five-pound box.

One hand full of crutches.

Enough to alter planning.

Maybe that was how equation changed.

Not all at once.

One term becoming nonzero.

I smiled in dark.

Tomorrow, if skin behaved, city got test.

Poor city.

# CHAPTER 425

## THE PEDESTRIAN

Morning skin boring.

Best possible news.

No heat.

No open line.

Lower-front mark gone.

Upper edge normal.

"Well?"

"Congratulations."

"Thank you."

"Technically absence of adverse tissue response after repeated load is positive evidence."

"I am."

"More."

I ate more.

Fourth bell.

One-hour trial.

Could work morning.

Did not.

Not because precious.

Masta wanted real-world data.

Real-world data did not require stupid data.

Dask went back mirror district to ask question he had apparently developed overnight.

"Healthy."

"It was more interesting before money."

Maro Pell, failed merchant.

One sale.

Retired.

His treat.

Not anxious.

Maybe anxious.

Body had known crutches nineteen years.

Today city got new grammar.

Potentially.

If Masta let me out door.

Hark & Venn smelled leather, wax, hot metal.

Masta checked stump.

"Boring."

"Yes."

"Good."

She handed shell.

I did.

Liner.

Seat.

First strap.

Second.

Check rotation.

Stand between rails.

Shell shifted tiny amount.

I sat.

Retightened first strap.

Again.

Better.

Narrow test foot today had shallow wet tread cut into sole.

Not final.

Still temporary support post.

Still pale ugly shell.

Still looked like furniture apprentice made leg after losing bet.

Annoying.

"One hour maximum."

"Yes."

"Flat route."

"Yes."

"No docks."

"Yes."

"No wet stone if avoidable."

"Yes."

"Crutches with you."

"Obviously."

"Yes."

"Cruel."

"Effective."

Route:

shop to Bread Street.

Bread Street to old fountain.

Fountain around market edge.

Back by Glass Lane.

No stairs.

One shallow curb cut.

Mostly paving.

Maybe twelve-minute crutch route normally.

Today one hour allowed because walking slow, stopping, checking.

Nali waited outside.

"You supervising?"

"No."

"Then why here?"

"Wanted see you swear at curb."

Good friend.

Masta stood in doorway.

"Go."

I put crutches down.

Left.

Right.

Crutches light.

Left.

Right.

Door threshold.

Tiny rise.

I stopped.

Normally crutches crossed without thought.

Now left test foot needed clear placement.

I shortened step.

Right foot through.

Fine.

Outside.

Duskport.

Same street.

Different height.

That surprised me.

Not literally much.

Pelvis closer level changed eye line tiny amount.

Shoulders not hanging between crutches changed how head sat.

Street looked normal.

I did not.

Nali said, "Don't stare at horizon."

"I wasn't."

"You were."

"Shut up."

She left.

Actually left.

Good.

This was mine.

First half block I used both crutches every step.

Light contact.

Then medium because left foot landed too far out.

Stop.

Reset.

Walk honestly.

Masta voice lived rent-free now.

At bread stall, woman recognized me.

"Peg-Leg Greg."

I looked down.

"Technically."

She leaned over counter.

"Oh!"

There.

Not joke only now.

She looked genuinely delighted.

"That's new."

"Very."

"How is it?"

"Bad."

She laughed.

"Looks good."

"Those are different statements."

"Want roll?"

I almost said no because hands.

Then stopped.

There.

Small thing.

I tucked both crutches under right arm.

Not ideal.

Stable enough standing.

Left hand took roll.

Right hand paid one bit.

Both hands did transaction while body stood on two contact points.

Not rails.

Not Masta courtyard.

Street.

I stared at roll.

Seller said, "You okay?"

"Yes."

"It's bread."

"I know."

"You look emotional."

"Fuck bread."

She laughed harder.

I put crutches back down.

Walked.

Roll in bag.

One block.

Concentration expensive.

Every left placement required thought.

Right knee wanted old lock when crowd passed close.

Shoulders wanted lift.

Crutches wanted become primary again because they knew job.

I let them when needed.

That was part I had not expected emotionally.

Using crutches harder did not feel like failure.

Yesterday maybe would.

Today they were another tool.

Leg one tool.

Crutches another.

Switch based on problem.

At old fountain I sat.

Timer by bell tower maybe eighteen minutes since shop.

I loosened nothing.

Masta said do not adjust outside unless obvious emergency because she wanted wear data.

Residual limb warm.

General warm.

No point heat.

No pain.

Left hip working.

Right calf less than expected.

Good.

Standing from low fountain harder than walking.

Needed crutches strongly.

Right leg did most rise.

Shell caught load late.

Mental note.

Not ready for low chairs without arms.

Inventory.

Market edge busier.

I shortened route slightly to avoid wet fish paving.

Masta said flat, not stupid.

At one shop door woman came out carrying crate.

I stepped aside.

Old crutch habit: swing body quickly, plant both, clear lane.

New shell: slower lateral move.

I nearly crossed left foot wrong.

Caught with crutch.

No fall.

Heart jumped anyway.

There.

Not magic.

Sideways sucked.

I stood still until body stopped pretending embarrassment was emergency.

"Not you."

Then I moved on.

Glass Lane had curb cut.

Shallow.

Nali had been right.

I swore.

Not because impossible.

Because slope changed ankle geometry I did not have.

Test foot was rigid enough that downhill placement wanted slap.

I used both crutches fully.

Short step.

Right.

Left.

Fine.

Then uphill other side easier.

Good.

Thirty-five minutes.

I was sweating.

Not cardio exactly.

Concentration plus unfamiliar muscles.

I carried crutches bundled for five steps on flat Glass Lane.

No box.

No rail.

Five.

Then six.

At seventh, left foot started drifting outward.

I stopped at six.

That felt more important than making seven.

Old me would have made seven.

Old old me.

First-life twenty-something me definitely.

First-life fifty-nine me maybe not.

Depends who watching.

I smiled.

Nobody.

Good.

Near shop, I realized something.

I had been thinking about route.

Not leg.

For maybe thirty seconds.

Just where going.

Bread Street.

Fountain.

Glass Lane.

Then body had done enough that attention moved outward.

Thirty seconds.

Tiny.

Huge.

That was first glimpse.

Not hands.

Not standing.

Not even steps.

Attention.

Crutches had become invisible years ago because skill made them background.

This thing was screaming foreground every second.

Until it wasn't.

Thirty seconds.

Potential.

Back at shop forty-eight minutes after leaving.

Masta looked at clock.

"Early."

"Pattern degrading."

"Where?"

"Left foot drifting outward after six unsupported steps. Sideways movement bad. Down curb cut worse than up. Low fountain stand needs crutches heavily. General warmth, no point heat."

"Pain?"

"No."

"Numb?"

"No."

"Wet?"

"Sweaty but not sloshing."

"Poetry. Sit."

Shell off.

Skin told story.

Upper edge pink.

Lower-front mark faint again.

New small red oval toward outer distal side.

Masta touched around it.

"Tender?"

"Little."

"How little?"

"Two of ten."

"Good."

She marked shell.

"That's why you came early."

"Yes."

"That's why you keep leg eventually."

I looked at her.

"By stopping before skin teaches lesson with blood."

Fair.

She shaved tiny amount from inner pad and marked outer pocket for relief.

Then inspected foot tread.

Wear more on outer rear edge.

"Yes."

"Narrow foot helps roll. Might be too narrow if this persists."

"So broad?"

"Maybe neither."

She pulled third blank.

Medium width.

Split groove shallower.

"Craft is disappointing."

"Because answer is middle?"

"Often."

"Terrible."

"You want dramatic foot?"

"A little."

"Pay noble shop."

No.

She checked side stay marks.

"Still think no reinforced stay."

"Cheaper?"

"Probably some."

"How some?"

Good.

No fake discount.

"Next?"

"No shell tomorrow."

Of course.

"Then?"

"If skin clears, another outside trial with medium foot. Maybe longer. Then I decide final socket changes."

She stared.

"You have actual leg."

I looked at right.

"Funny."

"You mean finished work limb."

"Yes."

"When it stops giving me new information every time you wear it."

There.

No date.

Annoying.

Correct.

I left on crutches.

Fast.

Easy.

Familiar.

Halfway Anchor's End I noticed speed difference.

Crutches won by lot.

No contest.

But at door I stopped.

Normally one crutch under arm, free hand door, awkward pivot.

I remembered bread stall.

Both hands.

Not yet.

Soon.

"Then nickname revoked."

"Thank gods."

"Crutch Greg."

"Worse."

"Hop Greg."

"Stop."

"Greg."

"Acceptable."

Bad.

Slow.

Tiring.

Useful.

Different.

Small.

She got it.

"Oh."

World map.

Small.

Quiet.

# CHAPTER 426

## THE SCOUT

No shell.

Masta had been clear.

My skin agreed.

Mostly.

Outer-distal red oval faded overnight but not gone.

Tender maybe one.

No heat.

No open skin.

So recovery day.

Crutches.

Fast.

Familiar.

Annoyingly excellent.

At breakfast Sori said, "You look disappointed."

"I'm on best mobility system I currently own."

"You look disappointed while saying that."

"Humans contain multitudes."

"Salt moss notes."

"You made notes?"

"Herbalist asked what color roots were at upper arch versus inner wall. I didn't know."

"So you're going back?"

"Maybe."

Sori pointed spoon at him.

"Not selling?"

"No."

"Good."

"I might sell information."

We stared.

Maro blinked.

"What?"

"Nothing," I said. "Capitalism found herb boy."

He frowned.

"I don't like that."

Healthy.

East Hook board had my name nowhere.

Flex work.

Could wait.

Could labor exchange.

Could spend day pretending one faded pressure mark required spiritual retreat.

Tavren stood with mug.

"No shell?"

"Recovery day."

"Good."

"Work?"

He pointed.

Board:

**MERRY WAKE / DIVE HOLD**

**NORTH PIER / PRIVATE CREW**

**SOUTH CHAIN / IRONWORK**

Nothing.

"Maybe afternoon intake," he said. "No promise."

"Labor board."

"Probably."

I turned.

"Greg."

I looked back.

Tavren held folded notice.

"This came through harbor office. Might suit you. Might not."

He handed it over.

Cheap gray paper.

Stamped Duskport Outer Works.

**SHORT CONTRACT: ROUTE CONDITION / LOAD RECORD / FIELD OBSERVATION**

Location:

**GREYFIN ROAD / SALT MARSH CAUSEWAY / NORTH REACH DEPOT**

Three days minimum.

Possibly five.

Travel supplied from Duskport.

Return uncertain by one day due tide/weather.

Pay:

**4c per full route day**

**5c if assigned observation ledger**

Meals bunk supplied at depot.

No hazard premium unless marsh closure active.

I read twice.

Outside Duskport.

Not far.

Far enough.

"What's Greyfin Road?"

"North coastal supply route. Marsh causeway about twelve miles out."

"Problem?"

"Outer Works thinks one section settling. Carrier guild says road fine and survey markers wrong."

"Why me?"

"Didn't say you. Notice says condition/tally hand familiar with irregular load records."

"You thought me."

"Maybe."

"Recommendation?"

"No."

Of course.

"Who owns contract?"

"Outer Works. Field lead named Enna Ro."

Pella Ro?

Could be nothing.

Duskport surnames repeated like gulls.

"Departure?"

"Tomorrow first bell if filled."

There.

I looked west.

Hark & Venn.

Recovery day today.

Possible medium-foot trial after.

Tomorrow departure would conflict.

Masta might murder contract.

Or me.

Tavren said, "Read whole notice."

I did.

**Applicants with medical/fitting appointments may join at second relay if pre-cleared. Second relay departs following morning.**

There.

Choice.

Not trap.

"Two departure windows."

"Yes."

"How long second relay behind first?"

"One day."

"Same pay?"

"Starts when you join."

"Good."

Tavren drank.

"You want it."

"Maybe."

"You read money once. Read route three times."

Rude observation.

"Where does it go after North Reach?"

"Road continues to **Merehook** and upland split. Contract doesn't."

Merehook.

Never heard.

Good.

A road beyond Duskport.

Actual road.

Not carriage here and back before dinner.

Something in chest moved.

Not destiny.

Interest.

Training brain, apparently, now applied to geography.

What can I do now that I could not do yesterday?

Not walk twelve miles on trial leg.

Definitely not.

But go somewhere while leg process continued?

Maybe.

First I needed facts.

"Where do I apply?"

"Outer Works annex by North Gate."

"Thanks."

"Not recommendation."

"I know."

"Tell Masta before you vanish."

"I know."

"Tell your people too."

I looked at him.

"Which people?"

"The three who keep appearing around you."

Oh.

Right.

People.

I went North Gate.

Duskport outer works annex looked like government had discovered damp wood.

Long room.

Benches.

Maps.

Mud.

Three clerks arguing over axle widths.

I liked immediately.

Application desk woman asked skills.

"Condition records. Load tally. Recovery intake. Basic field notes. No structural authority. No rigging authority."

"Magic survey?"

"Limited observations only. No certification."

"Can walk road?"

There.

I looked at crutches.

"On crutches, yes, depending distance and pace."

"Causeway team rides supply wagon between sections. Field checks mostly under two hundred paces at a time."

Good.

"Any marsh work?"

"No wading unless emergency evacuation."

Better.

"Medical limitation?"

"Left below-knee amputation. Fitting in progress."

Clerk looked at crutches once.

"Need accommodation?"

"Boarding step if wagon high. Maybe extra minute mounting."

She wrote.

No drama.

"Field lead decides. Wait."

I waited.

Ten minutes.

Woman in dark green coat came out.

Mid-thirties maybe.

Hair in tight braid.

Left cheek scar from nose to ear.

No smile.

"Greg?"

"Yes."

"Enna Ro."

There.

"Any relation to Pella Ro?"

She stopped.

"My sister."

Oh.

"Venn diver?"

"Yes."

"Calf cut."

Her face changed tiny amount.

"You were there."

"Shore tally."

"She said somebody kept red-hold until line status cleared."

"That was me."

"Good."

Not warm.

Useful.

"Sit."

We sat map table.

Greyfin Road ran north from Duskport along coast, then inland across broad salt marsh on raised stone-and-timber causeway.

North Reach Depot sat beyond marsh.

Marks every quarter mile.

Three sections under dispute.

Carriers reported roughness but no visible collapse.

Outer Works survey pegs showed one segment settling **three to five fingers** relative prior season.

Carrier guild claimed markers themselves had shifted because one benchmark post leaned after storm.

"Who's right?"

Enna said, "If I knew, I wouldn't hire observation hand."

Good.

"What matters if causeway is settling?"

"Heavy freight restriction. Possibly close one lane."

"And if markers wrong?"

"Unnecessary restriction costs carriers money and North Reach fuel supply arrives late."

"How much?"

"Enough they're angry."

Human problem.

"Anybody wanting answer badly?"

Enna looked at me.

"Everyone."

There.

Carrier guild wanted road open.

Outer Works wanted not kill anyone.

North Reach depot wanted fuel before weather turn.

Marsh villages wanted supply carts but also did not want causeway failure flooding culverts.

No villain.

Correct answer might still hurt someone.

Excellent.

I hated that I thought excellent.

"What would I do?"

"Ride section wagon. Record axle class, visible wheel response, marker readings as called by survey tech, drainage state, timber sound notes, and any mismatch between road behavior and benchmark claim."

"I don't interpret survey?"

"No."

"Who does?"

"Me and Halen Brusk. Licensed road surveyor."

"Carrier representative?"

"Jorren Pike rides first day."

Of course.

"So someone can argue live."

"He will."

"Good."

She almost smiled.

"Pella said you're annoying."

"Good annoying?"

"She was bleeding. Hard to classify."

Fair.

"Pay notice says four, five observation ledger."

"You get five if I assign master field ledger. I probably will after first section if your handwriting doesn't offend me."

"My handwriting is beautiful."

"Then I'm already suspicious."

I liked her.

"Second relay?"

"You need fitting?"

"Yes. Trial limb."

"How long?"

"Unknown. Shop deciding geometry."

"Can you leave tomorrow?"

"Probably shouldn't."

"Then second relay. I need certainty tonight."

There.

Commit.

Not forever.

Four or five days away from Duskport, maybe six with weather.

Then maybe back.

Maybe road beyond.

Merehook.

I said, "I need talk to my limbwright and companions."

Enna nodded.

"By sixth bell."

"If I join second relay, when does contract end?"

"Three field days minimum after arrival. Could extend two if disputed segment requires repeat loaded pass. You can refuse extension after minimum."

"Return transport guaranteed?"

"To Duskport, yes, first available works wagon. Weather may delay."

"Good."

"You ask better questions than most."

"Experience."

"With roads?"

"With bad jobs."

That got actual smile.

Small.

I left with conditional slot paper.

Hark & Venn next.

Masta read notice.

Then looked at me.

"No."

"No what?"

"Not tomorrow first relay."

"I know."

"Good."

"Second relay?"

She read again.

"Maybe."

Better.

"Skin?"

She checked.

Outer-distal oval nearly gone.

"Tomorrow medium foot trial. Short. Shop/courtyard. If boring, I can send shell with you?"

I froze.

"What?"

"Not finished. Trial use."

"Outside Duskport?"

"Maybe."

There.

That was new.

Masta held finger up.

"Maybe means maybe. If tomorrow shows same pressure problem, no. If clean, I can convert temporary post to sturdier travel test setup and give you strict wear schedule."

"How strict?"

"Very."

"Hours?"

"Probably thirty minutes morning, thirty evening first road day. Crutches primary. No marsh edge. No carrying load while walking unless flat and supervised by common sense, which worries me because yours is selective."

"Rude."

"Also daily skin check. If red mark lasts more than twenty minutes after removal, stop use until clear. If blister, stop. If pain increases, stop."

"Finished hardware?"

"Still no."

"Foot?"

"Medium test foot if tomorrow works."

"Side stay?"

"Still probably no reinforced stay."

"Cost?"

"Still not promising."

Of course.

"Would travel delay final build?"

"A few days."

"Would travel data help?"

"If you follow rules, yes. Different surfaces. Wagon mounting. Real sitting. Real fatigue."

There.

Road could become fitting data.

Not reason to go.

Useful if went.

"Do not choose contract because you think I need field test," Masta said.

"I wasn't."

"Good. Choose contract because you want contract."

That landed.

I did.

Want contract.

Not just money.

Road.

Marsh.

North Reach.

People arguing about whether causeway was safe.

Possibility of continuing farther after.

Merehook.

I wanted door.

Again.

Anchor's End.

Sori read conditional paper.

"Three to five days."

"Minimum three field days. Travel makes more."

"Maybe six total."

"Maybe."

Dask took notice.

"Marsh causeway?"

"Yes."

His eyes lit.

Bad.

"Settlement dispute?"

"Yes."
