Worse.

"Survey benchmarks?"

"Yes."

He looked at me.

"I'm coming."

"No."

"Why?"

"Because you just made face."

"What face?"

Sori said, "He made face."

Maro read over Dask shoulder.

"North Reach has salt fen."

Oh no.

"You're both terrible."

Sori said, "I want to go too."

I stared.

"Why?"

"Because we didn't come here to become your waiting room furniture."

Fair.

"Also," she added, "route-contract disputes are interesting."

"There it is."

"And if we stay in Duskport while you leave, we pay rooms separately and then maybe chase you later."

Money logic.

Strong.

"Contract only hires me."

"We can buy seats on second relay if available."

Dask already reading travel note.

"Non-contract passengers allowed to North Reach for two copper each if wagon capacity remains."

Sori looked at him.

"Useful."

"I read entire notice."

"Terrifying."

Maro said quietly, "I'd like to go."

There.

Different voice.

Not excitement exactly.

Want.

"Why?"

He took moment.

"Because road keeps going."

Oh.

Same.

We were all infected.

I sat.

"If you come, I'm not paying fares."

Sori said, "Obviously."

Dask said, "I can pay two copper."

Maro nodded.

"Me too."

Sori looked at me.

"You're not responsible for everybody just because you found notice."

That was aimed deeper.

I knew.

"Fine."

"Fine we're coming?"

"Fine you can try seats."

Dask immediately stood.

"North Gate closes applications sixth bell."

"Sit down."

"Why?"

"Because I haven't accepted mine."

Three faces.

I hated democracy.

Again.

Why hesitate?

Fitting.

Money.

Duskport work.

Carrow farther behind.

Unknown road.

Exactly.

Unknown road.

That was point.

I had spent years making subtraction normal.

Then Carrow made belonging normal.

Now Duskport had made possibility noisy again.

Not because leg fixed me.

Leg not even mine yet.

Because I had started asking what next instead of what safely fits existing life.

Could still say no.

Important.

But did I want no?

No.

I wanted road.

So I went back North Gate before sixth bell.

Enna Ro at desk.

"Decision?"

"Second relay."

"Certain?"

"Yes."

She stamped conditional slot.

**GREG / FIELD OBSERVATION HAND / SECOND RELAY**

"Report North Gate before first bell day after tomorrow. Pack for six days. Could be four. Could be seven if weather ugly."

"Return guaranteed eventually."

"Eventually is government specialty."

"Good."

"Your companions?"

I looked behind.

Sori, Dask, Maro at passenger desk.

Of course already there.

"Apparently."

Enna watched them.

"They useful?"

"Sometimes accidentally."

"Good enough."

Passenger seats confirmed.

Two copper each from them.

Their money.

Not mine.

That mattered enough to notice.

Back outside, road gate stood open.

Beyond Duskport walls, north road ran between low dunes until distance turned gray.

We were not leaving today.

Not tomorrow either.

Day after.

One fitting test between.

Maybe trial shell came.

Maybe not.

Either way contract existed.

Road existed.

Duskport had stopped being destination.

It was node.

That felt right.

Dask said, "Greyfin causeway uses paired settlement posts according to notice. If one benchmark leaned after storm, comparative seasonal readings could produce false settlement if reference geometry wasn't re-established."

Sori said, "We have not left gate."

"I'm preparing."

Maro asked, "Do marshes have biting flies?"

Nobody answered.

He looked worried.

Good.

Adventure.

At Anchor's End Pell heard.

"Leaving?"

"Temporarily."

"You said that about Duskport."

"Still true."

"Peg-Leg Greg becomes Road Greg."

"No."

"Marsh Greg."

"Worse."

"Government Greg."

"Absolutely not."

He grinned.

"Peg-Leg sticks."

Unfortunately.

I packed that night.

Not much.

Quietener.

WS-F/9.

HT-LP-4.

Tavi rock.

Clothes.

Notebook.

Repair kit.

Masta card.

Crutch spare wrap.

No shell.

Maybe tomorrow changed that.

Maybe not.

I looked at purse.

Forty-three copper.

No pay today.

Contract could make twelve minimum if three full route days at base, more if ledger.

Useful.

But money wasn't first thought.

That surprised me.

First thought was road.

Second was fitting.

Third money.

Carrow somewhere after all three.

Not less important.

Just farther.

I wrote short note to Cala/Lyssa/Tavi even though last reply barely left.

**Possible route north from Duskport. Greyfin causeway / North Reach. Three to five field days plus travel. Not leaving until day after tomorrow. I will send again from depot if post exists.**

Then Tavi line:

**Marsh probably has monsters. Dask says statistically this is irresponsible.**

Dask was downstairs.

He had not said that.

Could later.

I slept badly.

Not worry.

Anticipation.

Training brain applied to road was apparently called being awake.

Annoying.

But there.

Duskport still had unfinished leg.

Unfinished work.

Unfinished people.

And now road north.

Good.

Story could hold more than one unfinished thing.

# CHAPTER 427

## THE TRAVELER

Packing for six days took less time than packing for three.

Three days invited optimism.

Six invited socks.

I had two shirts.

One spare trousers.

Underthings.

Rain wrap.

Notebook.

Repair kit.

Crutch wrap.

Quietener.

Current reader.

Load tile.

Tavi rock.

Porter harness.

Money.

That was most life.

Sori had list.

Of course.

"You have food?"

"Outer Works feeds contract hand."

"On wagon?"

"First day ration."

"After contract?"

"I buy food."

"With what container?"

I looked at bag.

She looked at me.

"Container," I said.

"Good."

Dask had packed three pamphlets, two notebooks, spare charcoal, measuring cord, a little brass angle square, and souvenir mirror.

"Why mirror?"

"I paid for it."

"That doesn't make it useful."

"It makes leaving it here irrational."

"No it doesn't."

"It might be useful."

"For what?"

He thought.

"Reflecting."

Maro had less than me.

One bag.

Blanket roll.

Herb paper.

Knife.

Cup.

Shell necklace now tied around strap.

Sori looked at his bag.

"That's it?"

"Yes."

"For six days?"

"Maybe seven."

"That does not improve answer."

He shrugged.

"Road has towns."

There.

Different species.

I packed one extra pair socks.

Growth.

Morning stump mark gone.

Actually gone.

No tenderness.

No heat.

I pressed outer-distal spot.

Nothing.

Fourth shell session.

Last Duskport day.

Road tomorrow.

I tried not to make those same sentence.

Hark & Venn had trial build on bench.

Different foot.

Medium width.

Not broad dock foot.

Not narrow travel test.

Middle.

Disappointing craft.

Masta had also changed support post.

Temporary one replaced by darker ash shaft with metal ferrule above foot and leather-wrapped junction under shell.

Still not final.

Looked less like furniture.

"Road test?" I asked.

"Maybe."

"You enjoy maybe."

"Maybe keeps fools alive."

Fair.

She checked skin.

Then shell.

Then me.

"Put it on."

I did.

Better first seat.

Straps held.

Stand.

Rails.

Weight.

Medium foot felt different immediately.

Narrow one rolled easier forward.

This one planted.

Not broad-foot planted.

Less side uncertainty.

I took four rail steps.

Turn.

Four back.

"Again," Masta said.

Again.

She watched pelvis.

I watched nothing.

Walk honestly.

Courtyard.

Crutches light.

Left.

Right.

Left.

Right.

Medium foot less clever.

Better.

I hated that.

Masta saw face.

"What?"

"I wanted narrow one."

"Why?"

"Looked faster."

"Wasn't."

"Eventually maybe."

"Eventually you can buy second foot."

There.

Not one perfect leg.

System.

Nali had different wet-deck foot.

Of course.

"How much second foot?"

"You don't own first."

"Planning."

"Stop."

I stopped.

Twenty courtyard minutes.

No outer drift until late.

Turn still ugly.

Side step still bad.

Low bench stand improved little because foot gave more lateral confidence, but crutches still did work.

Masta made me carry five-pound box again.

Both crutches right hand.

Box left.

Eight steps.

Nine.

At ten, pelvis tilted.

Stopped nine.

"Why stop?"

"Tilt."

"Good."

She took box.

Then made me do something stupid.

Cup.

Half full water.

"Why?"

"Because road doesn't carry test boxes."

"Road carries cups?"

"Inns do."

Fair.

Crutches bundled.

Cup right hand.

Left hand empty.

Six steps.

Water stayed mostly in cup.

"Mostly," Masta said.

"Floor thirsty."

"Bad excuse."

Then she sent me outside.

Not full route.

Shop front to corner and back.

Dry stone.

Maybe ten minutes.

Medium foot handled curb cut better.

Not good.

Better.

Down slope still rigid.

I used crutches fully.

No heroics.

Back.

Shell off.

Skin.

Pink upper edge.

No lower-front mark.

Outer-distal almost nothing.

Masta waited ten minutes.

Pink faded.

Waited another ten.

Gone.

She nodded once.

That was more exciting than applause.

"You can take it."

There.

I looked at build.

"Trial build."

"Trial build."

"Not mine."

"Mine until paid and final accepted."

"If wagon crushes it?"

"You owe me conversation I will enjoy more than you."

"Good."

"If marsh eats it, I charge you for what marsh keeps."

"Less good."

She gave rules.

Written.

Because she knew me.

**ROAD TEST / GREG / LEFT BKA**

Morning wear: maximum thirty minutes first day.

Evening wear: maximum thirty minutes.

Not consecutive.

No loaded walking except ordinary light hand carry on flat stable surface.

No marsh edge.

No mud deeper than sole tread.

No wagon jumping.

No running.

No ladders.

No worksite wear unless Enna and Greg both agree surface is stable and wear window remains.

Crutches primary.

Skin check immediately after removal and twenty minutes later.

Persistent redness at twenty minutes means no second wear period.

Blister, open skin, numbness, rising pain, point heat: stop until Masta or qualified fitter.

Daily notes:

surface.

time.

pain.

heat.

redness.

pattern failure.

"You forgot mood," I said.

"I don't care."

"Cruel."

"If mood causes blister, write it."

I folded paper.

Then she gave me two spare liners.

"Included?"

"Trial supplies. Return what you don't ruin."

"If I ruin both?"

"Then you learned something expensive."

"How expensive?"

"Go on road, Greg."

I smiled.

She looked at me.

"One more thing."

"What?"

"Do not make leg prove trip was good idea."

That landed.

"I know."

"You know many things."

"Some."

"Road can be good and leg can have bad day. Leg can be good and road can be stupid. Keep separate."

"Yes."

"And if you come back with bloody stump because you wanted story?"

"You remove foot."

"I remove your other one."

"Aggressive craft."

"Effective."

I left shop on crutches.

Trial build wrapped in waxed canvas across porter harness.

Not wearing it.

Carrying it.

Strange.

My future leg weighed enough to be annoying.

Good problem.

North Gate briefing at second bell.

Outer Works annex had map on wall.

Greyfin Road ran north from Duskport through low farms, then east around salt fen, then narrow raised causeway across marsh fingers toward North Reach Depot.

Three marked check sections.

Section One: south approach.

Section Two: old timber culvert reach.

Section Three: north stone shoulder near benchmark dispute.

Halen Brusk, licensed surveyor, was thinner than expected.

No reason expected thick.

Gray beard.

Ink on fingers.

He spoke like man who had spent career being interrupted by people who wanted one number.

"Settlement is not one number."

Jorren Pike said, "It is when one number closes road."

There.

Carrier Guild representative broad, red-faced, expensive raincoat.

Not villain.

Just already annoyed.

Enna stood between map and table.

"This is briefing. Save argument for marsh."

Jorren folded arms.

Halen unfolded ruler.

Threatening in different way.

Enna pointed.

"First relay establishes control points today. We follow tomorrow with repeated readings, wheel response, drainage, timber notes, and loaded-pass observation if weather permits."

She looked at me.

"You get section ledger after first stop."

"Master?"

"If first stop is clean."

"Pay?"

"Four until assigned. Five for full days carrying master ledger."

Good.

Jorren looked at crutches.

Then harness.

Then waxed bundle.

"You field hand?"

"Observation."

"Survey?"

"No."

"Engineer?"

"No."

"Then what are you observing?"

"What they tell me to."

He stared.

"Useful."

"Sometimes."

Enna almost smiled.

Halen did not.

"The problem," Halen said, "is reference."

He tapped three points.

"Old benchmark B-seven leans three degrees east after spring storm. Guild claims prior season comparison is contaminated because B-seven moved. They may be right."

Jorren said, "We are right."

"They may be right," Halen repeated, louder.

"Outer Works secondary markers suggest settlement independent of B-seven. Those markers may also have moved because marsh moves."

"Exactly," Jorren said.

"You saying exactly does not make us friends."

Sori would like him.

"So," Enna said, "we are not going north to prove road bad. We are not going north to prove benchmark bad. We are going north to make claims smaller until somebody with authority can decide what road can carry."

Good.

Jorren said, "Meanwhile fuel carts wait."

"Yes."

"North Reach has two days reserve at normal burn."

There.

New number.

Enna looked at him.

"Then bring me honest axle classes tomorrow."

"I will."

"Not lightened wagons for demonstration."

His face hardened.

"You accusing guild?"

"I am removing temptation before it becomes accusation."

Silence.

Human problem.

Correct procedure did not make people like each other.

Halen tapped north shoulder.

"Village carts use this spur. If heavy freight restricted, local carts may still pass depending class. Do not write 'road closed' unless I say road closed."

I wrote that.

Jorren noticed.

"You write everything?"

"No."

"What don't you write?"

"Things nobody says."

He looked at me longer.

"We'll see."

Probably.

Briefing ended with supply issue.

Outer Works gave me bedroll, route ration, waxed ledger cover, two pencils, chalk, small folding rule.

All loaned except ration.

I signed.

No shopping chapter.

Excellent.

Enna checked wagon boarding.

High bed.

Rear step narrow.

Normally I could manage with crutches and arm pull.

She asked if I wanted stool.

"Yes."

Simple.

She wrote **BOARDING STOOL / SECOND RELAY**.

No dignity died.

Training brain liked that too.

Baseline.

Solve actual problem.

Not imagined one.

At Anchor's End, companions had transformed one table into departure disaster.

Sori had food.

Dask had maps.

Maro had somehow acquired second blanket.

"Where from?"

"Pell."

Pell shouted from desk, "Deposit!"

"What deposit?"

"If he loses blanket, one copper."

Maro said, "I didn't pay yet."

"Then hostage arrangement."

"That's not deposit," Sori said.

"Inn law."

"You don't have inn law."

"I have desk."

Hard to argue.

I put trial build on chair.

Everyone stopped.

Dask first.

"You're taking it?"

"Trial."

Maro smiled.

Sori looked at waxed bundle.

"Rules?"

I handed paper.

She read.

All of it.

Then handed back.

"Good."

"That's all?"

"What do you want?"

"Lecture."

"Masta already wrote one."

"Fair."

Dask asked, "Can you wear it during observations?"

"Maybe stable surfaces inside wear window. Not default."

"Would altered posture affect measurement?"

There.

I paused.

"Maybe."

He nodded.

"Then record whether worn."

Fuck.

"That's good."

"Obviously."

New training brain met Dask.

Dangerous combination.

Sori said, "Do not become two idiots."

"No promises," Dask said.

I looked at him.

"That was almost joke."

"It was accurate forecast."

Worse.

We ate early.

No Black Keel.

No strong beer.

Road before first bell meant sleep mattered.

Pell gave wake knock schedule.

Sori checked passenger chits.

Maro tied shell necklace tighter.

Dask packed mirror again after I removed it once as public service.

He found it.

Unfortunate.

I wrote Carrow note even though yesterday's had barely left.

**Leaving north before first bell. Greyfin Road, then North Reach. Three field days minimum. I have trial leg with me, not finished one. I am not walking marsh in it. Tell Tavi that specifically.**

Then added:

**Road keeps going after depot. I don't know if we do.**

That was honest.

I sealed.

Pell promised morning post sack.

Upstairs, bags ready.

Crutches beside bed.

Trial build wrapped at foot.

For first time since leaving Carrow, departure did not feel like temporary excursion inside temporary excursion.

Bright Gull had been trip.

Sable Quay had been stop.

Duskport had become place.

Now road had another road after it.

That changed scale.

I thought about first plan.

Two nights.

Three days.

Cute.

I had been gone long enough for Carrow letters to chase me.

Now I was leaving address they knew.

Not disappearing.

Moving.

Difference mattered.

Trial build did too.

Tomorrow morning I might wear it thirty minutes on stable road.

Or skin might complain and I would carry it all day.

Either way wagon went north.

Good.

That was right.

I did not need every unfinished thing solved before next thing began.

Apparently adulthood was mostly carrying unfinished things between towns.

First life should have taught me.

Maybe it did.

I was bad student.

I slept.

Before first bell came early.

# CHAPTER 428

## THE WITNESS

Before first bell was offensive.

Not morally.

Personally.

North Gate lamps still burned.

Duskport smelled like wet stone, horse breath, old beer, and people who had made regrettable scheduling decisions.

Second relay wagon waited under arch.

Two wagons actually.

Outer Works wagon first.

Passenger/supply wagon second.

Enna stood beside first with clipboard.

Of course awake.

Halen looked awake in technical sense.

Jorren looked furious at morning itself.

Good.

Shared enemy.

Boarding stool sat behind wagon.

Someone had painted **GREG** on it.

I stared.

Enna said, "Problem?"

"No."

"Then use it."

I used it.

Easy.

Annoying.

Useful.

Baseline.

Sori climbed passenger wagon without ceremony.

Dask nearly fell because he was reading map while stepping.

Maro caught his sleeve.

"Thank you," Dask said.

"Look where feet go."

"I was."

"You were looking north."

"North is where feet are going."

Sori said, "Not if you die at gate."

Pell had come to see us off.

Why.

"You run inn."

"Desk survives fifteen minutes."

"Does it?"

"Probably."

He handed Maro blanket receipt.

Then pointed at waxed leg bundle strapped behind my seat.

"Peg-Leg Greg officially exported."

"Trial."

"Foreign market doesn't care."

"We're still in same region."

"Foreign enough."

Gate opened.

Wagon moved.

Duskport passed backward.

Not dramatic.

No music.

Stone arch.

Warehouse wall.

Beer sign.

Fish carts waiting outside gate.

Then road.

There.

Actual road.

Greyfin ran north between low walls and wet fields.

Duskport hinterland flatter than Carrow side.

Long drainage ditches.

Farmhouses built on shallow stone rises.

Windbreak rows bent east.

Salt sat faint in air even after harbor disappeared behind us.

First hour mostly wheels.

Horses.

Mud at road edges.

Workers heading south toward city.

Milk cart.

Two charcoal wagons.

One funeral cart covered gray cloth.

Nobody stopped story for us.

Good.

At first relay post, Enna called ten minutes.

Stable yard.

Packed gravel.

Flat.

Dry enough.

I looked at waxed bundle.

Then clock.

Morning thirty-minute window.

Could use here.

Could save for field section.

Masta: don't make leg prove trip.

Field section mattered more.

I left it wrapped.

Training brain unhappy.

Good sign.

Dask noticed.

"You're not using it?"

"Not yet."

"Surface is appropriate."

"So is later."

He considered.

"Resource allocation."

"Sure."

Sori handed me tea.

"He means patience."

"I know."

"Do you?"

"Sometimes."

North of relay post road narrowed.

Fields thinned.

Ground turned pale.

Grass shorter.

Water showed between hummocks.

Salt fen.

Not marsh yet.

Fen first.

Silver grass.

Shallow pools reflecting white sky.

Birds with long red legs yelling like offended accountants.

Maro leaned so far from passenger wagon I expected him to fall.

Sori grabbed back of coat.

"I wasn't falling."

"You were planning it."

"There are flowers."

"Flowers remain if you sit."

Dask pointed toward road marker.

"Those posts are leaning opposite wind."

Sori said, "You're passenger."

"I can possess eyes recreationally."

Fair.

First section came near second bell.

South approach.

Outer Works first-relay wagon already there.

Three survey poles.

Two chalked wheel lanes.

One loaded carrier wagon parked off road.

Village carts queued farther back.

Not many.

Enough.

People waiting because our answer mattered.

There.

Field work.

Enna climbed down.

"Passengers stay at relay shelter or east verge. Not survey lane."

Sori nodded.

Dask opened mouth.

She pointed at him.

"Especially you."

He closed mouth.

Excellent authority.

I used stool.

Then crutches.

Waxed trial build stayed wagon.

Road surface first.

Packed stone over timber/earth base.

Slight crown.

Drain ditch both sides.

South approach looked normal until you watched wheels.

Then maybe not.

First village cart rolled under Enna's instruction.

Light axle.

Two wheels.

No visible sink beyond ordinary rut.

Second cart heavier.

Left wheel dipped slightly near chalk mark S-one-C.

Not dramatic.

Half finger maybe at rim.

Could be existing rut.

I wrote:

**08:17 / LOCAL CART / 2-AXLE LIGHT / LEFT WHEEL VISIBLE DIP AT S1-C / MAGNITUDE NOT MEASURED / NO AUDIBLE TIMBER RESPONSE**

Enna glanced.

"Good."

Third wagon Carrier Guild.

Jorren's people.

Four-wheel fuel wagon.

Not full fuel load.

Halen asked axle class.

Driver answered.

Jorren answered different.

Silence.

Driver looked at Jorren.

There.

Not corruption.

Problem.

Enna said, "Driver first."

Driver swallowed.

"Class six empty. Class nine loaded. We're half water ballast today, so seven maybe eight depending rear."

Jorren said, "Declared seven."

Halen said, "Declared is not measured."

Jorren's jaw tightened.

"You asked for demonstration pass. We brought one."

"We asked honest axle class," Enna said.

"It is honest."

"Then let scale team write it."

Roadside portable scale blocks came out.

Not instant.

Twenty minutes.

Everyone waiting.

Village woman in queue shouted, "My milk doesn't care about your blocks."

Nobody answered.

Good answer unavailable.

Scale result: front class seven equivalent.

Rear closer eight.

So Jorren was not lying exactly.

Driver wasn't either.

Different shorthand.

Halen looked offended by ambiguity as profession.

"Run."

Wagon moved.

At S-one-C rear left wheel dropped visibly more than front.

Timber beneath gave one low knock.

Not crack.

Knock.

Halen held up hand.

"Again slower."

Jorren said, "You want us making rut worse?"

"I want repeatability."

Enna said, "One repeat. Then stop."

Second pass same wheel lane.

Dip repeated.

Low knock again.

Maybe same depth.

I wrote exact observations.

No `settlement`.

No `failure`.

No `safe`.

Jorren watched page.

"You didn't write cause."

"No."

"Why not?"

"Didn't observe one."

"Useful."

Hard to tell if insult.

Probably both.

Halen set level staff.

First relay had established temporary control from stone abutment farther south, independent of B-seven.

Today repeated elevation at S-one-C showed surface lower than yesterday by almost nothing.

Within expected reading variation.

But compared with last-season archived shoulder notch, current top was down around two fingers.

Maybe.

Except archived notch itself had uncertain repair history.

Halen muttered for two full minutes.

Jorren said, "So nothing."

Halen turned.

"No. Not nothing. We have repeated wheel response and historical mismatch. We do not yet have reference chain strong enough to call long-term settlement magnitude. Those are different statements."

"Can fuel cross?"

"Not my authority."

"Can it cross?"

"You keep asking surveyor transport policy."

Jorren looked at Enna.

"Can it cross?"

Enna said, "One monitored class-eight test pass has crossed. That is what happened."

"North Reach has two days."

"I know."

"Then somebody needs say yes or no."

Village woman again:

"Some of us also need road."

She had milk cart.

Jorren turned.

"I'm trying keep road open."

"For your wagons."

"For fuel."

"And your fee."

His face went redder.

"Both can be true."

That shut something down.

Not conflict.

Simplification.

Good.

Enna pointed at shelter.

"Ten minutes. Then drainage walk."

I checked time.

Still no trial leg.

Field section stable enough near shelter.

Could use morning wear now.

But road job active.

I asked Enna.

"I have a thirty-minute prosthetic road-test window. Stable surface only. If I wear it during drainage walk, I record it because posture may affect my observations. Any objection?"

She looked at surface.

Then route.

Drainage walk stayed on packed shoulder south/east, no ditch entry.

"Crutches primary. Stop if it distracts records."

"Yes."

"And Halen, don't hand him the staff."

Halen said, "I wasn't going to."

Enna looked at him.

"Good. Keep it that way."

Trial build on.

Wagon step/stool made donning annoying but possible seated.

Liner.

Socket.

Straps.

Medium foot.

Stand.

Stable.

Not graceful.

Maro saw from shelter.

Raised hand.

I raised mine.

Then immediately grabbed crutch because balance shifted.

Progress.

Drainage shoulder first five minutes fine.

Crutches light contact.

Notebook in left hand.

That was new travel affordance.

Normally notebook meant stop or tuck crutch.

Now I could move slowly with one crutch doing more work, other light, notebook open.

Not both hands free.

Still better.

I recorded:

**PROSTHETIC TRIAL WORN 08:54-**

Because Dask was right.

Annoying.

Drainage itself mattered.

South ditch flowed.

East culvert mouth partially choked by salt grass and storm wrack.

Not blocked.

Reduced.

Water line stain showed recent higher stand.

Halen measured.

Enna asked road tender when last cleared.

"Six days."

"Before rain?"

"Before."

Jorren said, "Then that's maintenance, not settlement."

Halen said, "Drainage loading can produce settlement."

"Can."

"Yes."

"Did it?"

"I dislike you."

"Likewise."

Enna said, "Excellent. Keep working."

I almost laughed and lost left-foot placement.

Crutch caught.

There.

Attention cost.

I stopped.

Reset.

Wrote **brief lateral placement error while laughing; surface flat; no pain** in private fit notes, not road ledger.

Two systems.

Keep separate.

Masta would approve maybe.

Maybe.

At twelve minutes, foot felt stable.

At eighteen, left hip tired.

At twenty-two, shoulder started lifting.

I switched crutches to full support and kept shell as contact only.

At twenty-six, enough.

I stopped before thirty.

Removed shell at wagon.

Skin pink upper edge.

No point pain.

No outer-distal mark visible immediately.

Timer.

Twenty-minute check later while Halen argued with culvert tender:

pink gone.

Good.

No evening decision yet.

Road came first.

Enna gave me new ledger.

Bigger.

Waxed cover.

More columns.

"Master observation," she said.

There.

"Today?"

"From now. First section was clean."

Pay five copper for full days carrying master ledger.

Today became five if finished clean.

Not earned yet.

Good.

I signed handoff time.

Jorren noticed.

"Promotion?"

"More paper."

"Same thing in government."

Enna said, "Outer Works isn't government."

He looked at her.

"You collect road fees."

"Contract authority."

"Government with invoice."

Halen laughed.

First time.

We moved north before noon.

Passenger wagon followed.

Sori asked at water stop, "How bad?"

"Which thing?"

"Road."

"Don't know."

She nodded.

Good answer.

Dask asked, "Did you record prosthetic wear state?"

"Yes."

He looked pleased enough to be irritating.

Maro held handful pale seed heads.

"These grow only where salt water reaches sometimes."

"Allowed?"

"Road tender said yes."

Good.

Independent people.

By midday fen became marsh.

Real marsh.

Causeway rose above water fingers on old timber and stone.

No rail.

Low posts every fifty paces.

Water dark green-brown.

Reeds taller than person.

Bird calls everywhere.

And something large moved once beneath surface parallel to wagon.

Tavi would ask.

I looked.

Only wake.

Sea monster maybe inland edition.

Second section at old timber culvert.

Here road looked worse.

Not collapsed.

Wavy.

One shoulder cracked.

Standing water on north side despite ditch cut.

First-relay chalk marks showed two timber-sound points.

Halen tapped decking through access slots.

One solid.

One dull.

Jorren heard dull.

Everyone did.

His face changed.

Small.

Not surrender.

Concern.

"That wasn't on spring report."

Halen said, "Correct."

"Could be local timber."

"Yes."

"Not whole causeway."

"Yes."

"Then don't close whole causeway."

"I have not closed anything."

Jorren pointed at dull point.

"Can you shore that?"

Enna answered.

"Maybe by tomorrow if crib crew available."

"Fuel convoy reaches south post tonight."

There.

Clock tightened.

Village representative from north side arrived on pony.

Older woman.

Blue scarf.

Name **Tera Voss**.

She wanted school and clinic carts allowed regardless heavy-freight decision.

Jorren wanted controlled fuel passage tonight or dawn.

Halen wanted enough data from section three before recommendation.

Enna wanted no one moving heavy convoy until field authority saw north benchmark zone.

All reasonable.

No clean answer.

Tera said, "If you hold everything, clinic oil arrives with guild fuel."

Jorren said, "Exactly."

She looked at him.

"I wasn't helping you."

"Still true."

Enna rubbed temple.

"We reach section three by fourth bell if weather holds. Decision conference at North Reach after. Until then current restriction stands: class six local traffic, no heavy freight."

Jorren said, "Fuel loses another half day."

"Yes."

"And if road turns out fine?"

"Then we lost half day."

"Who pays?"

Nobody answered.

Because somebody always did.

We kept moving.

That was road.

Not scenery.

Not freedom.

Not job only.

A line carrying everyone's problem at once.

By third bell clouds built west.

Wind flattened reeds.

Section three ahead somewhere beyond bend.

North Reach smoke faint on horizon.

Duskport gone completely.

Not visible.

Not practical distance for dinner.

Good.

I sat front of Outer Works wagon with master ledger on knees.

Trial limb wrapped behind me again.

Crutches under bench.

Enna drove.

Halen reread notes.

Jorren rode second horse alongside because sitting made him argue more.

Maybe less.

Hard to tell.

I looked north.

Road kept going.

Now finally we were on it.

And I was being paid to write down what it did.

# CHAPTER 429

## THE SCRIBE

North Reach smoke stayed ahead for forty minutes.

Road did that.

Showed destination.

Kept charging distance.

Section Three sat on stone shoulder where marsh narrowed against low black ridge.

First solid ground since farms.

Mostly.

Causeway widened into old turnout with three stone posts, one survey pillar, one dead tree, and enough people waiting to make evidence socially inconvenient.

Fuel guild had representative cart.

Outer Works first relay had two tenders.

Village had Tera plus three others.

Halen looked pleased.

Not by people.

By stones.

"Finally," he said.

Jorren climbed down.

"You say that now."

B-seven obvious.

Waist-high dressed stone with brass cap.

Leaning east.

Three degrees maybe.

Could see without instrument.

Halen walked around it like doctor around patient he disliked personally.

"Do not touch."

Nobody touching.

He pointed to base.

Hairline separation west side.

Fresh mortar patch south.

Jorren said, "There."

"There what?"

"Moved."

"Yes."

That quieted everyone.

Jorren blinked.

"You're agreeing?"

"I have agreed benchmark moved since yesterday's briefing. Question is when, how much, and whether other references moved with it."

"Then old comparison is bad."

"Comparison to B-seven alone is bad."

"That's what guild said."

"Guild said road is fine because B-seven moved. Those are not same sentence."

Jorren rubbed face.

"I hate surveyors."

"Profession survives."

Enna pointed me toward first-relay table.

"Ledger."

Work.

Good.

I took time.

Section.

Weather.

Surface.

Traffic hold.

People present by role, not opinion.

Halen set instrument on ridge-side pin R-three.

That one was iron driven into exposed black stone.

Older than causeway repair.

First relay had uncovered it yesterday under lichen and dirt.

Second control farther north, R-four, sat in quarry face.

Two solid-rock references.

Better.

Not perfect.

Nothing perfect.

Halen ran line.

Assistant held rod at B-seven cap.

Then road crown.

Then north shoulder notch.

Again.

Again from R-four.

Numbers went into his survey book, not mine.

Mine got observations and references used.

Jorren paced.

Tera did not.

She watched road south.

"Fuel at south post yet?" she asked.

Jorren checked pocket watch.

"Should be within half bell."

"Clinic has enough lamp oil tonight. Maybe tomorrow."

"Fuel load includes clinic drums."

"I know."

"Then why say like I'm withholding?"

"Because your heavy wagons carry their own problem with solution."

Jorren opened mouth.

Closed.

"Fair," he said.

Progress.

Halen finished first set.

"Again."

Jorren groaned.

"You just did again."

"Now temperature settled."

"Temperature moved road?"

"Instrument."

"Of course."

I liked Jorren more every hour.

Not because right.

Because suffering honestly.

While Halen repeated, I inspected road surface with Enna.

No shell.

Crutches.

Morning wear enough.

Stone turnout dry and stable enough technically, but field attention mattered more.

Masta gave allowance, not quota.

Road crown near B-seven showed two old patched wheel ruts.

North shoulder had narrow crack running diagonal from drainage slot toward edge.

First relay chalk marked yesterday end points.

Today crack looked same by eye.

I measured length with folding rule.

Recorded.

Width variable, mostly hairline, one point maybe half nail thickness.

Not settlement amount.

Crack geometry.

Enna said, "Good."

Drainage slot below was clear.

Water moving.

Unlike Section Two.

That mattered.

Halen finally called us.

"B-seven is useless for elevation comparison."

Jorren said, "Thank you."

Halen held up finger.

"Do not enjoy yet."

"I knew there was second sentence."

"Rock controls agree with each other within acceptable repeat. Relative to them, north road crown is lower than archived quarry-face tie by between two and three fingers."

Silence.

Tera asked, "Meaning?"

Halen looked pained.

Not because question bad.

Because answer long.

"Meaning either road crown lowered, archived tie was recorded badly, quarry face itself changed, or some combination. But R-four face mark is intact, and archive sketch matches current notch geometry. So I place more confidence in actual road lowering here than at South Approach."

Jorren said, "How much confidence?"

"Enough to recommend against unrestricted heavy freight until loaded response is observed. Not enough to call whole causeway failed."

There.

Smaller claim.

Painful enough.

Enna asked, "Can we run monitored heavy pass here?"

Halen looked at road.

"One."

Tera said, "With fuel wagon?"

Jorren said, "Not full convoy."

"Which one?"

"Lead tank wagon."

"How heavy?"

"Class ten loaded."

Enna shook head.

"Not first pass."

"Then what proves anything?"

"Class eight test wagon."

"We already did class eight south."

"Different section."

Jorren swore softly.

Tera said, "If eight passes?"

Enna answered carefully.

"Then we know eight passed this section under monitored conditions once."

"And clinic oil?"

"Still decision after all three sections."

Tera's jaw tightened.

"You people have sentence for everything except yes."

Enna met her eyes.

"Yes."

Tera almost laughed.

Didn't.

First relay had test wagon staged north side.

Class eight verified on scale blocks.

No argument this time.

Halen placed chalk reference at crack.

Tender put sounding plate near shoulder.

Enna assigned me fixed observation position on ridge side.

"Do not move to see better."

"Yes."

"If something happens, write after you're safe."

"Yes."

Jorren said, "Comforting."

Wagon approached walking pace.

Two horses.

Driver pale.

Front axle crossed first stone seam.

Nothing.

Rear.

Small vibration through road.

I felt through right crutch tip.

Not measurement.

Did not write yet.

At north crack, left rear wheel sank visible amount.

Less than South dip maybe.

Different surface.

Then sound.

Not timber knock.

Stone grind.

Short.

Deep.

Driver stopped without instruction.

Good driver.

Nobody breathed.

Enna raised hand.

"Hold."

Halen crouched near crack but did not cross chalk.

Crack had extended.

Not much.

Visible.

Maybe finger length beyond yesterday chalk.

Today pre-pass endpoint also marked.

Now new hairline ran past by roughly two fingers.

I wrote time.

Axle class.

Wheel position.

Sound.

Stop.

Visible crack extension beyond pre-pass chalk approximately two fingers.

No cause.

No safe.

No failed.

Jorren saw.

"Fuck."

Tera closed eyes.

Not victory.

Important.

Enna said, "Back it?"

Halen snapped, "No."

Driver froze.

Halen stood.

"Forward off section. Slowly. No reverse over same edge."

Enna repeated to driver.

Wagon moved.

Another small grind.

Then clear.

Halen inspected.

He took longer than anyone wanted.

Jorren stopped pacing.

Tera sat on stone.

I kept ledger open.

Wind moved marsh grass.

North Reach smoke ahead.

Fuel south.

Road between.

Halen finally stood.

"Heavy freight hold remains."

Jorren said nothing.

"Class eight?" Tera asked.

"I would not repeat class eight here until shoulder is opened and inspected."

"Class six local?"

Halen looked at Enna.

Policy.

Enna looked at road, then Tera.

"Single class-six local carts. Walking pace. One at a time. No passing on Section Three. Spotter present until inspection."

Jorren asked, "Clinic drums can be broken to class six carts."

Everyone looked at him.

He shrugged.

"They can. Fuel convoy can't economically, but clinic drums can."

Tera said, "How many?"

"Two carts maybe three depending drums."

"Guild charge?"

There.

Jorren's face changed.

Business.

"Normal transfer plus local haul."

Tera stood.

"No."

"I'm not donating team."

"Village already pays fuel rate."

"Fuel rate assumes convoy delivery. Breaking load costs labor and delay."

"Road problem isn't ours."

"Not mine either."

"Your wagons use it."

"So do yours."

Enna said, "Not my decision."

Tera turned on her.

"Convenient."

"Correct."

"Still convenient."

"Yes."

That one hurt.

Correct procedure.

No clean answer.

Jorren rubbed jaw.

"Guild will move clinic drums to south post without surcharge."

Tera waited.

"Local haul from south post is village responsibility."

"We don't have three empty teams sitting there."

"I can release one guild light team after convoy parks. One."

"Free?"

He looked physically ill.

"Yes."

Tera nodded once.

"I can find second."

Not compromise solved everything.

But clinic oil moved.

Fuel still didn't.

Enna wrote temporary traffic order.

I copied exact time into master ledger.

**SECTION THREE HEAVY HOLD CONTINUES. CLASS SIX LOCAL SINGLE-PASS UNDER SPOTTER. NO REPEAT CLASS EIGHT PENDING SHOULDER INSPECTION.**

Authority: Enna Ro field lead on Halen Brusk survey recommendation.

Names mattered.

Who decided mattered.

My handwriting did not make it mine.

First relay stayed to spot traffic.

We moved north.

North Reach Depot arrived before sunset.

Not town exactly.

Depot with town growing around it.

Fuel sheds.

Repair yard.

Two inns.

A schoolhouse with bell.

Clinic whitewashed blue at door.

Rows worker cottages.

Marsh on three sides and ridge road continuing north-east.

Beyond depot, signpost.

**MEREHOOK 2 DAYS**

**UPLAND FORK 3 DAYS**

I looked too long.

Enna said, "Don't plan past the contract yet."

"I wasn't."

"You looked too long."

"That proves nothing."

"Good."

Interesting.

Outer Works bunkhouse had six beds and one table.

Companions had passenger lodging at Reed Lamp across lane, included only through tonight in their fare.

Sori found me before supper.

"How bad?"

"More bad."

"Technical."

"Class eight extended crack at north shoulder. Heavy hold. Local six still moving one at time."

"Fuel?"

"Stopped south. Clinic drums getting broken out."

"Who pays?"

"Everyone arguing."

"So normal."

"Basically."

Dask asked, "Did benchmark move?"

"Yes."

"Then guild was right."

"About benchmark."

He frowned.

"But road also moved?"

"Probably. Halen has stronger evidence north."

"Both?"

"Apparently world supports multiple annoying truths."

Dask looked pleased.

Of course.

Maro had found depot cook.

"Soup."

"Good?"

"Hot."

Different question.

We ate.

After supper Enna finished day sheets.

She checked my ledger.

Slowly.

Too slowly.

I resisted explaining handwriting.

She stopped at class-eight event.

"You felt vibration through crutch?"

I had put that in side note marked **personal sensation / not instrument**.

"Yes."

"Why include?"

"Because I observed it, but not enough to elevate."

"Good. Keep side note separate."

She turned page.

"You wrote Jorren offered one light team."

"Traffic/logistics note after order."

"Good."

Another page.

Then closed.

"Five copper."

There.

First full field day.

Master ledger carried most day.

Earned.

She counted five.

I counted after.

Forty-eight.

Not silver.

Closer.

Money wanted become leg.

Road wanted become road.

Both could be true.

Jorren had taught me.

"Tomorrow," Enna said, "shoulder opening at first light. Crib crew coming from depot. Halen wants exposed subgrade before deciding whether class eight can resume."

"Fuel reserve?"

"Depot steward says one and three-quarter days at normal burn."

Less.

"Weather?"

"Rain after midday."

Of course.

"Contract day two?"

"Yes."

"Master?"

She tapped ledger.

"Unless you become stupid overnight."

"I'll try not."

"Don't try creatively."

Fair.

I went Reed Lamp after.

Trial limb still wrapped.

Evening allowance unused.

Could use thirty minutes.

Stable depot lanes.

Skin good.

Time available.

I wanted to.

That mattered.

I also had tired hip from morning wear, full field day, wagon, crutches, standing.

Not pain.

Just tired.

Baseline brain started calculating.

Thirty minutes tonight might produce useful data.

Or fatigue noise.

Tomorrow field day mattered more.

Masta's paper said maximum.

Not assignment.

I left shell wrapped.

Wrote:

**Evening wear: not used. General fatigue after field day. No skin concern. Preserve next-day quality.**

That felt annoyingly mature.

Sori saw.

"You didn't wear it."

"No."

"Good."

"Stop approving me."

"No."

Dask had map open.

Merehook circled.

Not by him.

Printed red route ring.

"Apparently Merehook has old tide observatory," he said.

Of course it did.

Maro said, "Salt cliffs."

Sori said, "We have three days contract minimum."

Nobody said after.

Didn't need.

I looked at sign through window.

Road northeast disappeared behind cottages.

Two days.

First plan had been two nights total.

Funny.

I counted money once.

Forty-eight copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Bits unknown.

Trial leg still not mine.

Causeway still not solved.

Fuel still south.

Rain coming.

Merehook existed.

Good day.

Complicated.

Those were becoming same thing.

# CHAPTER 430

## THE RECORDER

Rain made good clock.

Nothing like weather to make professionals stop pretending time abstract.

First light at Section Three.

Crib crew already there.

Four workers.

One narrow-bodied lifting jack.

Two timber crib stacks.

One pry bar long enough to become family heirloom.

Halen had coffee.

Enna had no coffee.

Jorren had mood.

Tera had brought two village carts and a clinic boy carrying sealed list.

I had ledger.

Good hierarchy.

Crutches only.

Trial build stayed wrapped at depot.

Morning skin fine.

Hip fine.

Road edge muddy from night dew and shoulder opening planned.

No reason to introduce new variable.

Baseline brain approved.

Annoying.

Section Three looked worse after workers removed surface stones.

Not catastrophic.

Worse.

Under patched wheel course was old cribbing.

Timber transverse to road.

Stone fill between.

Then newer repair blocks laid over older black marsh clay.

One crib member near crack had split along grain.

Not fully broken.

Split enough.

Beneath it, fill had washed out into dark pocket roughly arm width.

Water glinted below.

Halen crouched.

"There."

Jorren said, "That's local."

"Yes."

He sounded offended by correct statement.

Enna asked crib foreman, "Name?"

"Toll Venn."

Another Venn.

Of course.

No relation unless proven.

I wrote Toll Venn, crib foreman.

Toll tapped split timber with hammer.

Dull.

"Old piece."

"How old?" Halen asked.

"Older than repair stones. Could be original east shoulder."

"Can it carry class eight?"

"Not like this."

Jorren swore.

Tera did not smile.

Good.

Toll pointed at washout.

"Problem isn't just split. Fill went somewhere."

"Marsh," one worker said.

Toll looked at him.

"Brilliant."

Worker shrugged.

Different sarcasm academy risk.

But foremen allowed some.

Halen asked, "Drain slot?"

Worker crawled lower shoulder on plank.

"Clear mouth."

"Behind?"

"Can't see."

Toll said, "We open another two stones."

Enna nodded.

Traffic stayed held.

Class six village carts had queue south and north.

Not huge.

Enough.

Milk.

School cart.

Clinic supplies.

One old man with chickens.

Chickens hated infrastructure.

Reasonable.

Second stone came out.

Then third.

Pocket wider than first visible.

Not cavern.

Maybe two arm lengths along shoulder.

Fill had slumped toward old drainage seam.

Toll sat back.

"Can crib."

Jorren immediately: "Today?"

Toll looked at sky.

"Before hard rain, maybe temporary."

"Class ten?"

"No."

"Class eight?"

"Maybe after load test."

Jorren closed eyes.

Halen asked, "Class seven?"

Toll thought longer.

"I'd rather build for eight and limit to seven until settles."

There.

Practical engineering language.

Not miracle.

Not answer.

Enna asked, "Time?"

"Two bells to crib. Another half for stone and load spread if materials here."

"Rain?"

"If starts soft, we work. If hard, stop before fill turns soup."

Jorren said, "Fuel convoy can split class seven."

Everyone looked.

"How?" Enna asked.

"Not all. Some. Transfer from two tank wagons into four smaller road drums and two class-seven flatbeds."

Tera said, "How much fuel?"

"Maybe third convoy capacity first run."

"Enough for depot?"

Jorren looked at clinic boy.

Then Enna.

"Enough to buy day. Maybe day and half depending burn cuts."

Tera said, "And cost?"

Jorren sighed.

"Yes. Cost."

"Who?"

"Guild eats transfer labor if Works clears class seven temporary. Depot pays normal freight."

She narrowed eyes.

"Why?"

"Because sitting six loaded tank wagons south also costs me money."

Honest.

Tera nodded.

Not gratitude.

Alignment.

Enna said, "Nothing moves until Toll finishes and Halen recommends."

Jorren looked at sky.

"Rain doesn't care about recommendation."

Halen said, "Road doesn't care about fuel."

"People do."

"Then perhaps people should maintain roads before fuel sits on them."

There.

That one landed poorly.

Tera turned.

"Perhaps city should fund roads before village tax gets sent south."

Halen stopped.

Enna looked at him.

He rubbed forehead.

"That was not my jurisdiction."

Tera said, "No. It was your mouth."

Good.

Human.

Halen muttered, "Fair."

I did not write that in ledger.

Not relevant.

Wanted to.

Toll's crew worked.

My role changed from observing road to observing sequence.

Stone removed.

Split member exposed.

Temporary jack set on ridge-side bearing plate.

Load raised barely enough to insert crib block.

I wrote order, times, who directed.

No pretending I understood force distribution beyond obvious.

At one point Toll snapped, "No, not that block. Grain wrong."

Worker rotated timber.

Same size.

Different orientation.

I knew enough old-life construction to understand why.

I did not need to wonder.

Just wrote.

Marsh water below pulsed faintly with tide.

Not dramatic.

Enough to make hole feel alive.

Then rain started.

Soft.

Fine mist.

Toll looked up.

"We keep."

Workers covered open fill with canvas on marsh side.

Jorren checked watch every five minutes.

Tera stopped him on sixth.

"It won't go faster."

"Watch might threaten it."

"You think everything negotiates."

"Usually eventually."

She almost smiled.

Clinic boy asked me, "Are we open?"

"No."

"Will we?"

"Don't know."

"When will you know?"

"When somebody with authority tells me."

He frowned.

"What do you do?"

"Write down why they tell me."

He considered.

"That sounds boring."

"Best days are."

Not true lately.

But child did not need full philosophy.

By second bell cribbing in.

New timber sistered against split member.

Stone fill repacked around pocket with larger angular pieces, then smaller locked between.

Toll added two load-spread planks under road course across shoulder width.

Temporary.

Visible.

Not pretty.

"How long?" Enna asked.

"If dry? Days. Maybe week. If rain hard, inspect after every heavy pass. This isn't permanent repair."

"Class?"

"Test seven first."

Halen nodded.

Jorren exhaled like man had been holding breath since Duskport.

Enna: "Verified class seven test wagon. Not guild fuel first."

"Yes, yes."

"You sound learning."

"I am being domesticated by procedure."

"Poorly."

That was almost banter.

Test wagon from depot arrived.

Class seven verified on blocks.

Toll placed two chalk witnesses.

Halen set rod.

I took ridge-side fixed position.

Rain soft.

Road wet enough to darken stone, not slick.

Wagon crossed walking pace.

At repaired shoulder:

small compression sound.

No grind.

No visible crack extension.

Load-spread plank flexed slightly.

Crib stayed.

Wagon clear.

Everyone breathed.

Halen inspected.

Toll inspected.

Jorren looked from the repaired shoulder to the test wagon.

Enna said, "No."

He actually nodded.

Growth.

Halen recommendation:

Temporary class-seven restricted passage possible at Section Three under spotter.

One wagon at time.

Walking pace.

No stopping on repair.

Inspect shoulder after every third class-seven heavy pass and after any audible grind or visible movement.

Suspend immediately if rain becomes hard or water rises into exposed repair zone.

No class eight or above.

Section Two still had dull timber issue.

So not done.

Jorren said, "We shore Two."

Enna: "Crib crew goes there after meal."

Toll said, "If rain stays soft."

Tera asked, "Local six?"

"Continue," Enna said.

"Clinic oil?"

"Continue transfer."

"Fuel?"

Jorren said, "I can have first class-seven pair at south post by fourth bell."

Enna looked at Halen.

He held up hand.

"If Section Two gets shored and passes seven."

Jorren nearly screamed.

But didn't.

"Of course."

We moved south to Section Two.

Rain got stronger.

Not hard.

Yet.

North Reach behind us now.

Fuel beyond south.

Road problem in middle.

Section Two smelled like wet timber.

Dull member under culvert approach easier access than Section Three because shoulder lower.

Toll crawled under on plank.

"Rot pocket."

Halen crouched.

"Extent?"

"One member maybe adjacent end. Need probe."

Worker handed awl.

Toll pushed.

Wood accepted too much.

"There."

Jorren: "Can sister?"

"Yes."

"Today?"

"If you stop asking today, maybe today."

Good.

Crews worked.

This time actual hazard arrived quietly.

Rain runoff increased from east ditch.

Culvert mouth, partly choked yesterday, started backing water.

One worker stood knee-deep clearing wrack from upstream side.

Not me.

Obviously.

I recorded water rise against marked stake.

One finger over twenty minutes.

Then two.

Enna ordered traffic stopped entirely at Section Two while work under deck.

Village queue grew.

Tera arrived from north on pony again.

"School cart waiting."

"Crew under road," Enna said.

"How long?"

Toll shouted from below, "Ask rain."

Tera looked at sky.

"Rain says asshole."

Fair.

Then worker in ditch yelled.

Not panic.

Sharp.

"Move!"

Wrack dam gave.

Water surged through culvert mouth.

Not flood wall.

Fast brown push.

Worker below road scrambled backward on plank.

His boot slipped.

One leg dropped off edge into channel.

Toll grabbed harness strap.

Other worker grabbed Toll.

Chain.

I was ten paces away.

Crutches.

Old locked-in part noticed geometry instantly.

Do not run.

No need.

I planted right crutch, swung left side forward, got to dry anchor post two paces faster than careful walking.

Worker's loose tool bag sliding toward channel.

Not person.

Tool bag.

I caught strap with crutch tip and hooked it against stone.

Useful.

Not heroic.

Toll and other worker hauled man back.

Nobody injured except dignity.

Water blasted through cleared culvert.

Level dropped almost immediately.

Enna looked at me.

"You good?"

"Yes."

Worker on plank coughed.

"I'm good."

Toll said, "You owe channel apology."

"Fuck channel."

I liked crew.

I recorded event:

Wrack release.

Sudden increased culvert flow.

Worker footing loss during withdrawal.

Recovered by harness assistance.

No injury reported.

Tool bag caught before water entry.

No heroics from me.

Good.

Then something useful became obvious.

Water level on upstream stake fell more than three fingers in ten minutes after blockage cleared.

Halen noticed too.

"That matters."

Jorren appeared from north.

"How?"

"Section Two standing water load partly maintenance blockage, not only road settlement."

"So road fine here?"

Halen stared.

Jorren sighed.

"I know. Wrong sentence."

"Learning."

"Hate it."

Rot timber still real.

Drainage blockage also real.

Multiple truths again.

Toll sistered member once channel settled.

Added temporary bearing plate.

Rain sharpened.

Fourth bell nearing.

Enna asked weather runner.

"Hard band by fifth."

One bell.

Test seven here or no fuel today.

Toll inspected.

"Ready enough for one."

Halen: "One."

Enna called verified class-seven wagon.

Tera stood by.

Clinic cart had already crossed earlier under six.

At least that moved.

Class-seven test pass Section Two:

No dull knock.

One normal timber report.

No visible shoulder change.

Halen checked.

Toll checked.

Rain harder.

Enna decision:

Temporary class-seven passage permitted through Sections Two and Three until hard-rain suspension threshold.

One at time.

Spotters.

No class eight+.

Section One remains observed but passable at seven under same walking pace.

Jorren did math aloud.

"Two flatbeds today. Maybe third if transfer already done."

Tera said, "How much depot burn?"

"About day. Maybe little more."

"And tomorrow?"

"Depends rain."

There.

Not solved.

Bought time.

Fuel convoy broken into smaller class-seven loads.

Expensive.

Slow.

Enough to keep North Reach from dark tomorrow.

Maybe.

Jorren sent rider south.

Tera sent rider north.

Everyone moved.

That felt like decision.

Not answer.

By fifth bell hard rain hit.

Enna suspended class-seven heavy passage after **two successful transferred fuel loads** had crossed north.

Third held south.

Exactly enough to annoy everyone.

Toll covered repairs.

Spotters remained for local class six only until water threshold.

Then even locals would stop.

We rode North Reach in hard rain.

No trial limb.

Obviously.

At depot, fuel shed took two loads.

Steward recalculated reserve.

With restricted burn already ordered:

roughly **2.4 days**.

Better.

Not safe forever.

Time purchased.

I liked phrase too much.

Enna reviewed ledger after supper.

Long day.

Wet pages inside wax cover stayed mostly dry.

Mostly.

One corner looked like drowned insect.

She read.

Stopped at worker slip.

"Why no name?"

"No injury, event relevant to work condition. Name unnecessary unless safety report separately asks."

She nodded.

"Good."

Stopped at tool bag.

"You caught it?"

"Yes."

"With crutch?"

"Yes."

"Useful."

There.

Same word.

I smiled.

"What?"

"Nothing."

She continued.

Then pay.

Five copper.

Field day two.

Forty-eight to fifty-three.

First time number passed fifty again since deposit.

Not milestone exactly.

Felt like one.

She said, "Tomorrow we inspect after rain and decide whether temporary class seven survives. That's day three. Minimum complete if full day."

"Then?"

"If road stable, contract may end. If not, extension up to two days."

"And if ends?"

"Return wagon to Duskport available following morning."

There.

Return path.

Merehook sign outside.

Another path.

Sori came by bunkhouse doorway with tea.

"You look wet."

"Rain."

"I solved mystery."

"Thank you."

She handed cup.

"Fuel?"

"Two smaller loads made it. Hard rain stopped third. Reserve about two-point-four days with burn restrictions."

"Road?"

"Temporarily class seven after repairs. Needs post-rain check."

"So fixed?"

"No."

"Good."

"Why good?"

"Because if you said fixed after one day I'd know everyone lying."

Fair.

Dask and Maro waited Reed Lamp.

Dask had learned tide observatory at Merehook only opens public gallery every fourth day.

Apparently tomorrow was not fourth day.

He had acquired schedule.

Maro had learned salt cliffs had nesting glass gulls.

Neither asked to leave yet.

Good.

I unwrapped trial limb only to inspect dry.

No wear.

No problem.

Masta rule sheet dry inside wax sleeve.

I wrote:

**Road day two. No trial wear. Surface wet / worksite unstable / full-day fatigue. Skin normal.**

Baseline.

Not verdict.

Sometimes progress looked like not using thing.

Annoying.

I counted money.

Fifty-three copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Bits unknown.

One silver still abstract wall.

Road bought me five copper today.

Road cost guild transfer labor.

Road cost village time.

Road bought North Reach maybe another day.

Same object.

Different ledgers.

I had mine.

That was enough.

# CHAPTER 431

## THE INSPECTOR

Rain stopped sometime before dawn.

Marsh did not.

Water everywhere.

Road shining.

Ditches full.

Causeway edges breathing little brown streams into reeds.

I checked stump.

Normal.

Checked sky.

Rude gray.

Checked trial leg.

Still wrapped.

Good.

Breakfast porridge tasted like someone described salt from memory.

Less good.

Enna arrived with Halen before first bell.

"Post-rain inspection," she said.

"I guessed."

"Don't."

"Right."

Halen carried two instrument cases and expression of man expecting road to have committed paperwork overnight.

Toll waited outside with crib crew.

Jorren already there.

Tera too.

Nobody slept enough.

North Reach had fuel now.

Some.

That made people less desperate.

Not relaxed.

Different.

We rode south to Section Three first.

Temporary shoulder looked ugly.

Better than broken.

Worse than yesterday.

One load-spread plank had shifted half hand toward marsh side.

Not fallen.

Shifted.

Stone fill at outer pocket had settled.

New wet line showed water had risen almost to lower crib face overnight.

Toll crouched.

"Don't open traffic."

Jorren said nothing.

Good start.

Toll pulled plank.

Under it, sistered crib member still seated.

New timber intact.

Lock stone held.

But outer packed fill had slumped maybe fist depth near washout pocket.

Halen said, "Rain loaded water into same weakness."

Toll said, "Yes."

"Repair failed?" Tera asked.

Toll looked at her.

"No."

Jorren breathed.

"Repair survived. Fill settled. Different."

Jorren stopped breathing victory.

Learning.

Toll probed.

"Need repack. Add second outer crib block. Better drainage cut above pocket. Two hours if material here."

Enna asked, "Then seven?"

"Maybe. Test after."

Jorren sighed.

"Maybe again."

"You want guaranteed road, build one on mountain."

Maro would like Toll.

I recorded.

Time.

Rain state.

Plank shift.

Fill slump.

Crib intact.

Traffic remains closed pending repair/retest.

Not failure.

Not success.

Condition.

Section Two next.

That one surprised us.

Water lower.

Much lower.

Yesterday's cleared culvert ran strong brown stream.

Sistered timber still seated.

Bearing plate unmoved.

No fresh shoulder cracking.

Halen measured upstream stake.

"Better after hard rain than before clearing."

Jorren looked at him.

"Can I enjoy this one?"

"Cautiously."

"I'll take it."

Tera said, "Maintenance mattered."

Halen nodded.

"Substantially here."

"And timber?"

"Still rotted. Repair still temporary."

"Both."

"Yes."

She nodded.

No fight.

Just fact now.

Section One was worse than yesterday.

Not dramatic.

Annoying.

The low timber knock had become two knocks.

One under left wheel path.

One nearer center.

Drainage ditch that had been partly choked was now full and spilling over lip.

No crack.

No obvious settlement.

But road surface had developed shallow soft patch around repeated wheel dip.

Halen walked it.

Toll probed from edge.

Probe sank deeper than he liked.

"Different problem," Toll said.

Jorren said, "Of course."

"Probably saturated fill over old deck or voiding around timber. Need open side enough to see."

Enna looked sky.

No rain currently.

"How long?"

"One hour to know. More if bad."

Fuel third transfer sat south.

One class-seven load.

Not full convoy.

Waiting.

North Reach had 2.4 days restricted reserve.

Not emergency this minute.

Which made decision harder in useful way.

Jorren said, "We could hold third load."

Everyone looked at him.

He frowned.

"What?"

Tera said, "Nothing."

"I know face."

"You said hold."

"We have two loads across. Road changed overnight. I'm not driving third because yesterday worked."

Halen stared.

"What?"

"Don't make event."

"I am experiencing professional joy."

"Quietly."

Enna said, "We inspect Section One."

We did.

Road edge opened smaller than Three.

Under surface stone, wet packed fill.

Below that old timber deck.

Not crib exactly.

Two longitudinal stringers with transverse boards.

One board split.

Not main issue.

Water had washed fine fill down through gap between boards, leaving shallow void under wheel path.

Every heavy pass compressed surface into missing support.

Knock was deck answering.

Toll pointed.

"There."

Halen said, "So settlement local to fill loss."

"Likely."

"Cause drainage?"

Toll looked uphill ditch.

"Drainage plus old board spacing plus years. Pick one if you need blame."

Tera said, "We need repair."

"Better sentence."

Repair simpler than Three.

Open enough to pack coarse stone through side.

Add board patch over split.

Clear ditch fully.

Cut little relief channel so overflow exits before crossing road crown.

No magic.

Shovels.

Stone.

Wood.

People.

I recorded sequence.

Then something changed.

Not road.

Argument.

Depot steward arrived by light cart.

Name Bera Tann.

Small woman, broad hat, voice like ledger slammed shut.

"I need corridor reopened by third bell."

Enna said, "Need is not authority."

"I know. I'm telling you what happens if not."

Jorren said, "Reserve is two-point-four days."

Bera looked at him.

"At restricted burn."

"Yes."

"Restricted burn means dye house half shift, bathhouse cold mornings, east kiln idle, school heat reduced if night temperature drops, and clinic protected."

There.

Numbers had people inside.

Tera said, "School heat reduced?"

"Not today. If we stay restricted three more days, yes."

Jorren said, "Third load buys what?"

"Roughly half day at restricted burn. Less if kiln resumes."

"Full convoy?"

"Several days."

Everyone looked south.

Six tank wagons too heavy.

Still.

Toll kept working.

Smart.

Bera said, "I don't need full convoy today. I need enough time for permanent repair decision without choosing which town function gets cold."

Enna nodded.

"Understood."

Tera asked, "Then why not keep dye house closed?"

Bera looked at her.

"Because forty-two people work there and fourteen are paid by shift."

Tera stopped.

Human problem.

Again.

Nobody evil.

Everyone expensive.

Jorren said, "Guild can stage two more class-seven transfers by tomorrow if corridor holds."

"Cost?" Bera asked.

"Guild absorbs transfer labor through tomorrow. After that I need revised freight agreement."

Tera said, "Convenient deadline."

Jorren's face tightened.

"I have crews working twice and tank wagons sitting. I can eat cost for emergency. I cannot turn emergency into business model."

Bera said, "Fair."

Tera did not.

But she didn't argue.

Road repair finished near second bell.

Section One test first.

Class-seven wagon.

Walking pace.

I stood fixed outside wheel line.

Crutches.

Ledger.

No shell.

Wheel crossed old dip.

Surface compressed slightly.

No knock.

Second path.

No knock.

Toll checked edge.

Packed stone stayed.

Halen marked surface.

"Again after full route."

Section Two.

Clean.

Normal timber report.

No water rise.

Section Three after repack/additional crib block.

This was one everyone watched.

Class-seven wagon approached.

Outer plank flexed.

Small.

No shift.

No grind.

No visible crack extension.

Toll waited.

Halen measured.

Enna asked, "Recommendation?"

Toll said, "Seven, one at time, walking, inspect every third. Suspend on rising water or movement. Same as yesterday, but now I know rain settles outer fill. Need permanent drainage and shoulder rebuild before eight."

Halen said, "Agreed."

Enna wrote temporary corridor order through next inspection window.

Not permanent.

Not all-clear.

Third fuel transfer crossed.

Then one local grain cart.

Then clinic return cart.

Traffic had shape now.

Not open.

Managed.

At third bell, Enna sent us back north for midday records.

I ate bread and cheese sitting outside bunkhouse.

Actual decent bread.

I considered saying fuck bread.

Didn't.

Growth.

Trial leg sat wrapped beside me.

Road lanes around depot were drying.

Stable gravel.

No field work for half bell.

Skin normal.

Body tired but not yesterday tired.

Masta's first-day road-test limits had been first road day.

She had not given exact later progression because she expected notes and return fitting.

That mattered.

I could wear thirty minutes because prior allowance existed.

Could also not invent progression.

So I used same conservative window.

Twenty minutes.

Depot lane.

No worksite.

No load.

Crutches with me.

Medium foot.

First steps stiff.

Then less.

North Reach gravel different from Duskport paving.

Tiny stones rolled under foot.

Not dangerous.

Informative.

Medium width helped.

Crutches light.

I walked from bunkhouse to Reed Lamp.

Maybe eighty paces.

Stopped twice.

Turn at door ugly.

Still.

Maro saw me through window and came out.

"Road leg."

"Trial leg."

"On road."

"Technically depot."

"Road place."

Hard to argue.

He walked beside me without helping.

Good.

We went to signpost.

MEREHOOK 2 DAYS.

UPLAND FORK 3 DAYS.

I stood there with both crutches lightly touching gravel.

Not because needed every second.

Because Masta said bring them.

Maro looked at Merehook arrow.

"Salt cliffs."

"You said."

"Glass gulls nest there."

"Are they actually glass?"

"No."

"Disappointing."

"Wings shine."

"Better."

He looked at leg.

"Hurts?"

"No."

"Good."

Then arrow.

"You going?"

There.

"Don't know."

"Okay."

No persuasion.

Maro good at road.

Back after twenty-two minutes.

Shell off.

Light upper pink.

No outer-distal mark.

No lower-front.

Twenty minutes later pink gone.

I wrote notes.

**North Reach depot gravel. 22 min. Stable. Two stops. Turns still poor. Mild left hip fatigue. No pain. Upper pink cleared within 20.**

Useful.

Afternoon was records and one more corridor inspection.

Third-pass check at Section Three showed outer fill seated, plank unchanged.

Section One packed repair held under three class-seven/local passes.

Section Two drainage stayed clear.

No more fuel crossed before end shift because transfer crew had not prepared another load.

Good.

No forcing traffic just because corridor existed.

At last bell Enna closed master ledger.

"Minimum done."

There.

Three days.

She checked pages.

"Five copper."

Counted.

Fifty-eight.

"Extension?" I asked.

"Outer Works wants one more inspection day if weather holds."

I waited.

"Do you need me?"

"Need? No. First relay can carry. Want? Yes. You're clean and already know sequence."

"Same rate?"

"Five with master."

"Return wagon?"

"Duskport wagon leaves tomorrow after first bell. If you take extension, next guaranteed Works return is day after tomorrow, maybe later if rain closes causeway."

There.

Choice.

"Can I decide tonight?"

"Before morning board."

"Good."

She looked at me.

"Road face."

"Stop saying that."

"No."

I went Reed Lamp.

Sori had already learned.

Of course.

"Minimum complete," she said.

"Yes."

"Return wagon tomorrow."

"Yes."

"Extension offered."

"How?"

"Bunkhouse walls have mouths."

"That's concerning architecture."

"Pell would charge extra."

Dask had map.

Maro had soup.

Again.

"What do you want?" Sori asked.

Not what should we do.

What do you want.

I sat.

Duskport.

Masta.

Finished fitting eventually.

Two Venn flex days.

Anchor's End.

Known earning routes.

Carrow farther south beyond all that.

Merehook.

Two days north-east.

Tide observatory.

Salt cliffs.

Road I had not seen.

Fifty-eight copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Trial leg not mine.

Could go back.

Should maybe go back.

That was interesting.

Before, return had been default.

Now it was option.

"I don't want extension," I said.

Dask looked up.

Sori waited.

Maro ate soup.

"I also don't want Duskport wagon."

There.

Sori smiled little.

"Merehook."

"Maybe."

"That's yes wearing hat."

"I want sleep before making expensive decisions."

"Passenger coach north leaves second bell," Dask said.

Of course he knew.

"Cost?"

"Three copper to Fen Crossing. Another two from Fen to Merehook if same-day seat available."

Five copper.

Each.

Not nothing.

My money wanted become leg.

Road wanted become road.

Both still true.

Sori said, "My seat is my problem."

Dask said, "Mine too."

Fifty-eight.

**Fen Crossing. Missed connection yesterday. Going Merehook today if seats hold. Outer Works contract finished clean. Trial leg still trial leg. I am sending this because apparently moving farther away does not make communication less important. Annoying. I miss you.**

Sori said, "Which one?"

Excellent.

Woman blinked.

Market man laughed once.

"There."

"Shut up, Corren."

Name.

Woman looked at Sori.

"North Reach trade weight."

Sori said, "Some. Not certified."

"I have saltstone sold inland measure. Market converted to Merehook lower-coast measure after weighing and added handling because conversion put crate over band."

Corren said, "Because that's rule."

"Rule changed last month."

"Posted last month."

"At lower gate. I came upper road."

There.

Not mystery exactly.

Information asymmetry.

Sori looked at me.

I looked away.

Beautiful market.

Birds.

Could leave.

Woman said, "I don't need you decide. Just tell me if North Reach measure written here means what I think."

She produced invoice.

Concrete bounded question.

Sori took it.

Of course.

She read.

Longer than comfortable.

"This says North Reach dry freight weight, class B."

Woman said, "Yes."

Corren said, "And lower-coast conversion table makes it over handling band."

Sori said, "Maybe. I don't know your conversion table."

Good.

Woman's name turned out **Hara Quill**.

Saltstone cutter from White Shelf inland of Merehook.

Corren Dey was market custody clerk, not owner.

Hara wanted crate released today because buyer's ship left tomorrow morning.

Corren wanted fee paid because if he released undercharged freight, difference came out of his bond account.

There.

Somebody wanted something badly.

Both.

Correct procedure already hurting somebody.

Hara said, "Table at lower gate isn't table I agreed under."

Corren said, "Market doesn't care what road you entered."

"Then post rules at road I entered."

"Not my department."

"Your hand is on my crate."

"Because custody is my department."

Good.

Sori handed invoice back.

"She's right about what invoice says. That doesn't tell me whether your conversion is lawful."

Corren nodded.

"Thank you."

Hara looked furious.

Not at Sori exactly.

At answer insufficient.

I knew feeling.

Greg skill problem.

Facts narrow.

Pain remains.

Hara said, "Market office closes seventh bell."

Dask checked sun.

"Less than half bell."

Everyone looked at him.

"What?"

Useful.

Corren said, "Appeal clerk upstairs."

Hara said, "He told me tomorrow."

"Then tomorrow."

"Ship leaves tomorrow."

Corren's jaw tightened.

"I know."

He didn't like it either.

There.

We had arrived Merehook less than two bells ago.

Already city had problem.

Not ours.

Maybe.

Sori asked Hara, "How much fee?"

"Three copper extra."

Not ruinous maybe.

Depending margins.

"And crate value?"

"Buyer owes twenty-eight copper on delivery. I already paid cutting crew."

There.

Three copper could protect twenty-eight.

Simple?

No.

Hara said, "If I pay wrong fee every shipment, they make wrong rule real."

There.

Not just three copper.

Corren said, "If I waive three because you yell, every seller yells."

Also.

Sori looked at me.

"Don't."

"Still not."

"You're thinking."

"Allowed."

"Dangerous."

True.

I looked at market office stairs.

Then sea.

Then old instrument lane.

Then Hara's crate.

Visitor.

No job.

No obligation.

Could walk away.

That was new too.

I had fifty copper.

Trial leg upstairs.

Merehook around me.

Observatory not visited.

Salt cliffs barely seen.

Artifact I wanted but didn't need waiting for six copper.

And a stranger's problem sitting directly in path.

I asked one question.

"Where is posted conversion table?"

Corren pointed downhill.

"Lower gate."

"How far?"

"Ten minutes if you have two legs."

He looked at crutches.

"Sorry."

"Accurate."

Sori sighed.

"Greg."

"One question."

"That was two."

Dask said, "Technically three if counting clarification embedded in first sequence."

"Nobody asked you."

Maro held feather up to sun.

"I can go look."

There.

Independent person.

Not assistant by default.

"Do you want to?" I asked.

He shrugged.

"Downhill. Birds."

Reasonable.

Sori rubbed forehead.

"We have been here two hours."

"Visitor efficiency," I said.

She laughed despite herself.

Good.

We had not solved anything.

Excellent.

Merehook had room for four idiots.

Apparently it had paperwork too.

Lower coast.

North Reach dry.

Quietly.

"Different bond. Different ledger."

"My bond account, yes."

Bad laugh.

"Correct."

"Wonderful."

Also correct.

Employment shoulders.

I knew.

Good boundary.

"Not automatic."

Of course.

Corren hesitated.

That mattered.

"If seller posts full disputed amount plus one copper security."

"Three disputed. One security."

"Four returned."

"If I lose?"

"Three becomes fee. One returns."

"After review."

"Tomorrow?"

"Maybe."

She closed eyes.

Buyer pays twenty-eight on delivery.

Four now.

"Why didn't you say that?" she asked.

"Not compliment."

Still here physically.

Mentally birds.

Good.

Seven copper total maybe.

She counted again.

"If I post four, I have three left until buyer pays."

There.

The loophole wasn't free.

Just different shape of pain.

I asked, "Buyer here?"

"Ship office lower harbor."

"Physical delivery to ship scale."

"Can they come here?"

Hara frowned.

"Maybe because their ship leaves."

She looked at clock.

"Ten minutes."

Corren looked at her.

Hara stared.

"Naturally."

One bit mattered less than four copper.

Corren rang little brass bell.

Boy appeared from somewhere.

Corren wrote:

**HARA QUILL / SALTSTONE / CRATE 18-Q / DISPUTE / BUYER SHIP DEPARTURE MORNING / REQUEST AUTHORIZED REPRESENTATIVE.**

Runner left.

Seven bell sounded while Corren filled provisional security slip.

Office closed.

Technically.

Custody still open for release window.

Hara put four copper down.

Slowly.

I felt it.

Not mine.

Still.

Four copper was almost my Merehook travel from Fen and bunk.

Money had weight when watched.

Corren counted.

Sealed three into small dispute envelope.

One into separate security line.

"Do not lose this receipt."

"I will eat it."

"Please don't."

"Then stop talking like paper is edible."

Different voices.

Good.

Crate still held until buyer representative arrived or Hara elected release-to-carrier without delivery confirmation.

"Then ship scale closed."

"Yes."

"So I lose ship."

"Possibly."

Hara leaned on counter.

Nobody talked.

This was the part procedure couldn't solve.

Clock.

Distance.

Somebody else's schedule.

Hara said, "Contract says delivery to ship scale."

"Then no."

Simple.

No clever wording magic.

"I saw him downhill."
