Maro shrugged.

"Fast."

Useful but not quantitative.

Ten minutes.

Then fifteen.

Custody workers began closing side shutters.

Corren did not rush them.

Wouldn't matter.

Hara paced.

Sori sat.

I stood on crutches and watched entrance.

Dask finally returned from instrument lane carrying cracked tide index like sacred relic.

"What happened?"

Sori said, "Administrative suspense."

"Good kind?"

"No."

He joined.

Five minutes before gate close, runner came back alone.

Hara's face dropped.

Then runner said, "Buyer clerk coming. Bad knee."

Hara stared at him.

"You couldn't help?"

"She has cane. Told me stop fussing."

Fair.

Corren checked clock.

"Gate can remain open if authorized party has been summoned before close and custody supervisor approves delay."

Hara rounded on him.

"That one you had ready?"

"Because now condition exists."

"I hate this city."

"Common."

Corren sent another bell.

Supervisor arrived.

Older man.

Name Sel Ordan.

No nonsense.

Corren explained in four sentences.

Sel asked, "Security posted?"

"Yes."

"Runner time?"

Corren gave.

"Buyer representative confirmed approaching?"

Runner nodded.

Sel wrote ten-minute extension.

Not twenty.

Not mercy unlimited.

Ten.

"After that gate closes."

Hara said, "She's on bad knee."

Sel said, "Then you may meet her at upper lane and bring authorization. Gate does not need person, needs signed buyer receipt authority."

Hara froze.

Then ran.

Actually ran.

Down market lane.

Corren called, "Crate stays!"

"Obviously!"

She disappeared.

I looked at Sori.

"Do we follow?"

"Why?"

Excellent question.

We didn't.

Not our race.

Six minutes.

Seven.

Eight.

Hara came back walking fast beside woman maybe fifty with gray braid and ironwood cane.

Buyer clerk.

Bad knee.

Angry at stairs.

"Who designed this fucking town?"

Merehook finally had universal truth.

She signed buyer authority accepting market release into bonded cart custody and agreeing ship scale confirmation could occur first thing before loading.

Hara said, "Will ship wait?"

Buyer clerk said, "Captain will wait one bell if cargo physically at lower yard before dawn. After that tide matters more than you."

Cost again.

Not money exactly.

Sleep.

Cart.

Timing.

Hara nodded.

"I can do that."

Corren released crate into bonded night-cart chain.

Cart fee?

Two copper, as Hara expected tomorrow.

Except tonight required night surcharge one bit.

Hara stared at ceiling.

"One bit," she said.

Corren said, "Carrier, not market."

"I know. I'm practicing suffering."

She paid.

Crate moved.

Not solved.

But moving.

Her four copper remained tied until review.

She would still need dawn delivery.

Buyer still could reject at scale if crate issue.

Market rule still maybe bad.

Corren still had bond exposure if paperwork wrong.

Nobody whole.

"Thank you."

I said, "Maro copied table."

Maro had wandered to doorway again.

"Birds."

"Yes."

Sori said, "Greg asked one useful question."

"Two," Dask said.

"Nobody asked you."

"I counted."

Hara laughed.

Better laugh.

"Very."

"Because cutters polish the pretty side and let backside teach religion."

I wrote that mentally.

Maybe literally later.

Corren closed gate.

He looked exhausted.

Hara left with cart chain.

We remained.

"What?"

"Found employment without getting paid."

"I asked questions."

"Employment shoulders."

"No ledger."

"Progress."

Fair.

We walked uphill toward lodging.

Merehook after market close changed.

Wind louder.

Shops shuttered.

Upper road bowls singing faintly.

One old throat-glass bowl gave higher tone before gust reached lane.

I stopped.

Sori saw.

"No."

"I didn't say anything."

"Your artifact face is worse than road face."

"I don't have artifact face."

Dask said, "You do."

Traitor.

At old-instrument lane, seller was closing.

Cliff-line keeper still there.

Six copper.

Black frame.

Oval ceramic.

Could darken after threshold pull.

Not certified.

Interesting.

Could be useful with harness.

Could be useful with crutch-load experiments.

Could be useful with future limb load testing maybe.

Could also be six copper I didn't have to spend.

Fifty copper.

Leg quote still silver plus eighteen.

Road still cost money.

Merehook had two more days of interesting without trying.

I walked past.

Seller did not lower price.

Good.

"You wanted it."

"Yes."

"Why not buy?"

"Because wanting is not accounting."

Then another farther along.

Wave of warning moving through city before wind itself.

That was magic.

Or engineering.

Dangerous.

Normal.

Good.

Held it toward lamp.

# CHAPTER 435

## THE ONLOOKER

Merehook morning started with bells arguing with birds.

Birds won.

Glass gulls nested somewhere above lower harbor and had opinions about dawn.

Sharp opinions.

Persistent.

One landed on roof across lane and screamed into chimney like chimney owed money.

Maro was already outside.

Of course.

He stood in road with legal feather tucked behind ear.

"You slept?" I asked.

"Yes."

"How much?"

"Enough."

Suspicious answer.

Dask came down stairs carrying cracked tide index like newborn child.

"Observatory lower gallery opens second bell."

"You checked?"

"Twice."

"Why twice?"

"First board had yesterday's date."

Responsible obsession.

Sori arrived from nicer lodging with washed hair and rested face.

Rich.

"Hara update," she said.

I looked at her.

"How do you know?"

"Market porter at breakfast. Crate made lower yard before dawn. Ship scale accepted buyer authority. Cargo loaded. Appeal review still pending."

Good.

Not solved.

Moving.

"Security?"

"Still held."

"Hara?"

"Apparently called market office three names before breakfast."

"Healthy."

Sori nodded.

Done.

No chase.

No second act paperwork.

Dask lifted tide index.

"Observatory."

"Yes."

"Now?"

"Second bell."

"We can arrive early."

"Why?"

He looked at me like stupid.

Right.

Old Tide Eye sat above middle shelf on ridge spur pointed seaward.

Not highest place in Merehook.

Highest useful.

Road climbed through houses, then warehouses, then fewer houses, then stone retaining walls with wind bowls set every hundred paces.

Morning bowls sounded one by one.

Low note below us.

Then another higher up.

Then next.

Wind had sequence.

Could hear gust climbing road before feel it.

Old throat glass maybe.

New bronze maybe.

I wanted labels.

Merehook did not provide labels for free.

Observatory appeared around bend.

Round white stone base.

Copper roof gone green.

Three narrow towers.

One tower capped with black glass slit.

Another had open cage of brass rings.

Third looked broken.

Not ruin.

Working building with old bones.

Gate sign:

**OLD TIDE EYE**

**LOWER GALLERY PUBLIC: SECOND TO FIFTH BELL**

**UPPER INSTRUMENT FLOOR CLOSED EXCEPT SURVEY OFFICE**

**CLIFF WALK CLOSED DURING NESTING**

Underneath someone added:

**YES THIS INCLUDES YOU**

Maro read last line.

"Fair."

Dask said, "It doesn't specify species."

Sori said, "Do not litigate sign."

Good rule.

Entry lower gallery cost one bit.

We each paid own.

Copper unchanged.

Inside smelled mineral oil, old paper, salt, warm metal.

Perfect.

Lower gallery was not museum exactly.

Working leftovers displayed around current work.

Big central room opened around circular floor well covered by brass grate.

Through grate, dark shaft descended into cliff.

Could hear water far below.

Not waves.

Breathing.

A woman at desk said, "Do not drop anything through grate. If you drop anything through grate, it belongs to sea."

Dask held tide index closer.

"Understood."

Woman looked at cracked brass instrument.

"That already belongs to rubbish."

Dask's face hurt.

"It is historic."

"It is decorative copy from east-market tourist line."

Silence.

Sori turned away.

Maro looked at ceiling.

I failed.

Laughed.

Dask stared at me.

"You knew?"

"No."

"Then why?"

"Because she killed you."

Desk woman said, "Not dead. Two-bit lesson. Cheap."

Dask examined index.

"It has real calibration marks."

"Copied from Plate Six. Back side says Gull & Son Novelties if polish dirt."

Dask polished dirt.

There.

Tiny stamp.

**GULL & SON / MEREHOOK**

He closed eyes.

"I still like it."

Desk woman nodded.

"Then worth two bits."

Good.

Her name badge said **Orla Fen**.

Not Fen Crossing relation question.

Not doing that anymore.

Lower gallery held old tide machines.

Not clocks.

Some looked like clocks.

Wall arcs with weights.

Glass tubes stained green.

Brass floats.

Stone disks with blackened edges.

One giant suspended ring held clear curved glass thicker than hand at center.

Dask stopped breathing normally.

Orla noticed.

"That one's dead."

Dask whispered, "How?"

"University owns ring. Coastal Survey owns stand. Merehook owns floor under it. Lens ownership disputed because donor deed says instrument, not component. Nobody pays restoration until lawyers decide nouns."

I looked at Sori.

She said, "Do not."

"Wasn't."

"You were."

Probably.

Plaque:

**SECOND TIDE EYE, 114 YEARS SERVICE**

**PRESSURE-LENS / LONG SWELL COMPARATOR**

Old system.

Orla explained because Dask had face.

"Cliff shaft connects to sea chamber below. Pressure rise moves column. Column drives ring. Lens bends light against fixed shore stars. Gives long swell before harbor feels it."

"Magic?" I asked.

"Some."

"Helpful."

"Lens is tideglass. Pressure column mechanical. Shore-star plate is charged. Ring compensation is springs. Which part you want call magic?"

"All parts that make my life harder."

"Then building magic."

Fair.

Current system used smaller instruments upper floor.

More accurate.

Easier replacement.

Less beautiful.

Always.

Dask walked around dead Tide Eye three times.

Maro drifted toward windows.

Glass gulls swept beyond cliff.

Sori found wall of measure standards.

Of course she did.

"Greg."

Dangerous tone.

I went.

Wall displayed three brass reference bars.

**LOWER COAST WET HANDLING**

**MEREHOOK SHELF FREIGHT**

**NORTH REACH DRY FREIGHT**

Different lengths.

Not wildly.

Enough.

Sori stared.

"There are actually three."

"Bera warned."

Orla said behind us, "Four if counting old cliff measure."

Sori turned slowly.

"Why?"

Orla shrugged.

"Different trades standardized at different times. Lower harbor cared wet cargo volume and tide exposure. Shelf market cared cart handling. North road cared dry axle weight. People later decided having one word for three measures was efficient."

"People decided poorly."

"People decide locally."

Sori read explanatory card.

There was conversion chart.

Not same as market gate table.

Historical.

She frowned.

"This is why Hara's crate crossed band."

Orla said, "Maybe."

"Maybe?"

"Current tariff changed. Gallery card three years old."

Sori looked offended personally.

"Why leave it up?"

"History."

"It looks current."

"Top says historical reference."

Tiny letters.

Sori inhaled.

"Merehook is a hostile act against contracts."

Orla smiled.

"Welcome."

Maro called from window.

"Bird."

We went.

Glass gull close.

Large.

Gray-white body.

Outer flight feathers translucent at edges with mineral sheen.

When wing turned, sunlight flashed through like mica.

Not glass.

Better.

Bird rode cliff updraft without flapping.

Then another.

Then twenty.

Maro forgot us.

Good.

Independent reason paid off.

A bell rang upper floor.

Not public bell.

Work bell.

Orla looked toward stair.

Then wall needle moved.

Small bronze pointer over blue arc.

She stood.

"Huh."

Dask immediately appeared beside her.

"What?"

"Long swell rise."

"How much?"

"Not enough for harbor closure. Enough west stairs spray in maybe half bell."

She checked second dial.

Then walked to wall speaking tube.

"Lower gallery to survey. Public comparator showing two marks west-swell. Confirm?"

Muffled answer.

She listened.

"Upper says one and half."

Dask said, "Why difference?"

Orla covered tube.

"Because old comparator exaggerates when cliff chamber warm after east wind."

"Then why keep it?"

"Because sometimes exaggeration starts earlier."

There.

Interesting.

Old imperfect system retained because error pattern useful.

Greg brain liked immediately.

Not because artifact power.

Because failure had shape.

"How often old one catches early?" I asked.

Orla looked at me.

"Enough nobody removes it. Not enough to certify warning from it alone."

Beautiful.

"So upper checks it."

"And harbor bowls. And outer buoy line when working."

"When working?"

"One buoy missing."

"Of course."

Orla uncovered tube.

"Gallery comparator plus two marks. Upper one-half. Recommend west stair caution only, no harbor flag."

Answer.

Then she pulled small yellow tag and hung it outside window.

Not alarm.

Local caution.

People below knew.

Within twenty minutes, west-facing lower stair visibly sprayed harder.

Maro watched water hit stone.

Dask watched needle.

Sori watched people reroute market baskets to inner stair without panic.

I watched system.

No one instrument knew truth.

Old Tide Eye exaggerated.

New instrument lagged maybe.

Wind bowls gave another piece.

Actual water another.

People combined.

Familiar.

Different nouns.

Orla said, "You four tourists?"

"Visitors," Sori said.

I looked at her.

"Important distinction?"

"Apparently."

Orla pointed my crutches.

"Upper public stair ugly. There is service ramp around east side, but public not allowed unless staff escort."

Dask's soul left body.

"Upper?"

"Lower public only today."

Returned.

Sad.

"Why mention ramp?" I asked.

"Cliff terrace outside gallery. Better bird view. Public terrace is twelve steps. Ramp reaches same level through service side. I can open terrace gate if you don't wander."

Accommodation.

Not secret access.

Good.

"Yes," I said.

Maro said, "Yes."

Dask said, "Does terrace view upper instrument windows?"

Orla stared.

"Do not become reason I stop being nice."

"Understood."

Terrace wind strong.

Ramp rough but manageable on crutches.

Trial leg stayed wrapped at lodging.

Correct.

No need.

Salt cliffs opened below.

Merehook harbor south.

North cliffs continued pale and broken.

Glass gulls nested in protected shelves under us.

Maro leaned safely behind rail.

"There."

Nest shelves had shiny threads woven through grasses.

"What are those?" I asked.

Orla said, "Salt silk. Gulls steal it from cliff spiders."

Maro said, "Cliff spiders?"

"Not today."

Reasonable.

Dask ignored biology.

He looked at roof apparatus.

Wind vane above observatory had four glass fins, each slightly different color.

"What does that do?"

Orla said, "Nothing."

Dask looked devastated.

"Decorative replacement after lightning."

"Why glass?"

"Donor liked glass."

World cruel.

Then I saw thing.

Mounted under terrace eave.

Small brass frame with two milky disks and dangling black stone bead.

Not active display.

Retired maybe.

Tag:

**CROSSWIND DELAY WITNESS / MODEL C-2**

**RETIRED FROM NORTH CLIFF ROAD**

Orla followed my eyes.

"Old road witness."

"What does it witness?"

"Wind bowl gives local gust. That compares gust arrival at two points. First disk brightens when lower sensor trips, second when upper trips. Bead drops along marks by delay. Tells road keeper whether front is climbing fast or slow."

I stared.

Scene grammar.

Not just wind strength.

Travel timing.

Could change when leave shelter.

When cross exposed lane.

When move load.

"Still works?"

"No sensors attached. Frame probably. Disks maybe."

"Why retired?"

"Wire run broke every storm. New road uses independent bowls and bell relay. Less precise delay, less maintenance."

"For sale?"

"Observatory doesn't sell collection pieces."

Good.

Saved money by institution.

"Any surplus?"

Orla narrowed eyes.

"You collect broken infrastructure?"

"Apparently."

Dask said, "He does."

Traitor.

Orla thought.

"Coastal Survey auction shed first sixth-day. Retired road instruments, buoy parts, damaged bowls, calibration scrap. Public lot after offices take useful pieces."

There.

Not free.

Not now.

Future access.

"When sixth-day?"

"Two days."

Sori looked at me immediately.

Employment shoulders cousin.

Auction shoulders.

"We're not staying two days because Greg saw brass," she said.

"I didn't say."

"Your face did."

Enna's road face traveled.

Bad.

Dask said, "Auction may contain observatory parts."

Sori pointed at him.

"You are worse."

True.

Maro still watched gulls.

"I can stay two days."

Great.

Democracy failing.

Orla laughed.

"You people arrive yesterday?"

"Yes."

"Merehook gets everyone eventually."

"Meaning?"

"Town vertical. Takes time to leave."

Good line.

Back inside, lower gallery had small shop.

Not souvenirs only.

Retired/calibration pieces sold with clear labels.

Mostly low-value:

old brass scale cards, one bit.

cracked tideglass slivers, two bits, decorative/no instrument use.

spent shore-star tiles, one bit.

wind-bowl ceramic throat inserts, damaged, 2c.

One object more serious.

**DELAY BEAD FRAME / TRAINING UNIT**

Small version of terrace witness.

No remote sensors.

Manual trigger sockets.

Used to teach timing calibration.

Price **7c**.

Works locally if triggered by two contacts.

No certified field housing.

No wires.

No sensor pair.

Seven copper for incomplete teaching frame.

Rational.

I picked it up.

Heavy little brass.

Black bead ran smooth when Orla triggered left, waited, triggered right.

Dropped to mark.

Could measure time between two events mechanically/magically without clock reaction.

Potential:

magic attempt start/end?

load onset/release?

gait timing?

warning delays?

Could become useful.

Also seven copper.

Fifty to forty-three.

Travel-work limb still one silver plus eighteen, deposit only twenty paid.

Road still cost.

Auction in two days might have better/worse.

"No," I said.

Put back.

Orla nodded.

"Good."

"Why good?"

"People buy training units thinking they bought sensors."

"I knew."

"You asked three sensor questions. Better than most."

Dask said, "I could use it with manual triggers."

Sori said, "No."

"I didn't ask you."

"Correct."

He stared.

Then put his own hand away from purse.

Growth contagious.

We left near fourth bell.

Maro wanted lower cliff legal overlook.

Dask wanted university annex schedule.

Sori wanted current shelf-measure conversion pamphlet because apparently hate became research.

I wanted lunch.

We separated.

Also good.

I got fish bread for one bit.

Sat middle terrace overlooking cargo ramp.

No job.

No dispute.

No ledger.

Just food.

Wind bowls sang lower road.

A few breaths later banner beside me snapped.

Old system had told me before body.

I watched people who lived here react without thinking.

Hand on hat.

Cart rope checked.

Awning pulled.

Then continue.

Infrastructure became reflex.

That mattered.

Carrow had its own invisible knowledge.

Duskport too.

Now Merehook starting.

Not mine.

Could learn.

Different.

Dask found me an hour later with actual paper, not purchase.

Miracle.

"University annex public lecture tomorrow fifth bell. Tidal harmonics and cliff-chamber lag."

"You know those words?"

"Some."

"Dangerous."

Sori arrived holding three conversion pamphlets.

"Merehook government printed current table correctly in exactly one place I visited."

"Which?"

"Harbor customs. Market pamphlet still old footer. Coach yard has abbreviated table without transition note. Observatory has historical table labeled in ant-sized print."

"You going to fix city?"

She looked offended.

"No."

Then paused.

"I may write a letter."

Employment shoulders.

Beautiful.

Maro returned with no new feathers.

"Didn't buy?"

"Already have one."

Best financial mind among us.

He had salt dust on sleeve and looked happy.

"Cliff spiders?" I asked.

"Saw web. No spider."

"Good."

"Why good?"

"Size unknown."

He thought.

"Fair."

We walked toward lodging.

At market corner Hara Quill appeared.

Alive.

Tired.

Happy enough.

"Loaded," she said.

"Good."

"Buyer paid twenty-eight."

"Better."

"Market review says come back tomorrow. Security still held."

"Of course."

She pointed at me.

"I hate your town."

"Not my town."

"Good answer."

Then she went somewhere with money.

Problem still problem.

Also she got paid.

Enough.

At lodging, I counted.

Fifty copper.

Still.

Bits less.

Seven-copper delay frame not mine.

Six-copper cliff-line keeper not mine.

Four-copper throat glass not mine.

This town dangerous.

Not because monsters.

Because everything useful had price tag.

I understood that kind of danger.

Tomorrow could leave.

Could stay.

Auction in two days.

Lecture tomorrow.

Cliffs still not fully seen.

No obligation.

That remained new.

I wrote one line in notebook:

**Old systems survive when their errors are useful.**

Then another:

**Useful is not same as worth buying.**

Closed notebook.

Onlooker.

Better than owner.

For now.

# CHAPTER 436

## THE PROSPECTOR

Staying cost money.

Merehook made that clear before breakfast.

Two copper for floor bunk tonight.

Food bits.

Auction two days away.

Dask lecture tomorrow.

Maro wanted north cliff path if nesting wardens opened it.

Sori wanted to compare one more customs table because apparently rage could become hobby.

I wanted all of it.

Problem.

I had fifty copper.

Also leg costing one silver plus eighteen copper.

Also road.

Also no idea what silver meant in copper because world remained committed to being inconvenient.

I ate cheap oat cake.

"We staying?" Dask asked.

"Are you?"

"Lecture."

"That's tomorrow."

"Yes."

"So you're staying."

"Correct."

Maro said, "Cliff path maybe opens afternoon."

Sori drank tea.

"I paid for actual bed. I intend to use city around it at least another day."

Three answers.

None mine.

Good.

I went market.

Not to buy.

To look for money.

Different.

Merehook morning work board sat under customs arch.

Not jobs exactly.

Short labor.

**TWO HANDS / LOWER SALT STORE / 2C HALF DAY**

No.

**CLIFF STAIR SCRAPE / OWN TOOLS / 3C**

Also no.

**COUNTING HAND / FISH AUCTION / 2C + MEAL**

Maybe.

**UPPER ROPE HOUSE / LINE TAG SORT / PIECE RATE**

Interesting.

I asked board clerk.

"Rope house?"

"Menn Coil. Upper shelf east. They sort retired line tags before Survey surplus."

There.

Auction adjacent.

Not protagonist destiny.

Actual prep labor.

"Pay?"

"Depends lot. Usually two to four copper half day if you can read marks and don't steal."

"High standards."

"Merehook declining."

I took chit.

Menn Coil smelled like hemp, tar, salt, and old rain.

Warehouse open cliff side with rope racks from floor to roof.

Woman at desk had shaved head and three brass rings through one ear.

"Board chit."

I handed.

She looked at crutches.

Then me.

"Tag sorting seated."

Good.

"Can read coast marks?"

"Some. Not Merehook-specific."

"Can ask instead of invent?"

"Yes."

"Four bins. Keep. Scrap. Survey return. Unknown. Wrong bin costs me more than you earn."

"Comforting."

"I'm Pera Menn."

Name new enough.

Good.

She pointed table.

Crates of old line hardware.

Ceramic threshold tags.

Brass eyelets.

Lead seals.

Tiny iron frames.

My cliff-line keeper cousin had family reunion.

Pera caught face.

"Don't shop my scrap while paid."

"I wasn't."

"You were spiritually shopping."

Fair.

Work simple until not.

Most tags had clear marks.

Survey crest meant return.

Cracked ceramic scrap.

Current certified green slash keep.

No mark unknown.

Then old tags.

Numbers without units.

Symbols changed.

I asked.

Pera answered when knew.

When didn't, unknown.

Good system.

Half bell in, I found black ceramic oval with two dark crescents.

Not crack.

Load witness.

Old style.

Mark **8H**.

"Used?"

Pera glanced.

"Triggered twice maybe. Scrap."

"Can reset?"

"Heat. But those old ones drift after third reset."

"How know twice?"

"Crescent layering. First goes brown-black. Reset leaves ghost. Second dark rides ghost."

Useful.

My HT-LP-4 pressure crescent came to mind.

Different witness.

Same family of evidence.

I put scrap.

Pera watched.

"Most people pocket that after asking."

"Most people get paid?"

"Not after."

Good.

Second crate had wind-bowl throat inserts.

Broken.

Clouded.

Some chipped.

One looked intact.

I held to light.

Milky green.

Pera said, "Unknown."

"Looks good."

"Looks is why unknown. Survey rejected lot because response marks missing. Could be road-grade. Could be decorative replacement. Could be old factory second."

"Can test?"

"Not here."

Unknown.

I put unknown.

This was excellent anti-shopping training.

At second bell, warehouse alarm rang.

Not fire.

Three short.

Everyone stopped.

Pera walked cliff opening.

Wind bowls outside had changed tone.

Low to high.

Then high cut out.

"Shelf gust," she said.

Workers started closing outer shutters.

I moved because table nearest opening had loose paper tags.

Grabbed weight bar.

Both hands problem.

Crutches problem.

No trial leg.

I hooked left elbow around one crutch, right hand bar, left hand paper stack.

Ugly.

Worked.

Then gust hit.

Warehouse inhaled.

Loose rope ends lifted.

One hanging coil swung off peg.

Big.

Not deadly unless under.

Worker under.

"Down!"

He ducked.

Coil hit table edge and rolled.

I moved sideways badly.

Crutch tip slipped half hand on hemp dust.

Body corrected before thought.

Right shoulder into rack.

Left crutch recovered.

Coil rolled past.

Pera kicked chock under it.

Done.

Worker swore.

I swore.

Pera looked at me.

"You good?"

"Yes."

Shoulder sting.

No injury.

Crutch fine.

"You yelled first."

"Coil moved."

"Saw."

No medal.

Good.

Wind outside screamed maybe thirty seconds.

Then bowls dropped tone.

Pera reopened one shutter slit.

"Old bowl warned late."

"Which?"

She pointed road corner.

New bronze bowl still vibrating.

Older throat-glass one had gone high first but cut out before gust.

"Glass chipped last week. Should've been replaced."

"Still warned."

"Badly."

"Useful error?"

She looked.

"Observatory got to you."

Apparently phrase local enough.

We reset warehouse.

Worker I'd warned was named Torl.

He said thanks once.

Then made me help count fallen tags.

Correct relationship.

At midday Pera reviewed bins.

Three wrong.

Out of many.

One I had put scrap should have been Survey return because crest hidden under corrosion.

One unknown was keep because current maker mark tiny edge.

One keep was unknown because green slash counterfeit old reseller mark.

"Not bad," she said.

"How much?"

"Three copper."

"Board said two to four."

"You earned three. Four if no wrong. Two if five wrong. One if thief. Zero if stupid enough to argue after thief."

Clear economy.

She paid.

Fifty-three.

There.

Staying tonight now cost less emotionally.

I asked, "These bins going auction?"

"Some. Survey return doesn't. Keep doesn't. Scrap mostly weight sale. Unknown gets mixed inspection lots tomorrow."

"Public can inspect?"

"Auction morning, one bell before."

"Can test?"

"Depends lot. Powered items no. Passive mechanical usually if clerk permits. Charged glass no handling without bond."

Useful.

"Any point coming if I have fifty-three copper and need most of it?"

Pera laughed.

Not mean.

"Then don't bid like collector. Bid like worker. Decide what problem you're buying solution for before bell starts."

That one worth more than three copper.

"Also," she said, "Survey people love lots. They'll bundle one useful thing with six pounds garbage because paperwork hates small objects."

"Excellent."

"Bring bag."

"I'm not saying I'm coming."

"You asked four auction questions."

Fair.

I left.

Sori found me eating fish pie on wall.

"You got job."

"Half day."

"Employment shoulders."

"Temporary shoulders."

"How much?"

"Three."

"Good."

"Stop approving me."

"No."

She had papers.

Of course.

"Market pamphlet is outdated. Harbor customs current. Upper coach board missing transition note. I wrote one letter."

"One?"

"Three drafts. One sent."

"Growth."

"Fuck you."

Nice.

Maro came back with salt on boots.

"Path open."

"See spiders?"

"One."

He sat.

"Big?"

He held fingers maybe palm width.

"That's medium."

"For spider?"

"For fantasy spider."

"It lives on cliff."

"Everything gets points for location."

He had silk strand in folded paper.

"Legal?"

"Warden gave. From fence."

Good.

Dask spent morning university annex reading public lecture notice like it might change.

He arrived with two pages questions.

"You know lecture isn't interrogation."

"Questions are permitted."

"Two pages?"

"Not all."

Worse.

We compared plans.

Dask lecture tomorrow fifth bell.

Auction day after.

Maro wanted another cliff walk maybe.

Sori had no obligation but wanted see harbor customs archive because one clerk told her old tables stored there.

I had fifty-three copper.

Staying tonight two.

Tomorrow another two if staying auction eve.

Food bits.

Then auction temptation.

Then road afterward.

Could earn again.

Maybe.

Not guaranteed.

I looked at trial leg bundle back lodging.

Money wanted become leg.

Auction wanted become options.

Road wanted become more road.

Too many mouths.

I paid tonight bunk.

Two copper.

Fifty-one.

Decision one day at time.

No need prepay future curiosity.

Afternoon stable enough for trial wear.

I used twenty-five minutes in middle-shelf market lanes.

Not stairs.

Cargo ramps.

Medium foot on packed stone.

Crutches light.

Task: buy soap and carry small paper parcel from one stall to lodging.

One hand parcel.

One hand free enough for coin.

Crutches bundled only for final flat stretch.

Fourteen steps carrying parcel.

Stopped because gait got sloppy.

Not because pain.

That distinction felt important.

At lodging shell off.

Upper pink mild.

One faint lower-front line.

Gone at twenty minutes.

Note.

**Merehook middle shelf. 25 min. Packed stone/cargo ramps. Light parcel. 14 unsupported carry steps on final flat. Stopped on gait degradation. No pain. Faint lower-front line cleared within 20.**

No celebration.

Good data.

Evening I went back old-instrument lane.

Cliff-line keeper still six copper.

Seller recognized me.

"Still six."

"I didn't ask."

"Face did."

Apparently city studied faces.

I did not buy.

Delay frame still seven at observatory shop if I wanted climb again.

Did not.

Instead I bought nothing and wrote list.

Not object list.

Problem list.

**Need to measure timing between two events without trusting my count.**

**Need passive load witness where I cannot watch continuously.**

**Need warning before environmental change reaches me.**

**Need mobility that frees hands.**

Last one already had price.

One silver plus eighteen.

Others could wait.

That was maybe what Pera meant.

Don't buy object.

Buy solved problem.

Dask leaned over shoulder.

"You made procurement framework."

"I made list."

"Same."

"No."

"Functionally."

I closed notebook.

"Lecture tomorrow."

"Yes."

"Auction after."

"Yes."

"We might leave before auction."

He paused.

Good.

"Okay."

Actually okay.

Not manipulation.

"Do you want auction?"

"Yes."

"Enough to pay two nights and lose road day?"

He thought.

"I don't know yet."

"Good."

"Stop approving me."

"No."

Stole Sori.

Worked.

Outside, wind bowls started low song.

Not warning.

Just evening wind.

I had fifty-one copper.

Three more than morning after paying tonight? No.

One more than morning.

Work three.

Bed two.

That was road economics.

Not dramatic.

Important.

Auction in two days.

Leg farther away if I got stupid.

Maybe closer if I got useful.

Prospecting wasn't finding treasure.

Mostly deciding which holes not to dig.

# CHAPTER 437

## THE DECIDER

Auction cost started before auction.

Two copper for another night.

Food.

Time.

Opportunity to make worse decisions tomorrow.

I sat on lodging step with notebook open.

**Stay for auction:**

Cost at least two copper lodging.

Maybe more.

Maybe buy nothing.

Maybe buy something useful.

Maybe buy garbage because useful object attached to six pounds bureaucracy.

**Leave before auction:**

Save money.

Keep road moving.

Miss unknown opportunities.

Dask lecture fifth bell today regardless.

Maro cliff path today.

Sori archive maybe.

Me.

No obligation.

Annoying.

Decision harder without emergency.

Emergency decides for you.

Freedom makes paperwork.

I closed notebook.

Sori came out with tea.

"You made list."

"No."

"It's open."

"Former list."

"What are you deciding?"

"Whether auction worth staying."

"Do you want to stay?"

"That's not same question."

"I know."

Rude competence.

"Do you?" she asked.

I looked uphill where upper shelf buildings caught morning light.

Observatory somewhere beyond.

Coastal Survey offices farther east.

Auction tomorrow.

"Yes."

"Then what's actual question?"

"Whether wanting justifies cost."

"Better."

"Stop approving me."

"No."

She sat.

"I'm staying tonight. Archive clerk said I can see prior freight tables this afternoon."

"You know you don't work here."

"I know. That's why I can leave whenever annoyed."

Good argument.

Maro had already gone.

Dask was polishing tourist tide index.

For lecture.

I asked why.

"Presentation."

"You're not presenting."

"Still."

Fine.

I decided not to hunt another job.

One work day enough.

Every chapter didn't need employment.

Instead I went lower harbor.

Not because task.

Because after days of roads and shelves I had barely stood near Merehook water.

Cargo ramp down easier than stairs.

Still long.

Crutches warmed shoulders.

Trial leg stayed wrapped at lodging.

Recovery day.

Good.

Lower harbor was louder than Duskport in different way.

Less broad warehouse roar.

More cliff echo.

Hammer strike bounced between stone walls.

Gulls screamed and got doubled.

Wind bowls sang above ramps.

Water slapped pilings under cliff shadow.

Ships sat close because harbor mouth narrow.

Some had tall deep keels.

Some squat coastal tubs.

One black-hulled packet had two mastheads with glass fins that shimmered in crosswind.

Not decorative maybe.

I looked too long.

Dock porter said, "Wind vanes."

"Magic?"

"Little."

"What do they do?"

"Tell captain which layer lies."

Good sentence.

I asked.

He pointed.

"Lower fin reads deck wind. Upper reads mast wind. If different enough, you don't trust harbor bowl alone."

Layered warning.

Another problem solved by disagreement.

Merehook apparently built entire personality around not trusting one instrument.

Respect.

Hara found me near bonded cart yard.

She looked less furious.

Not happy.

Less furious.

"Security came back," she said.

"All four?"

"All four."

There.

"Appeal approved?"

"Transition exception. They stamped it before second bell."

"Good."

"Still paid night cart. Still lost sleep. Still almost missed ship."

"Also true."

"I filed notice complaint."

"Of course."

She narrowed eyes.

"You sound like woman I met yesterday."

Probably Sori.

"Dangerous city."

Hara laughed.

Better laugh.

Then pointed at me.

"You buying Survey junk tomorrow?"

Apparently whole city knew.

"Maybe."

"Don't buy cracked tension ceramic unless seller lets you heat-reset it first."

"Why?"

"Some darken pretty and tell nothing."

"Useful."

"I cut stone. We use line tags on lifts."

There.

Artifact knowledge from work, not lore dispenser.

"Auction worth staying?"

She shrugged.

"If you know why you're there."

Everyone same lesson.

Conspiracy.

"If you don't?"

"Then merchants know why you're there."

Worse.

I wrote that down.

She saw.

"You really do write too much."

"Apparently."

At third bell, harbor alarm changed.

Not emergency.

One long, two short.

Workers started moving carts away from outer quay.

I asked porter.

"Swell set."

"Observatory?"

"And outer bell. West stair spray probably up again."

Old Tide Eye warning route now ordinary city behavior.

Not dramatic.

Integrated.

Good.

I watched crews chain two light carts together before pulling uphill.

Why?

Wind.

Single empty cart easier to flip.

Paired carts heavier and stable.

Merehook answered environment with weird habits accumulated into normal.

That was what I wanted from travel.

Not different wallpaper.

Different grammar.

At midday I found auction posting outside Survey yard.

Public preview tomorrow first bell.

Auction second.

Terms:

cash settlement same day.

No credit under one silver lot.

Charged glass requires handling bond.

Certified Survey staff first-claim period ended today.

Lots posted by number only until morning inspection.

No refunds on `as retired` condition unless clerk records material misdescription.

There.

Real risk.

I copied rules.

Not lots.

Could decide attendance tonight.

Still.

Then human pressure found us anyway.

Near customs ramp, crowd around one wagon.

Not fight.

Argument.

Two fishermen.

One cart owner.

One harbor tally clerk.

Broken axle.

Cart loaded with iced whitefish.

Wheel canted.

Fish going bad if not moved.

Owner wanted unload onto customs floor.

Clerk said floor bonded for sealed import only, local fish not authorized there.

Fisherman said then let them block ramp while transferring.

Clerk said ramp had outbound tide cargo due.

Everyone had legitimate problem.

I stopped ten paces away.

Employment shoulders threatened.

I rolled them back.

Not mine.

Then one fisherman recognized Hara? No.

Recognized my crutches maybe.

"You know rigging?"

Bad question.

"Some."

"Can you tell if we can sling axle enough to roll cart?"

There.

Now bounded.

"I can look. Not certify."

"Fine."

Cart axle split near hub.

Wheel leaned but not detached.

Load maybe too much for improvised carry.

They had rope.

Bad rope.

Frayed.

I said, "Don't lift whole cart on that."

Owner said, "We just need six paces."

"Still whole cart."

Harbor clerk said, "There is dock jack twenty paces down."

Everyone stared.

Fisherman said, "Why didn't you say?"

"You were yelling about floor authorization."

Fair.

They fetched jack.

Problem changed.

Not solved.

Need support once raised.

Harbor carpenter had blocking.

Five minutes later they had wheel relieved enough to unload fish into two handcarts without blocking ramp.

I did nothing except say frayed rope bad.

Excellent.

Clerk looked at me.

"You Survey?"

"No."

"Good."

"Why good?"

"Survey would write form."

Merehook had opinions.

I left before becoming fish employee.

Fourth bell approached.

University annex uphill.

Dask lecture.

I met Sori outside.

She carried copies.

"Archive?"

"Yes."

"Fun?"

"No."

"Then why face?"

"Because old table changes explain three current abbreviations."

"Fun."

"Fuck you."

We entered.

Lecture room small.

Thirty seats.

Maybe eighteen people.

Students.

Two harbor workers.

One old woman knitting.

Dask front row.

Of course.

Speaker **Ilen Marr** arrived late with chalk dust on sleeve.

Mid-forties.

Hair tied poorly.

Talk title exactly what notice promised:

**TIDAL HARMONICS AND CLIFF-CHAMBER LAG IN MEREHOOK OBSERVATION SYSTEMS**

Dask looked spiritually fulfilled.

I prepared for suffering.

Lecture better than expected.

Ilen did not explain tides from first principles.

Blessing.

She focused on why cliff chambers distort timing.

Main idea:

A pressure signal entering narrow sea chamber does not arrive as simple copy of offshore wave.

Geometry stores and releases pressure.

Some frequencies amplify.

Some cancel.

Temperature changes gas pocket compressibility.

Cracks alter effective chamber volume.

So two instruments connected to same sea can disagree without either being broken.

Dask wrote violently.

I wrote one sentence:

**Delay can belong to medium, not source.**

That mattered.

Magic.

Body.

Tools.

If external effect arrived late, maybe not weak attempt alone.

Could be channel.

Could be environment.

Could be me.

Could be all.

Useful caution.

Not breakthrough.

After talk, questions.

Dask had two pages.

He asked one.

Good restraint.

"If chamber geometry changes slowly, how do you distinguish instrument drift from medium drift?"

Ilen smiled.

Actual good question.

"You don't from one instrument."

Of course Merehook answer.

"You need independent reference, repeated conditions, or controlled excitation if system permits. Usually all three badly."

Dask nodded like receiving sacred text.

Someone else asked about rebuilding old chamber.

Ilen said impossible without changing thing measured.

Another asked whether magical damping could stabilize response.

"Yes," Ilen said, "and then you've built different instrument."

Good.

After lecture, Dask cornered her.

Not literally.

Socially.

He showed tourist tide index.

Ilen said, "That's souvenir."

"I know now."

"Good souvenir."

Dask glowed.

She asked where from.

Merehook market.

Then told him maker copied housing from an old South Shelf training dial.

So fake had lineage.

Dask loved it more.

I left him there.

Sori went with me.

"You got something from that."

"Maybe."

"What?"

"Delay can belong to medium, not source."

She waited.

"Magic?"

"Maybe."

"Leg?"

"Maybe."

"People?"

I looked at her.

"Unfortunately."

She laughed.

Evening decision.

Back lodging.

Maro returned from cliff path with no purchases and one sketch of spider web geometry.

Dask returned with lecture notes and no new object.

Miracles.

We ate cheap stew.

I put money on blanket.

Fifty-one copper.

If stay tonight, two copper.

Already tonight? Wait.

Current lodging paid through morning from last chapter.

Need tonight to reach auction tomorrow.

Two copper.

Forty-nine.

Auction budget separate.

Road after.

Leg.

I asked group.

"I'm staying auction."

Sori said, "Okay."

Dask said, "Also."

Maro thought.

"I might leave after morning cliff path if north cart goes."

There.

Not hive.

"Without us?"

"Maybe."

"Where?"

"Salt farms east. One day."

Interesting.

Sori said, "I might stay one more day after auction if customs archive lets me copy old table set."

Also interesting.

Party could fragment.

Good.

I paid two copper for night.

Fifty-one to forty-nine.

Decision made.

Auction tomorrow.

Not because artifact destiny.

Because I wanted to see what became available, understood cost, and chose it anyway.

That felt different from impulse.

I wrote auction cap.

Not total spending cap yet.

Problem caps.

**Timing tool:** max 5c unless complete enough to use immediately.

**Passive load witness:** max 4c unless reset state demonstrated.

**Environmental warning:** max 4c unless portable and independent.

**Unknown lot:** max 2c, only if one identifiable useful component already worth that.

Then one more:

**Do not spend leg money because auction brain says rarity.**

Hard to quantify.

Important.

Dask leaned over.

"You made procurement framework again."

"Auction limits."

"Same family."

"Go away."

"We're sharing room."

I sent note before dinner.

Not Carrow directly maybe. Posting route south via Merehook/Fen.

**Lerrin. North coast. Dask with me. Maro east salt farms. Sori stayed Merehook one more day. Bought one retired timing demonstrator at Survey auction for 5c. Still have leg trial build. Fine. Not heading home yet. - Greg**

Paid bit postage.

No copper.

Then dinner.

Cheap stew.

Dask unfolded local beacon notice.

Of course.

"Public head walk tomorrow second bell."

"Of course."

"Old south relay housing displayed."

"Of course."

"There is also repair yard."

"Public?"

"No."

"Then stop mentally entering it."

"I wasn't."

"Spiritually trespassing."

Pera useful everywhere.

He smiled.

I looked around attic.

Two bunks empty.

Sori not here.

Maro not here.

Carrow very not here.

Just me and Dask in another town because we chose same road today.

That was different from party.

Companion maybe.

Not permanent.

Not assigned.

Someone whose road overlapped mine because both wanted what was ahead.

For now.

Outside, beacon light flashed through attic window.

White.

Pause.

Amber.

Pause.

Green.

Dask counted under breath.

I didn't.

I had tool for that now.

Later.

Tomorrow maybe beacon works.

After that maybe packet.

Maybe north road.

Maybe something else.

Forty copper.

One unfinished leg.

One questionable timing device.

One companion.

No return ticket.

Good.

# CHAPTER 440

## THE LOOKOUT

Lerrin woke in colors.

White from south head.

Amber farther north.

Green once from bay mouth.

Then nothing for maybe half minute.

Then white again.

I watched through attic window while Dask put on boots.

"What does white mean?"

"Depends station."

"You don't know."

"Not yet."

"Growth."

He looked offended.

Good.

South Lamp House breakfast cost one bit for oat porridge and something claimed to be apple.

Might once have known apple.

Public head walk opened second bell.

We went.

Trial leg stayed wrapped.

Not because skin bad.

Because South Head path looked like stone, stairs, railings, exposed wind, and public people who would step around me while staring at lights.

Crutches better.

Tool for terrain.

Not moral issue.

Lerrin town stretched along bay instead of stacking up cliff.

Low black roofs.

Oil stores behind stone walls.

Signal masts everywhere.

Not decorative.

Shop roofs had little numbered plates facing head.

Warehouses had painted circles visible from towers.

Even fish market bell had colored shutters.

Dask pointed.

"Red shutter."

"Yes."

"Why?"

Fish seller said, without looking up, "Means market wants harbor runner and not fire brigade."

Dask slowed.

"There are color conventions below beacon level."

Seller looked at him now.

"There are colors everywhere, love."

Dask nearly wrote that down.

I stopped him with face.

He wrote anyway.

South Head public path began behind packet office.

A sign:

**PUBLIC WALK / SECOND TO FOURTH BELL**

**STAY BEHIND WHITE LINE**

**DO NOT TOUCH MIRRORS**

**DO NOT SHOUT SIGNAL COLORS AS JOKE**

Specific rules told history.

Attendant at gate wore blue coat with one silver stripe.

"Two bits each."

We paid.

Copper unchanged.

"Repair yard closed. Upper relay closed. Public platform only. If bell rings twice, path clears immediately."

Dask said, "What does three mean?"

"Not your problem."

Excellent.

South Head was less tower than machine village.

Short stone pylons stood in rows along outer ridge.

Some held mirrors.

Some glass lamps.

Some metal shutters.

Two tall masts carried suspended glass drums like giant lantern lenses without flame.

Between them ran narrow channels in paving.

Protected wire maybe.

Or light conduit.

Or both.

Beacon staff moved constantly.

Not priests.

Workers.

One cleaned salt from mirror with rag.

One swapped colored plates.

One checked tiny brass windows in pedestal.

One yelled at gull.

Gull ignored institutional authority.

Public path had plaques.

Dask read every one.

I read maybe half.

Old South Relay housing sat under shelter.

Big iron frame with rotating shutter wheel.

No longer connected.

Plaque said first network used line-of-sight mirror flashes by day and oil-color drums by night.

Later charged glass improved distance.

Current system mixed all three because replacement schedules were apparently written by someone who hated uniformity.

Dask loved it.

"Hybrid generations."

"Yes."

"Different failure modes."

"Yes."

"Redundancy."

"Sometimes."

He looked at me.

"Sometimes?"

"Different systems can also disagree."

He smiled.

"Good."

We had become terrible people.

Third pylon flashed white.

Far north answered amber.

Then closer inland station answered amber.

South Head worker stopped wiping mirror.

Another looked up.

No alarm.

Just attention.

Dask stopped talking.

Good instinct.

Far north flashed amber again.

South pylon answered white-white.

I didn't know language.

Could feel grammar.

Question maybe.

Confirmation maybe.

A worker crossed public line to pedestal and opened brass panel.

Gate attendant said, "Keep moving."

Public continued.

Then two bells rang.

There.

Everyone stopped.

Attendant voice changed.

"Public off head. Now please."

No panic.

We turned.

Dask looked back once.

"Don't," I said.

"I wasn't."

"You were spiritually trespassing."

He sighed.

Halfway down path, packet-office clerk came running uphill.

Actually running.

Not good for bureaucracy.

"South Head! Vey packet is at tide gate!"

Beacon worker coming down shouted, "Then hold her!"

"Captain says amber is stale!"

"Captain can say it from dock!"

There.

Human problem.

By time we reached packet yard, argument already had five people.

Captain of Vey packet.

Packet clerk.

Beacon duty officer.

Young relay runner.

Old woman holding basket who seemed uninvolved but emotionally committed.

Vey packet visible at lower quay.

Narrow twin-masted vessel.

Cargo already aboard.

Maybe twenty passengers.

Tide going out.

Captain had red scarf and expression of person losing money by minute.

"North Nine has been amber since dawn," she said. "Sea is fine. Bellweather runner came through an hour ago."

Duty officer said, "North Nine amber is not sea-state only."

"I know what amber means."

"Then you know it can mean visibility relay uncertain."

"I can see north cape."

"You cannot see Vey approach from here."

"My lookout can from outer water."

"After you leave protected bay."

Captain swore.

Packet clerk said, "If she misses this tide, next departure tomorrow. Twenty-three passengers, perishables, mail."

Duty officer said, "If North Nine cannot confirm green corridor, departure stays local-risk only."

Captain said, "I'm accepting local risk."

"Passengers didn't buy local-risk passage."

That changed room.

Good problem.

Not simple.

Captain could go.

Maybe.

But passengers' contract assumed network-confirmed route.

Correct procedure mattered.

So did tide.

Nobody asked me anything.

Excellent.

Dask whispered, "What happened?"

"Don't know."

We stood edge.

Packet office board updated:

**VEY / HOLD / NORTH RELAY UNCERTAIN**

Passengers started coming inside.

Young man angry.

Woman with baby tired.

Two traders talking spoilage.

One old sailor said he'd sailed without colored lamps for thirty years.

Someone told him congratulations.

Then beacon runner arrived from head.

"North Nine receiving. Return line intermittent."

Duty officer: "Cause?"

"Unknown. Bellweather Seven repeating amber because Nine hasn't returned sequence complete."

Captain: "So it's relay fault."

Duty officer: "Maybe."

"Sea is fine."

"Maybe."

"You people love maybe."

"Sea loves killing people who hate it."

Not sarcasm.

Old professional fight.

Dask leaned closer to me.

"Return line intermittent. Could be timing."

"Could be anything."

"Yes."

Growth continued.

Duty officer ordered manual optical confirmation from South Head to Bellweather Seven, then Bellweather to North Nine if visibility permitted.

Packet clerk groaned.

"How long?"

"If clean, twenty minutes."

Captain looked at tide marker.

"I have thirty-five before channel costs me draft."

There.

Clock.

Dask's face did thing.

"No," I said.

"I didn't say anything."

"Your face wants Lot Sixty-Seven."

"It measures delay."

"It has sticky bead and no seconds scale."

"Correct."

"We are not calibrating national beacon network with five-copper auction trash."

"Regional."

"Dask."

"I agree."

Good.

We waited.

This was more educational than public walk.

Not because we were involved.

Because city operating under uncertainty was better than plaque.

South Head flashed sequence.

White-white-amber-white.

Bellweather answered maybe fifteen seconds later.

Hard to know.

My count unreliable.

Interesting.

Did not unpack tool.

Again.

Bellweather then flashed north.

We could barely see next station.

Tiny white point.

Wait.

Nothing.

Wait.

Amber.

Duty officer said, "Again."

Captain said nothing.

Second sequence.

South.

Bellweather.

North.

This time north answered white.

Then green.

Packet room changed.

Not celebration.

Motion.

Captain: "That's corridor green."

Duty officer: "One clean return. Need two."

"You'll cost tide."

"Need two."

Captain slammed palm table.

Not tantrum.

Fear with money attached.

"If second takes twenty, I lose departure."

Duty officer said, quieter, "If I lower standard because your tide is expensive, then standard isn't standard."

Captain stared.

There was no villain.

Again.

I hated book sometimes.

Third sequence began.

This one fast at South.

Bellweather answered.

North nothing.

Five seconds.

Ten.

Dask muttered numbers.

I let him.

Fifteen.

North flashed white.

Then green.

Duty officer looked wall clock.

"Two clean returns. Corridor green. Packet may depart under normal terms."

Captain was already moving.

Packet clerk shouted down quay.

Passengers surged.

Problem solved?

Not entirely.

Captain had maybe twelve minutes before safe draft threshold.

Loading already done.

People had to board fast.

Mother with baby couldn't move fast.

Old sailor could but complained anyway.

Packet crew started sorting.

No pushing.

Priority families down first.

Freight doors shut.

Captain herself came back for old woman with cane.

Interesting.

She wasn't monster.

Just wanted ship out.

Vey packet cast off with maybe four minutes to spare.

Duty officer watched from yard.

No satisfaction.

Just next board entry.

Dask exhaled.

"That was excellent."

Packet clerk looked at him.

"It was terrible."

"Technically excellent."

"You're not helping."

"Correct."

We left.

South Head public walk remained closed.

Fine.

Lerrin had shown itself anyway.

We ate lunch on sea wall.

Dask drew signal sequence from memory.

"You sure?" I asked.

"Mostly."

"Then label mostly."

He did.

Good.

I looked at Road Response Kit in pack.

Could characterize safely now.

Not network.

Bench.

Low stakes.

We found empty stone table behind Lamp House sheltered from wind.

No trial leg today.

Road Response Kit out.

Dry cloth.

No oil.

Track inspected.

Sticky near four.

I mounted clamp to table.

Nonstandard jaw held.

Test one: Dask displace tongue, I press manual trigger when I see movement.

We repeated ten times.

Marks mostly between two and three.

One near four.

Sticky zone?

Maybe.

Swapped roles.

Dask mostly one to two.

Again.

Not time.

Comparison.

Then controlled test.

Instead of reaction, Dask displaced tongue and pressed manual trigger himself immediately with other hand.

Ten repetitions.

Mostly below one mark.

Two hung near four.

There.

Instrument error visible.

Not human reaction.

Sticky track created false long delays occasionally.

Good.

We wrote:

**Lot 67 characterization, Lerrin sheltered table. Manual same-person immediate trigger: 8/10 below one mark, 2/10 false long near fourth-mark hesitation. Comparative human-response readings unreliable until sticky-track failure isolated. Do not use for body/magic conclusions yet.**

Dask said, "Can disassemble?"

"Not until know track material."

"Could ask beacon repair people."

"Public repair yard closed."

"Could ask someone outside."

"Ask, yes. Enter, no."

He nodded.

We packed.

Tool had failed usefully.

Not broken.

Characterized enough to know current limitation mattered.

That felt like progress.

Afternoon I walked lower bay alone while Dask went looking for public beacon manuals.

Packet board now:

**VEY DEPARTED**

**OLD MERROW / TOMORROW THIRD BELL / 4C**

**HARROW CAPE / WEATHER HOLD / CHECK DAWN**

**BELLWEATHER ROAD COACH / TOMORROW SECOND BELL / 2C**

There.

Next roads with prices.

Vey departure gone today.

Could take another on fifth day later.

Old Merrow tomorrow four copper.

Bellweather road two.

Harrow Cape uncertain.

I asked clerk.

"What's Old Merrow?"

"Island town."

"How far?"

"Packet half day if sea kind."

"Why four copper?"

"Water."

"Compelling."

"You asked."

"Harrow Cape?"

"Farther north-west. Rough crossing. Weather permitting means weather actually permitting, not passengers having feelings."

"Bellweather?"

"Road town. Beacon relay. Two copper coach, maybe five bells."

"Lodging?"

"Cheap. Boring."

"Useful."

"If you like boring."

I did sometimes.

Could stay Lerrin.

One copper bunk.

Could go Bellweather for two.

Could go Old Merrow for four.

Could wait Harrow Cape.

Dask would probably want Bellweather because relay station.

Maybe I didn't.

Interesting.

At dusk Dask returned with pamphlet and expression.

"Bellweather Seven has public lower relay room."

Of course.

"Tomorrow?"

"Second bell coach."

"Two copper."

"Yes."

"You going?"

"Probably."

"Good."

He looked.

"You?"

"Don't know."

He accepted.

Actual growth.

We paid another one copper each for South Lamp House because tonight already needed.

Mine forty to thirty-nine.

Food bits.

No other copper.

Before bed, I used trial leg.

Not outside.

Attic corridor.

Flat wood.

Twenty minutes.

Task was pack rearrangement.

Both hands.

Road Response Kit now needed better place.

I stood at bunk, trial leg bearing enough to free hands while I moved harness straps, wrapped brass frame, shifted clothes, retied crutch wrap.

Minimal walking.

Maybe eight flat steps total.

More standing than walking.

At fifteen minutes hip tired.

At eighteen gait on turn sloppy.

Stopped twenty.

Skin:

upper pink mild.

Lower-front faint.

Cleared at twenty.

No pain.

Note.

**Lerrin South Lamp House. 20 min. Indoor flat wood. Standing packing task, both hands. Minimal steps. Hip fatigue ~15 min. Turn quality degraded ~18. Mild upper/lower-front pink cleared.**

Tool and leg interacted indirectly.

Artifact created packing work.

Leg freed hands to do packing.

Tiny system.

Real.

Dask read pamphlet while I checked skin.

"Bellweather signal room has old line-delay drum."

"Good for it."

"Public display."

"Congratulations."

"You're tired."

"Yes."

"You don't want Bellweather?"

I thought.

"Maybe I do. Maybe I want boat."

"Old Merrow?"

"Maybe."

He looked at pamphlet.

Then me.

"We can split."

Simple.

No hurt.

"Yes."

That was becoming normal.

Outside beacon lights moved through sequence.

White.

Amber.

Green.

Someone far away watched.

Someone answered.

Nobody owned whole line.

Every station only saw part and trusted next station to carry rest.

Maybe companion worked like that too.

Maybe not.

Too neat.

I rejected metaphor.

Good.

Tomorrow I would choose road or water.

Tonight I watched lights.
